================================================================================
     STAGE 6: MIDDLE SCHOOL — GRADES 7-8 (Age ~12-14) — QUESTIONS AND ANSWERS
     Pre-algebra & algebra begins
================================================================================

How to use this file (note for the parent / teacher)
----------------------------------------------------
- There are 38 topics (6.1 to 6.38, the same as in topic.txt). Each topic
  has:
    * WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN   the checklist of skills in that topic
    * PART A — DESCRIPTIVE        12 questions (D1-D12): real-life
                                  problems, worked examples, "explain why"
                                  and short proof questions, each with the
                                  answer and "Teacher explains" (the method
                                  step by step as a teacher shows it on the
                                  board, common mistakes, and a "Now you
                                  try" question for the student)
    * PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY  12 questions (M1-M12): pure number,
                                  algebra and diagram drills with answers.
                                  Most have many parts (a, b, c ...) so
                                  that EVERY case of this stage is
                                  practised — every law of exponents,
                                  every identity and factorisation type,
                                  every angle pair with parallel lines,
                                  every transformation rule, every sum of
                                  two dice, and so on — not just one
                                  example.
    * TEACHING TIPS               what to do if the student struggles, and
                                  how to make it harder
- In total: 456 descriptive + 456 mathematical questions, then a mixed
  revision test (equal descriptive and mathematical parts) and a readiness
  checklist for Stage 7.
- What is new in Stage 6: rational and irrational numbers, exponent laws
  and scientific notation; ALGEBRA in earnest (identities, factorising,
  algebraic fractions, multi-step equations, inequalities, simultaneous
  equations); proportion and compound interest; straight-line graphs,
  slope and functions; parallel-line angles, congruence and PYTHAGORAS;
  polygons, circles, surface area and volume of solids; transformations
  and similarity; statistics (histograms, pie charts, scatter graphs, box
  plots, sampling); probability with tree diagrams and counting; the first
  PROOFS; modular arithmetic; and digital tools.
- Order: roughly follow the topic numbers. Suggested strands to run side
  by side through the year:
    Number              6.1-6.5
    Algebra             6.6-6.12, 6.15-6.18
    Proportion & money  6.13-6.14
    Geometry & measure  6.19-6.28
    Statistics          6.29-6.32
    Probability         6.33-6.35
    Reasoning & tools   6.36-6.38
- Method: keep using pictures and models alongside symbols — algebra
  tiles and area models for identities and factorising, balance pictures
  for equations, graphs for systems, dynamic geometry (GeoGebra/Desmos)
  for angle and transformation facts, and simulations for probability.
- Write working neatly: one step per line, "=" signs lined up, reasons
  given for geometry steps ("alternate angles", "angle sum of a
  triangle"), and units with every answer.
- Do 45-60 minutes a day: 2-3 descriptive questions and 2-3 drills per
  session. Revisit earlier topics weekly (mixed practice beats blocked
  practice for long-term memory).
- Always ask: "Why does this work?", "Can you check it another way?"
  and "Is your answer reasonable?"
- Revise Stage 5 (A5.txt) whenever the student finds something difficult.


================================================================================
TOPIC 6.1 — RATIONAL NUMBERS: PROPERTIES, NUMBER LINE, OPERATIONS
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - A RATIONAL NUMBER is any number that can be written as p/q, where p and q
    are INTEGERS and q ≠ 0: 3/4, −5/7, 6 (= 6/1), 0 (= 0/1), 0.75 (= 3/4),
    −2 1/2 (= −5/2). Every integer and every fraction is rational.
  - The minus sign can be written in three places: −3/4 = (−3)/4 = 3/(−4). Also
    (−3)/(−4) = 3/4.
  - STANDARD FORM (simplest form): the denominator is POSITIVE and p and q have
    no common factor except 1. To get it, move the sign to the top and divide p
    and q by their HCF: 18/(−24) = −3/4.
  - EQUIVALENT rational numbers: multiply or divide the numerator and the
    denominator by the SAME non-zero integer: −2/3 = −4/6 = 8/(−12).
  - On the NUMBER LINE, cut each unit into q equal parts; positive rationals go
    to the right of 0, negative ones to the left. −3/4 is the mirror image of
    3/4.
  - COMPARING: make the denominators positive and equal (or cross-multiply): the
    one further RIGHT is greater. Every negative number is less than 0, and 0 is
    less than every positive number: −5/6 < −2/3 < 0 < 1/2.
  - Between any two different rational numbers there are INFINITELY MANY
    rational numbers: the mean (a + b)/2 is always between a and b, or use
    equivalent fractions with bigger denominators.
  - OPERATIONS: add and subtract with a common denominator; multiply numerators
    and denominators (cancel first); to divide, multiply by the RECIPROCAL. The
    sign rules are the same as for integers.
  - PROPERTIES: the rationals are CLOSED under +, − and × (and ÷ except by 0). +
    and × are COMMUTATIVE and ASSOCIATIVE; − and ÷ are not. × DISTRIBUTES over +
    and −. 0 is the additive IDENTITY, 1 the multiplicative identity. The
    additive inverse of a/b is −a/b; the RECIPROCAL (multiplicative inverse) of
    a/b is b/a; 0 has no reciprocal.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. Which of these are rational numbers? Write each one in the form p/q: 7, −12,
    0, 0.6, −1.25, 2 1/3, −5/(−8).
    Answer: All of them. 7 = 7/1, −12 = −12/1, 0 = 0/1, 0.6 = 3/5, −1.25 = −5/4,
    2 1/3 = 7/3, −5/(−8) = 5/8.
    Teacher explains: A number is rational if we can write it as p/q with p and
    q integers and q not zero. An integer n is n/1. A terminating decimal is a
    fraction over 10, 100, 1,000 ...: 0.6 = 6/10 = 3/5 and 1.25 = 125/100 = 5/4,
    so −1.25 = −5/4. A mixed number becomes an improper fraction: 2 1/3 = (2 × 3
    + 1)/3 = 7/3. A negative divided by a negative is positive, so −5/(−8) =
    5/8. Zero is rational (0/1, or 0/7), but 7/0 is NOT a number at all: we can
    never divide by 0. Recurring decimals such as 0.333... = 1/3 are rational
    too. Now you try: write −0.35 and 4 2/5 as p/q. (−7/20; 22/5)

D2. Write 18/(−24) and (−36)/(−60) in standard form.
    Answer: 18/(−24) = −3/4 and (−36)/(−60) = 3/5.
    Teacher explains: Step 1: make the denominator positive by multiplying the
    top and the bottom by −1: 18/(−24) = (−18)/24. Step 2: divide both by the
    HCF. HCF(18, 24) = 6, so (−18)/24 = (−3)/4 = −3/4. For (−36)/(−60): multiply
    top and bottom by −1 to get 36/60; HCF(36, 60) = 12, so 36/60 = 3/5. Check:
    3 and 5 have no common factor and the denominator is positive, so 3/5 is in
    standard form. Common mistakes: leaving a minus sign in the denominator, and
    dividing by a common factor that is not the HIGHEST (18/24 = 9/12 is not yet
    simplest). Now you try: 45/(−75) and (−28)/(−98). (−3/5; 2/7)

D3. Fill in the blanks so that the rational numbers are equivalent: −3/5 = 6/__
    = __/(−25) = −27/__.
    Answer: −3/5 = 6/(−10) = 15/(−25) = −27/45.
    Teacher explains: Find what the KNOWN part was multiplied by, then do the
    same to the other part. −3 → 6 is × (−2), so 5 × (−2) = −10: 6/(−10). 5 →
    −25 is × (−5), so −3 × (−5) = 15: 15/(−25). −3 → −27 is × 9, so 5 × 9 = 45:
    −27/45. Check each by putting it in standard form: 6/(−10) = −3/5, 15/(−25)
    = −3/5, −27/45 = −3/5. Multiplying the top and bottom by a NEGATIVE number
    is allowed: it changes both signs, and the value stays the same. Now you
    try: 4/(−7) = −16/__ = __/(−35). (28; 20)

D4. Show −3/4, 5/4, −1 1/2 and 7/4 on a number line.
                  P        Q                       R     S
        <---+--+--+--+--+--+--+--+--+--+--+--+--+--+--+--+--+--->
           −2          −1           0           1           2
    Answer: Cut each unit into 4 equal parts. −1 1/2 = −6/4 is at P, −3/4 at Q,
    5/4 at R and 7/4 at S.
    Teacher explains: The denominator 4 tells us to cut every unit (0 to 1, 1 to
    2, −1 to 0, −2 to −1) into 4 equal quarters. From 0 count 5 quarters to the
    RIGHT for 5/4 (one whole and one quarter) and 7 quarters for 7/4. For −3/4
    count 3 quarters to the LEFT of 0; it is the mirror image of 3/4. −1 1/2 =
    −6/4, so count 6 quarters to the left; it is halfway between −1 and −2.
    Common mistake: counting quarters to the left from −1 instead of from 0. The
    order on the line gives −6/4 < −3/4 < 5/4 < 7/4. Now you try: where are −5/3
    and 2/3 on a line cut into thirds? (5 thirds left of 0; 2 thirds right of 0)

D5. The temperature in a freezer was −3/4 °C at one time and −5/7 °C later.
    Which temperature was lower? Also compare −5/9 and 4/(−7).
    Answer: −3/4 < −5/7, so −3/4 °C was lower. −5/9 > 4/(−7).
    Teacher explains: Method 1 (common denominator): LCM(4, 7) = 28. −3/4 =
    −21/28 and −5/7 = −20/28. −21 < −20, so −21/28 < −20/28: −3/4 is further
    LEFT on the number line, so it is smaller (colder). Method 2
    (cross-multiplication, with POSITIVE denominators): compare (−3) × 7 = −21
    with (−5) × 4 = −20. For −5/9 and 4/(−7) = −4/7: (−5) × 7 = −35 and (−4) × 9
    = −36; −35 > −36, so −5/9 > −4/7. Common mistake: thinking −3/4 is greater
    because 3/4 > 5/7. For negative numbers the one with the BIGGER size is the
    SMALLER number. Now you try: compare −2/3 and −7/10. (−2/3 > −7/10)

D6. Arrange in ascending order: −2/3, 5/(−6), 1/2, −7/12, 0, 3/4.
    Answer: 5/(−6) < −2/3 < −7/12 < 0 < 1/2 < 3/4.
    Teacher explains: First make every denominator positive: 5/(−6) = −5/6. The
    LCM of 3, 6, 2, 12 and 4 is 12, so write each with denominator 12: −2/3 =
    −8/12, −5/6 = −10/12, 1/2 = 6/12, −7/12, 0 = 0/12, 3/4 = 9/12. Now order the
    numerators: −10 < −8 < −7 < 0 < 6 < 9. Write the ORIGINAL numbers in that
    order. Quick check: the negatives come first, then 0, then the positives.
    Now you try: arrange −1/2, 2/(−5), −3/10 in ascending order. (−1/2 < 2/(−5)
    < −3/10)

D7. Find five rational numbers between −1/3 and 1/4. Explain why there are
    infinitely many.
    Answer: For example −3/12, −2/12, −1/12, 0, 1/12, 2/12 (any five of these).
    In standard form: −1/4, −1/6, −1/12, 0, 1/12, 1/6.
    Teacher explains: Make the denominators equal: LCM(3, 4) = 12, so −1/3 =
    −4/12 and 1/4 = 3/12. The numerators −3, −2, −1, 0, 1, 2 lie between −4 and
    3, so −3/12, ..., 2/12 lie between. If we need MORE numbers, use a bigger
    common denominator: −1/3 = −40/120 and 1/4 = 30/120, giving 69 numbers
    −39/120, ..., 29/120 in between. Another way: the MEAN is always between:
    (−1/3 + 1/4) ÷ 2 = (−1/12) ÷ 2 = −1/24. We can take the mean again and
    again, so the process never ends: between ANY two different rationals there
    are infinitely many rationals. (There is no 'next' rational after 0!) Now
    you try: the number halfway between −1/2 and 1/3. (−1/12)

D8. The water level in a tank fell by 3/8 m in the morning and rose by 5/6 m in
    the evening. Find the total change. Then add −5/12 and −7/18.
    Answer: −3/8 + 5/6 = −9/24 + 20/24 = 11/24, so the level rose 11/24 m
    overall. −5/12 + (−7/18) = −15/36 + (−14/36) = −29/36.
    Teacher explains: A fall is negative, a rise is positive. To add, use the
    LCM of the denominators: LCM(8, 6) = 24. −3/8 = −9/24 and 5/6 = 20/24. Now
    add the numerators like integers: −9 + 20 = 11, so 11/24. For the second:
    LCM(12, 18) = 36, −5/12 = −15/36, −7/18 = −14/36, and (−15) + (−14) = −29:
    −29/36. Common mistakes: adding the denominators (−3/8 + 5/6 is NOT 2/14),
    and losing the minus sign. Always simplify at the end. Now you try: −7/10 +
    4/15. (−13/30)

D9. A diver is 5/6 m below the surface of the water and a bird is 3/4 m above
    it. How far apart are they? Work out 3/4 − (−5/6).
    Answer: 3/4 − (−5/6) = 3/4 + 5/6 = 9/12 + 10/12 = 19/12 = 1 7/12 m.
    Teacher explains: Heights above the water are positive and depths below are
    negative: the bird is at 3/4 and the diver at −5/6. Distance apart = higher
    − lower = 3/4 − (−5/6). Subtracting a number means ADDING ITS ADDITIVE
    INVERSE: − (−5/6) becomes + 5/6. LCM(4, 6) = 12, so 9/12 + 10/12 = 19/12.
    More examples: −2/3 − 1/6 = −4/6 − 1/6 = −5/6 and −1/2 − (−3/5) = −1/2 + 3/5
    = −5/10 + 6/10 = 1/10. Common mistake: 3/4 − 5/6 = −1/12 (forgetting that
    the diver is BELOW). Now you try: −3/7 − (−1/2). (1/14)

D10. Multiply: (−7/12) × (8/21) and (−3/5) × (−10/9). Show the cancelling.
     Answer: (−7/12) × (8/21) = −2/9 and (−3/5) × (−10/9) = 2/3.
     Teacher explains: Rule: (a/b) × (c/d) = (a × c)/(b × d). Decide the SIGN
     first: negative × positive is negative, negative × negative is positive.
     Then cancel common factors across the top and bottom BEFORE multiplying: 7
     and 21 share 7 (1 and 3), 8 and 12 share 4 (2 and 3). So (7/12) × (8/21) =
     (1 × 2)/(3 × 3) = 2/9, and the answer is −2/9. For the second: 3 and 9
     share 3 (1 and 3), 10 and 5 share 5 (2 and 1), giving (1 × 2)/(1 × 3) =
     2/3, positive. Cancelling first keeps the numbers small. Special cases: any
     number × 0 = 0, × 1 = itself, × (−1) = its additive inverse. Now you try:
     (−15/16) × (−4/25). (3/20)

D11. A ribbon 15/2 m long is cut into pieces each 3/4 m long. How many pieces
     are there? Also work out (−4/9) ÷ (8/15).
     Answer: 15/2 ÷ 3/4 = 15/2 × 4/3 = 10 pieces. (−4/9) ÷ (8/15) = (−4/9) ×
     (15/8) = −5/6.
     Teacher explains: Dividing by a number is the same as multiplying by its
     RECIPROCAL (turn it upside down): the reciprocal of 3/4 is 4/3. 15/2 × 4/3:
     cancel 15 and 3 (5 and 1) and 4 and 2 (2 and 1), giving 5 × 2 = 10. Check:
     10 × 3/4 = 30/4 = 15/2. For the second, the reciprocal of 8/15 is 15/8;
     cancel 4 and 8 (1 and 2), 15 and 9 (5 and 3): −(1 × 5)/(3 × 2) = −5/6.
     Signs: different signs give a negative answer. The reciprocal of a negative
     number is negative: the reciprocal of −2/7 is −7/2. Only the SECOND number
     is turned over. 0 ÷ (any non-zero number) = 0, but dividing BY 0 is
     impossible. Now you try: (−9/10) ÷ (−3/5). (3/2)

D12. Take a = −2/3, b = 3/4 and c = −5/6. Check the commutative, associative and
     distributive properties with these numbers, and show that subtraction is
     not commutative.
     Answer: a + b = b + a = 1/12; a × b = b × a = −1/2; (a + b) + c = a + (b +
     c) = −3/4; (a × b) × c = a × (b × c) = 5/12; a × (b + c) = a × b + a × c =
     1/18; a − b = −17/12 but b − a = 17/12.
     Teacher explains: Work each side separately. a + b = −8/12 + 9/12 = 1/12,
     and b + a = 1/12 too. a × b = (−2/3) × (3/4) = −1/2 = b × a. (a + b) + c =
     1/12 + (−10/12) = −9/12 = −3/4 and a + (b + c) = −2/3 + (−1/12) = −9/12 =
     −3/4. (a × b) × c = (−1/2) × (−5/6) = 5/12 and a × (b × c) = (−2/3) ×
     (−5/8) = 5/12. Distributive: a × (b + c) = (−2/3) × (−1/12) = 1/18, and a ×
     b + a × c = −1/2 + 5/9 = −9/18 + 10/18 = 1/18. But a − b = −17/12 and b − a
     = 17/12: they are additive inverses, not equal. Also 0 is the additive
     identity (a + 0 = a), 1 is the multiplicative identity, a + (−a) = 0 and a
     × (1/a) = 1. The distributive law helps mental work: (−3/7) × (14/15) +
     (−3/7) × (1/15) = (−3/7) × 1 = −3/7. Now you try: is (a ÷ b) equal to (b ÷
     a) for a = −2/3, b = 3/4? (No: −8/9 and −9/8)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. Write in standard form.
    a) 12/(−18)   b) −24/(−36)    c) 35/49      d) −45/60        e) 0/(−5)
    f) 16/(−4)    g) −21/28       h) 27/(−81)   i) −100/(−125)   j) 14/35
    k) −66/99     l) 72/(−108)    m) −13/39     n) 48/64         o) −120/150
    p) 9/(−1)     q) −44/(−121)   r) 56/(−84)   s) −17/17        t) 90/225
    Answers: a) −2/3   b) 2/3   c) 5/7   d) −3/4   e) 0   f) −4   g) −3/4
             h) −1/3   i) 4/5   j) 2/5   k) −2/3   l) −2/3   m) −1/3   n) 3/4
             o) −4/5   p) −9   q) 4/11   r) −2/3   s) −1   t) 2/5

M2. Fill in the blank so that the two rational numbers are equal.
    a) −3/5 = __/20        b) 2/(−7) = (−8)/__    c) −4/9 = __/(−36)
    d) 5/6 = (−25)/__      e) −1/2 = __/50        f) 7/(−8) = 21/__
    g) −9/11 = __/44       h) 3/4 = (−27)/__      i) −5/12 = __/(−60)
    j) 6/(−13) = 18/__     k) −2/3 = __/99        l) 11/15 = (−44)/__
    m) 4/(−5) = __/100     n) −10/17 = 30/__      o) 8/9 = __/(−72)
    p) −12/25 = (−36)/__   q) 1/(−1) = __/7       r) −7/20 = 42/__
    Answers: a) −12   b) 28   c) 16   d) −30   e) −25   f) −24   g) −36   h) −36
             i) 25   j) −39   k) −66   l) −60   m) −80   n) −51   o) −64   p) 75
             q) −7   r) −120

M3. Line 1 is cut into thirds and line 2 into fifths. Write the rational number
    at each point (standard form). For the last four, name the two consecutive
    integers it lies between.
    Line 1:
             A    B         C    D         E    F         G    H
    <---+----+----+----+----+----+----+----+----+----+----+----+----+--->
       −2             −1              0              1              2
    Line 2:
                 J           K     L        M           N        P
    <---+--+--+--+--+--+--+--+--+--+--+--+--+--+--+--+--+--+--+--+--+--->
       −2             −1              0              1              2
    a) point A                           b) point B
    c) point C                           d) point D
    e) point E                           f) point F
    g) point G                           h) point H
    i) point J                           j) point K
    k) point L                           l) point M
    m) point N                           n) point P
    o) between which integers is −3/2?   p) between which integers is 5/2?
    q) between which integers is −9/4?   r) between which integers is −1/10?
    Answers: a) −5/3   b) −4/3   c) −2/3   d) −1/3   e) 1/3   f) 2/3   g) 4/3
             h) 5/3   i) −7/5   j) −3/5   k) −1/5   l) 2/5   m) 6/5   n) 9/5
             o) −2 and −1   p) 2 and 3   q) −3 and −2   r) −1 and 0

M4. Write <, > or =.
    a) −3/4 __ −5/7          b) −5/9 __ 4/(−7)        c) 2/3 __ −3/4
    d) −1/2 __ 0             e) 3/5 __ 5/8            f) −7/8 __ −8/9
    g) −2/4 __ 3/(−6)        h) 5/(−12) __ −3/8       i) 0 __ −1/100
    j) −11/12 __ −12/13      k) 7/4 __ 12/7           l) −6/5 __ −5/4
    m) 9/(−12) __ −15/20     n) −1/3 __ 1/(−4)        o) 13/15 __ 8/9
    p) −4/3 __ −1            q) −99/100 __ −100/101   r) 1/2 __ (−3)/(−6)
    s) −5/3 __ −17/10        t) 2/(−9) __ −3/13
    Answers: a) <   b) >   c) >   d) <   e) <   f) >   g) =   h) <   i) >   j) >
             k) >   l) >   m) =   n) <   o) <   p) <   q) >   r) =   s) >   t) >

M5. Arrange a) to h) in ascending order (smallest first) and i) to l) in
    descending order (largest first).
    a) −2/3, 5/(−6), 1/2, −7/12, 0, 3/4
    b) −1/2, 2/(−5), −3/10
    c) 3/8, −5/8, 1/(−8), 7/8, −3/8
    d) 4/5, −4/5, 4/(−3), 3/4, −1
    e) −7/10, −2/3, −3/4, −5/6
    f) 5/12, −1/4, 1/3, −2/(−3), −1/6
    g) −9/4, −2, −11/5, −7/3
    h) 1/10, −1/10, 1/(−100), 1/100, 0
    i) 6/7, −7/6, 7/(−8), 8/7
    j) −3/2, 5/(−4), −7/5, −13/10
    k) 2/9, −1/3, 5/(−18), 1/6, −4/9, 0
    l) −15/16, 15/(−17), −14/15, −16/17
    Answers:
      a) 5/(−6) < −2/3 < −7/12 < 0 < 1/2 < 3/4
      b) −1/2 < 2/(−5) < −3/10
      c) −5/8 < −3/8 < 1/(−8) < 3/8 < 7/8
      d) 4/(−3) < −1 < −4/5 < 3/4 < 4/5
      e) −5/6 < −3/4 < −7/10 < −2/3
      f) −1/4 < −1/6 < 1/3 < 5/12 < −2/(−3)
      g) −7/3 < −9/4 < −11/5 < −2
      h) −1/10 < 1/(−100) < 0 < 1/100 < 1/10
      i) 8/7 > 6/7 > 7/(−8) > −7/6
      j) 5/(−4) > −13/10 > −7/5 > −3/2
      k) 2/9 > 1/6 > 0 > 5/(−18) > −1/3 > −4/9
      l) 15/(−17) > −14/15 > −15/16 > −16/17

M6. Find the rational numbers asked for (standard form).
    a) halfway between −1/2 and 1/3
    b) halfway between −1/3 and 1/4
    c) halfway between 1/4 and 1/2
    d) halfway between −3/4 and −1/4
    e) halfway between −2 and −1
    f) halfway between 2/5 and 3/5
    g) halfway between −5/6 and −2/3
    h) halfway between 0 and 1/8
    i) halfway between −1/10 and 1/10
    j) halfway between 7/8 and 1
    k) halfway between −4/3 and −5/4
    l) halfway between 1/6 and 1/5
    m) 3 numbers cutting −1/2 to 1/3 into 4 equal parts
    n) 3 numbers cutting −1/3 to 1/4 into 4 equal parts
    o) 3 numbers cutting 1/4 to 1/2 into 4 equal parts
    p) 3 numbers cutting −3/4 to −1/4 into 4 equal parts
    q) 3 numbers cutting −2 to −1 into 4 equal parts
    r) 3 numbers cutting 2/5 to 3/5 into 4 equal parts
    s) 3 numbers cutting −5/6 to −2/3 into 4 equal parts
    t) 3 numbers cutting 0 to 1/8 into 4 equal parts
    Answers:
      a) −1/12
      b) −1/24
      c) 3/8
      d) −1/2
      e) −3/2
      f) 1/2
      g) −3/4
      h) 1/16
      i) 0
      j) 15/16
      k) −31/24
      l) 11/60
      m) −7/24, −1/12, 1/8
      n) −3/16, −1/24, 5/48
      o) 5/16, 3/8, 7/16
      p) −5/8, −1/2, −3/8
      q) −7/4, −3/2, −5/4
      r) 9/20, 1/2, 11/20
      s) −19/24, −3/4, −17/24
      t) 1/32, 1/16, 3/32

M7. Add. Give the answer in standard form.
    a) (−3/8) + 5/6         b) (−5/12) + (−7/18)    c) 2/3 + 3/4
    d) 2/3 + (−3/4)         e) (−2/3) + 3/4         f) (−2/3) + (−3/4)
    g) (−7/10) + 4/15       h) 5/9 + (−5/9)         i) (−1/2) + 0
    j) 3 + (−7/4)           k) (−4) + (−2/5)        l) 11/14 + (−3/7)
    m) (−9/16) + 5/24       n) (−1/6) + (−1/10)     o) 7/12 + (−13/20)
    p) (−3/5) + 3/5         q) 5/8 + (−1)           r) (−13/15) + (−7/10)
    s) 1/4 + (−1/3)         t) (−2/7) + 9/14
    Answers: a) 11/24   b) −29/36   c) 17/12   d) −1/12   e) 1/12   f) −17/12
             g) −13/30   h) 0   i) −1/2   j) 5/4   k) −22/5   l) 5/14
             m) −17/48   n) −4/15   o) −1/15   p) 0   q) −3/8   r) −47/30
             s) −1/12   t) 5/14

M8. Subtract. Give the answer in standard form.
    a) 3/4 − (−5/6)        b) (−3/7) − (−1/2)     c) 2/3 − 3/4
    d) 2/3 − (−3/4)        e) (−2/3) − 3/4        f) (−2/3) − (−3/4)
    g) (−2/3) − 1/6        h) (−1/2) − (−3/5)     i) 0 − (−4/9)

    m) 2 − (−3/8)          n) (−1) − 5/12         o) 7/16 − (−9/20)
    p) (−11/12) − (−3/4)   q) 1/9 − 1/6           r) (−3/10) − 2/25
    s) (−5/3) − (−7/2)     t) 4/21 − (−5/14)
    Answers: a) 19/12   b) 1/14   c) −1/12   d) 17/12   e) −17/12   f) 1/12
             g) −5/6   h) 1/10   i) 4/9   j) 0   k) 1/18   l) −29/30   m) 19/8
             n) −17/12   o) 71/80   p) −1/6   q) −1/18   r) −19/50   s) 11/6
             t) 23/42

M9. Multiply. Cancel first where you can.
    a) (−7/12) × 8/21        b) (−3/5) × (−10/9)      c) 4/9 × 3/8
    d) 4/9 × (−3/8)          e) (−4/9) × 3/8          f) (−4/9) × (−3/8)
    g) (−15/16) × (−4/25)    h) (−5/7) × 0            i) (−5/7) × 1

    s) 10/21 × (−27/40)      t) (−16/45) × (−15/64)
    Answers: a) −2/9   b) 2/3   c) 1/6   d) −1/6   e) −1/6   f) 1/6   g) 3/20
             h) 0   i) −5/7   j) 5/7   k) −5/3   l) 5/6   m) 1   n) 1   o) −7/15
             p) −18/5   q) 1/4   r) 9/16   s) −9/28   t) 1/12

M10. Divide (multiply by the reciprocal).
     a) 15/2 ÷ 3/4            b) (−4/9) ÷ 8/15         c) (−9/10) ÷ (−3/5)
     d) 5/6 ÷ 10/9            e) 5/6 ÷ (−10/9)         f) (−5/6) ÷ 10/9
     g) (−5/6) ÷ (−10/9)      h) 0 ÷ (−3/7)            i) (−3/7) ÷ 1
     j) (−3/7) ÷ (−1)         k) (−6) ÷ 3/4            l) 7/8 ÷ (−14)
     m) (−12/25) ÷ (−18/35)   n) 1 ÷ (−5/9)            o) (−2/3) ÷ (−2/3)
     p) 21/32 ÷ (−7/24)       q) (−1/4) ÷ 4            r) 11/12 ÷ (−33/16)
     s) (−20/27) ÷ (−5/9)     t) 3/5 ÷ (−6/5)

              h) 0   i) −3/7   j) 3/7   k) −8   l) −1/16   m) 14/15   n) −9/5
              o) 1   p) −9/4   q) −1/16   r) −4/9   s) 4/3   t) −1/2

M11. Work out. Then compare a) with b), c) with d), i) with j) and k) with l).
     a) (−2/3) × (3/4 + (−5/6))          b) (−2/3) × 3/4 + (−2/3) × (−5/6)
     c) (1/2 − 3/4) ÷ (−5/8)             d) 1/2 − 3/4 ÷ (−5/8)
     e) (−3/7) × 14/15 + (−3/7) × 1/15   f) (−5/6 + 1/3) × (−4/5)
     g) 2/5 × (−3/4) − 3/4 × (−7/5)      h) (−1/2)³
     i) (3/4 − 5/6) − (−1/3)             j) 3/4 − (5/6 − (−1/3))
     k) (−8/9 ÷ 2/3) ÷ (−4/5)            l) −8/9 ÷ (2/3 ÷ (−4/5))
     m) (−7/5) × (−3/2) + 1/10           n) 5/(−12) + 7/18 − 1/(−4)
     o) (2/3)² − (1/2)²                  p) (−1 + 1/2) × (−1 − 1/2)
     q) 1/(−2) × 4/(−9) × (−27/8)        r) (3/5 − 1)(3/5 + 1)

              h) −1/8   i) 1/4   j) −5/12   k) 5/3   l) 16/15   m) 11/5   n) 2/9
              o) 7/36   p) 3/4   q) −3/4   r) −16/25

M12. a) to j): write the additive inverse and the reciprocal. k) to t): name the
     property shown.
     a) 3/4
     b) −2/7
     c) 5
     d) −1
     e) 1
     f) 0
     g) −9/4
     h) 1/8
     i) −13/6
     j) −7/10
     k) −2/3 + 3/4 = 3/4 + (−2/3)
     l) (−1/2) × (4/5) = (4/5) × (−1/2)
     m) (1/2 + 1/3) + 1/4 = 1/2 + (1/3 + 1/4)
     n) (2 × 1/3) × 3/5 = 2 × (1/3 × 3/5)
     o) 3/7 × (1/2 + 1/3) = 3/7 × 1/2 + 3/7 × 1/3
     p) −5/9 + 0 = −5/9
     q) (−5/9) × 1 = −5/9
     r) 4/11 + (−4/11) = 0
     s) (−6/13) × (−13/6) = 1
     t) (−3/4) × 0 = 0

       b) 2/7; −7/2

       d) 1; −1
       e) −1; 1
       f) 0; none
       g) 9/4; −4/9
       h) −1/8; 8
       i) 13/6; −6/13
       j) 7/10; −10/7
       k) commutative (+)
       l) commutative (×)
       m) associative (+)
       n) associative (×)
       o) distributive
       p) additive identity
       q) multiplicative identity
       r) additive inverse
       s) multiplicative inverse
       t) multiplication by 0

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: go back to integers (A5 topic 5.15) and fraction work; draw a
  number line for every comparison; write every negative rational in brackets;
  decide the sign first, then work with sizes; always put answers into standard
  form. Harder: prove that the mean of a and b lies between them; find the
  rational number with the smallest denominator between 5/8 and 7/11 (2/3); show
  that 1/2 + 1/6 + 1/12 + ... + 1/(n(n + 1)) = n/(n + 1); investigate whether
  the set of rationals is closed under taking square roots (it is not: √2).


================================================================================
TOPIC 6.2 — EXPONENTS AND POWERS: LAWS OF EXPONENTS, NEGATIVE EXPONENTS
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - In aⁿ, a is the BASE and n the EXPONENT (index, power): aⁿ = a × a × ... × a
    (n factors). 2⁵ = 32 is read '2 to the power 5'.
  - LAWS OF EXPONENTS (same base a ≠ 0; m and n integers): aᵐ × aⁿ = aᵐ⁺ⁿ;  aᵐ ÷
    aⁿ = aᵐ⁻ⁿ;  (aᵐ)ⁿ = aᵐⁿ.
  - Same exponent, different bases: aⁿ × bⁿ = (ab)ⁿ and aⁿ ÷ bⁿ = (a/b)ⁿ: 2³ ×
    5³ = 10³, (2/3)⁴ = 2⁴/3⁴ = 16/81.
  - ZERO exponent: a⁰ = 1 for every a ≠ 0 (because aⁿ ÷ aⁿ = 1).
  - NEGATIVE exponent: a⁻ⁿ = 1/aⁿ, the reciprocal of aⁿ: 2⁻³ = 1/8, 10⁻² = 1/100
    = 0.01, (2/3)⁻² = (3/2)² = 9/4. A negative exponent does NOT make the number
    negative.
  - NEGATIVE BASES: (−2)³ = −8 (odd power, negative) but (−2)⁴ = 16 (even power,
    positive). −2⁴ means −(2⁴) = −16: the exponent belongs only to the 2 unless
    there are brackets.
  - The laws need the SAME BASE and MULTIPLICATION or DIVISION. There is no law
    for a² + a³, and 2³ × 3² is NOT 6⁵.
  - To simplify, write every number as a power of a prime (4 = 2², 27 = 3³, 6 =
    2 × 3), collect each base, then use the laws. To solve 2ˣ = 32, write both
    sides with the same base: 2ˣ = 2⁵, so x = 5.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. Explain why 2³ × 2⁴ = 2⁷. Then simplify a⁵ × a³ and 10² × 10 × 10⁴.
    Answer: 2³ × 2⁴ = 2⁷ = 128; a⁵ × a³ = a⁸; 10² × 10 × 10⁴ = 10⁷.
    Teacher explains: Write the powers out: 2³ × 2⁴ = (2 × 2 × 2) × (2 × 2 × 2 ×
    2). That is 3 + 4 = 7 factors of 2, so 2⁷. In general aᵐ × aⁿ = aᵐ⁺ⁿ: when
    multiplying powers of the SAME base, ADD the exponents. a⁵ × a³ = a⁵⁺³ = a⁸.
    Remember that 10 = 10¹, so 10² × 10¹ × 10⁴ = 10²⁺¹⁺⁴ = 10⁷. Common mistakes:
    multiplying the exponents (2³ × 2⁴ = 2¹² is WRONG) and multiplying the bases
    (2³ × 2⁴ = 4⁷ is WRONG). The base stays the same. Now you try: 5⁴ × 5² and x
    × x⁶. (5⁶; x⁷)

D2. Explain why 3⁷ ÷ 3⁴ = 3³. Then simplify x⁹ ÷ x⁵ and 7⁵ ÷ 7⁵.
    Answer: 3⁷ ÷ 3⁴ = 3³ = 27; x⁹ ÷ x⁵ = x⁴; 7⁵ ÷ 7⁵ = 7⁰ = 1.
    Teacher explains: Write it as a fraction and cancel: 3⁷ ÷ 3⁴ = (3 × 3 × 3 ×
    3 × 3 × 3 × 3)/(3 × 3 × 3 × 3). Four 3s cancel, leaving 7 − 4 = 3 factors:
    3³. In general aᵐ ÷ aⁿ = aᵐ⁻ⁿ: when dividing powers of the same base,
    SUBTRACT the exponents (top minus bottom). x⁹ ÷ x⁵ = x⁹⁻⁵ = x⁴. 7⁵ ÷ 7⁵:
    anything divided by itself is 1, and the law gives 7⁵⁻⁵ = 7⁰, so 7⁰ must be
    1. Common mistake: dividing the exponents (x⁹ ÷ x³ = x³ is WRONG; it is x⁶).
    Now you try: 2¹⁰ ÷ 2⁶ and y⁸ ÷ y. (2⁴ = 16; y⁷)

D3. Explain why (5²)³ = 5⁶. How is it different from 5² × 5³?
    Answer: (5²)³ = 5⁶ = 15,625 (exponents 2 × 3 = 6), but 5² × 5³ = 5⁵ = 3,125.
    Teacher explains: (5²)³ means 5² used 3 times: 5² × 5² × 5² = 5²⁺²⁺² = 5⁶.
    Three groups of 2 factors is 2 × 3 = 6 factors. In general (aᵐ)ⁿ = aᵐⁿ: a
    power of a power, MULTIPLY the exponents. But 5² × 5³ is a PRODUCT of two
    powers, so ADD: 5⁵. Compare (x⁴)⁵ = x²⁰ with x⁴ × x⁵ = x⁹. Negative
    exponents follow the same law: (2³)⁻² = 2⁻⁶ = 1/64 and (a⁻²)⁻³ = a⁶. Now you
    try: (3²)⁴ and (y³)⁻¹. (3⁸; y⁻³ = 1/y³)

D4. Work out (2 × 5)³ in two ways. Then find (2/3)⁴ and (3x)².
    Answer: (2 × 5)³ = 10³ = 1,000 and 2³ × 5³ = 8 × 125 = 1,000. (2/3)⁴ =
    16/81. (3x)² = 9x².
    Teacher explains: (2 × 5)³ = (2 × 5) × (2 × 5) × (2 × 5). Rearranging, this
    is (2 × 2 × 2) × (5 × 5 × 5) = 2³ × 5³. So (ab)ⁿ = aⁿbⁿ: the power goes to
    EVERY factor inside the bracket. The same for a quotient: (2/3)⁴ = (2 × 2 ×
    2 × 2)/(3 × 3 × 3 × 3) = 2⁴/3⁴ = 16/81, so (a/b)ⁿ = aⁿ/bⁿ. For (3x)² = 3² ×
    x² = 9x². Common mistake: (3x)² = 3x² (forgetting to square the 3). Useful
    backwards: 4³ × 25³ = (4 × 25)³ = 100³ = 1,000,000. Now you try: (2a)⁵ and
    (5/2)³. (32a⁵; 125/8)

D5. Why is 5⁰ = 1? Is 0⁰ also 1?
    Answer: 5⁰ = 5³ ÷ 5³ = 125 ÷ 125 = 1. 0⁰ is not defined at this level.
    Teacher explains: Two ways. (1) The division law: 5³ ÷ 5³ = 5³⁻³ = 5⁰, but
    also 125 ÷ 125 = 1, so 5⁰ = 1. (2) The pattern: 5³ = 125, 5² = 25, 5¹ = 5;
    each step down DIVIDES by 5, so the next is 5 ÷ 5 = 1 = 5⁰. So a⁰ = 1 for
    every non-zero a: 7⁰ = 1, (−3)⁰ = 1, (2/5)⁰ = 1, x⁰ = 1. Common mistake: 5⁰
    = 0 or 5⁰ = 5. Careful with where the zero power sits: 3x⁰ = 3 × 1 = 3, but
    (3x)⁰ = 1. For 0⁰ the argument fails, since 0³ ÷ 0³ means dividing by 0;
    mathematicians treat it separately. Now you try: 9⁰ + 4⁰ and 2 × 10⁰. (2; 2)

D6. Continue the pattern 2³, 2², 2¹, 2⁰ downwards. What do 2⁻¹, 2⁻² and 2⁻³
    mean?
        2³  = 8
        2²  = 4        (÷ 2)
        2¹  = 2        (÷ 2)
        2⁰  = 1        (÷ 2)
        2⁻¹ = 1/2      (÷ 2)
        2⁻² = 1/4      (÷ 2)
        2⁻³ = 1/8      (÷ 2)
    Answer: 2⁻¹ = 1/2, 2⁻² = 1/4, 2⁻³ = 1/8. In general a⁻ⁿ = 1/aⁿ.
    Teacher explains: Each step down, the exponent falls by 1 and the value is
    divided by 2, so after 2⁰ = 1 come 1/2, 1/4, 1/8. The law agrees: 2³ ÷ 2⁶ =
    2³⁻⁶ = 2⁻³, and by cancelling 8/64 = 1/8. So a NEGATIVE exponent means the
    RECIPROCAL of the positive power: 10⁻³ = 1/10³ = 1/1,000 = 0.001, 5⁻² =
    1/25, x⁻⁴ = 1/x⁴. For a fraction, turn it over: (2/3)⁻² = (3/2)² = 9/4. And
    1/a⁻ⁿ = aⁿ: 1/3⁻² = 9. Common mistake: 2⁻³ = −8 or −6. A negative exponent
    never makes the value negative. Now you try: 4⁻², (1/5)⁻¹ and 10⁻⁴. (1/16;
    5; 0.0001)

D7. Find (−2)³, −2³, (−2)⁴ and −2⁴. When is a power of a negative number
    positive?
    Answer: (−2)³ = −8, −2³ = −8, (−2)⁴ = 16, −2⁴ = −16.
    Teacher explains: (−2)³ = (−2) × (−2) × (−2): the first two give +4, then 4
    × (−2) = −8. (−2)⁴ = (−2) × (−2) × (−2) × (−2) = 16: the minus signs pair
    off. Rule: a negative base to an EVEN power is positive, to an ODD power
    negative. So (−1)¹⁰⁰ = 1 and (−1)⁹⁹ = −1. WITHOUT brackets, the exponent
    acts only on the number next to it: −2⁴ = −(2 × 2 × 2 × 2) = −16.
    Calculators and textbooks follow this rule. Also (−3)⁻² = 1/(−3)² = 1/9 but
    (−2)⁻³ = 1/(−8) = −1/8. Now you try: (−3)⁴, −3⁴ and (−1/2)³. (81; −81; −1/8)

D8. A sheet of paper is 0.1 mm thick. It is folded in half again and again. How
    thick is the pile after 10 folds? After how many folds is it thicker than 1
    m?
    Answer: After 10 folds: 0.1 × 2¹⁰ = 0.1 × 1,024 = 102.4 mm. After 14 folds
    it is 0.1 × 2¹⁴ = 1,638.4 mm, more than 1 m (13 folds give only 819.2 mm).
    Teacher explains: Each fold DOUBLES the thickness, so after n folds the
    thickness is 0.1 × 2ⁿ mm. Make a table of powers of 2: 2¹⁰ = 1,024, 2¹³ =
    8,192, 2¹⁴ = 16,384. 1 m = 1,000 mm, so we need 0.1 × 2ⁿ > 1,000, that is 2ⁿ
    > 10,000. 2¹³ = 8,192 is too small and 2¹⁴ = 16,384 is enough. Powers grow
    very fast: 42 folds would reach beyond the Moon! Doubling (×2 each time) is
    called exponential growth. Now you try: a rumour is told to 3 new people
    every hour, starting with one person. How many new people hear it in hour 5?
    (3⁵ = 243)

D9. Simplify (2⁵ × 3⁴) ÷ (6² × 3) and (25 × 5⁻³) ÷ 5⁻⁴.
    Answer: (2⁵ × 3⁴) ÷ (6² × 3) = 2³ × 3 = 24; (25 × 5⁻³) ÷ 5⁻⁴ = 5³ = 125.
    Teacher explains: Step 1: write every number as a power of a prime: 6² = (2
    × 3)² = 2² × 3², so the bottom is 2² × 3² × 3 = 2² × 3³. Step 2: deal with
    each prime separately: 2⁵ ÷ 2² = 2³ and 3⁴ ÷ 3³ = 3¹. Step 3: 2³ × 3 = 24.
    For the second: 25 = 5², so 5² × 5⁻³ = 5⁻¹, and 5⁻¹ ÷ 5⁻⁴ = 5⁻¹⁻⁽⁻⁴⁾ = 5³ =
    125. Be careful when subtracting a negative exponent: −1 − (−4) = 3. Common
    mistake: trying to use the laws on 2⁵ × 3⁴ (different bases). Now you try:
    (4³ × 3²) ÷ (2⁵ × 9). (2)

D10. Simplify (x³y²)⁴ ÷ (x²y)³ and (2a²b)³ × (a⁻¹b)².
     Answer: (x³y²)⁴ ÷ (x²y)³ = x⁶y⁵; (2a²b)³ × (a⁻¹b)² = 8a⁴b⁵.
     Teacher explains: Work in stages. Powers of brackets first: (x³y²)⁴ = x¹²y⁸
     (each exponent × 4) and (x²y)³ = x⁶y³. Then divide letter by letter: x¹²⁻⁶
     = x⁶ and y⁸⁻³ = y⁵. For the second: (2a²b)³ = 2³a⁶b³ = 8a⁶b³ (do not forget
     to cube the 2) and (a⁻¹b)² = a⁻²b². Multiply: numbers 8, a⁶⁻² = a⁴ (6 +
     (−2) = 4), b³⁺² = b⁵. Write the answer with numbers first, then letters in
     alphabetical order, and use positive exponents at the end: e.g. 6a²b⁻³ =
     6a²/b³. Now you try: (3m²n)² × m⁻³ and 12p⁵q² ÷ 4p²q⁴. (9mn²; 3p³/q²)

D11. Solve 2ˣ = 32, 3ˣ⁺¹ = 81 and 2ˣ = 1/8.
     Answer: 2ˣ = 32 gives x = 5; 3ˣ⁺¹ = 81 gives x = 3; 2ˣ = 1/8 gives x = −3.
     Teacher explains: Write both sides as powers of the SAME base, then the
     exponents must be equal. 32 = 2⁵, so 2ˣ = 2⁵ and x = 5. 81 = 3⁴, so 3ˣ⁺¹ =
     3⁴, x + 1 = 4 and x = 3. 1/8 = 1/2³ = 2⁻³, so x = −3. Check each answer: 2⁵
     = 32, 3³⁺¹ = 81, 2⁻³ = 1/8. Harder ones: 4ˣ = 64 is (2²)ˣ = 2⁶, so 2x = 6
     and x = 3 (or simply 4³ = 64); 2³ × 2ˣ = 2⁹ gives 3 + x = 9, x = 6. Now you
     try: 5ˣ = 625, 10ˣ = 0.001 and 7ˣ = 1. (4; −3; 0)

D12. Find the mistake in each: a) 2³ × 3² = 6⁵; b) a² + a³ = a⁵; c) 3² × 3⁴ =
     9⁶; d) (x²)³ = x⁵; e) 5⁻² = −25.
     Answer: a) 2³ × 3² = 8 × 9 = 72 (6⁵ = 7,776). b) a² + a³ cannot be
     simplified. c) 3² × 3⁴ = 3⁶ = 729. d) (x²)³ = x⁶. e) 5⁻² = 1/25.
     Teacher explains: a) The laws only work for the SAME base (or the same
     exponent: 2³ × 3³ = 6³ = 216 is correct). Here both base and exponent
     differ, so just work it out. b) The laws are for × and ÷, not +. a² and a³
     are unlike terms; test a = 2: 4 + 8 = 12, but 2⁵ = 32. c) Keep the base:
     3⁶, not 9⁶. d) Power of a power: MULTIPLY exponents. e) A negative exponent
     means reciprocal, not negative. Testing with small numbers is a powerful
     way to catch mistakes. Now you try: is (a + b)² = a² + b²? (No: a = 1, b =
     2 gives 9 and 5)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. Write as a single power (aᵐ × aⁿ = aᵐ⁺ⁿ).
    a) 2³ × 2⁴              b) 5 × 5⁶               c) 10² × 10³ × 10
    d) a⁵ × a³              e) x × x⁷               f) y² × y² × y²
    g) 3⁻² × 3⁵             h) 7⁻⁴ × 7⁻¹            i) p⁻³ × p³
    j) (−2)³ × (−2)²        k) m⁴ × m⁰              l) (1/2)² × (1/2)³
    m) 4⁶ × 4⁻⁶             n) b¹⁰ × b⁻⁴            o) (−5) × (−5) × (−5)
    p) t⁸ × t⁻⁹             q) (2/3)⁻¹ × (2/3)⁻²    r) z³ × z⁴ × z⁵
    s) 11⁰ × 11²            t) k⁻² × k⁻³ × k
    Answers: a) 2⁷   b) 5⁷   c) 10⁶   d) a⁸   e) x⁸   f) y⁶   g) 3³   h) 7⁻⁵
             i) p⁰   j) (−2)⁵   k) m⁴   l) (1/2)⁵   m) 4⁰   n) b⁶   o) (−5)³
             p) t⁻¹   q) (2/3)⁻³   r) z¹²   s) 11²   t) k⁻⁴

M2. Write as a single power (aᵐ ÷ aⁿ = aᵐ⁻ⁿ).
    a) 3⁷ ÷ 3⁴         b) x⁹ ÷ x⁵           c) 10⁸ ÷ 10⁸       d) a³ ÷ a⁷
    e) 2⁻³ ÷ 2⁴        f) 5⁻² ÷ 5⁻⁶         g) y⁸ ÷ y          h) (−4)⁹ ÷ (−4)⁶
    i) m⁰ ÷ m³         j) (3/4)⁵ ÷ (3/4)²   k) 6⁴ ÷ 6⁻¹        l) p⁻⁵ ÷ p⁻²
    m) 9¹² ÷ 9¹¹       n) q⁶ ÷ q⁶           o) 10⁻² ÷ 10³      p) n¹⁵ ÷ n²⁰
    q) (−1)⁷ ÷ (−1)²   r) 8 ÷ 8⁻¹           s) c¹⁰ ÷ c⁴ ÷ c³   t) 2²⁰ ÷ 2⁵ ÷ 2⁵
    Answers: a) 3³   b) x⁴   c) 10⁰   d) a⁻⁴   e) 2⁻⁷   f) 5⁴   g) y⁷   h) (−4)³
             i) m⁻³   j) (3/4)³   k) 6⁵   l) p⁻³   m) 9¹   n) q⁰   o) 10⁻⁵
             p) n⁻⁵   q) (−1)⁵   r) 8²   s) c³   t) 2¹⁰

M3. Write as a single power ((aᵐ)ⁿ = aᵐⁿ).
    a) (5²)³           b) (x⁴)⁵           c) (2³)⁻²          d) (a⁻²)⁻³
    e) (10⁵)⁰          f) (7¹)⁴           g) (y⁻¹)⁶          h) ((−3)²)³
    i) ((1/2)³)²       j) (p⁷)⁻¹          k) (4⁻³)²          l) (b⁰)⁹
    m) (6²)²           n) (m¹⁰)¹⁰         o) ((−2)⁵)⁻¹       p) ((2/5)⁻¹)⁻¹
    q) ((3²)²)²        r) ((x⁻¹)²)³       s) (2³)⁴ × 2⁻⁵     t) (a²)³ × (a³)²
    Answers: a) 5⁶   b) x²⁰   c) 2⁻⁶   d) a⁶   e) 10⁰   f) 7⁴   g) y⁻⁶
             h) (−3)⁶   i) (1/2)⁶   j) p⁻⁷   k) 4⁻⁶   l) b⁰   m) 6⁴   n) m¹⁰⁰
             o) (−2)⁻⁵   p) (2/5)¹   q) 3⁸   r) x⁻⁶   s) 2⁷   t) a¹²

M4. Remove the brackets ((ab)ⁿ = aⁿbⁿ, (a/b)ⁿ = aⁿ/bⁿ).
    a) (2x)³       b) (3a²b)²     c) (−2p)⁵      d) (x/2)³      e) (2/3)⁴
    f) (−3/4)³     g) (5/y)²      h) (ab)⁷       i) (−xy²)⁴     j) (4m³n)³
    k) (a/b)⁵      l) (2x²/3)²    m) (−1/2)⁶     n) (10k)⁴      o) (3/(2t))³
    p) (−5q⁴)²     q) (xyz)³      r) (−2a/b²)³   s) (7/10)²     t) (0.2c)²
    Answers: a) 8x³   b) 9a⁴b²   c) −32p⁵   d) x³/8   e) 16/81   f) −27/64
             g) 25/y²   h) a⁷b⁷   i) x⁴y⁸   j) 64m⁹n³   k) a⁵/b⁵   l) 4x⁴/9
             m) 1/64   n) 10,000k⁴   o) 27/(8t³)   p) 25q⁸   q) x³y³z³
             r) −8a³/b⁶   s) 49/100   t) c²/25

M5. Find the value as a fraction or an integer.
    a) 5⁰         b) (−7)⁰       c) (3/4)⁰           d) 2⁻¹
    e) 2⁻³        f) 10⁻²        g) 5⁻²              h) (−3)⁻²
    i) (−2)⁻³     j) (1/2)⁻¹     k) (2/3)⁻²          l) (−4/5)⁻¹
    m) 1/3⁻²      n) 3⁻¹ + 6⁻¹   o) (2⁻¹ + 3⁻¹)⁻¹    p) 4⁰ + 4⁻¹
    q) (1/10)⁻³   r) 8⁻¹ × 2³    s) (5⁻¹ − 10⁻¹)⁻¹   t) 2⁻² × 3⁻¹
    u) 10⁻⁴       v) (−1)⁻⁵      w) 3 × 10⁰          x) (3 × 10)⁰
    Answers: a) 1   b) 1   c) 1   d) 1/2   e) 1/8   f) 1/100 = 0.01   g) 1/25
             h) 1/9   i) −1/8   j) 2   k) 9/4   l) −5/4   m) 9   n) 1/2   o) 6/5
             p) 5/4   q) 1,000   r) 1   s) 10   t) 1/12   u) 1/10,000 = 0.0001
             v) −1   w) 3   x) 1

M6. a) to l): write with positive exponents only. m) to x): write as a power of
    the base shown (the exponent will be negative).
    a) x⁻³                     b) 3a⁻²
    c) (2y)⁻¹                  d) a⁻²b³
    e) 1/m⁻⁴                   f) 5p⁻¹q⁻²
    g) (x/y)⁻²                 h) −4k⁻³
    i) x⁻¹y                    j) 1/(2t⁻³)
    k) (3n)⁻²                  l) a⁰b⁻⁵
    m) 1/8 as a power of 2     n) 1/1,000 as a power of 10
    o) 1/49 as a power of 7    p) 0.01 as a power of 10
    q) 1/81 as a power of 3    r) 1/x⁵ as a power of x
    s) 1/32 as a power of 2    t) 0.001 as a power of 10
    u) 1/y as a power of y     v) 1/125 as a power of 5
    w) 9/4 as a power of 2/3   x) 1/(a²)³ as a power of a
    Answers: a) 1/x³   b) 3/a²   c) 1/(2y)   d) b³/a²   e) m⁴   f) 5/(pq²)

             m) 2⁻³   n) 10⁻³   o) 7⁻²   p) 10⁻²   q) 3⁻⁴   r) x⁻⁵   s) 2⁻⁵

M7. Find the value. Watch the brackets.
    a) (−2)³      b) −2³      c) (−2)⁴        d) −2⁴       e) (−1)¹⁰⁰
    f) (−1)⁹⁹     g) −1¹⁰⁰    h) (−3)²        i) −3²       j) (−5)³
    k) (−10)⁴     l) −(−2)³   m) (−1/2)⁵      n) (−2/3)²   o) −(2/3)²
    p) (−1)⁰      q) −5⁰      r) (−3)³ + 3³   s) (−2)⁶     t) (−2)² × (−2)³

    Answers: a) −8   b) −8   c) 16   d) −16   e) 1   f) −1   g) −1   h) 9
             i) −9   j) −125   k) 10,000   l) 8   m) −1/32   n) 4/9   o) −4/9

M8. Simplify and find the value.
    a) (2⁵ × 2³) ÷ 2⁴     b) 3⁴ × 3⁻² ÷ 3
    c) (5²)³ ÷ 5⁴         d) (2⁵ × 3⁴) ÷ (6² × 3)
    e) (25 × 5⁻³) ÷ 5⁻⁴   f) (4³ × 3²) ÷ (2⁵ × 9)
    g) 2³ × 5³            h) 16 × 2⁻³
    i) (10⁻²)² × 10⁶      j) (3⁻¹ × 2)⁻²

    m) (2/3)³ × (3/2)⁵    n) (1/2)⁻³ − (1/3)⁻²
    o) 12² ÷ (2³ × 3)     p) (9² × 4³) ÷ (6⁴ × 2)

    Answers: a) 16   b) 3   c) 25   d) 24   e) 125   f) 2   g) 1,000   h) 2
             i) 100   j) 9/4   k) 9   l) 1   m) 9/4   n) −1   o) 6   p) 2   q) 1
             r) 49   s) 25/16   t) 10

M9. Simplify. Give answers with positive exponents.
    a) (x³y²)⁴ ÷ (x²y)³   b) (2a²b)³ × (a⁻¹b)²
    c) 3x² × 4x⁵          d) 12a⁷ ÷ 4a³
    e) (−2m³)²            f) (−2m³)³
    g) 5p⁻² × 2p⁵         h) (a²b⁻³)²
    i) 6x⁴y³ ÷ (−3xy⁵)    j) (3x²)² × (2x)³
    k) (3m²n)² × m⁻³      l) 12p⁵q² ÷ 4p²q⁴
    m) (−a)⁴ × a⁻⁴        n) (10x⁻¹)² × x³
    o) (2y)⁻³ × 16y⁵      p) (x²y³)⁰ × 7x
    q) 15a³b⁻² ÷ 5a⁻¹b    r) (−3k²)² ÷ (3k)³

    Answers: a) x⁶y⁵   b) 8a⁴b⁵   c) 12x⁷   d) 3a⁴   e) 4m⁶   f) −8m⁹   g) 10p³

             n) 100x   o) 2y²   p) 7x   q) 3a⁴/b³   r) k/3   s) 1   t) 2p/q⁴

M10. Solve for x.
     a) 2ˣ = 32         b) 3ˣ = 1/9        c) 10ˣ = 0.001     d) 5ˣ = 1
     e) 2ˣ⁺¹ = 64       f) 4ˣ = 64         g) 2³ × 2ˣ = 2⁹    h) 5ˣ ÷ 5² = 5⁶
     i) (3²)ˣ = 3⁸      j) 8 = 2ˣ          k) (1/2)ˣ = 8      l) 7ˣ = 7
     m) 3ˣ⁻¹ = 27       n) 2ˣ = 1/64       o) 10ˣ = 100,000   p) 6ˣ = 1/36
     q) (2/3)ˣ = 9/4    r) 2²ˣ = 256       s) 9ˣ = 81         t) 11ˣ = 1/11
     Answers: a) x = 5   b) x = −2   c) x = −3   d) x = 0   e) x = 5   f) x = 3
              g) x = 6   h) x = 8   i) x = 4   j) x = 3   k) x = −3   l) x = 1
              m) x = 4   n) x = −6   o) x = 5   p) x = −2   q) x = −2   r) x = 4
              s) x = 2   t) x = −1

M11. True or false? If false, give the correct value or say what is wrong.
     a) 2³ × 3² = 6⁵       b) a² + a³ = a⁵    c) 3² × 3⁴ = 3⁶
     d) (2³)² = 2⁵         e) 2³ × 5³ = 10³   f) 5⁰ = 0
     g) 2⁻³ = −8           h) (−3)² = −3²     i) x⁶ ÷ x² = x³
     j) (ab)³ = a³b³       k) 4⁻¹ = 0.25      l) (a/b)⁻¹ = b/a
     m) 10⁻² = −100        n) (x²)³ = x⁸      o) 2¹⁰ = 1,024
     p) 3⁰ + 3⁰ = 2        q) (−1)³ = 1       r) 2x⁰ = 1
     s) a⁵ ÷ a⁵ = a⁰ = 1   t) (2a)² = 2a²
     Answers: a) false (72)   b) false (cannot be simplified)   c) true
              d) false (2⁶)   e) true   f) false (1)   g) false (1/8)
              h) false (9 and −9)   i) false (x⁴)   j) true   k) true   l) true
              m) false (0.01)   n) false (x⁶)   o) true   p) true
              q) false (−1)   r) false (2)   s) true   t) false (4a²)

M12. Write as a single power of the base given in brackets.
     a) 8⁵ (base 2)           b) 27² (base 3)          c) 16⁻¹ (base 2)
     d) 4³ × 8² (base 2)      e) 9⁴ ÷ 27 (base 3)      f) 125 × 25⁻¹ (base 5)
     g) 1/32 (base 2)         h) 0.001 (base 10)       i) 1/81 (base 3)
     j) (1/4)⁻³ (base 2)      k) 100³ (base 10)        l) 2⁵ × 4⁻² (base 2)
     m) 49² × 7⁻⁵ (base 7)    n) 0.2 (base 5)          o) 1 (base 2)
     p) (1/9)² × 3 (base 3)   q) 32³ ÷ 16⁴ (base 2)    r) 1,000,000 (base 10)
     Answers: a) 2¹⁵   b) 3⁶   c) 2⁻⁴   d) 2¹²   e) 3⁵   f) 5¹   g) 2⁻⁵
              h) 10⁻³   i) 3⁻⁴   j) 2⁶   k) 10⁶   l) 2¹   m) 7⁻¹   n) 5⁻¹
              o) 2⁰   p) 3⁻³   q) 2⁻¹   r) 10⁶

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: always write the powers out as repeated multiplication until the
  laws feel obvious; keep a card with the seven laws and one numerical example
  each; test any doubtful rule with small numbers (a = 2). Use the pattern table
  (divide by the base each step) to rebuild a⁰ = 1 and a⁻ⁿ = 1/aⁿ. Harder: show
  that 2¹⁰⁰ + 2¹⁰⁰ = 2¹⁰¹; find the last digit of 7²⁰²⁶ (7, 9, 3, 1 repeat: 9);
  compare 2³⁰⁰ and 3²⁰⁰ by writing them as (2³)¹⁰⁰ and (3²)¹⁰⁰ (3²⁰⁰ is bigger);
  solve 2ˣ + 2ˣ = 64 (x = 5); meet fractional exponents: 9 to the power 1/2 is
  3, because (3)² = 9.


================================================================================
TOPIC 6.3 — SCIENTIFIC NOTATION
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - SCIENTIFIC NOTATION (standard form): a number written as a × 10ⁿ, where 1 ≤
    a < 10 and n is an integer: 4,500,000 = 4.5 × 10⁶, 0.00032 = 3.2 × 10⁻⁴.
  - For a LARGE number (10 or more) n is positive: it is the number of places
    the decimal point moves LEFT to give a number from 1 to 10. For a SMALL
    number (less than 1) n is negative: the point moves RIGHT. For a number from
    1 to 10, n = 0.
  - To convert back, multiply by 10ⁿ: move the point n places RIGHT if n is
    positive, LEFT if n is negative: 6.5 × 10⁻⁴ = 0.00065.
  - 45 × 10⁶ and 0.36 × 10⁻² are NOT in standard form (45 ≥ 10 and 0.36 < 1): 45
    × 10⁶ = 4.5 × 10⁷ and 0.36 × 10⁻² = 3.6 × 10⁻³.
  - COMPARE by the power of 10 first (bigger exponent, bigger number, for
    positive numbers); if the powers are equal, compare the a parts.
  - MULTIPLY: multiply the a parts and ADD the exponents; DIVIDE: divide the a
    parts and SUBTRACT the exponents. Then adjust so that 1 ≤ a < 10: 5 × 10³ ×
    4 × 10⁵ = 20 × 10⁸ = 2 × 10⁹.
  - ADD or SUBTRACT: first write both with the SAME power of 10.
  - SIGNIFICANT FIGURES: count from the first non-zero digit. 149,597,871 to 3
    s.f. is 1.50 × 10⁸; leading zeros in 0.00045 are not significant (2 s.f.).
  - Indian units: 1 lakh = 10⁵, 1 crore = 10⁷, 1 lakh crore = 10¹². Metric: 1 km
    = 10³ m, 1 mm = 10⁻³ m, 1 μm (micrometre) = 10⁻⁶ m, 1 nm (nanometre) = 10⁻⁹
    m.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. The Sun is about 149,600,000 km from the Earth. Write this in scientific
    notation.
    Answer: 149,600,000 km = 1.496 × 10⁸ km.
    Teacher explains: Step 1: place the decimal point after the FIRST non-zero
    digit to get a number between 1 and 10: 1.496. Step 2: count how many places
    the point moved to the LEFT from its original place (at the end of
    149,600,000): 1 4 9 6 0 0 0 0 → 8 places. Step 3: write 1.496 × 10⁸. Check:
    1.496 × 100,000,000 = 149,600,000. Drop the zeros at the end of 1.496000.
    Common mistakes: counting the zeros only (5 zeros, but 8 places), and
    writing 14.96 × 10⁷ (true, but 14.96 is not less than 10). Now you try: the
    Moon is 384,400 km away. (3.844 × 10⁵ km)

D2. A red blood cell is about 0.000007 m across and a human hair is about
    0.00008 m thick. Write both in scientific notation. Which is thicker?
    Answer: 0.000007 m = 7 × 10⁻⁶ m; 0.00008 m = 8 × 10⁻⁵ m. The hair is thicker
    (about 11 times).
    Teacher explains: For a number less than 1, move the point to the RIGHT
    until it is just after the first non-zero digit. 0.000007 → 7: the point
    moves 6 places right, so the exponent is −6: 7 × 10⁻⁶. 0.00008 → 8: 5
    places, so 8 × 10⁻⁵. Check: 10⁻⁶ = 1/1,000,000 and 7/1,000,000 = 0.000007.
    Compare: 10⁻⁵ is bigger than 10⁻⁶ (−5 > −6), so the hair is thicker. 8 ×
    10⁻⁵ ÷ (7 × 10⁻⁶) = 80/7 ≈ 11. Common mistake: a positive exponent for a
    small number. Numbers less than 1 always have a NEGATIVE exponent. Now you
    try: 0.00032. (3.2 × 10⁻⁴)

D3. Write 3.02 × 10⁵ and 6.5 × 10⁻⁴ as ordinary numbers.
    Answer: 3.02 × 10⁵ = 302,000; 6.5 × 10⁻⁴ = 0.00065.
    Teacher explains: Multiplying by 10⁵ moves the decimal point 5 places to the
    RIGHT; fill the empty places with zeros: 3.02 → 30.2 → 302 → 3,020 → 30,200
    → 302,000. Multiplying by 10⁻⁴ (dividing by 10⁴) moves the point 4 places to
    the LEFT: 6.5 → 0.65 → 0.065 → 0.0065 → 0.00065. Check the size: 10⁻⁴ =
    0.0001, and 6.5 ten-thousandths is 0.00065. Count: after the point there are
    3 zeros before 65, one less than 4. Now you try: 7.05 × 10⁶ and 1.2 × 10⁻⁷.
    (7,050,000; 0.00000012)

D4. Rita wrote 45 × 10⁶ and 0.36 × 10⁻² as her answers. Explain why they are not
    in scientific notation and correct them.
    Answer: 45 × 10⁶ = 4.5 × 10⁷ and 0.36 × 10⁻² = 3.6 × 10⁻³.
    Teacher explains: In scientific notation the first number must be at least 1
    and less than 10. 45 is too big: 45 = 4.5 × 10¹, so 45 × 10⁶ = 4.5 × 10¹ ×
    10⁶ = 4.5 × 10⁷. When a gets 10 times SMALLER, the power gets 10 times
    BIGGER (exponent + 1), so the value is unchanged. 0.36 is too small: 0.36 =
    3.6 × 10⁻¹, so 0.36 × 10⁻² = 3.6 × 10⁻³. When a gets 10 times BIGGER, the
    exponent goes DOWN by 1. Check with ordinary numbers: 45,000,000 and 0.0036.
    Now you try: 123 × 10⁻⁵ and 0.07 × 10⁸. (1.23 × 10⁻³; 7 × 10⁶)

D5. The mass of the Earth is 5.97 × 10²⁴ kg and the mass of Venus is 4.87 × 10²⁴
    kg. Which is heavier? Also compare 3.2 × 10⁻⁵ and 9.8 × 10⁻⁶.
    Answer: The Earth is heavier (5.97 > 4.87, same power). 3.2 × 10⁻⁵ > 9.8 ×
    10⁻⁶.
    Teacher explains: First look at the powers of 10. For Earth and Venus they
    are the same (10²⁴), so compare the a parts: 5.97 > 4.87. For the second
    pair the powers differ: −5 > −6, so 10⁻⁵ is ten times 10⁻⁶ and 3.2 × 10⁻⁵ is
    bigger even though 3.2 < 9.8. Check with ordinary numbers: 0.000032 and
    0.0000098. Common mistake: comparing only the front numbers. The exponent
    matters most. (For negative numbers such as −3 × 10⁴ the order reverses, as
    on any number line.) Now you try: which is larger, 7 × 10⁸ or 1.1 × 10⁹?
    (1.1 × 10⁹)

D6. Light travels about 3 × 10⁵ km in one second. How far does it travel in one
    hour (3.6 × 10³ s)?
    Answer: 3 × 10⁵ × 3.6 × 10³ = 10.8 × 10⁸ = 1.08 × 10⁹ km.
    Teacher explains: Multiply the front numbers: 3 × 3.6 = 10.8. Multiply the
    powers of 10 by ADDING exponents: 10⁵ × 10³ = 10⁸. So 10.8 × 10⁸. This is
    not yet standard form, since 10.8 ≥ 10: 10.8 = 1.08 × 10¹, giving 1.08 × 10⁹
    km (about a billion km). The commutative and associative laws let us
    regroup: (3 × 10⁵) × (3.6 × 10³) = (3 × 3.6) × (10⁵ × 10³). With negative
    exponents: (4 × 10⁻³) × (2.5 × 10⁻⁴) = 10 × 10⁻⁷ = 1 × 10⁻⁶. Now you try: (6
    × 10⁴) × (7 × 10⁻²). (4.2 × 10³)

D7. About 1.43 × 10⁹ people live in India, whose area is about 3.29 × 10⁶ km².
    Find the number of people per km², to 2 significant figures.
    Answer: 1.43 × 10⁹ ÷ (3.29 × 10⁶) ≈ 0.435 × 10³ ≈ 4.3 × 10² people per km²,
    about 430.
    Teacher explains: Divide the front numbers: 1.43 ÷ 3.29 ≈ 0.4347 (by
    calculator or long division). Divide the powers by SUBTRACTING exponents:
    10⁹ ÷ 10⁶ = 10³. So about 0.4347 × 10³. Adjust: 0.4347 = 4.347 × 10⁻¹, so
    4.347 × 10². To 2 significant figures: 4.3 × 10² = 430. Estimate first to
    check: 1.4 billion ÷ 3.3 million ≈ 1,400 ÷ 3.3 ≈ 420. A second example: (8 ×
    10⁻³) ÷ (2 × 10⁴) = 4 × 10⁻⁷. Now you try: (9 × 10⁸) ÷ (3 × 10⁻²). (3 ×
    10¹⁰)

D8. A hydrogen atom has a mass of about 1.67 × 10⁻²⁷ kg. What is the mass of 6 ×
    10²³ hydrogen atoms?
    Answer: 1.67 × 10⁻²⁷ × 6 × 10²³ = 10.02 × 10⁻⁴ = 1.002 × 10⁻³ kg, about 1
    gram.
    Teacher explains: Multiply the front numbers: 1.67 × 6 = 10.02. Add the
    exponents: −27 + 23 = −4. So 10.02 × 10⁻⁴ kg. Adjust: 10.02 = 1.002 × 10¹,
    and −4 + 1 = −3: 1.002 × 10⁻³ kg. Since 10⁻³ kg = 1 g, this is about 1.002
    g. Scientists use scientific notation because atoms are so tiny and their
    numbers so huge; writing 0.00000000000000000000000000167 kg would be
    impossible to read. Now you try: the mass of an electron is 9.1 × 10⁻³¹ kg.
    What is the mass of 2 × 10⁵ electrons? (1.82 × 10⁻²⁵ kg)

D9. Work out 3.2 × 10⁵ + 4.5 × 10⁴ and 6 × 10⁻³ − 8 × 10⁻⁴.
    Answer: 3.2 × 10⁵ + 4.5 × 10⁴ = 3.65 × 10⁵; 6 × 10⁻³ − 8 × 10⁻⁴ = 5.2 ×
    10⁻³.
    Teacher explains: We may only add the front numbers when the powers of 10
    are the SAME (like adding 3 tens and 4 tens). Rewrite 3.2 × 10⁵ as 32 × 10⁴.
    Then 32 × 10⁴ + 4.5 × 10⁴ = 36.5 × 10⁴ = 3.65 × 10⁵. For the second, 6 ×
    10⁻³ = 60 × 10⁻⁴, and 60 × 10⁻⁴ − 8 × 10⁻⁴ = 52 × 10⁻⁴ = 5.2 × 10⁻³. Check
    with ordinary numbers: 320,000 + 45,000 = 365,000 and 0.006 − 0.0008 =
    0.0052. Common mistake: 3.2 + 4.5 = 7.7, giving 7.7 × 10⁹ or 7.7 × 10⁵. Now
    you try: 7.1 × 10⁶ − 9 × 10⁵. (6.2 × 10⁶)

D10. The Earth–Sun distance is 149,597,871 km. Write it to 3 significant
     figures. Write 0.0003049 to 2 significant figures. How many significant
     figures has 0.00450?
     Answer: 149,597,871 ≈ 1.50 × 10⁸ km (3 s.f.); 0.0003049 ≈ 3.0 × 10⁻⁴ (2
     s.f.); 0.00450 has 3 significant figures.
     Teacher explains: Significant figures are counted from the FIRST NON-ZERO
     digit. For 149,597,871 the first three are 1, 4, 9; the next digit is 5, so
     round up: 150 (million), which is 1.50 × 10⁸. The final 0 in 1.50 is kept
     to SHOW 3 s.f. For 0.0003049 the zeros before 3 are only place holders and
     do not count; the first two significant figures are 3 and 0, and the next
     digit is 4, so round down: 3.0 × 10⁻⁴. For 0.00450: the leading zeros do
     not count, but the final 0 after the 5 does (it was written on purpose): 4,
     5, 0. In scientific notation every digit of a is significant, which is one
     reason scientists like it. Now you try: 6,371 km (the Earth's radius) to 2
     s.f. (6.4 × 10³ km)

D11. A government budget is about ₹48 lakh crore. Write this in rupees in
     scientific notation. How many crore is 3.5 × 10⁹ rupees?
     Answer: ₹48 lakh crore = 48 × 10⁵ × 10⁷ = 48 × 10¹² = ₹4.8 × 10¹³. 3.5 ×
     10⁹ rupees = 3.5 × 10⁹ ÷ 10⁷ = 350 crore.
     Teacher explains: Change each Indian unit into a power of 10: 1 lakh =
     1,00,000 = 10⁵, 1 crore = 1,00,00,000 = 10⁷, so 1 lakh crore = 10⁵ × 10⁷ =
     10¹². Then 48 × 10¹² = 4.8 × 10¹ × 10¹² = 4.8 × 10¹³. In the international
     system that is 48 trillion (a trillion is 10¹²). For the second, the number
     of crores is the amount ÷ 10⁷: 3.5 × 10⁹⁻⁷ = 3.5 × 10² = 350. Scientific
     notation makes switching between the Indian and international systems easy:
     just count powers of 10. Now you try: 25 lakh in scientific notation. (2.5
     × 10⁶)

D12. Light travels at 3 × 10⁸ m/s and the Sun is 1.5 × 10¹¹ m away. How long
     does sunlight take to reach us? A calculator shows 2.5E−7: what does it
     mean?
     Answer: Time = 1.5 × 10¹¹ ÷ (3 × 10⁸) = 0.5 × 10³ = 5 × 10² s = 500 s = 8
     min 20 s. 2.5E−7 means 2.5 × 10⁻⁷ = 0.00000025.
     Teacher explains: Time = distance ÷ speed. Divide the front numbers: 1.5 ÷
     3 = 0.5, and the powers: 10¹¹ ÷ 10⁸ = 10³. 0.5 × 10³ = 5 × 10² = 500
     seconds, and 500 s = 8 × 60 + 20 s = 8 minutes 20 seconds. So we see the
     Sun as it was about 8 minutes ago! Calculators show scientific notation
     with an E (for 'exponent'): 2.5E−7 is 2.5 × 10⁻⁷ and 6.02E23 is 6.02 ×
     10²³. Do not read 2.5E−7 as 2.5 − 7. Now you try: how long does light take
     to travel 3.84 × 10⁸ m to the Moon? (1.28 s)

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: use a place-value chart and physically move a decimal-point card;
  always check the answer by estimating ('is it huge or tiny?'); remember 'big
  numbers, positive exponent; small numbers, negative exponent'. Harder:
  calculate how many seconds you have lived and write it in scientific notation;
  find the number of atoms in a grain of sand; use a calculator's EXP key;
  compare the sizes of a virus (10⁻⁷ m), a cell, a person, the Earth and the
  Milky Way (10²¹ m) on a powers-of-10 scale.


================================================================================
TOPIC 6.4 — SQUARES AND SQUARE ROOTS; CUBES AND CUBE ROOTS
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - The SQUARE of n is n² = n × n. A PERFECT SQUARE is the square of a whole
    number: 1, 4, 9, 16, 25, ... Know the squares from 1² to 30² and the cubes
    from 1³ to 15³.
  - A perfect square ends in 0, 1, 4, 5, 6 or 9 (never 2, 3, 7, 8), and ends in
    an EVEN number of zeros. The square of an even number is even, of an odd
    number odd.
  - Patterns: 1 + 3 + 5 + ... + (2n − 1) = n²; there are 2n non-square numbers
    between n² and (n + 1)²; (n + 1)² − n² = 2n + 1.
  - The SQUARE ROOT √m is the non-negative number whose square is m: √576 = 24
    since 24² = 576. (−24)² is also 576, but √ always means the positive root.
  - Square root by PRIME FACTORISATION: pair up equal prime factors and take one
    from each pair: 576 = (2 × 2)(2 × 2)(2 × 2)(3 × 3), so √576 = 2 × 2 × 2 × 3
    = 24. By the LONG-DIVISION method: group the digits in pairs from the
    decimal point and work pair by pair.
  - ESTIMATE a non-perfect square root between consecutive integers: 49 < 50 <
    64, so 7 < √50 < 8 (nearer 7). √(a/b) = √a/√b: √(9/16) = 3/4, √0.49 = 0.7.
  - The CUBE of n is n³; the CUBE ROOT ∛m is the number whose cube is m. By
    prime factorisation, group equal factors in THREES: 2,744 = 2³ × 7³, so
    ∛2,744 = 14. Cube roots of negative numbers are negative: ∛(−27) = −3.
  - SMALLEST MULTIPLIER (or divisor) to make a perfect square: multiply (or
    divide) by each prime that is left without a partner. For cubes, complete
    (or remove) the incomplete triples.
  - PYTHAGOREAN TRIPLES: a² + b² = c², e.g. 3, 4, 5. For any m > 1, 2m, m² − 1,
    m² + 1 is a triple: m = 4 gives 8, 15, 17.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. A square courtyard is paved with 576 square tiles. How many tiles are along
    each side? Use prime factorisation.
        2 | 576
        2 | 288
        2 | 144
        2 |  72
        2 |  36
        2 |  18
        3 |   9
        3 |   3
          |   1
    Answer: 576 = 2⁶ × 3², √576 = 2³ × 3 = 24 tiles along each side.
    Teacher explains: Divide by primes in a ladder (shown above): 576 = 2 × 2 ×
    2 × 2 × 2 × 2 × 3 × 3. Make PAIRS of equal factors: (2 × 2)(2 × 2)(2 × 2)(3
    × 3). Every factor has a partner, so 576 IS a perfect square. Take ONE
    factor from each pair: 2 × 2 × 2 × 3 = 24. Check: 24² = 576. In exponent
    form: 576 = 2⁶ × 3² and the square root halves every exponent: 2³ × 3¹ = 24.
    A number is a perfect square exactly when every exponent in its prime
    factorisation is EVEN. Now you try: √1,764. (1,764 = 2² × 3² × 7², √ = 42)

D2. Without calculating, which of these CANNOT be perfect squares: 1,057;
    23,453; 7,928; 222,222; 4,000; 1,069?
    Answer: 1,057, 23,453, 7,928, 222,222 and 4,000 cannot be squares. 1,069
    might be (it is not: 32² = 1,024 and 33² = 1,089).
    Teacher explains: Look at the last digit of the squares 0² to 9²: 0, 1, 4,
    9, 16, 25, 36, 49, 64, 81. They end in 0, 1, 4, 5, 6 or 9 only. So a number
    ending in 2, 3, 7 or 8 is never a perfect square: 1,057 (7), 23,453 (3),
    7,928 (8), 222,222 (2). A square ending in zeros has an EVEN number of them
    (10² = 100, 20² = 400, 100² = 10,000), so 4,000 with 3 zeros is not a
    square. The test only rules numbers OUT: 1,069 ends in 9, so it might be a
    square, but it lies between 32² = 1,024 and 33² = 1,089, so it is not. Now
    you try: can 65,000 or 8,281 be squares? (65,000 no; 8,281 yes, 91²)

D3. Add the first 1, 2, 3, 4, 5 odd numbers. What do you notice? Find 1 + 3 + 5
    + ... + 29 and the number of non-square numbers between 12² and 13².
    Answer: The sums are 1, 4, 9, 16, 25: the squares. 1 + 3 + ... + 29 = 15² =
    225. Between 144 and 169 there are 2 × 12 = 24 non-squares.
    Teacher explains: Picture: a square of dots grows by adding an L-shaped
    border. From a 3 × 3 square (9 dots) to 4 × 4 (16 dots) we add 3 + 3 + 1 = 7
    dots, the 4th odd number. So the sum of the first n odd numbers is n². The
    odd numbers up to 29 are 1, 3, ..., 29: there are (29 + 1)/2 = 15 of them,
    so the sum is 15² = 225. In the same way (n + 1)² − n² = 2n + 1: 13² − 12² =
    25 = 2 × 12 + 1. The numbers strictly between 12² and 13² are 145 to 168:
    that is 25 − 1 = 24 = 2 × 12 numbers, none of them squares. Now you try: 1 +
    3 + 5 + ... + 49 and the number of non-squares between 20² and 21². (625;
    40)

D4. Find √5,776 by the long-division method.
                7  6
            +--------
          7 |  57 76
            |  49
            +--------
        146 |   8 76
            |   8 76
            +--------
            |      0
    Answer: √5,776 = 76.
    Teacher explains: Step 1: pair the digits from the right: 57 76. Step 2: the
    largest square not more than 57 is 7² = 49. Write 7 above and as the
    divisor; 57 − 49 = 8. Step 3: bring down the next pair: 876. Double the root
    so far (7 × 2 = 14) and find the digit d with 14d × d not more than 876: 146
    × 6 = 876 exactly. Write 6 above. Remainder 0, so √5,776 = 76. Check: 76² =
    5,776. The number of pairs (2) tells us the root has 2 digits. Common
    mistake: doubling the last DIGIT instead of the whole root so far. Now you
    try: √106,929 (3 pairs). (327)

D5. Find √12.25 by the long-division method, and √0.0729.
               3. 5
           +--------
         3 |  12.25
           |   9
           +--------
        65 |   3 25
           |   3 25
           +--------
           |      0
    Answer: √12.25 = 3.5; √0.0729 = 0.27.
    Teacher explains: For decimals, pair the digits OUTWARDS from the decimal
    point: 12 . 25 (whole-number part to the left, decimal part to the right).
    3² = 9 ≤ 12, remainder 3. Put the decimal point in the root when we cross
    the point. Bring down 25: 325. Double 3 is 6; 65 × 5 = 325. So √12.25 = 3.5;
    check 3.5² = 12.25. For 0.0729: pairs 0 . 07 29, and √729 = 27 with the
    point placed so that 0.27² = 0.0729 (2 decimal places in the root, 4 in the
    square). Common mistake: √0.09 = 0.3 is right, but √0.9 is NOT 0.3 (0.3² =
    0.09). Now you try: √17.64. (4.2)

D6. Between which two consecutive whole numbers does √50 lie? Find √50 to 1
    decimal place. Estimate √200 in the same way.
    Answer: 7 < √50 < 8 and √50 ≈ 7.1. 14 < √200 < 15 and √200 ≈ 14.1.
    Teacher explains: List squares near 50: 7² = 49 and 8² = 64. 49 < 50 < 64,
    so 7 < √50 < 8, and since 50 is much nearer to 49, √50 is just above 7. Try
    decimals: 7.1² = 50.41 (too big), so √50 is between 7.0 and 7.1; 7.05² =
    49.7025 < 50, so √50 is between 7.05 and 7.1 and rounds to 7.1. For √200:
    14² = 196 and 15² = 225, 14.1² = 198.81, 14.2² = 201.64, so √200 is between
    14.1 and 14.2; 14.15² = 200.2225 is too big, so √200 ≈ 14.1. Most square
    roots are NOT whole numbers (or even fractions); see topic 6.5. Now you try:
    √30 to 1 decimal place. (5.5)

D7. Find the smallest number by which 1,008 must be MULTIPLIED to give a perfect
    square, and the smallest number by which it must be DIVIDED. Find the square
    roots obtained.
    Answer: 1,008 = 2⁴ × 3² × 7. Multiply by 7: 7,056 = 84². Divide by 7: 144 =
    12².
    Teacher explains: Factorise: 1,008 = 2 × 2 × 2 × 2 × 3 × 3 × 7. Pair up: (2
    × 2)(2 × 2)(3 × 3) and 7 is left alone. To MULTIPLY to a square, give 7 a
    partner: 1,008 × 7 = 7,056 = (2² × 3 × 7)², so √7,056 = 4 × 3 × 7 = 84. To
    DIVIDE to a square, remove the lonely 7: 1,008 ÷ 7 = 144 = 12². If several
    primes are unpaired, multiply (or divide) by ALL of them: 2,352 = 2⁴ × 3 ×
    7², so the smallest multiplier is 3 (giving 7,056 = 84²). Now you try: the
    smallest multiplier for 2,028. (2,028 = 2² × 3 × 13², multiply by 3: 6,084 =
    78²)

D8. A ladder 17 m long leans against a wall, its foot 8 m from the wall. How
    high up the wall does it reach? Show how to make Pythagorean triples.
    Answer: √(17² − 8²) = √(289 − 64) = √225 = 15 m.
    Teacher explains: In a right-angled triangle the square on the longest side
    (hypotenuse) equals the sum of the squares on the other two sides
    (Pythagoras, topic 6.21). So height² + 8² = 17², height² = 289 − 64 = 225
    and height = 15 m. Numbers like 8, 15, 17 with a² + b² = c² are PYTHAGOREAN
    TRIPLES. A recipe: for any whole number m > 1, 2m, m² − 1 and m² + 1 always
    work. m = 2: 4, 3, 5; m = 3: 6, 8, 10; m = 4: 8, 15, 17; m = 5: 10, 24, 26;
    m = 6: 12, 35, 37. Check m = 4: 64 + 225 = 289. Multiples of a triple are
    triples too (6, 8, 10 = 2 × (3, 4, 5)). Now you try: the triple with m = 7.
    (14, 48, 50)

D9. A cube-shaped water tank holds 2,744 litres, and 1 litre = 1,000 cm³. Find
    the edge of a cube of volume 2,744 cm³, and ∛(−3,375).
        2 | 2,744
        2 | 1,372
        2 |   686
        7 |   343
        7 |    49
        7 |     7
          |     1
    Answer: 2,744 = 2³ × 7³, so ∛2,744 = 2 × 7 = 14 cm. ∛(−3,375) = −15.
    Teacher explains: Factorise 2,744 in a ladder: 2,744 = 2 × 2 × 2 × 7 × 7 ×
    7. Group equal factors in THREES: (2 × 2 × 2)(7 × 7 × 7). Take one from each
    triple: 2 × 7 = 14. Check: 14³ = 14 × 14 × 14 = 196 × 14 = 2,744. For
    negative numbers: (−15)³ = (−15) × (−15) × (−15) = −3,375, so ∛(−3,375) =
    −∛3,375 = −15, and 3,375 = 3³ × 5³. Unlike square roots, cube roots of
    negative numbers exist. If a factor is left over (not a full triple), the
    number is not a perfect cube. Now you try: ∛9,261. (9,261 = 3³ × 7³, ∛ = 21)

D10. Write the cubes 1³ to 10³. What are their last digits? Show that 1,729 is
     the sum of two cubes in two different ways.
     Answer: 1, 8, 27, 64, 125, 216, 343, 512, 729, 1,000. 1,729 = 1³ + 12³ = 9³
     + 10³.
     Teacher explains: Last digits: 1→1, 2→8, 3→7, 4→4, 5→5, 6→6, 7→3, 8→2, 9→9,
     0→0. Every digit appears, so, unlike squares, a cube can end in ANY digit.
     Notice the swaps 2↔8 and 3↔7. This lets us guess cube roots of perfect
     cubes quickly: ∛175,616 ends in 6 (since 6³ ends in 6), and 175 lies
     between 5³ = 125 and 6³ = 216, so the root is 56. Also cubes of negative
     numbers are negative, and ∛(27/64) = 3/4. 1,729 = 1 + 1,728 = 729 + 1,000:
     the mathematician Ramanujan noticed at once that it is the smallest number
     that is a sum of two cubes in two ways (the Hardy-Ramanujan number). Now
     you try: guess ∛13,824. (24)

D11. Find the smallest number by which 675 must be multiplied to give a perfect
     cube, and the smallest number by which it must be divided.
     Answer: 675 = 3³ × 5². Multiply by 5: 3,375 = 15³. Divide by 25: 27 = 3³.
     Teacher explains: Factorise: 675 = 3 × 3 × 3 × 5 × 5. The 3s make a
     complete triple; the 5s have only 2 of 3. To MULTIPLY to a cube, complete
     the triple with one more 5: 675 × 5 = 3,375 = (3 × 5)³ = 15³. To DIVIDE to
     a cube, remove the incomplete group 5 × 5 = 25: 675 ÷ 25 = 27 = 3³. Compare
     with squares, where we look for PAIRS. A number like 2⁴ × 3 needs × 2² × 3²
     = 36 to become 2⁶ × 3³ = 1,728 = 12³. Now you try: the smallest multiplier
     and divisor for 1,372 = 2² × 7³. (multiply by 2: 2,744 = 14³; divide by 4:
     343 = 7³)

D12. Find (0.3)², √(49/64), √0.0081 and 35². Explain the quick way to square a
     number ending in 5.
     Answer: (0.3)² = 0.09; √(49/64) = 7/8; √0.0081 = 0.09; 35² = 1,225.
     Teacher explains: (0.3)² = 0.3 × 0.3 = 0.09: the square has TWICE as many
     decimal places (2). A common mistake is 0.9. Going backwards, √0.0081 has
     half of 4 decimal places: √81 = 9, so 0.09. For fractions take the roots of
     the top and the bottom: √(49/64) = √49/√64 = 7/8, and √(2 7/9) = √(25/9) =
     5/3 (change a mixed number to an improper fraction first). Numbers ending
     in 5: for (10a + 5)², multiply a by (a + 1) and write 25 after it: 35² → 3
     × 4 = 12, so 1,225; 85² → 8 × 9 = 72, so 7,225. This works because (10a +
     5)² = 100a(a + 1) + 25. Now you try: 65², (1.1)² and √(1 9/16). (4,225;
     1.21; 5/4)
