TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: build squares with square tiles and cubes with unit cubes; keep a
  chart of squares to 30² and cubes to 15³ and test yourself daily; do prime
  factorisation with a ladder and circle the pairs (or triples) in colour.
  Harder: find √2 by long division to 3 decimal places (1.414); prove that a
  square leaves remainder 0 or 1 when divided by 4 (so 4k + 3 is never a
  square); find all 2-digit numbers whose square ends in the same two digits
  (25, 76); show that 1³ + 2³ + ... + n³ = (1 + 2 + ... + n)².


================================================================================
TOPIC 6.5 — INTRODUCTION TO IRRATIONAL NUMBERS (√2); REAL NUMBER LINE
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - The number families: NATURAL numbers N: 1, 2, 3, ...; WHOLE numbers W: 0, 1,
    2, ...; INTEGERS Z: ..., −2, −1, 0, 1, 2, ...; RATIONAL numbers Q: all p/q
    with q ≠ 0. Each family contains the one before it.
  - Every rational number has a decimal that TERMINATES (3/8 = 0.375) or RECURS
    (5/6 = 0.8(3)); and every terminating or recurring decimal is rational
    (0.(36) = 36/99 = 4/11). A fraction in lowest terms terminates exactly when
    its denominator has no prime factors other than 2 and 5.
  - An IRRATIONAL number cannot be written as p/q; its decimal goes on for ever
    WITHOUT repeating: √2 = 1.41421356..., π = 3.14159265...,
    0.101001000100001...
  - √2 is irrational (proof by contradiction). In the same way √n is irrational
    whenever n is a whole number that is not a perfect square: √3, √5, √6, √7,
    √8, √10 ...
  - The REAL numbers R are all the rationals together with all the irrationals.
    Every real number is a point on the number line and every point of the line
    is a real number (the REAL NUMBER LINE).
  - √2 can be marked exactly on the number line: it is the diagonal of a square
    of side 1 (Pythagoras). Swing it down with compasses. Build √3 from √2 and
    1, and √5 from 2 and 1.
  - Useful approximations: √2 ≈ 1.414, √3 ≈ 1.732, √5 ≈ 2.236, π ≈ 3.14159 (22/7
    ≈ 3.142857 and 3.14 are only approximations).
  - rational + irrational is irrational (2 + √3), and a non-zero rational × an
    irrational is irrational (3√2); but two irrationals can give a rational: √2
    × √2 = 2, √2 + (−√2) = 0.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. Put each number in every family it belongs to (N, W, Z, Q, irrational, R):
    7, 0, −4, 3/5, −2.75, 0.(3), √9, √2, π.
        REAL NUMBERS R
        |-- RATIONAL Q:   3/5, −2.75, 0.(3), ...
        |   |-- INTEGERS Z:   −4, −1, ...
        |       |-- WHOLE W:   0, ...
        |           |-- NATURAL N:   1, 7, √9 = 3, ...
        |-- IRRATIONAL:   √2, √3, √5, π, 0.1010010001...
    Answer: 7 and √9 = 3: N, W, Z, Q, R. 0: W, Z, Q, R. −4: Z, Q, R. 3/5, −2.75,
    0.(3): Q, R. √2 and π: irrational, R.
    Teacher explains: Picture the families as boxes inside boxes (the tree
    above). Natural numbers are the counting numbers; add 0 to get the whole
    numbers; add the negatives to get the integers; add all fractions p/q to get
    the rationals. Every natural number is also whole, integer, rational and
    real, so list ALL the families. −2.75 = −11/4 and 0.(3) = 1/3 are rational.
    Simplify first: √9 = 3 is natural! √2 and π are not rational: they sit in
    the separate IRRATIONAL box. Rationals and irrationals together make the
    REAL numbers. Common mistake: thinking √ always means irrational (√16 = 4,
    √(4/9) = 2/3). Now you try: classify −√25 and 22/7. (−5: Z, Q, R; 22/7: Q,
    R)

D2. Write 1/8, 3/11 and 5/6 as decimals. Which terminate? How can you tell
    without dividing?
    Answer: 1/8 = 0.125 (terminates); 3/11 = 0.(27) = 0.272727...; 5/6 = 0.8(3)
    = 0.8333... (both recur).
    Teacher explains: Divide the numerator by the denominator. For 3 ÷ 11 the
    remainders go 3, 8, 3, 8, ...: once a remainder repeats, the digits repeat.
    Dividing by 11 there are only 10 possible non-zero remainders, so a repeat
    MUST come: every fraction gives a terminating or a recurring decimal. The
    quick test: write the fraction in lowest terms and factorise the
    denominator. 8 = 2³ has only 2s, so it terminates (1/8 = 125/1,000). 11 and
    6 = 2 × 3 contain other primes, so they recur. Careful: 3/15 = 1/5
    terminates, although 15 contains 3; simplify first. Now you try: which of
    7/20, 2/9, 9/40, 1/12 terminate? (7/20 and 9/40)

D3. Write 0.(7), 0.(36) and 1.2(3) as fractions.
    Answer: 0.(7) = 7/9; 0.(36) = 36/99 = 4/11; 1.2(3) = 37/30.
    Teacher explains: Let x = 0.777... Then 10x = 7.777... Subtract: 10x − x =
    7, so 9x = 7 and x = 7/9. For a 2-digit block multiply by 100: x =
    0.3636..., 100x = 36.3636..., 99x = 36, x = 36/99 = 4/11. For 1.2333...: x =
    1.2333..., 10x = 12.333..., 100x = 123.333..., so 100x − 10x = 111 and x =
    111/90 = 37/30. Short cut: the block over as many 9s as it has digits (0.(7)
    = 7/9, 0.(123) = 123/999 = 41/333). So every recurring decimal is RATIONAL.
    Check by dividing: 4 ÷ 11 = 0.3636... Now you try: 0.(5) and 0.(12). (5/9;
    4/33)

D4. Prove that √2 is not a rational number.
    Answer: If √2 = p/q in lowest terms, both p and q turn out to be even, which
    is impossible. So √2 is irrational.
    Teacher explains: This is a PROOF BY CONTRADICTION: suppose the opposite and
    show it leads to nonsense. Step 1: suppose √2 = p/q, where p and q are whole
    numbers with NO common factor (lowest terms). Step 2: square: 2 = p²/q², so
    p² = 2q². Step 3: so p² is even, and then p is even (the square of an odd
    number is odd). Write p = 2k. Step 4: then 4k² = 2q², so q² = 2k², and q² is
    even, so q is even. Step 5: now p and q are BOTH even, so they have the
    common factor 2. This contradicts Step 1. So our supposition was false: √2
    cannot be written as p/q. The same idea proves that √3, √5, √6, ... are
    irrational. Now you try: which step would fail for √4? (Step 3's idea: √4 =
    2/1 really is rational)

D5. Square 1.4, 1.41, 1.414 and 1.415. What do the results tell you about √2?
    Answer: 1.4² = 1.96, 1.41² = 1.9881, 1.414² = 1.999396, 1.415² = 2.002225.
    So 1.414 < √2 < 1.415.
    Teacher explains: √2 is the number whose square is exactly 2. 1.4² is a bit
    less than 2, 1.41² is closer, 1.414² is very close, and 1.415² is just over
    2. So √2 is trapped between 1.414 and 1.415. We can keep going: √2 =
    1.41421356237... The digits never end and never fall into a repeating block
    (we proved it is irrational). This is also why the side and the diagonal of
    a square can never both be whole numbers of any unit: the diagonal is √2
    times the side. A calculator shows 1.414213562, but that is rounded;
    squaring it does not give exactly 2. Now you try: between which two numbers
    with 2 decimal places does √3 lie? (1.73 and 1.74)

D6. Show how to mark √2 exactly on the number line.
                    B
                   /|
              √2  / | 1
                 /  |
          <-----O---A-P-+----->
                0   1   2
    Answer: Draw a unit square on the segment from 0 to 1; its diagonal is √2.
    Swing the diagonal down to the number line with compasses.
    Teacher explains: Step 1: draw the number line and mark O at 0 and A at 1.
    Step 2: at A draw AB perpendicular to the line with AB = 1 unit. Step 3:
    join OB. By Pythagoras OB² = OA² + AB² = 1 + 1 = 2, so OB = √2. Step 4: with
    centre O and radius OB draw an arc cutting the number line at P. Then OP =
    √2 and P is the point √2 (about 1.414). The point −√2 is its mirror image on
    the left of 0. This shows that the number line has points that are not
    rational: the rationals alone leave 'holes', which the irrationals fill. Now
    you try: which point do you get with AB = 2 instead of 1? (√5)

D7. Construct √3 and √5 on the number line.
    Answer: √3: a right triangle with legs √2 and 1. √5: a right triangle with
    legs 2 and 1.
    Teacher explains: For √5: mark O at 0 and C at 2. At C draw CD = 1
    perpendicular to the line. Then OD² = 2² + 1² = 5, so OD = √5; swing OD down
    to the line with centre O to get √5 ≈ 2.236. For √3: first make √2 as in D6
    (OB = √2). At B draw BE = 1 perpendicular to OB; then OE² = (√2)² + 1² = 3,
    so OE = √3 ≈ 1.732. Repeating this (a new unit side at right angles each
    time) gives √4 = 2, √5, √6, ...: the SQUARE ROOT SPIRAL. Choose legs whose
    squares add up to the number you want: √10 from 3 and 1, √13 from 3 and 2.
    Now you try: which legs give √17 and √8? (4 and 1; 2 and 2)

D8. Is π equal to 22/7? Compare π, 22/7, 3.14 and 355/113.
    Answer: No. π = 3.14159265..., 22/7 = 3.(142857) = 3.142857..., 3.14 and
    355/113 = 3.14159292... are all approximations.
    Teacher explains: π is the ratio circumference ÷ diameter for every circle.
    It was proved (in 1761) that π is irrational: its decimals never end and
    never repeat, so no fraction equals it. 22/7 is a recurring decimal, so it
    is rational and cannot be π; it is too big by about 0.00126. 3.14 is too
    small by about 0.0016. The Chinese mathematician Zu Chongzhi found 355/113,
    correct to 6 decimal places. Aryabhata (499 CE) gave 62,832/20,000 = 3.1416.
    In calculations we use whichever approximation the question says. Order:
    3.14 < π < 355/113 < 22/7. Now you try: is 3.1416 bigger or smaller than π?
    (bigger)

D9. Which of these are irrational: √16, √20, √(4/9), 0.101001000100001..., 2 +
    √3, √2 × √8, 5.(12), π/π?
    Answer: Irrational: √20, 0.101001000100001... and 2 + √3. Rational: √16 = 4,
    √(4/9) = 2/3, √2 × √8 = √16 = 4, 5.(12) and π/π = 1.
    Teacher explains: Simplify first! √16 = 4 and √(4/9) = 2/3 are rational. 20
    is not a perfect square, so √20 is irrational. 0.1010010001... has a
    pattern, but the blocks of zeros keep getting longer, so no block REPEATS:
    irrational. 5.(12) recurs: rational (5 12/99). 2 + √3: if it were a rational
    r, then √3 = r − 2 would be rational, which is false; so rational +
    irrational is irrational. √2 × √8 = √16 = 4 and π/π = 1: irrational numbers
    CAN combine to give rationals. Now you try: √45, √(1/4), 3π. (irrational;
    1/2 rational; irrational)

D10. Find one rational number and one irrational number between √2 and √3.
     Answer: Rational: 1.5 (or 1.6, 1.7). Irrational: 1.505005000500005... or
     √2.5.
     Teacher explains: √2 ≈ 1.414 and √3 ≈ 1.732, so any decimal between them
     works for a rational: 1.5 = 3/2 (check: 1.5² = 2.25 lies between 2 and 3).
     For an irrational, write a decimal between them that never repeats:
     1.505005000500005... (the number of 0s grows each time). Or take the square
     root of a non-square number between 2 and 3: √2.5 or √(5/2) is irrational
     and lies between √2 and √3. Between ANY two different real numbers there
     are infinitely many rationals AND infinitely many irrationals. Now you try:
     an irrational number between 3 and 4. (e.g. √10, π or 3.303003000...)

D11. Arrange in ascending order: √5, 2.2, 9/4, π − 1, √4.8. Use √5 ≈ 2.236 and π
     ≈ 3.1416.
     Answer: π − 1 < √4.8 < 2.2 < √5 < 9/4.
     Teacher explains: Change everything to decimals to 3 or 4 places: √5 ≈
     2.236, 2.2, 9/4 = 2.25, π − 1 ≈ 2.1416, √4.8 ≈ 2.191 (2.19² = 4.7961 and
     2.2² = 4.84). Ordered: 2.1416, 2.191, 2.2, 2.236, 2.25. For square roots,
     you can also compare the SQUARES: 2.2² = 4.84 is between 4.8 and 5, so √4.8
     < 2.2 < √5. Keep enough decimal places to separate close numbers. Now you
     try: which is bigger, √10 or π? (√10 ≈ 3.162 > π)

D12. Is the sum of two irrational numbers always irrational? What about the
     product? Give examples, and say what the real number line means.
     Answer: No: √2 + (−√2) = 0 and (3 + √2) + (3 − √2) = 6 are rational, but √2
     + √3 is irrational. Products: √2 × √2 = 2 (rational), √2 × √3 = √6
     (irrational).
     Teacher explains: Test with examples: one counter-example is enough to show
     that a rule is NOT always true. (3 + √2) + (3 − √2): the √2 parts cancel,
     leaving 6. (1 + √2)(1 − √2) = 1 − 2 = −1 is rational too. But rational +
     irrational and (non-zero rational) × irrational are ALWAYS irrational. The
     REAL numbers are all the rationals and irrationals together; on the real
     number line every point stands for exactly one real number and every real
     number has exactly one point. Order, addition and multiplication work just
     as before. Now you try: is √2 × √18 rational? (yes: √36 = 6)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------

    a) 7       b) 0        c) −4   d) 3/5               e) −2.75   f) 0.(3)

M6. Use a calculator (or √2 ≈ 1.41421, √3 ≈ 1.73205, √5 ≈ 2.23607, π ≈ 3.14159)

M10. Rational or irrational? If rational, give the value.
     a) √2 × √2     b) √2 + √2               c) √3 × √12           d) √8 ÷ √2
     e) (√5)²       f) 2 + √3                g) √2 − √2            h) π − π
     i) 3√2 ÷ √2    j) (1 + √2) + (1 − √2)   k) (1 + √2)(1 − √2)   l) √2 × √3

M11. True or false?
     a) Every integer is a rational number.
     b) Every rational number is an integer.
     c) √9 is irrational.
     d) 0 is a natural number.
     e) π = 22/7.
     f) The sum of two irrational numbers is always irrational.
     g) Between any two rationals there is an irrational number.
     h) Every point on the number line is a rational number.
     i) Every real number is rational or irrational.
     j) A recurring decimal is irrational.
     k) √2/2 is irrational.
     l) √n is irrational for every whole number n.
     m) 0.(9) = 1.
     n) −√2 is a real number.

     Answers: a) true   b) false   c) false   d) false   e) false   f) false
              g) true   h) false   i) true   j) false   k) true   l) false
              m) true   n) true   o) false   p) false

M12. Give the value correct to 3 decimal places.
     a) √2        b) √3        c) √5        d) √6        e) √7        f) √8
     g) √10       h) √11       i) √12       j) √13       k) √15       l) √20
     m) π         n) 22/7      o) 355/113   p) ∛2
     Answers: a) 1.414   b) 1.732   c) 2.236   d) 2.449   e) 2.646   f) 2.828

              m) 3.142   n) 3.143   o) 3.142   p) 1.260

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: sort number cards into nested boxes (N, W, Z, Q) and a separate
  irrational box; square decimals on a calculator to 'hunt' for √2; do the √2
  construction with real compasses and measure (about 1.41 units). Harder: prove
  that √3 is irrational (use 'if p² is a multiple of 3 then p is'); prove that
  √2 + √3 is irrational (square it); explain why 0.(9) = 1; find the
  continued-fraction approximations 3/2, 7/5, 17/12, 41/29 of √2 and see how
  close each is.


================================================================================
TOPIC 6.6 — ALGEBRAIC EXPRESSIONS: LIKE TERMS, SIMPLIFICATION
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - An algebraic EXPRESSION is built from TERMS joined by + and −. In 5x²y − 3xy
    + 7 the terms are 5x²y, −3xy and 7. The sign in front belongs to the term.
  - In a term, the number part is the (numerical) COEFFICIENT and the letters
    are the VARIABLES: in −3xy the coefficient is −3. A term with no variable is
    a CONSTANT. x means 1x and −x means −1x.
  - An expression with 1 term is a MONOMIAL, 2 terms a BINOMIAL, 3 terms a
    TRINOMIAL; any such sum of terms (with whole-number powers) is a POLYNOMIAL.
    The DEGREE of a term is the sum of the powers of its variables (x²y has
    degree 3).
  - LIKE TERMS have exactly the same variables raised to the same powers: 3x²y
    and −5yx² are like; x²y and xy² are UNLIKE. Only like terms can be added or
    subtracted: 3x²y + x²y = 4x²y.
  - To ADD polynomials, collect like terms (a column layout helps). To SUBTRACT,
    change the sign of EVERY term being subtracted, then add.
  - BRACKETS: +(a − b) = a − b but −(a − b) = −a + b. Multiply out k(a + b) = ka
    + kb. Remove the innermost brackets first.
  - MULTIPLYING: multiply the coefficients and add the powers of each variable:
    (−3a²b)(4ab³) = −12a³b⁴. Multiply every term of one bracket by every term of
    the other: (2x + 3)(x − 4) = 2x² − 8x + 3x − 12 = 2x² − 5x − 12.
  - To EVALUATE, substitute the values in brackets and use the order of
    operations: 3x² at x = −2 is 3 × (−2)² = 12.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. In 5x²y − 3xy + 7 − x, list the terms, the coefficient of each term, the
    constant term and the degree of each term.
    Answer: Terms 5x²y, −3xy, 7, −x. Coefficients 5, −3, (7 is the constant),
    −1. Degrees 3, 2, 0, 1; the expression has 4 terms and degree 3.
    Teacher explains: Split the expression at every + or − sign that is not
    inside a bracket, and keep each sign WITH the term after it: 5x²y | −3xy |
    +7 | −x. The coefficient is the number multiplying the letters: −3xy has
    coefficient −3 (not 3), and −x = −1x has coefficient −1. 7 has no variable:
    it is the constant term. Degree: add the powers of the variables: x²y is x²
    × y¹, so 2 + 1 = 3; xy: 1 + 1 = 2; x: 1; a constant: 0. The degree of the
    expression is its highest term degree, 3. Now you try: the coefficient of
    ab² in 4a²b − ab² + 9. (−1)

D2. Sort into groups of like terms: 3x²y, −5xy², x²y, 7yx², 4xy, −xy², 2x²,
    −8xy, 0.5x²y.
    Answer: Group 1: 3x²y, x²y, 7yx², 0.5x²y. Group 2: −5xy², −xy². Group 3:
    4xy, −8xy. Group 4: 2x².
    Teacher explains: Like terms have the SAME letters with the SAME powers; the
    coefficient does not matter, and neither does the order of the letters (yx²
    = x²y, because multiplication is commutative). Check the power of EACH
    letter: x²y has x to the power 2 and y to the power 1, while xy² has x to
    the power 1 and y to the power 2, so they are UNLIKE, even though both use x
    and y and both have degree 3. 2x² has no y, so it is on its own. Common
    mistake: calling x²y and xy² like terms. Now you try: are 6ab²c and −cb²a
    like? (yes)

D3. Simplify 3x²y − 5xy² + x²y + 2xy² − 4 + 7.
    Answer: 4x²y − 3xy² + 3.
    Teacher explains: Underline like terms in the same way (x²y terms, xy²
    terms, constants) and bring them together, keeping each sign with its term:
    (3x²y + x²y) + (−5xy² + 2xy²) + (−4 + 7). Add the coefficients only: 3 + 1 =
    4, so 4x²y; −5 + 2 = −3, so −3xy²; −4 + 7 = 3. The letters and powers do NOT
    change when we add: 3x²y + x²y is 4x²y, not 4x⁴y². Unlike terms stay
    separate, so the answer has 3 terms. Check with x = 1, y = 2: the start
    gives 6 − 20 + 2 + 8 − 4 + 7 = −1 and the answer gives 8 − 12 + 3 = −1. Now
    you try: 6ab − 2a² + ab + 5a² − 9ab. (3a² − 2ab)

D4. Add 4a² − 3ab + 2b² and −a² + 5ab − 7, using a column layout.
             4a² − 3ab + 2b²
          + −a² + 5ab       − 7
          ---------------------
             3a² + 2ab + 2b² − 7
    Answer: 3a² + 2ab + 2b² − 7.
    Teacher explains: Write like terms in the same column, leaving a gap where a
    term is missing (as above). Then add each column: a²: 4 + (−1) = 3; ab: −3 +
    5 = 2; b²: 2 (nothing to add); constants: −7. The column method stops you
    adding unlike terms by accident. You can also write it in a row: (4a² − 3ab
    + 2b²) + (−a² + 5ab − 7) = 4a² − a² − 3ab + 5ab + 2b² − 7. Adding brackets
    with a + sign in front changes nothing inside. Now you try: (2x² + 3x − 1) +
    (x² − 5x + 4). (3x² − 2x + 3)

D5. Subtract 2x² − 6x − 1 from 5x² − 2x + 3.
             5x² − 2x + 3
          − (2x² − 6x − 1)      change every sign and add:

             5x² − 2x + 3
          + −2x² + 6x + 1
          ----------------
             3x² + 4x + 4
    Answer: (5x² − 2x + 3) − (2x² − 6x − 1) = 3x² + 4x + 4.
    Teacher explains: 'Subtract A from B' means B − A: the one after 'from'
    comes first. The minus sign in front of the bracket changes the sign of
    EVERY term inside: −(2x² − 6x − 1) = −2x² + 6x + 1. Then add, as in the
    columns above: 5x² − 2x² = 3x², −2x + 6x = 4x, 3 + 1 = 4. Common mistake:
    changing only the first sign, giving 5x² − 2x + 3 − 2x² − 6x − 1 = 3x² − 8x
    + 2. Check with x = 1: B = 6, A = −5 and B − A = 11, and 3 + 4 + 4 = 11. Now
    you try: subtract a² − 4ab + b² from 3a² + ab. (2a² + 5ab − b²)

D6. Simplify 7 − (3 − 2x) + 2(x − 4) − 3(1 − x) and 5 − [2x − (3 − x)].
    Answer: 7 − (3 − 2x) + 2(x − 4) − 3(1 − x) = 7x − 7; 5 − [2x − (3 − x)] = 8
    − 3x.
    Teacher explains: Remove one bracket at a time, watching the sign in front:
    −(3 − 2x) = −3 + 2x; +2(x − 4) = 2x − 8; −3(1 − x) = −3 + 3x (the −3
    multiplies BOTH terms, and (−3) × (−x) = +3x). Now collect: x terms 2x + 2x
    + 3x = 7x; numbers 7 − 3 − 8 − 3 = −7. For nested brackets work from the
    INSIDE out: 2x − (3 − x) = 2x − 3 + x = 3x − 3, then 5 − [3x − 3] = 5 − 3x +
    3 = 8 − 3x. Check with x = 2: 7 − (−1) + (−4) − (−3) = 7 and 7 × 2 − 7 = 7.
    Now you try: 4(2a − 1) − (a − 5). (7a + 1)

D7. A rectangular garden is (3x + 2) m long and (2x − 1) m wide. Find its
    perimeter. Anu buys 3 pens at ₹p each and 2 notebooks at ₹(p + 15) each;
    write the total cost.
    Answer: Perimeter = 2(3x + 2) + 2(2x − 1) = 10x + 2 m. Cost = 3p + 2(p + 15)
    = ₹(5p + 30).
    Teacher explains: Perimeter = 2 × length + 2 × width. Multiply out each
    bracket: 2(3x + 2) = 6x + 4 and 2(2x − 1) = 4x − 2. Collect: 6x + 4x = 10x
    and 4 − 2 = 2, so 10x + 2 m. Check with x = 3: the sides are 11 m and 5 m,
    so the perimeter is 32 m, and 10 × 3 + 2 = 32. For the shopping: 3 pens cost
    3p, 2 notebooks cost 2(p + 15) = 2p + 30, total 5p + 30 rupees. If p = 10:
    30 + 50 = ₹80 and 5 × 10 + 30 = 80. Writing and simplifying expressions is
    how we describe EVERY case at once. Now you try: the perimeter of a triangle
    with sides 2a, a + 5 and 3a − 2. (6a + 3)

D8. Multiply (−3a²b) × (4ab³). Find the area of a rectangle 3x cm by 5xy cm and
    the volume of a box 2a by 3b by 4c.
    Answer: (−3a²b) × (4ab³) = −12a³b⁴; area = 15x²y cm²; volume = 24abc.
    Teacher explains: Multiply in three steps: (1) the signs: − × + = −; (2) the
    coefficients: 3 × 4 = 12; (3) each letter, ADDING the powers: a² × a = a³, b
    × b³ = b⁴. So −12a³b⁴. Area: 3x × 5xy = 15x²y (x × x = x²). Volume: 2a × 3b
    × 4c = 24abc: different letters are just written side by side. Common
    mistakes: adding coefficients (7a³b⁴) or multiplying the powers (a²b³).
    Compare: 3x + 5x = 8x but 3x × 5x = 15x². Now you try: (−2mn²)(−5m³n).
    (10m⁴n³)

D9. Multiply out 3x(2x² − 5x + 4) and −2ab(a − 3b + 1).
                 |  2x²  |  −5x  |   4
          -------+-------+-------+------
            3x   |  6x³  | −15x² |  12x
    Answer: 3x(2x² − 5x + 4) = 6x³ − 15x² + 12x; −2ab(a − 3b + 1) = −2a²b + 6ab²
    − 2ab.
    Teacher explains: The distributive law: the term outside multiplies EVERY
    term inside. Draw arrows from 3x to each term: 3x × 2x² = 6x³, 3x × (−5x) =
    −15x², 3x × 4 = 12x. For −2ab, be careful with signs: −2ab × a = −2a²b, −2ab
    × (−3b) = +6ab², −2ab × 1 = −2ab. The grid above (an area model) shows the
    first product: the outside term down the side, the terms of the bracket
    along the top. 3 terms inside the bracket give 3 terms in the answer. Common
    mistake: multiplying only the first term: 3x(2x² − 5x + 4) = 6x³ − 5x + 4 is
    WRONG. Now you try: −4y(y² − 3y + 2). (−4y³ + 12y² − 8y)

D10. Multiply (2x + 3)(x − 4) and (a + 2b)(3a − b).
                  |   x   |  −4
           -------+-------+------
             2x   |  2x²  |  −8x
             3    |  3x   |  −12
     Answer: (2x + 3)(x − 4) = 2x² − 5x − 12; (a + 2b)(3a − b) = 3a² + 5ab −
     2b².
     Teacher explains: Each term of the first bracket multiplies each term of
     the second, so there are 2 × 2 = 4 products; the grid above shows them:
     2x², −8x, 3x, −12. Collect the like terms −8x + 3x = −5x. In words: First,
     Outer, Inner, Last. For the second: a × 3a = 3a², a × (−b) = −ab, 2b × 3a =
     6ab, 2b × (−b) = −2b², and −ab + 6ab = 5ab. Check with x = 1: (5)(−3) = −15
     and 2 − 5 − 12 = −15. Common mistake: multiplying only first × first and
     last × last (2x² − 12), which misses the middle terms. A binomial ×
     trinomial gives 6 products: (x + 2)(x² − x + 3) = x³ + x² + x + 6. Now you
     try: (3y − 2)(y + 5). (3y² + 13y − 10)

D11. Find the value of 3x² − 2xy + y² when x = −2 and y = 3.
     Answer: 3 × (−2)² − 2 × (−2) × 3 + 3² = 12 + 12 + 9 = 33.
     Teacher explains: Replace each letter by its value IN BRACKETS, then follow
     the order of operations (powers first, then ×, then + and −): 3 × (−2)² = 3
     × 4 = 12; −2 × (−2) × 3 = +12; 3² = 9. Total 33. Common mistakes: writing 3
     × −2² and getting −12 (the square must include the minus sign), and
     squaring before multiplying by 3 wrongly: 3x² means 3 × x², not (3x)².
     Simplify first if you can; it often makes the substitution easier: 4a − 2(a
     − b) = 2a + 2b, so at a = 7, b = −2 the value is 10. Now you try: a² − 3ab
     at a = −1, b = 4. (13)

D12. Think of a number, double it, add 6, halve the result and subtract the
     number you first thought of. Why is the answer always 3?
     Answer: With n: (2n + 6) ÷ 2 − n = n + 3 − n = 3.
     Teacher explains: Let the number be n. Doubling gives 2n; adding 6 gives 2n
     + 6; halving divides EVERY term by 2: n + 3; subtracting n leaves 3. The n
     disappears, so the answer does not depend on the number chosen. Try it: 10
     → 20 → 26 → 13 → 3. Algebra PROVES that the trick works for every number,
     which trying examples can never do. Make your own: multiply by 3, add 12,
     divide by 3, subtract the number: (3n + 12)/3 − n = 4. Now you try: add 5,
     multiply by 2, subtract 4, halve, subtract the starting number. What is
     left? (3)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. Find.
    a) coefficient of x² in 5x² − 3x + 7
    b) coefficient of x in 5x² − 3x + 7
    c) coefficient of ab² in 4a²b − ab² + 9
    d) coefficient of xy in 7x²y − 3xy + 2
    e) coefficient of y² in y³ − y² + y
    f) coefficient of p in 8 − p + 2p²
    g) coefficient of x²y in 3x²y − 5xy² + x²y
    h) coefficient of m in (3/4)m + 1
    i) coefficient of ab in a² + b² − 2ab
    j) coefficient of z³ in z³/5 − z
    k) coefficient of x in 4xy − 3x + y
    l) coefficient of t² in 2t − t² + 6t³
    m) constant term of 5x² − 3x + 7
    n) constant term of 2a − 9 + a²
    o) constant term of xy + 4
    p) constant term of 3p − 2q
    q) degree of 5x²y − 3xy + 7 − x
    r) degree of a⁴ − 3a²b³
    s) degree of 7
    t) degree of 2xyz + x²
    u) degree of 4m − 9
    v) degree of p²q² − p³
    Answers: a) 5   b) −3   c) −1   d) −3   e) −1   f) −1   g) 4   h) 3/4
             i) −2   j) 1/5   k) −3   l) −1   m) 7   n) −9   o) 4   p) 0   q) 3
             r) 5   s) 0   t) 3   u) 1   v) 4

M2. Like or unlike terms?
    a) 3x²y and −5yx²   b) x²y and xy²    c) 4ab and −ba       d) 7m and 7m²
    e) 2pq² and −q²p    f) 5 and −8       g) x and y           h) 3a²b³ and a³b²
    i) −xyz and 4zyx    j) 6t² and t²/2   k) 9ab²c and −cb²a   l) 2x and 2
    m) m²n and nm²      n) x³ and 3x      o) 0.5k and −k       p) ab and abc
    Answers: a) like   b) unlike   c) like   d) unlike   e) like   f) like
             g) unlike   h) unlike   i) like   j) like   k) like   l) unlike
             m) like   n) unlike   o) like   p) unlike

M3. Simplify by collecting like terms.
    a) 3x²y − 5xy² + x²y + 2xy² − 4 + 7   b) 6ab − 2a² + ab + 5a² − 9ab
    c) 4x + 3y − x + 2y                   d) 7p − 3q + 2 − 4p + q − 5
    e) a² + 2a² − 5a + a                  f) m + m + m − 2m
    g) 5xy − 3yx + xy                     h) 2x² − 3x + 4 − x² + 3x − 4
    i) 8k − (−3k) + k                     j) −4ab² + 3a²b − ab² + 2a²b
    k) x³ − 2x² + 3x³ + x − x²            l) 0.5t + 1.5t − t
    m) (1/2)x + (1/3)x                    n) 9 − 2y + 3y² − 7 + y − y²
    o) 3pq − 2p + 4qp − 5p                p) a + 2b + 3c − 3a − 2b − c
    q) 12x²y² − 5x²y² − 7x²y²             r) −3m²n + 4mn² − m²n + 2mn² − mn
    s) x − 2x + 3x − 4x + 5x              t) 2ab + 3bc − ab − 3cb + ac
    Answers: a) 4x²y − 3xy² + 3   b) 3a² − 2ab   c) 3x + 5y   d) 3p − 2q − 3
             e) 3a² − 4a   f) m   g) 3xy   h) x²   i) 12k   j) 5a²b − 5ab²
             k) 4x³ − 3x² + x   l) t   m) 5x/6   n) 2y² − y + 2   o) 7pq − 7p
             p) 2c − 2a   q) 0   r) 6mn² − 4m²n − mn   s) 3x   t) ab + ac

M4. Add.
    a) (4a² − 3ab + 2b²) + (−a² + 5ab − 7)
    b) (2x² + 3x − 1) + (x² − 5x + 4)
    c) (3p − 2q) + (5q − p)
    d) (x² − 1) + (2x + 1)
    e) (7m²n − 2mn²) + (−3m²n − mn² + mn)
    f) (a − b + c) + (b − c + a)
    g) (5y³ − 2y) + (−5y³ + 2y)
    h) (x² + xy + y²) + (x² − xy + y²)
    i) (0.5k + 2) + (1.5k − 3)
    j) (6 − 2t + t²) + (t² + 2t − 6)
    k) (3a²b − 4) + (2 − a²b)
    l) (p² − 2pq) + (3pq − q²)
    m) (x³ + 2x) + (3x² − x³)
    n) (4ab − 3bc + 2ca) + (bc − ca − ab)
    o) (−2x² + 5x − 3) + (−x² − 5x + 3)
    p) (m + 2n) + (2m + n)
    Answers:

      g) 0

      j) 2t²
      k) 2a²b − 2
      l) p² + pq − q²
      m) 3x² + 2x

      p) 3m + 3n

M5. Subtract the second expression FROM the first (first − second).

M7. Multiply.

M8. Multiply out.

M9. Multiply out and simplify.
    a) (2x + 3)(x − 4)       b) (a + 2b)(3a − b)        c) (3y − 2)(y + 5)
    d) (x + 2)(x + 3)        e) (x − 5)(x − 1)          f) (2p + 1)(2p − 1)
    g) (m − 3)(m + 7)        h) (4a − b)(a + 2b)        i) (x + y)(x − 2y)
    j) (5 − t)(3 + t)        k) (2x² + 1)(x − 3)        l) (k + 1)(k + 1)
    m) (ab − 2)(ab + 3)      n) (3x − 4y)(2x − 5y)      o) (x + 2)(x² − x + 3)
    p) (a − 1)(a² + a + 1)   q) (2m + 3)(m² − 2m − 1)   r) (p + q)(p − q + 1)
    Answers:
      a) 2x² − 5x − 12
      b) 3a² + 5ab − 2b²
      c) 3y² + 13y − 10
      d) x² + 5x + 6
      e) x² − 6x + 5
      f) 4p² − 1
      g) m² + 4m − 21
      h) 4a² + 7ab − 2b²
      i) x² − xy − 2y²
      j) 15 + 2t − t²
      k) 2x³ − 6x² + x − 3
      l) k² + 2k + 1
      m) a²b² + ab − 6
      n) 6x² − 23xy + 20y²
      o) x³ + x² + x + 6
      p) a³ − 1
      q) 2m³ − m² − 8m − 3
      r) p² − q² + p + q

M10. Find the value.
     a) 3x² − 2xy + y²;  x = −2, y = 3
     b) a² − 3ab;  a = −1, b = 4
     c) 5m − 2n;  m = 3, n = −4
     d) x³ − x;  x = −3
     e) 2p² + 3p − 5;  p = 1/2
     f) (a + b)(a − b);  a = 7, b = −3
     g) 4xyz;  x = 2, y = −1, z = −5
     h) t² − 6t + 9;  t = 3
     i) −k² + 4;  k = −2
     j) a²b − ab²;  a = 2, b = −1
     k) 3(x − 1) − 2(x + 1);  x = 10
     l) x² + y²;  x = −1/2, y = 1/3
     m) m³ + n³;  m = −2, n = 2
     n) (2a − b)²;  a = −1, b = −4
     o) pq − p − q;  p = 5, q = −5
     p) x² − 2x;  x = −3/2
     q) 10 − 3y²;  y = −2
     r) abc − (a + b + c);  a = 1, b = −2, c = 3
     Answers: a) 33   b) 13   c) 23   d) −24   e) −3   f) 40   g) 40   h) 0
              i) 0   j) −6   k) 5   l) 13/36   m) 0   n) 4   o) −25   p) 21/4
              q) −2   r) −8

M11. Simplify fully.
     a) 2x(x − 3) − 3(x² − 2x)            b) (x + 2)(x + 3) − x(x + 5)
     c) a(a + b) − b(a − b)               d) (p − 1)(p + 1) − (p² − 1)
     e) 3m(m − 2) + 2m(3 − m)             f) (2a + b)(a − b) + ab
     g) x²(x − 1) − x(x² − x)             h) (y + 4)(y − 4) + 16
     i) 4(x² − 1) − (2x − 1)(2x + 1)      j) (t + 1)(t + 2)(t + 3)
     k) ab(a − b) + b²(a + b)             l) (k − 2)² − (k² − 4k)
     m) 5 − 2(x − (3 − x))                n) (x + y)(x − y) − (x − y)(x − y)
     o) 2p²q × 3pq − 5p³q²                p) (3x − 1)(x + 2) − 3x(x + 1)
     Answers:
       a) −x²
       b) 6
       c) a² + b²
       d) 0
       e) m²
       f) 2a² − b²
       g) 0
       h) y²
       i) −3
       j) t³ + 6t² + 11t + 6
       k) a²b + b³
       l) 4
       m) 11 − 4x
       n) 2xy − 2y²
       o) p³q²
       p) 2x − 2

M12. True or false? If false, correct it.
     a) 2x + 3y = 5xy              b) −(x − 3) = −x − 3
     c) 3x × 2x = 6x               d) (2x)² = 2x²
     e) x² + x² = 2x²              f) x² × x³ = x⁶
     g) 5a − (2a − 3) = 3a + 3     h) 3(a + 4) = 3a + 4
     i) x²y + xy² = 2x²y²          j) (x + 3)(x − 2) = x² + x − 6
     k) 4 − 2(x + 1) = 2x + 2      l) −x² at x = −3 is 9
     m) a − b = b − a              n) 6x ÷ 2 = 3x
     o) (a + b)(a + b) = a² + b²   p) 2(3x)(4y) = 24xy
     Answers: a) false (cannot be simplified)   b) false (−x + 3)
              c) false (6x²)   d) false (4x²)   e) true   f) false (x⁵)
              g) true   h) false (3a + 12)   i) false (cannot be simplified)
              j) true   k) false (2 − 2x)   l) false (−9)
              m) false (only when a = b)   n) true   o) false (a² + 2ab + b²)
              p) true

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: use algebra tiles (x² tiles, x strips, unit squares, two colours
  for signs) to collect like terms and to build products as rectangles;
  highlight like terms in the same colour; check every simplification by
  substituting a small number such as x = 2. Harder: show that the sum of any
  three consecutive integers is a multiple of 3 (n + (n + 1) + (n + 2) = 3(n +
  1)); find the product (x + 1)(x + 2)(x + 3)(x + 4) and show it is one less
  than a perfect square; make up your own 'think of a number' tricks and prove
  them.


================================================================================
TOPIC 6.7 — ALGEBRAIC IDENTITIES: (a+b)², (a−b)², (a+b)(a−b), (x+a)(x+b)
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - An IDENTITY is an equality that is true for EVERY value of the variables,
    e.g. (a + b)² = a² + 2ab + b². (An equation such as 2x + 1 = 7 is true only
    for some values.)
  - The four standard identities: (a + b)² = a² + 2ab + b²; (a − b)² = a² − 2ab
    + b²; (a + b)(a − b) = a² − b²; (x + a)(x + b) = x² + (a + b)x + ab.
  - (a + b)² is NOT a² + b²: the middle term 2ab is the most common thing to
    forget. Check: (3 + 4)² = 49 but 3² + 4² = 25.
  - In (x + a)(x + b) the middle coefficient is the SUM a + b and the constant
    is the PRODUCT ab (with signs): (x + 5)(x − 3) = x² + 2x − 15.
  - Treat a whole term as 'a' or 'b': (3x + 2y)² = (3x)² + 2(3x)(2y) + (2y)² =
    9x² + 12xy + 4y².
  - Identities make mental arithmetic easy: 103² = (100 + 3)² = 10,609; 98² =
    (100 − 2)² = 9,604; 97 × 103 = 100² − 3² = 9,991.
  - Used in reverse they recognise perfect squares and differences of squares:
    49x² − 42xy + 9y² = (7x − 3y)²; 83² − 17² = 100 × 66.
  - Extension: (a + b + c)² = a² + b² + c² + 2ab + 2bc + 2ca; (a + b)³ = a³ +
    3a²b + 3ab² + b³; (a − b)³ = a³ − 3a²b + 3ab² − b³.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. A square of side (a + b) is cut into pieces as shown. Use the areas to prove
    that (a + b)² = a² + 2ab + b².
                a         b
           +---------+-----+
           |         |     |
         a |   a²    | ab  |
           |         |     |
           +---------+-----+
         b |   ab    | b²  |
           +---------+-----+
    Answer: Area of the big square = (a + b)². The four pieces are a², ab, ab
    and b², so (a + b)² = a² + 2ab + b².
    Teacher explains: The side of the big square is a + b, so its area is (a +
    b)². Cut it along the lines a from the corner, as in the picture. There is a
    square a × a (area a²), a square b × b (area b²) and TWO rectangles a × b
    (area ab each). The pieces fill the whole square with no overlap, so (a +
    b)² = a² + ab + ab + b² = a² + 2ab + b². The picture shows clearly why a² +
    b² is too small: it leaves out the two rectangles. Numerical check with a =
    5, b = 3: (5 + 3)² = 64 and 25 + 30 + 9 = 64. Now you try: draw the picture
    for (x + 4)² and write its area. (x² + 8x + 16)

D2. Prove (a + b)² = a² + 2ab + b² and (a − b)² = a² − 2ab + b² by multiplying
    out. Check both with a = 5, b = 3.
    Answer: (a + b)(a + b) = a² + ab + ba + b² = a² + 2ab + b²; (a − b)(a − b) =
    a² − ab − ba + b² = a² − 2ab + b². Check: 64 = 25 + 30 + 9 and 4 = 25 − 30 +
    9.
    Teacher explains: (a + b)² means (a + b) × (a + b). Multiply every term by
    every term: a × a = a², a × b = ab, b × a = ab, b × b = b², and ab + ab =
    2ab. For (a − b)²: a × a = a², a × (−b) = −ab, (−b) × a = −ab, (−b) × (−b) =
    +b². So the last term is +b² in BOTH identities; only the middle sign
    changes. Another way: (a − b)² is (a + (−b))², so put −b for b in the first
    identity. With a = 5, b = 3: (5 − 3)² = 4 and 25 − 30 + 9 = 4. Now you try:
    expand (x − 6)². (x² − 12x + 36)

D3. Show that (a + b)(a − b) = a² − b² by multiplying, and explain it with a
    picture.
    Answer: (a + b)(a − b) = a² − ab + ab − b² = a² − b².
    Teacher explains: Multiplying: a × a = a², a × (−b) = −ab, b × a = +ab, b ×
    (−b) = −b². The middle terms −ab and +ab CANCEL, leaving a² − b². Picture:
    from a square a × a cut out a small square b × b from one corner; the area
    left is a² − b². Cut the L-shape into two strips and move one: they form a
    rectangle (a + b) long and (a − b) wide. Check with a = 10, b = 3: 13 × 7 =
    91 and 100 − 9 = 91. This is called the DIFFERENCE OF TWO SQUARES. Examples:
    (x + 7)(x − 7) = x² − 49, (2p − 5q)(2p + 5q) = 4p² − 25q². Now you try: (3m
    + 4)(3m − 4). (9m² − 16)

D4. Explain the identity (x + a)(x + b) = x² + (a + b)x + ab and use it for (x +
    5)(x + 3), (x + 5)(x − 3), (x − 5)(x + 3) and (x − 5)(x − 3).
    Answer: x² + 8x + 15; x² + 2x − 15; x² − 2x − 15; x² − 8x + 15.
    Teacher explains: Multiply out: x × x = x², x × b + a × x = (a + b)x, a × b
    = ab. So the middle coefficient is the SUM of the two numbers and the last
    term is their PRODUCT. With signs: (x + 5)(x − 3): a = 5, b = −3, sum 2,
    product −15. (x − 5)(x + 3): sum −2, product −15. (x − 5)(x − 3): sum −8,
    product +15 (negative × negative). Notice the pattern of signs in the four
    answers. (x + a)(x − a) is the special case with sum 0: x² − a². Check (x +
    5)(x − 3) at x = 1: 6 × (−2) = −12 and 1 + 2 − 15 = −12. Now you try: (y −
    7)(y + 2). (y² − 5y − 14)

D5. Expand (3x + 2y)² and (5a − 1/2)².
    Answer: (3x + 2y)² = 9x² + 12xy + 4y²; (5a − 1/2)² = 25a² − 5a + 1/4.
    Teacher explains: Here the 'a' of the identity is 3x and the 'b' is 2y.
    Write the pattern with brackets first: (3x)² + 2(3x)(2y) + (2y)². Then work
    out each part: (3x)² = 9x² (square the 3 as well!), 2 × 3x × 2y = 12xy,
    (2y)² = 4y². For (5a − 1/2)²: (5a)² − 2(5a)(1/2) + (1/2)² = 25a² − 5a + 1/4.
    Common mistakes: writing 3x² instead of 9x², and writing the middle term as
    6xy (forgetting the 2). Check with x = 1, y = 1: (3 + 2)² = 25 and 9 + 12 +
    4 = 25. Now you try: (4m − 3n)². (16m² − 24mn + 9n²)

D6. Work out 103² and 98² in your head.
    Answer: 103² = (100 + 3)² = 10,000 + 600 + 9 = 10,609; 98² = (100 − 2)² =
    10,000 − 400 + 4 = 9,604.
    Teacher explains: Split the number into a round number and a small part. For
    103: a = 100, b = 3: a² = 10,000, 2ab = 2 × 100 × 3 = 600, b² = 9, total
    10,609. For 98 = 100 − 2 use (a − b)²: 10,000 − 2 × 100 × 2 + 4 = 10,000 −
    400 + 4 = 9,604. Choose the round number near the one you are squaring: 51²
    = (50 + 1)² = 2,500 + 100 + 1 = 2,601 and 49² = (50 − 1)² = 2,500 − 100 + 1
    = 2,401. Common mistake: forgetting the 2ab term (103² = 10,009 is wrong).
    Now you try: 1,002² and 997². (1,004,004; 994,009)

D7. Work out 97 × 103 and 47 × 53 in your head.
    Answer: 97 × 103 = (100 − 3)(100 + 3) = 10,000 − 9 = 9,991; 47 × 53 = (50 −
    3)(50 + 3) = 2,500 − 9 = 2,491.
    Teacher explains: When two numbers are the same distance either side of a
    round number, use (a − b)(a + b) = a² − b². 97 and 103 are both 3 away from
    100, so the product is 100² − 3². 47 and 53 are 3 away from 50, so 50² − 3².
    This also works with decimals: 9.8 × 10.2 = 10² − 0.2² = 100 − 0.04 = 99.96.
    Find the MIDDLE number first: halfway between 36 and 44 is 40, so 36 × 44 =
    40² − 4² = 1,600 − 16 = 1,584. Now you try: 198 × 202 and 61 × 59. (39,996;
    3,599)

D8. Work out 105 × 107 and 102 × 96 using (x + a)(x + b).
    Answer: 105 × 107 = 100² + (5 + 7) × 100 + 5 × 7 = 11,235; 102 × 96 = 100² +
    (2 − 4) × 100 + 2 × (−4) = 9,792.
    Teacher explains: Take x = 100. 105 × 107 = (100 + 5)(100 + 7) = x² + (a +
    b)x + ab with a = 5, b = 7: 10,000 + 1,200 + 35 = 11,235. For 102 × 96, a =
    2 and b = −4: 10,000 + (−2) × 100 + (−8) = 10,000 − 200 − 8 = 9,792. Check
    by long multiplication: 102 × 96 = 9,600 + 192 = 9,792. It also works when
    both numbers are below 100: 97 × 94 = 10,000 + (−3 − 6) × 100 + (−3) × (−6)
    = 10,000 − 900 + 18 = 9,118. Common mistake: forgetting that ab is negative
    when a and b have different signs. Now you try: 1,003 × 1,004. (1,007,012)

D9. Write 49x² − 42xy + 9y² as a square. What must be added to x² + 10x to make
    a perfect square? Find 83² − 17² quickly.
    Answer: 49x² − 42xy + 9y² = (7x − 3y)²; add 25: x² + 10x + 25 = (x + 5)²;
    83² − 17² = (83 + 17)(83 − 17) = 100 × 66 = 6,600.
    Teacher explains: Using an identity BACKWARDS: the first and last terms are
    squares, 49x² = (7x)² and 9y² = (3y)². Test the middle term: 2 × 7x × 3y =
    42xy, and its sign is minus, so it is (7x − 3y)². For x² + 10x: compare with
    x² + 2ax + a²: 2a = 10, so a = 5 and we add a² = 25 (this is called
    completing the square). For 83² − 17², the difference of squares is a
    product: (a + b)(a − b). This is much quicker than 6,889 − 289. Now you try:
    25m² + 40m + 16 as a square, and 55² − 45². ((5m + 4)²; 1,000)

D10. Two numbers a and b have a + b = 7 and ab = 10. Find a² + b² and (a − b)²
     without finding a and b.
     Answer: a² + b² = 7² − 2 × 10 = 29; (a − b)² = 29 − 20 = 9.
     Teacher explains: From (a + b)² = a² + 2ab + b² we get a² + b² = (a + b)² −
     2ab = 49 − 20 = 29. From (a − b)² = a² + b² − 2ab = 29 − 20 = 9 (so a − b
     is 3 or −3). Check: the numbers are 5 and 2: 25 + 4 = 29 and (5 − 2)² = 9.
     A famous one: if x + 1/x = 3, then x² + 1/x² = (x + 1/x)² − 2 = 9 − 2 = 7,
     because 2 × x × (1/x) = 2. Identities let us move between sums, products
     and squares. Now you try: a − b = 4, ab = 5; find a² + b². (26)

D11. Extension: find (a + b + c)² and use it to expand (x + 2y + 3)².
     Answer: (a + b + c)² = a² + b² + c² + 2ab + 2bc + 2ca; (x + 2y + 3)² = x² +
     4y² + 9 + 4xy + 12y + 6x.
     Teacher explains: Treat (a + b) as one term: (a + b + c)² = ((a + b) + c)²
     = (a + b)² + 2(a + b)c + c² = a² + 2ab + b² + 2ac + 2bc + c². In words: the
     squares of all three terms plus twice the product of every pair. A 3 × 3
     grid of pieces shows it as areas (3 squares and 6 rectangles). For x + 2y +
     3: squares x², 4y², 9; pairs 2(x)(2y) = 4xy, 2(2y)(3) = 12y, 2(3)(x) = 6x.
     Check with x = y = 1: (1 + 2 + 3)² = 36 and 1 + 4 + 9 + 4 + 12 + 6 = 36.
     Now you try: (a − b + c)². (a² + b² + c² − 2ab − 2bc + 2ca)

D12. Extension: expand (a + b)³ and (a − b)³, and use them to find 11³ and 99³.
     Answer: (a + b)³ = a³ + 3a²b + 3ab² + b³; (a − b)³ = a³ − 3a²b + 3ab² − b³.
     11³ = 1,331; 99³ = 970,299.
     Teacher explains: (a + b)³ = (a + b)(a + b)² = (a + b)(a² + 2ab + b²).
     Multiply: a³ + 2a²b + ab² + a²b + 2ab² + b³ = a³ + 3a²b + 3ab² + b³. The
     coefficients 1, 3, 3, 1 are a row of Pascal's triangle. Replace b by −b for
     (a − b)³: the signs alternate. 11³ = (10 + 1)³ = 1,000 + 300 + 30 + 1 =
     1,331. 99³ = (100 − 1)³ = 1,000,000 − 30,000 + 300 − 1 = 970,299. Common
     mistake: (a + b)³ = a³ + b³. Check with a = b = 1: 8, not 2. Now you try:
     (x + 2)³. (x³ + 6x² + 12x + 8)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. Expand using (a + b)² = a² + 2ab + b².
    a) (x + 3)²       b) (a + 7)²       c) (2a + 5)²      d) (3x + 2y)²
    e) (p + 1/2)²     f) (ab + c)²      g) (x² + 1)²      h) (4m + 3n)²
    i) (y + 10)²      j) (5 + 2k)²      k) (x/2 + 2)²     l) (6p + q)²
    m) (0.5m + 4)²    n) (xy + 3)²      o) (2a² + 3b)²    p) (t + 1/t)²
    q) (7 + 3x)²      r) (10a + 1)²     s) (a/3 + b/2)²   t) (x³ + 2)²
    Answers: a) x² + 6x + 9   b) a² + 14a + 49   c) 4a² + 20a + 25
             d) 9x² + 12xy + 4y²   e) p² + p + 1/4   f) a²b² + 2abc + c²
             g) x⁴ + 2x² + 1   h) 16m² + 24mn + 9n²   i) y² + 20y + 100
             j) 4k² + 20k + 25   k) x²/4 + 2x + 4   l) 36p² + 12pq + q²
             m) m²/4 + 4m + 16   n) x²y² + 6xy + 9   o) 4a⁴ + 12a²b + 9b²
             p) t² + 2 + 1/t²   q) 9x² + 42x + 49   r) 100a² + 20a + 1
             s) a²/9 + ab/3 + b²/4   t) x⁶ + 4x³ + 4

M2. Expand using (a − b)² = a² − 2ab + b².
    a) (x − 6)²      b) (a − 4)²      c) (3x − 1)²     d) (4m − 3n)²
    e) (5a − 1/2)²   f) (2p − 7q)²    g) (y − 10)²     h) (x² − 3)²
    i) (1 − 2k)²     j) (ab − 5)²     k) (−x − 3)²     l) (9 − y)²
    m) (x/3 − 3)²    n) (2t − 1/t)²   o) (0.2a − 5)²   p) (6m − 5n)²
    q) (x − y)²      r) (3 − a²)²     s) (7p − 2)²     t) (−2a + b)²
    Answers: a) x² − 12x + 36   b) a² − 8a + 16   c) 9x² − 6x + 1
             d) 16m² − 24mn + 9n²   e) 25a² − 5a + 1/4   f) 4p² − 28pq + 49q²
             g) y² − 20y + 100   h) x⁴ − 6x² + 9   i) 4k² − 4k + 1
             j) a²b² − 10ab + 25   k) x² + 6x + 9   l) y² − 18y + 81
             m) x²/9 − 2x + 9   n) 4t² − 4 + 1/t²   o) a²/25 − 2a + 25
             p) 36m² − 60mn + 25n²   q) x² − 2xy + y²   r) a⁴ − 6a² + 9
             s) 49p² − 28p + 4   t) 4a² − 4ab + b²

M3. Expand using (a + b)(a − b) = a² − b².
    a) (x + 7)(x − 7)         b) (3m + 4)(3m − 4)
    c) (2p − 5q)(2p + 5q)     d) (a − 1)(a + 1)
    e) (x² + y²)(x² − y²)     f) (10 + k)(10 − k)
    g) (1/2 + x)(1/2 − x)     h) (ab + c)(ab − c)
    i) (4 − 3y)(4 + 3y)       j) (x + 2y)(x − 2y)
    k) (0.3a − 1)(0.3a + 1)   l) (7m − n)(n + 7m)
    m) (−x + 5)(−x − 5)       n) (x/2 − 3)(x/2 + 3)
    o) (t + 1/t)(t − 1/t)     p) (p² − 9)(p² + 9)
    q) (2a + 3b)(3b − 2a)     r) (xy − 6)(xy + 6)
    s) (5 + 8z)(5 − 8z)       t) (11a − 1)(11a + 1)
    Answers: a) x² − 49   b) 9m² − 16   c) 4p² − 25q²   d) a² − 1   e) x⁴ − y⁴
             f) 100 − k²   g) 1/4 − x²   h) a²b² − c²   i) 16 − 9y²
             j) x² − 4y²   k) 9a²/100 − 1   l) 49m² − n²   m) x² − 25
             n) x²/4 − 9   o) t² − 1/t²   p) p⁴ − 81   q) 9b² − 4a²
             r) x²y² − 36   s) 25 − 64z²   t) 121a² − 1

M4. Expand using (x + a)(x + b) = x² + (a + b)x + ab.
    a) (x + 5)(x + 3)       b) (x + 5)(x − 3)
    c) (x − 5)(x + 3)       d) (x − 5)(x − 3)
    e) (y − 7)(y + 2)       f) (a + 1)(a + 9)
    g) (m − 4)(m − 6)       h) (p + 8)(p − 8)
    i) (x + 10)(x − 1)      j) (t − 12)(t + 3)
    k) (k + 1/2)(k + 1/4)   l) (x − 2)(x − 11)
    m) (n + 6)(n − 9)       n) (z − 1)(z + 1)
    o) (x + 0.5)(x − 1.5)   p) (a − 3)(a + 13)
    q) (2x + 1)(2x + 5)     r) (3p − 2)(3p + 4)
    s) (xy + 2)(xy − 7)     t) (x² + 3)(x² + 4)
    u) (y − 20)(y − 5)      v) (b + 4)(b + 4)
    w) (c − 15)(c + 4)      x) (m + 2/3)(m − 1/3)
    Answers: a) x² + 8x + 15   b) x² + 2x − 15   c) x² − 2x − 15
             d) x² − 8x + 15   e) y² − 5y − 14   f) a² + 10a + 9
             g) m² − 10m + 24   h) p² − 64   i) x² + 9x − 10   j) t² − 9t − 36
             k) k² + 3k/4 + 1/8   l) x² − 13x + 22   m) n² − 3n − 54   n) z² − 1
             o) x² − x − 3/4   p) a² + 10a − 39   q) 4x² + 12x + 5
             r) 9p² + 6p − 8   s) x²y² − 5xy − 14   t) x⁴ + 7x² + 12
             u) y² − 25y + 100   v) b² + 8b + 16   w) c² − 11c − 60
             x) m² + m/3 − 2/9

    a) 103²     b) 98²      c) 51²      d) 49²      e) 999²     f) 1,001²

M7. Use (x + a)(x + b) = x² + (a + b)x + ab with a round number x.
    a) 105 × 107       b) 102 × 96        c) 103 × 104       d) 1,003 × 1,004

M8. Fill in the missing term (the positive one) to make a perfect square, and
    write the square.
    a) x² + __ + 25      b) 4a² + 12ab + __
    c) __ − 30x + 25     d) 9p² − __ + 16q²

M9. Write as a square (a ± b)² or as a product (a + b)(a − b).
    a) x² + 6x + 9       b) 49x² − 42xy + 9y²
    c) a² − 16           d) 25m² − 36n²

M10. Use the identities (do not find a and b).
     a) a + b = 7, ab = 10: find a² + b²

     e) a + b = 10, ab = 21: find a² + b²

     k) a + b = 6, a − b = 2: find ab
     l) a + b = 9, ab = 20: find (a − b)²
     m) a + b = 1, ab = −6: find a² + b²

M11. Simplify using the identities.
     a) (x + 3)² − (x − 3)²               b) (a + b)² + (a − b)²

M12. Extension: expand a) to l); use (a ± b)³ for m) to r).
     a) (a + b + c)²       b) (x + 2y + 3)²      c) (a − b + c)²
     d) (x + y − 1)²       e) (a + b)³           f) (a − b)³

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: always write the pattern with brackets before simplifying ((3x)² +
  2(3x)(2y) + (2y)²); cut the (a + b)² square out of paper and rearrange it;
  check every expansion by putting in a = 1, b = 2 (or x = 1); say the rule
  aloud: 'square the first, twice the product, square the last'. Harder: prove
  that the product of four consecutive integers plus 1 is a perfect square; show
  that (a² + b²)(c² + d²) = (ac − bd)² + (ad + bc)²; find 1,234,567² − 1,234,566
  × 1,234,568 without a calculator (1); investigate Pascal's triangle for (a +
  b)⁴.


================================================================================
TOPIC 6.8 — FACTORISATION OF ALGEBRAIC EXPRESSIONS
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - To FACTORISE an expression is to write it as a PRODUCT of factors. It is
    expanding done backwards: expanding 3(x + 5) gives 3x + 15; factorising
    3x + 15 gives 3(x + 5). ALWAYS CHECK by expanding your answer.
  - COMMON FACTOR: find the HCF of all the terms (HCF of the numbers and the
    lowest power of each letter found in EVERY term) and take it outside a
    bracket: 6a²b − 9ab² = 3ab(2a − 3b). A bracket can also be a common factor:
    a(x + y) + 3(x + y) = (x + y)(a + 3).
  - GROUPING (four terms): pair the terms, take a common factor from each pair,
    and then take out the common bracket:
    ax + ay + bx + by = a(x + y) + b(x + y) = (x + y)(a + b).
  - DIFFERENCE OF SQUARES: a² − b² = (a + b)(a − b). Examples:
    x² − 49 = (x + 7)(x − 7); 9p² − 25q² = (3p + 5q)(3p − 5q). A SUM of squares
    such as x² + 49 cannot be factorised this way.
  - PERFECT SQUARES: a² + 2ab + b² = (a + b)² and a² − 2ab + b² = (a − b)².
    Test: first and last terms are squares and the middle term is 2 × a × b.
    x² − 10x + 25 = (x − 5)²; 4x² + 12x + 9 = (2x + 3)².
  - x² + bx + c: find two numbers that MULTIPLY to c and ADD to b.
    x² + 7x + 12 = (x + 3)(x + 4). Signs: c positive, b positive: both +; c
    positive, b negative: both −; c negative: one + and one −, the bigger number
    takes the sign of b.
  - ax² + bx + c (SPLITTING THE MIDDLE TERM): find two numbers that multiply to
    a × c and add to b, split bx into two terms, then group: 6x² + 11x + 3
    = 6x² + 2x + 9x + 3 = 2x(3x + 1) + 3(3x + 1) = (3x + 1)(2x + 3).
  - FACTORISE FULLY: always take out a common factor first, then look for a
    pattern: 2x² − 18 = 2(x² − 9) = 2(x + 3)(x − 3). Some expressions (such as
    x² + x + 1) cannot be factorised with whole numbers.
  - DIVIDING: divide a polynomial by a monomial term by term
    ((12x³ − 8x²) ÷ 4x = 3x² − 2x); divide by a binomial by factorising and
    cancelling ((x² + 5x + 6) ÷ (x + 2) = x + 3) or by long division. Check:
    quotient × divisor = dividend.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. A rectangular garden has area (12x + 18) square metres and its width is 6 m.
    Find its length by factorising. Then factorise 6a²b − 9ab².
    Answer: 12x + 18 = 6(2x + 3), so the length is (2x + 3) m.
    6a²b − 9ab² = 3ab(2a − 3b).
    Teacher explains: Area = width × length, so we need 12x + 18 written as
    6 × (something). Step 1: find the HCF of the terms 12x and 18. HCF of 12 and
    18 is 6; x is not in 18, so no letter goes in the HCF. Step 2: divide each
    term by the HCF: 12x ÷ 6 = 2x and 18 ÷ 6 = 3. Step 3: write
        12x + 18 = 6(2x + 3)        check: 6 × 2x + 6 × 3 = 12x + 18
    For 6a²b − 9ab² find the HCF part by part: numbers: HCF(6, 9) = 3; letter a:
    a² and a, lowest power a; letter b: b and b², lowest power b. So HCF = 3ab.
    Then 6a²b ÷ 3ab = 2a and 9ab² ÷ 3ab = 3b:
        6a²b − 9ab² = 3ab(2a − 3b)
    Common mistake: taking out only 3 or only 3a. The answer 3a(2ab − 3b²) is
    not wrong but is not FULLY factorised, because the bracket still has the
    common factor b. Now you try: 15p²q + 20pq². (5pq(3p + 4q))

D2. Factorise −4x − 8 (take out a negative factor) and a(x + y) + 3(x + y). Then
    factorise 5m(m − 2) − (m − 2).
    Answer: −4x − 8 = −4(x + 2); a(x + y) + 3(x + y) = (x + y)(a + 3);
    5m(m − 2) − (m − 2) = (m − 2)(5m − 1).
    Teacher explains: When the first term is negative it is often neater to take
    out a NEGATIVE common factor. −4x ÷ (−4) = x and −8 ÷ (−4) = +2, so
    −4x − 8 = −4(x + 2). Watch the sign inside: −4(x − 2) would expand to
    −4x + 8. In a(x + y) + 3(x + y) the whole bracket (x + y) is in both terms,
    so treat it like one letter, say B: aB + 3B = B(a + 3):
        a(x + y) + 3(x + y) = (x + y)(a + 3)
    In 5m(m − 2) − (m − 2), the second term is −1 × (m − 2). Taking out (m − 2)
    leaves 5m from the first term and −1 from the second:
        5m(m − 2) − 1(m − 2) = (m − 2)(5m − 1)
    Common mistake: forgetting the 1 and writing (m − 2)(5m). Check by
    expanding: (m − 2)(5m − 1) = 5m² − m − 10m + 2 = 5m² − 11m + 2, and
    5m(m − 2) − (m − 2) = 5m² − 10m − m + 2 = 5m² − 11m + 2. Now you try:
    2p(q − 3) + 7(q − 3) and −6y² − 9y. ((q − 3)(2p + 7) and −3y(2y + 3))

D3. Factorise by grouping: 6xy − 4y + 9x − 6. Then factorise ab − ac − b + c.
    Answer: 6xy − 4y + 9x − 6 = (3x − 2)(2y + 3);
    ab − ac − b + c = (b − c)(a − 1).
    Teacher explains: Four terms with no factor common to ALL of them: try
    GROUPING in pairs.
        6xy − 4y + 9x − 6
      = 2y(3x − 2) + 3(3x − 2)     (common factor from each pair)
      = (3x − 2)(2y + 3)           (3x − 2 is now a common bracket)
    The two brackets after step 1 MUST be the same; if they are not, try another
    pairing or a different sign. In ab − ac − b + c take out −1 from the second
    pair so that the brackets match:
        ab − ac − b + c
      = a(b − c) − 1(b − c)        (note −b + c = −1(b − c))
      = (b − c)(a − 1)
    Common mistake: writing −1(b + c) for −b + c. Check:
    (b − c)(a − 1) = ab − b − ac + c. Now you try: xy + 5x + 2y + 10 and
    pq − 4p − 3q + 12. ((y + 5)(x + 2) and (q − 4)(p − 3))

D4. Explain with a diagram why a² − b² = (a + b)(a − b). Use it to factorise
    x² − 49, 9x² − 25y² and 49 − 4m².
    Answer: x² − 49 = (x + 7)(x − 7); 9x² − 25y² = (3x + 5y)(3x − 5y);
    49 − 4m² = (7 + 2m)(7 − 2m).
    Teacher explains: Take a square of side a and cut a small square of side b
    from one corner. The L-shape left has area a² − b². Cut the L into two
    rectangles and put them end to end:
            <------ a ------>
            +-----------+---+
            |     A     |   |  a − b     A is a by (a − b)
            +-----------+---+
            |     B     | X |  b         X (b by b) is cut away
            +-----------+---+
            <-- a − b -->
        Turn B on its end and put it next to A:
            +---------------+-----+
            |       A       |  B  |  a − b
            +---------------+-----+
            <------ a ------> <-b->
    The new rectangle is (a + b) long and (a − b) wide, so
    a² − b² = (a + b)(a − b). Algebra agrees:
    (a + b)(a − b) = a² − ab + ab − b² = a² − b². To use it, write each term as
    a square: x² − 49 = x² − 7²; 9x² − 25y² = (3x)² − (5y)²;
    49 − 4m² = 7² − (2m)². Common mistakes: x² + 49 is a SUM of squares and does
    NOT factorise; (x − 7)² is x² − 14x + 49, not x² − 49. Now you try: 16a² − 1
    and 100 − 81p². ((4a + 1)(4a − 1) and (10 + 9p)(10 − 9p))

D5. Without a calculator work out 53² − 47², 101 × 99 and 7.5² − 2.5².
    Answer: 53² − 47² = 600; 101 × 99 = 9,999; 7.5² − 2.5² = 50.
    Teacher explains: Difference of squares makes some arithmetic quick.
        53² − 47² = (53 + 47)(53 − 47) = 100 × 6 = 600
        101 × 99  = (100 + 1)(100 − 1) = 100² − 1² = 10,000 − 1 = 9,999
        7.5² − 2.5² = (7.5 + 2.5)(7.5 − 2.5) = 10 × 5 = 50
    Check the first the long way: 53² = 2,809 and 47² = 2,209, and
    2,809 − 2,209 = 600. The factorised way needs no squaring at all. For
    101 × 99 we used the identity backwards: two numbers equally far either side
    of 100. Now you try: 65² − 35² and 48 × 52. (30 × 100 = 3,000 and
    50² − 2² = 2,496)

D6. Show that x² + 10x + 25 and 4x² − 12x + 9 are perfect squares and factorise
    them. Is x² + 8x + 25 a perfect square?
    Answer: x² + 10x + 25 = (x + 5)²; 4x² − 12x + 9 = (2x − 3)²; x² + 8x + 25 is
    not a perfect square.
    Teacher explains: The identities are a² + 2ab + b² = (a + b)² and
    a² − 2ab + b² = (a − b)². Test in three steps:
        x² + 10x + 25:  x² = (x)², 25 = 5², 2 × x × 5 = 10x  yes
                       so x² + 10x + 25 = (x + 5)²
        4x² − 12x + 9:  4x² = (2x)², 9 = 3², 2 × 2x × 3 = 12x  yes
                       middle sign −, so 4x² − 12x + 9 = (2x − 3)²
        x² + 8x + 25:   2 × x × 5 = 10x, not 8x  no
    Check: (2x − 3)² = (2x − 3)(2x − 3) = 4x² − 6x − 6x + 9 = 4x² − 12x + 9. The
    sign in the bracket is the sign of the middle term; the last term of a
    perfect square is always positive. Now you try: 9y² + 24y + 16 and
    m² − 14m + 49. ((3y + 4)² and (m − 7)²)

D7. Factorise x² + 7x + 12. Explain why we look for two numbers that multiply to
    12 and add to 7.
    Answer: x² + 7x + 12 = (x + 3)(x + 4).
    Teacher explains: Expand a general product:
    (x + p)(x + q) = x² + qx + px + pq = x² + (p + q)x + pq. So the two numbers
    p and q must ADD to the x-coefficient (7) and MULTIPLY to the constant (12).
    List the factor pairs of 12 and their sums:
        factor pair   1, 12   2, 6   3, 4
        sum             13      8      7   <- this one
    So p = 3, q = 4 and x² + 7x + 12 = (x + 3)(x + 4). Check:
    (x + 3)(x + 4) = x² + 4x + 3x + 12 = x² + 7x + 12. The order of the brackets
    does not matter. Now you try: x² + 9x + 20 and x² + 13x + 36.
    ((x + 4)(x + 5) and (x + 4)(x + 9))

D8. Factorise x² − 8x + 15, x² + 2x − 15 and x² − 2x − 15. What do the signs
    tell you?
    Answer: x² − 8x + 15 = (x − 3)(x − 5); x² + 2x − 15 = (x + 5)(x − 3);
    x² − 2x − 15 = (x − 5)(x + 3).
    Teacher explains: Look at the constant first. In x² − 8x + 15 the product is
    +15 but the sum is −8, so BOTH numbers are negative: (−3) × (−5) = 15 and
    (−3) + (−5) = −8. In x² ± 2x − 15 the product is −15, so the numbers have
    DIFFERENT signs; pairs 1, 15 and 3, 5 have differences 14 and 2, so use 3
    and 5. The bigger number takes the sign of the middle term:
        constant   middle   the two numbers
          +          +      both +
          +          −      both −
          −          +      one + one −, bigger one +
          −          −      one + one −, bigger one −
        x² + 2x − 15:  +5 and −3   (5 × (−3) = −15, 5 − 3 = 2)
        x² − 2x − 15:  −5 and +3   ((−5) × 3 = −15, −5 + 3 = −2)
    Common mistake: (x + 5)(x − 3) for x² − 2x − 15. Expanding gives
    x² + 2x − 15, so the signs are the wrong way round. Now you try:
    x² − 11x + 24, x² + 4x − 21 and x² − x − 42. ((x − 3)(x − 8), (x + 7)(x − 3)
    and (x − 7)(x + 6))

D9. Factorise 6x² + 11x + 3 by splitting the middle term.
    Answer: 6x² + 11x + 3 = (3x + 1)(2x + 3).
    Teacher explains: Step 1: multiply the first and last coefficients:
    a × c = 6 × 3 = 18. Step 2: find two numbers that multiply to 18 and add to
    11: 2 and 9 (1 + 18 = 19, 2 + 9 = 11, 3 + 6 = 9). Step 3: split 11x into
    2x + 9x and group in pairs:
        6x² + 11x + 3
      = 6x² + 2x + 9x + 3
      = 2x(3x + 1) + 3(3x + 1)
      = (3x + 1)(2x + 3)
    Check: (3x + 1)(2x + 3) = 6x² + 9x + 2x + 3 = 6x² + 11x + 3. Why does it
    work? The two numbers are exactly the ones that make the grouping brackets
    match. Writing 9x + 2x instead gives 3x(2x + 3) + 1(2x + 3), the same
    answer. Now you try: 2x² + 7x + 3 and 3x² + 10x + 8. ((2x + 1)(x + 3) and
    (3x + 4)(x + 2))

D10. Factorise 3x² − 5x − 2. Then factorise FULLY 2x² − 8 and 3x³ − 12x² + 12x.
     Answer: 3x² − 5x − 2 = (3x + 1)(x − 2); 2x² − 8 = 2(x + 2)(x − 2);
     3x³ − 12x² + 12x = 3x(x − 2)².
     Teacher explains: For 3x² − 5x − 2: a × c = 3 × (−2) = −6. Two numbers with
     product −6 and sum −5: −6 and +1. Split and group:
          3x² − 6x + x − 2
        = 3x(x − 2) + 1(x − 2)
        = (x − 2)(3x + 1)
     FACTORISE FULLY means keep going until no bracket can be factorised again.
     ALWAYS look for a common factor first:
         2x² − 8 = 2(x² − 4) = 2(x + 2)(x − 2)
         3x³ − 12x² + 12x = 3x(x² − 4x + 4) = 3x(x − 2)²
     Common mistake: (2x + 4)(x − 2) for 2x² − 8. It expands correctly, but
     2x + 4 still has the factor 2, so it is not fully factorised. Now you try:
     5x² + 13x − 6 and 4x² − 100. ((5x − 2)(x + 3) and 4(x + 5)(x − 5))

D11. Divide (12x³ − 8x² + 4x) ÷ 4x and (x² + 5x + 6) ÷ (x + 2). Show the long
     division for (2x² + 7x + 3) ÷ (x + 3).
     Answer: 3x² − 2x + 1; x + 3; 2x + 1.
     Teacher explains: Dividing by a MONOMIAL: divide every term by it.
         12x³ ÷ 4x = 3x²,   −8x² ÷ 4x = −2x,   4x ÷ 4x = 1
         so (12x³ − 8x² + 4x) ÷ 4x = 3x² − 2x + 1
     Common mistake: forgetting the last term, because 4x ÷ 4x = 1, not 0.
     Dividing by a BINOMIAL: factorise and cancel: x² + 5x + 6 = (x + 2)(x + 3),
     so dividing by (x + 2) leaves x + 3. When you cannot see the factors, use
     long division, just like with numbers:
                 2x + 1
              ___________
       x + 3 ) 2x² + 7x + 3      2x² ÷ x = 2x
               2x² + 6x          2x × (x + 3)
               --------
                      x + 3      subtract, bring down +3; x ÷ x = 1
                      x + 3      1 × (x + 3)
                      -----
                          0      remainder 0
     Check: (x + 3)(2x + 1) = 2x² + x + 6x + 3 = 2x² + 7x + 3. Now you try:
     (15a²b − 10ab²) ÷ 5ab and (x² − 2x − 24) ÷ (x − 6). (3a − 2b and x + 4)

D12. Check these factorisations by expanding. Correct any that are wrong: (i)
     x² − 9x + 20 = (x + 4)(x + 5); (ii) x² − 16 = (x − 4)²; (iii)
     4x² + 6x = 2(2x² + 3x).
     Answer: (i) wrong: (x − 4)(x − 5). (ii) wrong: (x + 4)(x − 4). (iii)
     correct but not fully factorised: 2x(2x + 3).
     Teacher explains: Expanding is the fastest check. (i)
     (x + 4)(x + 5) = x² + 9x + 20; the middle sign is wrong. Both numbers must
     be negative: (x − 4)(x − 5) = x² − 9x + 20. (ii) (x − 4)² = x² − 8x + 16,
     which has an x-term; the difference of squares gives
     (x + 4)(x − 4) = x² − 16. (iii) 2(2x² + 3x) = 4x² + 6x is true, but the
     bracket still has the common factor x, so the full answer is 2x(2x + 3).
     You can also test a factorisation with a number: put x = 1 in (i):
     1 − 9 + 20 = 12 but (1 + 4)(1 + 5) = 30, so it is wrong. Now you try: is
     x² + x − 12 = (x − 4)(x + 3)? (no: that gives x² − x − 12; correct is
     (x + 4)(x − 3))

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. Find the HCF of the terms.
    a) 12x, 18                b) 8a, 12b                c) 6x², 9x
    d) 14p, 21pq              e) 8a²b, 12ab²            f) 15x³, 25x²
    g) 4m, 6m², 10m³          h) 18xy, 27x²y            i) 9, 12y, 15y²
    j) 20p²q², 30pq³          k) 7a², 14a, 21           l) 16x⁴, 24x³, 8x²
    m) 5ab, 7bc               n) 36m²n, 48mn², 60mn     o) 11x, 13y
    p) 2x²y³, 3x³y²           q) 45a³b², 75a²b³         r) 6x, 10x², 14x³, 18x⁴
    Answers: a) 6   b) 4   c) 3x   d) 7p   e) 4ab   f) 5x²   g) 2m   h) 9xy
             i) 3   j) 10pq²   k) 7   l) 8x²   m) b   n) 12mn   o) 1   p) x²y²
             q) 15a²b²   r) 2x

M2. Factorise by taking out the highest common factor. When the first term is
    negative, take out a negative factor.
    a) 12x + 18            b) 5a − 20             c) 8p + 12q
    d) 7m − 7              e) 3x² + 6x            f) 10y² − 15y

M3. Factorise. Take out a common bracket, or group the terms in pairs first.

             m) (x − 5)(x + 4)   n) (a + 3)(2a − 1)   o) (n + 1)(m + 1)
             p) (q − 2)(3p + 5)   q) (x + 2)(x² + 3)   r) (b − 2)(a − 3)

M4. Factorise using a² − b² = (a + b)(a − b). Factorise fully.

             o) (11 + 12m)(11 − 12m)   p) (6x + 7y)(6x − 7y)
             q) (a² + b)(a² − b)   r) (x + 4)(x − 2)   s) (a + b + c)(a + b − c)

M11. Factorise fully (common factor first, then look for a pattern).
     a) 2x² − 18           b) 3x² + 15x + 18     c) 5a² − 5
     d) 4x² − 100          e) 2m² − 12m + 18     f) x³ − 9x

              s) 3(2x + 3y)(2x − 3y)   t) y(x − 6)(x + 2)

M12. Divide. Check each answer by multiplying: quotient × divisor = dividend.
     a) (12x³ − 8x² + 4x) ÷ 4x          b) (15a²b − 10ab²) ÷ 5ab
     c) (6m² + 9m) ÷ 3m                 d) (20p³ − 5p²) ÷ 5p²
