TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: build sequences with counters, tiles or matchsticks; write the
  position n above each term in a table; find the difference and compare with
  the times table of d; for geometric sequences say "times r each time" and use
  a calculator for big powers; always check the nth term with n = 1 and n = 2.
  Harder: sums of arithmetic sequences (1 + 2 + ... + 100 = 5050, Gauss's
  trick), quadratic sequences with constant second differences (nth term
  an² + bn + c), compound interest as a geometric sequence, the golden ratio in
  Fibonacci ratios, and spreadsheet formulas such as =A1+3 or =A1*2 to generate
  long sequences.


================================================================================
TOPIC 6.18 — SYSTEMS OF TWO LINEAR EQUATIONS (GRAPHING, SUBSTITUTION,
             ELIMINATION)
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - A SYSTEM of two linear equations in x and y is a pair of equations to be
    solved TOGETHER. A SOLUTION is an ordered pair (x, y) that makes BOTH
    equations true.
  - GRAPHICALLY, each equation is a straight line and the solution is the point
    where the lines cross. Draw both lines accurately on the same axes and read
    the intersection point.
  - SUBSTITUTION: make one variable the subject of one equation (y = ... or
    x = ...), substitute it into the other equation, solve, then substitute back
    to find the second variable.
  - ELIMINATION: add or subtract the equations to remove one variable. If the
    coefficients are not already equal (or opposite), first multiply one or both
    equations so that they are. Same signs: subtract. Opposite signs: add.
  - Solutions can be negative or fractional. Keep fractions exact; do not round.
  - SPECIAL CASES: if the lines are parallel (same slope, different intercepts),
    there is NO solution; the working ends in a false statement such as 0 = 3.
    If the two equations describe the SAME line, there are INFINITELY MANY
    solutions; the working ends in 0 = 0.
  - WORD PROBLEMS: choose letters for the two unknowns, write two equations from
    the two facts, solve, and answer in words with units.
  - ALWAYS CHECK: substitute the answer into BOTH original equations.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. Solve by drawing graphs: x + y = 5 and y = x + 1.
    Answer: x = 2, y = 3. The lines cross at (2, 3).
    Teacher explains: Make a small table for each line.
           x + y = 5:   x   0   1   2   3   5
                        y   5   4   3   2   0
           y = x + 1:   x   0   1   2   3   4
                        y   1   2   3   4   5
    Plot both lines on the same axes (a for the first line, b for the second, X
    where they meet):
                 y
          6   a  |  .  .  .  .  b  .
          5   .  a  .  .  .  b  .  .
          4   .  |  a  .  b  .  .  .
          3   .  |  .  X  .  .  .  .
          2   .  |  b  .  a  .  .  .
          1   .  b  .  .  .  a  .  .
          0 --b--+--+--+--+--+--a--+-x
         −1   .  |  .  .  .  .  .  a
             −1  0  1  2  3  4  5  6
    Every point on line a makes the first equation true; every point on line b
    makes the second true. Only the crossing point X lies on BOTH lines, so it
    is the solution: (2, 3). Check: 2 + 3 = 5 and 2 + 1 = 3. Both true. The
    tables show it too: (2, 3) appears in both. Graphs are good for SEEING the
    solution, but if the answer is not a whole number, like (1.4, 2.7), it is
    hard to read exactly; then algebra is better. Now you try: solve y = 2x and
    x + y = 6 by graphing. ((2, 4))

D2. Draw the lines y = x + 2 and y = x − 1. How many solutions does the system
    have?
    Answer: None. The lines are parallel and never meet.
    Teacher explains: Both lines have slope 1 (they climb at the same rate) but
    different y-intercepts, 2 and −1. Plot them (p for y = x + 2, q for
    y = x − 1):
                       y
          6   .  .  .  |  .  .  .  p
          5   .  .  .  |  .  .  p  .
          4   .  .  .  |  .  p  .  .
          3   .  .  .  |  p  .  .  q
          2   .  .  .  p  .  .  q  .
          1   .  .  p  |  .  q  .  .
          0 --+--p--+--+--q--+--+--+-x
         −1   p  .  .  q  .  .  .  .
         −2   .  .  q  |  .  .  .  .
         −3   .  q  .  |  .  .  .  .
         −4   q  .  .  |  .  .  .  .
             −3 −2 −1  0  1  2  3  4
    The gap between them is always 3 units upwards, so they never cross: NO
    point lies on both lines, and the system has NO SOLUTION. Algebra agrees:
    x + 2 = x − 1 gives 2 = −1, which is false. A false statement at the end of
    the working means "no solution". Compare: y = x + 2 and 2y = 2x + 4 are the
    SAME line (divide the second by 2); every point on it is a solution, so
    there are INFINITELY MANY solutions. Now you try: how many solutions has
    y = 3x + 1, y = 3x + 5? (none, parallel lines)

D3. Solve by substitution: y = 3x − 2 and 2x + y = 13.
    Answer: x = 3, y = 7.
    Teacher explains: The first equation already tells us what y is: 3x − 2. So
    in the second equation REPLACE y by (3x − 2), in brackets:
           2x + (3x − 2) = 13
                 5x − 2 = 13
                     5x = 15
                      x = 3
    Now substitute x = 3 back into the simpler equation:
    y = 3 × 3 − 2 = 9 − 2 = 7.
           Check:  y = 3x − 2:   3 × 3 − 2 = 7        ✓
                   2x + y = 13:  2 × 3 + 7 = 13       ✓
    Substitution works best when one equation is already "y = ..." or "x = ...".
    Common mistake: stopping after x = 3 and forgetting y, or checking in only
    one equation. Now you try: y = x + 4 and 3x + y = 24. (x = 5, y = 9)

D4. Solve by substitution: x + 2y = 7 and 3x − y = 7.
    Answer: x = 3, y = 2.
    Teacher explains: Neither equation is solved for a letter yet, so first
    choose the easiest letter to make the subject. In x + 2y = 7 the x has
    coefficient 1: x = 7 − 2y. Substitute into the second equation:
           3(7 − 2y) − y = 7
            21 − 6y − y = 7
                21 − 7y = 7
                    −7y = −14
                      y = 2
    Then x = 7 − 2 × 2 = 3. Check: 3 + 2 × 2 = 7 ✓ and 3 × 3 − 2 = 7 ✓. Common
    mistakes: 3(7 − 2y) = 21 − 2y (multiply BOTH terms in the bracket by 3), and
    −7y = −14 giving y = −2 (negative ÷ negative is positive). Now you try:
    2x + y = 11 and x − 3y = −5. (y = 11 − 2x; x = 4, y = 3)

D5. Solve by elimination: 3x + 2y = 16 and 5x − 2y = 8.
    Answer: x = 3, y = 3.5 (or 7/2).
    Teacher explains: Look at the y terms: +2y and −2y are OPPOSITES, so ADDING
    the equations removes y:
             3x + 2y = 16
           + 5x − 2y = 8
           -------------
             8x      = 24     so  x = 3
    Substitute x = 3 into the first equation: 9 + 2y = 16, 2y = 7,
    y = 7/2 = 3.5. The answer need not be a whole number: keep it as a fraction
    or an exact decimal. Check in the SECOND equation:
    5 × 3 − 2 × 3.5 = 15 − 7 = 8 ✓. Rule: OPPOSITE signs, ADD; SAME signs,
    SUBTRACT. Now you try: 2x + 3y = 7 and 4x − 3y = 5. (x = 2, y = 1)

D6. Solve by elimination: 4x + 3y = 10 and 4x − y = −6.
    Answer: x = −1/2, y = 4.
    Teacher explains: The x terms are the SAME (4x and 4x), so SUBTRACT the
    second equation from the first. Be careful with every sign:
             4x + 3y = 10
           − (4x − y = −6)
           ---------------
                  4y = 16      because 3y − (−y) = 4y and 10 − (−6) = 16
    So y = 4. Substitute: 4x + 12 = 10, 4x = −2, x = −2/4 = −1/2.
           Check:  4 × (−1/2) + 3 × 4 = −2 + 12 = 10   ✓
                   4 × (−1/2) − 4     = −2 − 4 = −6    ✓
    Common mistake: 3y − (−y) = 2y. Subtracting a negative ADDS. Writing the
    subtraction in brackets helps. Now you try: 5x + 2y = 1 and 5x − 4y = 13.
    (y = −2, x = 1)

D7. Solve by elimination: 2x + 3y = 12 and x − y = 1.
    Answer: x = 3, y = 2.
    Teacher explains: No terms match yet. Multiply the second equation by 3 so
    the y terms become +3y and −3y:
           (1)      2x + 3y = 12
           (2) × 3: 3x − 3y = 3
           add:     5x      = 15      x = 3
    Substitute into (2): 3 − y = 1, so y = 2. Check in (1):
    2 × 3 + 3 × 2 = 6 + 6 = 12 ✓. Multiply EVERY term of the equation, including
    the right-hand side: 3 × 1 = 3. Common mistake: writing 3x − 3y = 1. You
    could also multiply (2) by 2 and subtract to remove x; any correct route
    gives the same answer. Now you try: 3x + 2y = 19 and x + y = 7.
    (x = 5, y = 2)

D8. Solve: 3x + 4y = 5 and 2x − 3y = −8.
    Answer: x = −1, y = 2.
    Teacher explains: Here we must multiply BOTH equations. To remove y, make
    the y coefficients 12 and −12 (12 is the LCM of 4 and 3):
           (1) × 3:   9x + 12y = 15
           (2) × 4:   8x − 12y = −32
           add:      17x       = −17      x = −1
    Substitute into (1): 3 × (−1) + 4y = 5, −3 + 4y = 5, 4y = 8, y = 2.
           Check (2):  2 × (−1) − 3 × 2 = −2 − 6 = −8   ✓
    Choose the variable that needs the SMALLER multipliers. To remove x instead
    we would use (1) × 2 and (2) × 3 (6x in both) and subtract. Now you try:
    2x + 5y = 1 and 3x − 2y = 11. (x = 3, y = −1)

D9. Solve (a) 2x + 4y = 10 and x + 2y = 5; (b) x + 2y = 5 and 2x + 4y = 7. What
    happens?
    Answer: (a) Infinitely many solutions: both equations are the same line. (b)
    No solution: the lines are parallel.
    Teacher explains: (a) Multiply the second equation by 2: 2x + 4y = 10,
    exactly the first equation. Subtracting gives 0 = 0, which is always true.
    Every point on the line, such as (5, 0), (1, 2), (3, 1), (−1, 3), is a
    solution.
    (b) Multiply the first equation by 2: 2x + 4y = 10. Subtract the second:
    0 = 10 − 7 = 3, which is FALSE. No pair (x, y) can make 2x + 4y equal to
    both 10 and 7, so there is no solution. In slope form: y = −(1/2)x + 5/2 and
    y = −(1/2)x + 7/4, same slope, different intercepts: parallel lines.
           one solution      lines cross      working ends with x = ..., y = ...
           no solution       parallel lines   working ends with a false 0 = 3
           infinitely many   the same line    working ends with 0 = 0
    Now you try: 3x − y = 4 and 6x − 2y = 8. (0 = 0: infinitely many solutions)

D10. (a) The sum of two numbers is 48 and their difference is 12. Find them. (b)
     At a cricket match, adult tickets cost ₹120 and child tickets ₹70. A family
     buys 8 tickets for ₹810. How many of each?
     Answer: (a) 30 and 18. (b) 5 adult and 3 child tickets.
     Teacher explains: (a) Let the numbers be x and y (x the bigger). Then
     x + y = 48 and x − y = 12. Add: 2x = 60, x = 30; then y = 48 − 30 = 18.
     Check: 30 + 18 = 48 ✓, 30 − 18 = 12 ✓.
     (b) Let a = number of adult tickets, c = number of child tickets. One fact
     is about HOW MANY, the other about HOW MUCH:
            number:  a + c = 8
            money:   120a + 70c = 810
     Substitute c = 8 − a: 120a + 70(8 − a) = 810, 120a + 560 − 70a = 810,
     50a = 250, a = 5. So c = 3. Check: 5 + 3 = 8 ✓,
     120 × 5 + 70 × 3 = 600 + 210 = 810 ✓. Always write "Let a = ..." first, and
     answer in words. Now you try: 10 pens and pencils cost ₹64; a pen costs ₹10
     and a pencil ₹4. How many pens? (p + q = 10, 10p + 4q = 64: 4 pens, 6
     pencils)

D11. (a) A money box has 30 coins, all ₹2 and ₹5 coins, worth ₹99 in total. How
     many of each? (b) A father is 3 times as old as his son. In 10 years he
     will be twice as old as his son. Find their ages now.
     Answer: (a) 17 coins of ₹2 and 13 coins of ₹5. (b) Son 10 years, father 30
     years.
     Teacher explains: (a) Let t = number of ₹2 coins and f = number of ₹5
     coins.
            t + f = 30          (number of coins)
            2t + 5f = 99        (value in rupees)
     Multiply the first by 2: 2t + 2f = 60. Subtract from the second: 3f = 39,
     f = 13, t = 17. Check: 17 + 13 = 30 ✓; 34 + 65 = 99 ✓. Do not mix up the
     NUMBER of coins with their VALUE.
     (b) Let s = son's age and d = father's age now. d = 3s. In 10 years:
     d + 10 = 2(s + 10). Substitute: 3s + 10 = 2s + 20, s = 10, d = 30. Check:
     30 = 3 × 10 ✓; in 10 years 40 = 2 × 20 ✓. Common mistake: adding 10 years
     to only ONE person. Now you try: 20 coins of ₹1 and ₹2 are worth ₹32. How
     many ₹2 coins? (12)

D12. A shopkeeper mixes tea costing ₹400 per kg with tea costing ₹250 per kg to
     make 30 kg of a blend worth ₹300 per kg. How much of each tea does he use?
     Which method is best?
     Answer: 10 kg of the ₹400 tea and 20 kg of the ₹250 tea.
     Teacher explains: Let x kg of the ₹400 tea and y kg of the ₹250 tea be
     used. The weights add up: x + y = 30. The VALUES add up:
     400x + 250y = 300 × 30 = 9000. Divide the second equation by 50 to make it
     simpler: 8x + 5y = 180. Now eliminate y: 5 × (x + y = 30) gives
     5x + 5y = 150; subtract from 8x + 5y = 180: 3x = 30, x = 10; y = 20.
            Check:  10 + 20 = 30 kg                           ✓
                    400 × 10 + 250 × 20 = 4000 + 5000 = 9000   ✓  (= 300 × 30)
     Choosing a method: use SUBSTITUTION when a letter is already alone or has
     coefficient 1; use ELIMINATION when the coefficients of one letter are
     equal, opposite or easy to match; use a GRAPH to see the answer or to show
     how many solutions there are. Sense check: ₹300 is nearer to ₹250 than to
     ₹400, so there should be more of the cheaper tea. Correct. Now you try: mix
     milk at ₹60 per litre with milk at ₹40 per litre to get 50 litres at ₹48
     per litre. (20 litres at ₹60, 30 litres at ₹40)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. Is the given pair (x, y) a solution of the system? It must satisfy BOTH
    equations.
    a) x + y = 5;  x − y = 1;  (3, 2)
    b) x + y = 5;  x − y = 1;  (2, 3)
    c) 2x + y = 7;  x − y = −1;  (2, 3)
    d) 3x − 2y = 4;  x + y = 3;  (1, 2)
    e) y = 2x − 1;  x + y = 8;  (3, 5)
    f) y = 2x − 1;  x + y = 8;  (4, 4)
    g) 4x + y = 0;  x − y = 5;  (1, −4)
    h) x + 3y = −2;  2x − y = 3;  (1, −1)
    i) 5x − 2y = 1;  x + 2y = 5;  (1, 2)
    j) x = 2y;  3x − y = 10;  (2, 4)
    k) 2x + 5y = 1;  3x + 4y = 5;  (−1, 3)
    l) 6x − y = 4;  2x + y = 4;  (1, 2)
    m) x − y = 0;  x + y = 0;  (0, 0)
    n) x + 2y = 4;  2x + 4y = 8;  (2, 1)
    o) y = −x;  y = x + 4;  (2, −2)
    p) x/2 + y = 3;  x − y = 3;  (4, 1)
    Answers:
      a) yes
      b) no (fails equation 2)
      c) yes
      d) no (fails equation 1)
      e) yes
      f) no (fails equation 1)
      g) yes
      h) yes
      i) yes
      j) no (fails both equations)
      k) no (fails both equations)
      l) yes
      m) yes
      n) yes
      o) no (fails equation 2)
      p) yes

M2. Solve by graphing: make a table of values for each line (x from −4 to 4),
    draw both lines on the same axes and read the crossing point. Check it in
    both equations.
    a) y = x + 1;  y = −x + 5        b) y = 2x;  y = −x + 6
    c) y = x − 2;  y = −2x + 7       d) y = 3x − 4;  y = x
    e) y = −x + 3;  y = x − 1        f) y = 2x + 1;  y = 2x − 3
    g) y = (1/2)x + 2;  y = −x + 5   h) y = 3;  y = x + 1
    i) y = x;  y = −x − 4            j) y = −2x + 4;  y = x − 5
    k) y = 2x − 1;  y = −3           l) y = 3x + 2;  y = −x − 2
    Answers: a) (2, 3)   b) (2, 4)   c) (3, 1)   d) (2, 2)   e) (2, 1)
             f) no solution (parallel)   g) (2, 3)   h) (2, 3)   i) (−2, −2)
             j) (3, −2)   k) (−1, −3)   l) (−1, −1)

M3. Solve by substitution. (The first equation already gives y.)
    a) y = 2x − 1;  3x + y = 14     b) y = 3x − 2;  2x + y = 13
    c) y = x + 4;  3x + y = 24      d) y = −x + 6;  3x + y = 10
    e) y = 2x;  x + y = 6           f) y = 4x − 3;  x − y = −3
    g) y = −2x + 1;  3x + y = 0     h) y = x − 5;  2x + 3y = 5
    i) y = 3x;  5x − y = −4         j) y = −3x + 10;  2x + y = 7
    k) y = 5x − 8;  x + 2y = 6      l) y = x + 1;  4x − 2y = −8
    m) y = 2x − 7;  3x − 2y = 9     n) y = −x;  6x + y = −20
    o) y = 6x − 1;  x − y = 1       p) y = −4x + 2;  2x + 3y = −4
    Answers: a) x = 3, y = 5   b) x = 3, y = 7   c) x = 5, y = 9
             d) x = 2, y = 4   e) x = 2, y = 4   f) x = 2, y = 5
             g) x = −1, y = 3   h) x = 4, y = −1   i) x = −2, y = −6
             j) x = 3, y = 1   k) x = 2, y = 2   l) x = −3, y = −2
             m) x = 5, y = 3   n) x = −4, y = 4   o) x = 0, y = −1
             p) x = 1, y = −2

M4. Solve by substitution. First make x or y the subject of the equation where
    it has coefficient 1 (or −1).
    a) x + 2y = 7;  3x − y = 7      b) 2x + y = 11;  x − 3y = −5
    c) x − y = 3;  2x + 3y = 11     d) 3x + y = 7;  5x + 2y = 13
    e) x + 4y = 5;  2x − y = −8     f) −x + y = 3;  3x + 2y = 16
    g) x + 3y = −5;  4x − 5y = −3   h) 4x + y = 1;  3x − 2y = 9
    i) x − 2y = 6;  3x + 4y = 18    j) 2x − y = 2;  5x + 3y = −17
    k) x + 5y = 6;  2x + 7y = 6     l) 7x + y = 5;  2x + 3y = 15
    Answers: a) x = 3, y = 2   b) x = 4, y = 3   c) x = 4, y = 1
             d) x = 1, y = 4   e) x = −3, y = 2   f) x = 2, y = 5
             g) x = −2, y = −1   h) x = 1, y = −3   i) x = 6, y = 0
             j) x = −1, y = −4   k) x = −4, y = 2   l) x = 0, y = 5

M5. Solve by elimination: add or subtract the equations to remove one letter.
    (Opposite signs: add. Same signs: subtract.)
    a) x + y = 7;  x − y = 3          b) 3x + 2y = 17;  5x − 2y = 7
    c) 2x + 3y = 11;  2x − y = 7      d) 4x + 3y = 10;  4x − y = −14
    e) 5x + 2y = 11;  3x + 2y = 9     f) x + 4y = 11;  −x + 2y = 1
    g) 6x − 5y = 10;  2x + 5y = 30    h) 3x + 7y = 11;  3x + 2y = 1
    i) 2x − 3y = 3;  −2x + 5y = 3     j) 7x + 4y = −8;  2x − 4y = 8
    k) x + 3y = 11;  x + y = 1        l) 5x − 2y = 15;  5x + 3y = −10
    m) −3x + 4y = 6;  3x + 2y = 12    n) 8x + y = −1;  3x + y = 4
    o) 2x + 9y = 17;  2x − 9y = −1    p) 4x − 3y = −3;  −4x + 7y = −9
    Answers: a) x = 5, y = 2   b) x = 3, y = 4   c) x = 4, y = 1
             d) x = −2, y = 6   e) x = 1, y = 3   f) x = 3, y = 2
             g) x = 5, y = 4   h) x = −1, y = 2   i) x = 6, y = 3
             j) x = 0, y = −2   k) x = −4, y = 5   l) x = 1, y = −5
             m) x = 2, y = 3   n) x = −1, y = 7   o) x = 4, y = 1
             p) x = −3, y = −3

M6. Solve by elimination. Multiply ONE equation first so that the coefficients
    of x or of y match.
    a) 2x + 3y = 12;  x − y = 1      b) 3x + 2y = 19;  x + y = 7
    c) 4x + y = 7;  3x + 2y = 9      d) x + 2y = 2;  3x + 5y = 7
    e) 5x − 3y = 3;  x + y = 7       f) 2x + y = 1;  7x + 3y = 1
    g) 6x + 5y = 16;  2x − y = 0     h) 3x − 4y = 10;  x + 2y = 0
    i) x − 3y = 3;  4x + 5y = −22    j) 2x + 7y = 3;  x + 3y = 2
    k) 3x + y = −2;  5x + 2y = 0     l) 4x − y = −6;  7x − 3y = −18
    m) x + y = −2;  2x + 5y = 8      n) 9x + 2y = 8;  3x + y = 1
    Answers: a) x = 3, y = 2   b) x = 5, y = 2   c) x = 1, y = 3
             d) x = 4, y = −1   e) x = 3, y = 4   f) x = −2, y = 5
             g) x = 1, y = 2   h) x = 2, y = −1   i) x = −3, y = −2
             j) x = 5, y = −1   k) x = −4, y = 10   l) x = 0, y = 6
             m) x = −6, y = 4   n) x = 2, y = −5

M7. Solve by elimination. Multiply BOTH equations first (use the LCM of the
    coefficients).
    a) 3x + 4y = 5;  2x − 3y = −8    b) 2x + 5y = 1;  3x − 2y = 11
    c) 4x + 3y = 10;  3x + 2y = 7    d) 5x + 2y = 8;  3x + 7y = −1
    e) 2x + 3y = 8;  5x + 4y = 6     f) 3x − 5y = 10;  4x + 3y = 23
    g) 6x + 5y = 2;  4x + 3y = 0     h) 7x + 2y = 3;  5x + 3y = −1
    i) 2x − 7y = 1;  3x − 4y = 8     j) 5x + 4y = 3;  4x + 5y = −3
    k) 3x + 2y = 2;  7x + 5y = 2     l) 4x − 5y = 7;  6x + 7y = −33
    Answers: a) x = −1, y = 2   b) x = 3, y = −1   c) x = 1, y = 2
             d) x = 2, y = −1   e) x = −2, y = 4   f) x = 5, y = 1
             g) x = −3, y = 4   h) x = 1, y = −2   i) x = 4, y = 1
             j) x = 3, y = −3   k) x = 6, y = −8   l) x = −2, y = −3

M8. Solve by any method. The answers are fractions or negative numbers: keep
    them exact.
    a) 2x + y = 4;  4x − 3y = −7     b) 3x + 2y = 16;  5x − 2y = 8
    c) 4x + 3y = 10;  4x − y = −6    d) 3x + 3y = 3;  3x − 3y = 1
    e) 2x + 2y = 4;  3x − y = −4     f) 6x + y = 1;  3x + 3y = −2
    g) 3x − 4y = 4;  6x + 2y = −7    h) 5x + y = −1;  x + y = 2
    i) 2x − 5y = 4;  4x + 5y = 11    j) x − y = 1;  3x + y = −6
    k) 4x + 6y = 4;  2x − 3y = 4     l) 3x + 3y = −14;  x − 3y = −6
    Answers: a) x = 1/2, y = 3   b) x = 3, y = 7/2   c) x = −1/2, y = 4
             d) x = 2/3, y = 1/3   e) x = −1/2, y = 5/2   f) x = 1/3, y = −1
             g) x = −2/3, y = −3/2   h) x = −3/4, y = 11/4   i) x = 5/2, y = 1/5
             j) x = −5/4, y = −9/4   k) x = 3/2, y = −1/3   l) x = −5, y = 1/3

M9. One solution, no solution, or infinitely many solutions? Compare slopes and
    intercepts, or eliminate and see what is left. If there is one solution,
    find it.
    a) x + y = 5;  2x + 2y = 10       b) x + y = 5;  x + y = 7
    c) y = 2x + 1;  y = 2x − 3        d) y = 2x + 1;  y = −2x + 1
    e) 3x − y = 4;  6x − 2y = 8       f) 3x − y = 4;  6x − 2y = 5
    g) x − 2y = 3;  2x − y = 3        h) 4x + 6y = 12;  2x + 3y = 6
    i) 4x + 6y = 12;  2x + 3y = 5     j) y = 5;  x = 2
    k) y = 3;  y = −1                 l) x − y = 0;  3x = 3y
    m) 2x + y = 1;  4x + 2y = 3       n) 5x + 2y = 9;  10x + 4y = 18
    o) x + 4y = 6;  3x + 2y = 8       p) y = (1/2)x + 1;  x − 2y = −2
    Answers:
      a) infinitely many (same line)
      b) no solution (parallel lines)
      c) no solution (parallel lines)
      d) one solution: x = 0, y = 1
      e) infinitely many (same line)
      f) no solution (parallel lines)
      g) one solution: x = 1, y = −1
      h) infinitely many (same line)
      i) no solution (parallel lines)
      j) one solution: x = 2, y = 5
      k) no solution (parallel lines)
      l) infinitely many (same line)
      m) no solution (parallel lines)
      n) infinitely many (same line)
      o) one solution: x = 2, y = 1
      p) infinitely many (same line)

M10. Clear the fractions or decimals first (multiply each equation by a suitable
     number), then solve.
     a) x/2 + y/3 = 4;  x − y = 3
     b) x/3 + y/4 = 3;  x/3 − y/2 = 0
     c) 0.5x + 0.2y = 3;  x + y = 9
     d) x/2 − y = 1;  x + y/2 = 7
     e) 0.3x + 0.4y = 2.5;  0.2x − 0.1y = 0.2
     f) (x + 1)/2 = y;  3x − 2y = 5
     g) x/5 + y/2 = 3;  2x/5 − y/2 = 0
     h) 1.2x + 0.5y = 5.8;  0.4x − 0.5y = 0.6
     i) x/4 + y/6 = 1;  x/2 − y/3 = 6
     j) (x − y)/2 = 1;  (x + y)/3 = 4
     k) 0.1x + 0.1y = 1;  0.3x − 0.2y = 0.5
     l) y/3 − x/6 = 1/2;  x + y = 6
     Answers: a) x = 6, y = 3   b) x = 6, y = 4   c) x = 4, y = 5
              d) x = 6, y = 2   e) x = 3, y = 4   f) x = 3, y = 2
              g) x = 5, y = 4   h) x = 4, y = 2   i) x = 8, y = −6
              j) x = 7, y = 5   k) x = 5, y = 5   l) x = 3, y = 3

M11. Rearrange each equation into the form ax + by = c, then solve.
     a) 2(x + y) = 3x − 4;  x + y = 10
     b) 3(x − 1) = y + 2;  2x = y + 3
     c) y = 2(x − 3);  y = −x + 6
     d) 4x = 3y + 1;  2y = x − 4
     e) x = 3y;  x − y = 8
     f) 5(x − y) = 10;  x + 2y = 11
     g) y − 3 = 2(x − 1);  y + 1 = −(x − 4)
     h) 2x + 3 = y + 7;  3x − 2 = 2y
     i) (x + y)/2 = 5;  x − 3 = y + 1
     j) 7 − x = 2y;  3y = x + 3
     Answers: a) x = 8, y = 2   b) x = 2, y = 1   c) x = 4, y = 2
              d) x = −2, y = −3   e) x = 12, y = 4   f) x = 5, y = 3
              g) x = 2/3, y = 7/3   h) x = 6, y = 8   i) x = 7, y = 3
              j) x = 3, y = 2

M12. A student gives the answer shown. Check it in BOTH equations. If it is
     wrong, find the correct solution.
     a) x + y = 9;  x − y = 3;  claim (6, 3)
     b) x + y = 9;  x − y = 3;  claim (3, 6)
     c) 2x + 3y = 12;  x − y = 1;  claim (3, 2)
     d) 3x + 2y = 16;  5x − 2y = 8;  claim (3, 3)
     e) 4x + 3y = 10;  4x − y = −6;  claim (1/2, 4)
     f) y = 3x − 2;  2x + y = 13;  claim (3, 7)
     g) 3x + 4y = 5;  2x − 3y = −8;  claim (1, 2)
     h) x + 2y = 7;  3x − y = 7;  claim (3, 2)
     i) 2x − y = 5;  x + y = 1;  claim (2, −1)
     j) 5x + y = 3;  x − 2y = 5;  claim (1, −2)
     k) 6x + 5y = 2;  4x + 3y = 0;  claim (−3, 4)
     l) x/2 + y = 4;  x − y = 2;  claim (5, 3)
     Answers:
       a) correct
       b) wrong (fails equation 2); correct: x = 6, y = 3
       c) correct
       d) wrong (fails both equations); correct: x = 3, y = 7/2
       e) wrong (fails both equations); correct: x = −1/2, y = 4
       f) correct
       g) wrong (fails both equations); correct: x = −1, y = 2
       h) correct
       i) correct
       j) correct
       k) correct
       l) wrong (fails equation 1); correct: x = 4, y = 2

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: start with systems where one equation is already y = ...; draw
  both lines on squared paper to see what a solution means; line up x under x
  and y under y before eliminating; write the operation ("(2) × 3", "(1) + (2)")
  beside each new line; check every answer in BOTH equations; for word problems,
  write "Let x = ..." and one equation per fact. Harder: three equations in
  three unknowns, systems with a parameter k (for which k is there no
  solution?), a system with a quadratic such as y = x² and y = x + 2, break-even
  problems (cost and income lines), and checking graph solutions in Desmos or
  GeoGebra.


================================================================================
TOPIC 6.19 — LINES AND ANGLES: TRANSVERSALS, ANGLES WITH PARALLEL LINES
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - A TRANSVERSAL is a line that crosses two (or more) other lines. With two
    lines it makes 8 angles, 4 at each crossing. In our standard figure the
    angles are numbered 1, 2 (above) and 3, 4 (below) at the top crossing, and
    5, 6, 7, 8 in the same positions at the bottom crossing.
  - At every crossing: VERTICALLY OPPOSITE angles are equal, and angles forming
    a LINEAR PAIR (next to each other on a straight line) add up to 180°.
  - When the two lines are PARALLEL: CORRESPONDING angles (same position at each
    crossing, an F shape) are equal; ALTERNATE INTERIOR angles (between the
    lines, on opposite sides of the transversal, a Z shape) are equal; ALTERNATE
    EXTERIOR angles are equal; CO-INTERIOR angles (between the lines, on the
    same side, a C or U shape) add up to 180°.
  - So with parallel lines there are only TWO different sizes: all the acute
    angles are equal, all the obtuse angles are equal, and
    acute + obtuse = 180°. Knowing ONE angle gives all eight.
  - The facts work BACKWARDS too (converses): if corresponding angles are equal,
    or alternate angles are equal, or co-interior angles add up to 180°, then
    the lines ARE parallel. Vertically opposite angles are equal for any lines,
    so they prove nothing about parallel lines.
  - ANGLE CHASING: move from angle to angle, writing the reason each time
    ("alternate angles, l ∥ m"). For a ZIGZAG between two parallel lines, the
    angle at the bend equals the sum of the two alternate angles; for an
    ARROWHEAD (C shape) the three angles add up to 360°. Draw a third parallel
    line through the bend to prove it.
  - ALGEBRA: set up an equation from the fact (equal angles: 3x + 10 = 5x − 30;
    co-interior: (2x + 15) + (3x − 5) = 180), solve for x, then find the angles
    and check them.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. In the figure, the lines l and m are parallel and t is a transversal. Name
    the pair of angles in each case: (a) ∠1 and ∠5, (b) ∠3 and ∠6, (c) ∠2 and
    ∠7, (d) ∠4 and ∠6, (e) ∠1 and ∠4, (f) ∠5 and ∠6.
                                t
                               /
                           1  / 2
          l  ----------------*--------------->
                          3 / 4
                           /
                       5  / 6
          m  ------------*------------------->
                      7 / 8
                       /
                      /
    Answer: (a) corresponding; (b) alternate interior; (c) alternate exterior;
    (d) co-interior; (e) vertically opposite; (f) a linear pair.
    Teacher explains: First separate the angles into INTERIOR ones, between l
    and m (∠3, ∠4, ∠5, ∠6), and EXTERIOR ones, outside them (∠1, ∠2, ∠7, ∠8).
           corresponding        same position            1&5, 2&6, 3&7, 4&8
           alternate interior   inside, opposite sides   3&6, 4&5
           alternate exterior   outside, opposite sides  1&8, 2&7
           co-interior          inside, same side        3&5, 4&6
           vertically opposite  same crossing, across    1&4, 2&3, 5&8, 6&7
           linear pair          same crossing, beside    1&2, 1&3, 2&4, ...
    Letter shapes help: corresponding angles sit in an F, alternate angles in a
    Z (or N), co-interior angles in a C (or U). Trace the letter on the figure
    with a finger. Common mistake: calling ∠3 and ∠5 alternate because they are
    "inside"; they are on the SAME side of t, so they are co-interior. Now you
    try: name ∠4 and ∠5, and ∠2 and ∠6. (alternate interior; corresponding)

D2. In the figure, l ∥ m and ∠2 = 65°. Find the other seven angles, giving a
    reason for each.
                                t
                               /
                          ∠1  / 65°
          l  ----------------*--------------->
                         ∠3 / ∠4
                           /
                      ∠5  / ∠6
          m  ------------*------------------->
                     ∠7 / ∠8
                       /
                      /
    Answer: ∠3 = ∠6 = ∠7 = 65°; ∠1 = ∠4 = ∠5 = ∠8 = 115°.
    Teacher explains: Write one line per angle, with the reason:
           ∠1 = 180° − 65° = 115°   (linear pair with ∠2)
           ∠3 = 65°                 (vertically opposite ∠2)
           ∠4 = 115°                (vertically opposite ∠1)
           ∠6 = 65°                 (corresponding to ∠2, l ∥ m)
           ∠5 = 115°                (corresponding to ∠1, l ∥ m)
           ∠7 = 65°                 (vertically opposite ∠6)
           ∠8 = 115°                (vertically opposite ∠5)
    Look at the pattern: the four "narrow" (acute) angles are all 65° and the
    four "wide" (obtuse) angles are all 115°, and 65° + 115° = 180°. So one
    angle gives all eight. Check: at each crossing the four angles add up to
    360°: 65 + 115 + 65 + 115 = 360. Common mistake: using the parallel-line
    facts when the lines are NOT marked parallel. Look for the arrow marks or
    the words "l ∥ m". Now you try: ∠5 = 128°; find ∠3 and ∠8. (∠3 = 52°,
    co-interior; ∠8 = 128°, vertically opposite)

D3. We accept that corresponding angles are equal when lines are parallel. Using
    this, PROVE that (a) alternate interior angles are equal, and (b)
    co-interior angles add up to 180°.
    Answer: (a) ∠3 = ∠2 (vertically opposite) and ∠2 = ∠6 (corresponding), so
    ∠3 = ∠6. (b) ∠3 + ∠5 = ∠3 + ∠1 = 180°.
    Teacher explains: A proof is a chain of statements, each with a reason that
    is already known. Use the standard figure with l ∥ m.
           Statement               Reason
           (a) ∠3 = ∠2             vertically opposite angles
               ∠2 = ∠6             corresponding angles, l ∥ m
               so ∠3 = ∠6          both equal ∠2
           (b) ∠5 = ∠1             corresponding angles, l ∥ m
               ∠1 + ∠3 = 180°      linear pair (angles on line l)
               so ∠5 + ∠3 = 180°   replace ∠1 by the equal ∠5
    And why are vertically opposite angles equal? ∠1 + ∠2 = 180° and
    ∠2 + ∠4 = 180° (both linear pairs), so ∠1 and ∠4 are both "180° minus ∠2":
    ∠1 = ∠4. In a proof we must NOT just say "it looks equal" or measure: we
    give reasons. Now you try: prove that alternate EXTERIOR angles ∠1 and ∠8
    are equal. (∠1 = ∠5, corresponding; ∠5 = ∠8, vertically opposite)

D4. A straight road crosses two parallel railway lines. The road makes an angle
    of 58° with the first railway line (measured as in the figure). What angles
    does the road make with the second line?
                                t
                               /
                              / 58°
          R1  ---------------*--------------->
                            /
                           /
                          / a
          R2  -----------*------------------->
                        / b
                       /
                      /
    Answer: a = 58° and b = 122° (and the other angles there are 58° and 122°).
    Teacher explains: The rails R1 and R2 are parallel and the road is the
    transversal. The 58° angle at R1 and angle a at R2 are in the SAME position
    (above the rail, right of the road): corresponding angles, so a = 58°. Angle
    b is a linear pair with a (both on R2, next to each other), so
    b = 180° − 58° = 122°. Road builders and surveyors use this: if a road meets
    one rail at 58°, it meets every parallel rail at 58°. Engineers also check
    that two rails are really parallel by measuring such angles. Check: the
    co-interior angles at the two rails, 122° and 58°, add up to 180°. Now you
    try: if the angle at R1 were 90°, what would all the angles be? (all eight
    are 90°)

D5. Use the standard figure (l and m are NOT known to be parallel). In each case
    decide whether l ∥ m: (a) ∠3 = 72° and ∠6 = 72°; (b) ∠4 = 110° and ∠6 = 70°;
    (c) ∠4 = 110° and ∠6 = 75°; (d) ∠1 = 125° and ∠4 = 125°.
    Answer: (a) yes; (b) yes; (c) no; (d) cannot tell.
    Teacher explains: Now we use the facts BACKWARDS (the converses). (a) ∠3 and
    ∠6 are alternate interior angles and they are equal, so l ∥ m. (b) ∠4 and ∠6
    are co-interior and 110° + 70° = 180°, so l ∥ m. (c) 110° + 75° = 185°, not
    180°, so the lines are NOT parallel. On the left of t the co-interior angles
    are ∠3 = 70° and ∠5 = 105°, total 175°, less than 180°: on that side the
    lines lean towards each other and meet.
    (d) ∠1 and ∠4 are vertically opposite: they are ALWAYS equal, whether or not
    l ∥ m. This gives no information. To prove lines parallel, the two angles
    must be at DIFFERENT crossings. Now you try: ∠2 = 64° and ∠7 = 64°.
    Parallel? (yes, alternate exterior angles are equal)

D6. Two corresponding angles are (3x + 10)° and (5x − 30)°, and the lines are
    parallel. Find x and the angles.
    Answer: x = 20; both angles are 70°.
    Teacher explains: Corresponding angles on parallel lines are EQUAL, so write
    an equation:
           3x + 10 = 5x − 30
                10 = 2x − 30      (take 3x from both sides)
                40 = 2x           (add 30)
                 x = 20
    Now find the angles: 3 × 20 + 10 = 70 and 5 × 20 − 30 = 70. They are equal,
    which checks the answer. The other angles at each crossing are
    180° − 70° = 110°. Common mistakes: stopping at x = 20 (the question wants
    the ANGLES too), and adding the two expressions to 180° (that is for
    co-interior angles or linear pairs, not equal pairs). Always decide first:
    EQUAL or ADD TO 180°? Now you try: alternate angles (4x − 15)° and
    (2x + 35)°. (x = 25; 85°)

D7. Two co-interior angles between parallel lines are (2x + 15)° and (3x − 5)°.
    Find x and both angles.
    Answer: x = 34; the angles are 83° and 97°.
    Teacher explains: Co-interior angles ADD UP TO 180°:
           (2x + 15) + (3x − 5) = 180
                      5x + 10 = 180
                           5x = 170
                            x = 34
    Angles: 2 × 34 + 15 = 83 and 3 × 34 − 5 = 97. Check: 83 + 97 = 180. Correct.
    Notice the angles are NOT equal: one is acute and one is obtuse. If we had
    wrongly set them equal, 2x + 15 = 3x − 5 would give x = 20 and angles 55°
    and 55°, which add up to 110°, not 180°: a clear sign of the wrong fact. Now
    you try: a linear pair (x + 40)° and (2x + 20)°. (x = 40; 80° and 100°)

D8. Lines l and m are parallel. A zigzag goes from A on l to P and then to B on
    m, as shown. The angles at A and B are 40° and 55°. Find the angle x at P.
          l  ------A-------------------->
                    \  40°
                     \
                      \
                    x  P
                      /
                     /
                    /  55°
          m  ------B-------------------->
    Answer: x = 95°.
    Teacher explains: Draw a THIRD line through P parallel to l and m (a dotted
    line going left). It cuts the angle x into two parts.
           upper part = 40°   (alternate to the 40° at A, transversal AP)
           lower part = 55°   (alternate to the 55° at B, transversal BP)
           x = 40° + 55° = 95°
    So for a zigzag the angle at the bend is the SUM of the two angles at the
    parallel lines (on the other side). This is the "Z-rule" used twice. Common
    mistake: x = 180° − 40° − 55° = 85°, treating the shape as a triangle; there
    is no triangle here. Check with a protractor on a careful drawing. Now you
    try: the angles at A and B are 35° and 70°. (x = 105°)

D9. Lines l and m are parallel. This time the given angles at A and B are on the
    LEFT, as shown: 120° and 130°. Find the angle x at P.
          l  ------A-------------------->
              120°  \
                     \
                      \
                    x  P
                      /
                     /
              130°  /
          m  ------B-------------------->
    Answer: x = 110°.
    Teacher explains: Again draw a line through P parallel to l and m. The upper
    part of x and the 120° angle are CO-INTERIOR angles (transversal AP, same
    side, between the parallel lines), so the upper part is 180° − 120° = 60°.
    In the same way the lower part is 180° − 130° = 50°. So
    x = 60° + 50° = 110°.
           shortcut:  120° + 130° + x = 360°   so   x = 360° − 250° = 110°
    For this "arrowhead" shape the three marked angles always add up to 360°:
    (180 − a) + (180 − b) = x gives a + b + x = 360. Compare with D8: same
    drawing, but the marked angles at A and B are on the other side. Always
    check WHICH side of the segment an angle is on before choosing the rule. Now
    you try: angles 140° and 150° at A and B on the left. (x = 70°)

D10. A is a point on line l, and B and C are points on line m, with l ∥ m. At A,
     the angle between l (to the left) and AB is 50°, and the angle between l
     (to the right) and AC is 60°. Find all three angles of triangle ABC.
            l  -----------------A-----------------
                       50°    /   \    60°
                             /     \
                            /       \
            m  -----------B-----------C-----------
     Answer: ∠ABC = 50°, ∠ACB = 60°, ∠BAC = 70°.
     Teacher explains: AB is a transversal of l and m, so the 50° at A and ∠ABC
     are ALTERNATE interior angles: ∠ABC = 50°. In the same way, with
     transversal AC: ∠ACB = 60°. At A the three angles 50°, ∠BAC and 60° lie
     along the straight line l, so ∠BAC = 180° − 50° − 60° = 70°. Notice:
     50° + 70° + 60° = 180°. The three angles of the triangle are exactly the
     three angles along the straight line l. This is the famous PROOF that the
     angles of every triangle add up to 180° (see topic 6.20). Now you try: with
     35° and 75° at A. (∠B = 35°, ∠C = 75°, ∠A = 70°)

D11. Two straight lines cross at a point. (a) Two angles next to each other are
     x° and (2x + 30)°. Find all four angles. (b) Two vertically opposite angles
     are (3y − 20)° and (y + 40)°. Find y and the angle.
     Answer: (a) x = 50: the angles are 50°, 130°, 50°, 130°. (b) y = 30; the
     angle is 70°.
     Teacher explains: (a) Neighbouring angles at a crossing form a linear pair,
     so they add up to 180°: x + 2x + 30 = 180, 3x = 150, x = 50. The angles are
     50° and 2 × 50 + 30 = 130°, and the opposite angles are equal to these:
     50°, 130°, 50°, 130° (total 360°).
     (b) Vertically opposite angles are EQUAL: 3y − 20 = y + 40, 2y = 60,
     y = 30. Angle = 3 × 30 − 20 = 70 = 30 + 40. Check.
               \                   /
                 \     130°      /
                   \           /
                     \       /
                       \   /
                50°      X     50°
                       /   \
                     /       \
                   /   130°    \
                 /               \
     Common mistake: making all four angles add up to 180°; round a point they
     make 360°. Now you try: neighbouring angles (4x)° and (x + 30)°. (x = 30:
     120° and 60°)

D12. Riya wrote three statements. Find the mistake in each and correct it. (i)
     "Co-interior angles are equal." (ii) "In the figure ∠1 = ∠4, so l ∥ m."
     (iii) "If l and m are not parallel, corresponding angles are still equal."
     Answer: (i) False: co-interior angles ADD UP TO 180° (they are equal only
     when both are 90°). (ii) Wrong reasoning: ∠1 and ∠4 are vertically opposite
     and are always equal, so this proves nothing. (iii) False: corresponding
     angles are equal ONLY when the lines are parallel.
     Teacher explains: (i) Draw two parallel lines and a slanting transversal:
     the co-interior angles are clearly one acute and one obtuse, e.g. 70° and
     110°. Only a perpendicular transversal makes them equal (90° and 90°).
     (ii) Every pair of crossing lines has equal vertically opposite angles,
     parallel or not. To PROVE l ∥ m we need angles at the TWO different
     crossings: equal corresponding angles, equal alternate angles, or
     co-interior angles adding up to 180°.
     (iii) Draw l and m so that they meet far away on the right. The transversal
     meets them at different "tilts", so the corresponding angles differ; e.g.
     60° and 75°. The equal-angle facts are exactly what "parallel" means. Good
     mathematicians always ask: "What do I KNOW, and what is the reason?" Now
     you try: is "alternate angles add up to 180°" true? (no, they are equal;
     only if both are 90° do they also add to 180°)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. Use the standard figure (l ∥ m). Name the relationship between the two
    angles and say whether they are equal or add up to 180°.
                                t
                               /
                           1  / 2
          l  ----------------*--------------->
                          3 / 4
                           /
                       5  / 6
          m  ------------*------------------->
                      7 / 8
                       /
                      /
    a) ∠1, ∠5   b) ∠3, ∠6   c) ∠2, ∠7   d) ∠4, ∠6   e) ∠1, ∠4   f) ∠5, ∠6
    g) ∠2, ∠6   h) ∠4, ∠5   i) ∠3, ∠5   j) ∠1, ∠8   k) ∠6, ∠7   l) ∠3, ∠7
    m) ∠2, ∠3   n) ∠4, ∠8   o) ∠7, ∠8   p) ∠1, ∠7   q) ∠5, ∠8   r) ∠1, ∠2
    s) ∠2, ∠8   t) ∠3, ∠4
    Answers:
      a) corresponding (equal)
      b) alternate interior (equal)
      c) alternate exterior (equal)
      d) co-interior (add to 180°)
      e) vertically opposite (equal)
      f) linear pair (add to 180°)
      g) corresponding (equal)
      h) alternate interior (equal)
      i) co-interior (add to 180°)
      j) alternate exterior (equal)
      k) vertically opposite (equal)
      l) corresponding (equal)
      m) vertically opposite (equal)
      n) corresponding (equal)
      o) linear pair (add to 180°)
      p) same-side exterior (add to 180°)
      q) vertically opposite (equal)
      r) linear pair (add to 180°)
      s) same-side exterior (add to 180°)
      t) linear pair (add to 180°)

M2. Standard figure, l ∥ m. One angle is given; find all the others.
    a) ∠2 = 65°    b) ∠3 = 72°    c) ∠1 = 130°   d) ∠6 = 48°    e) ∠5 = 101°
    f) ∠8 = 145°   g) ∠4 = 90°    h) ∠7 = 33°    i) ∠1 = 99°    j) ∠6 = 17°
    k) ∠5 = 160°   l) ∠3 = 58°
    Answers:
      a) ∠2 = ∠3 = ∠6 = ∠7 = 65°; ∠1 = ∠4 = ∠5 = ∠8 = 115°
      b) ∠2 = ∠3 = ∠6 = ∠7 = 72°; ∠1 = ∠4 = ∠5 = ∠8 = 108°
      c) ∠2 = ∠3 = ∠6 = ∠7 = 50°; ∠1 = ∠4 = ∠5 = ∠8 = 130°
      d) ∠2 = ∠3 = ∠6 = ∠7 = 48°; ∠1 = ∠4 = ∠5 = ∠8 = 132°
      e) ∠2 = ∠3 = ∠6 = ∠7 = 79°; ∠1 = ∠4 = ∠5 = ∠8 = 101°
      f) ∠2 = ∠3 = ∠6 = ∠7 = 35°; ∠1 = ∠4 = ∠5 = ∠8 = 145°
      g) all eight angles are 90°
      h) ∠2 = ∠3 = ∠6 = ∠7 = 33°; ∠1 = ∠4 = ∠5 = ∠8 = 147°
      i) ∠2 = ∠3 = ∠6 = ∠7 = 81°; ∠1 = ∠4 = ∠5 = ∠8 = 99°
      j) ∠2 = ∠3 = ∠6 = ∠7 = 17°; ∠1 = ∠4 = ∠5 = ∠8 = 163°
      k) ∠2 = ∠3 = ∠6 = ∠7 = 20°; ∠1 = ∠4 = ∠5 = ∠8 = 160°
      l) ∠2 = ∠3 = ∠6 = ∠7 = 58°; ∠1 = ∠4 = ∠5 = ∠8 = 122°

M3. Two angles made by a transversal with PARALLEL lines are described: one is
    given and the other is x. Find x.
    a) corresponding: 74°, x          b) alternate interior: 74°, x
    c) co-interior: 74°, x            d) vertically opposite: 38°, x
    e) linear pair: 38°, x            f) alternate exterior: 125°, x
    g) co-interior: 125°, x           h) corresponding: 90°, x
    i) linear pair: 90°, x            j) co-interior: 1°, x
    k) alternate interior: 163°, x    l) co-interior: 97°, x
    m) linear pair: 119°, x           n) corresponding: 43°, x
    o) co-interior: 60°, x            p) alternate exterior: 12°, x
    q) linear pair: 175°, x           r) co-interior: 142°, x
    s) vertically opposite: 101°, x   t) same-side exterior: 66°, x
    Answers: a) 74°   b) 74°   c) 106°   d) 38°   e) 142°   f) 125°   g) 55°
             h) 90°   i) 90°   j) 179°   k) 163°   l) 83°   m) 61°   n) 43°
             o) 120°   p) 12°   q) 5°   r) 38°   s) 101°   t) 114°

M4. Standard figure, but l and m are NOT known to be parallel. Is l ∥ m? Give
    the reason, or say "cannot tell".
    a) ∠3 = 72°, ∠6 = 72°     b) ∠4 = 110°, ∠6 = 70°
    c) ∠4 = 110°, ∠6 = 75°    d) ∠1 = 125°, ∠4 = 125°
    e) ∠2 = 64°, ∠7 = 64°     f) ∠1 = 120°, ∠5 = 120°
    g) ∠1 = 120°, ∠5 = 115°   h) ∠3 = 80°, ∠5 = 100°
    i) ∠3 = 80°, ∠5 = 80°     j) ∠2 = 50°, ∠6 = 50°
    k) ∠4 = 131°, ∠5 = 131°   l) ∠2 = 40°, ∠8 = 140°
    m) ∠6 = 90°, ∠4 = 90°     n) ∠1 = 101°, ∠8 = 99°
    o) ∠5 = 70°, ∠8 = 70°     p) ∠1 = 108°, ∠7 = 72°
    Answers:
      a) yes (alternate interior angles equal)
      b) yes (co-interior angles, sum 180°)
      c) no (co-interior angles, sum 185°)
      d) cannot tell (vertically opposite angles at the same crossing)
      e) yes (alternate exterior angles equal)
      f) yes (corresponding angles equal)
      g) no (corresponding angles not equal)
      h) yes (co-interior angles, sum 180°)
      i) no (co-interior angles, sum 160°)
      j) yes (corresponding angles equal)
      k) yes (alternate interior angles equal)
      l) yes (same-side exterior angles, sum 180°)
      m) yes (co-interior angles, sum 180°)
      n) no (alternate exterior angles not equal)
      o) cannot tell (vertically opposite angles at the same crossing)
      p) yes (same-side exterior angles, sum 180°)

M5. The two angles are EQUAL (corresponding, alternate or vertically opposite).
    Find x and the angle.
    a) (3x + 10)° = (5x − 30)°    b) (4x − 15)° = (2x + 35)°
    c) (x + 40)° = 3x°            d) (7x − 8)° = (5x + 12)°
    e) (2x + 25)° = (4x − 35)°    f) 6x° = (4x + 30)°
    g) (9x − 20)° = (7x + 10)°    h) (x + 90)° = 4x°
    i) (5x + 7)° = (8x − 20)°     j) (3x − 12)° = (x + 40)°
    k) (10x − 5)° = (6x + 55)°    l) 2x° = (x + 45)°
    m) (4x + 18)° = (7x − 24)°    n) (x/2 + 30)° = (x − 10)°
    o) (3(x + 5))° = (2x + 40)°   p) (8x − 60)° = (5x + 15)°
    Answers: a) x = 20, angle 70°   b) x = 25, angle 85°   c) x = 20, angle 60°
             d) x = 10, angle 62°   e) x = 30, angle 85°   f) x = 15, angle 90°
             g) x = 15, angle 115°   h) x = 30, angle 120°   i) x = 9, angle 52°
             j) x = 26, angle 66°   k) x = 15, angle 145°   l) x = 45, angle 90°
             m) x = 14, angle 74°   n) x = 80, angle 70°   o) x = 25, angle 90°
             p) x = 25, angle 140°

M6. The two angles ADD UP TO 180° (co-interior angles or a linear pair). Find x
    and both angles.
    a) (2x + 15)° + (3x − 5)° = 180°    b) (x + 40)° + (2x + 20)° = 180°
    c) x° + (2x + 30)° = 180°           d) 4x° + (x + 30)° = 180°
    e) (3x + 10)° + (x + 50)° = 180°    f) (5x − 20)° + (2x + 60)° = 180°
    g) x° + x° = 180°                   h) (6x + 18)° + 3x° = 180°
    i) (2x − 10)° + (2x + 30)° = 180°   j) (x + 25)° + (4x − 20)° = 180°
    k) 7x° + 2x° = 180°                 l) x/2° + (x + 30)° = 180°
    m) (3x − 15)° + (x − 5)° = 180°     n) (8x + 4)° + (x + 5)° = 180°
    Answers:
      a) x = 34; 83° and 97°
      b) x = 40; 80° and 100°
      c) x = 50; 50° and 130°
      d) x = 30; 120° and 60°
      e) x = 30; 100° and 80°
      f) x = 20; 80° and 100°
      g) x = 90; 90° and 90°
      h) x = 18; 126° and 54°
      i) x = 40; 70° and 110°
      j) x = 35; 60° and 120°
      k) x = 20; 140° and 40°
      l) x = 100; 50° and 130°
      m) x = 50; 135° and 45°
      n) x = 19; 156° and 24°

M7. Two straight lines cross and one angle is given. Find the other three
    angles, going round the point from the given one. In m) to p), find x first,
    then all four angles.
    a) one angle 30°
    b) one angle 45°
    c) one angle 90°
    d) one angle 112°
    e) one angle 67°
    f) one angle 150°
    g) one angle 5°
    h) one angle 179°
    i) one angle 88°
    j) one angle 121°
    k) one angle 36°
    l) one angle 164°
    m) angles x and 3x next to each other
    n) angles (x + 20)° and (2x + 10)° next to each other
    o) vertically opposite angles (5x − 40)° and (3x + 20)°
    p) vertically opposite angles (2x + 6)° and (4x − 50)°
    Answers:
      a) 150°, 30°, 150°
      b) 135°, 45°, 135°
      c) 90°, 90°, 90°
      d) 68°, 112°, 68°
      e) 113°, 67°, 113°
      f) 30°, 150°, 30°
      g) 175°, 5°, 175°
      h) 1°, 179°, 1°
      i) 92°, 88°, 92°
      j) 59°, 121°, 59°
      k) 144°, 36°, 144°
      l) 16°, 164°, 16°
      m) x = 45; angles 45°, 135°, 45°, 135°
      n) x = 50; angles 70°, 110°, 70°, 110°
      o) x = 30; angles 110°, 70°, 110°, 70°
      p) x = 28; angles 62°, 118°, 62°, 118°

M8. Zigzag between parallel lines l and m (figure): the angle at the bend is
    x = a + b.
          l  ------A-------------------->
                    \  a
                     \
                      \
                    x  P
                      /
                     /
                    /  b
          m  ------B-------------------->
    a) a = 40°, b = 55°; x = ?    b) a = 35°, b = 70°; x = ?
    c) a = 20°, b = 30°; x = ?    d) a = 45°, b = 45°; x = ?
    e) a = 60°, b = 25°; x = ?    f) a = 15°, b = 80°; x = ?
    g) a = 50°, b = 50°; x = ?    h) a = 72°, b = 18°; x = ?
    i) a = 33°, b = 44°; x = ?    j) a = 65°, b = 75°; x = ?
    k) a = 28°, b = 90°; x = ?    l) a = 100°, b = 25°; x = ?
    m) x = 95°, a = 40°; b = ?    n) x = 120°, a = 70°; b = ?
    o) x = 90°, a = 36°; b = ?    p) x = 135°, a = 60°; b = ?
    Answers: a) x = 95°   b) x = 105°   c) x = 50°   d) x = 90°   e) x = 85°
             f) x = 95°   g) x = 100°   h) x = 90°   i) x = 77°   j) x = 140°
             k) x = 118°   l) x = 125°   m) b = 55°   n) b = 50°   o) b = 54°
             p) b = 75°

M9. Arrowhead between parallel lines l and m (figure): the marked angles satisfy
    a + b + x = 360°.
          l  ------A-------------------->
                 a  \
                     \
                      \
                    x  P
                      /
                     /
                 b  /
          m  ------B-------------------->
    a) a = 120°, b = 130°; x = ?   b) a = 140°, b = 150°; x = ?
    c) a = 100°, b = 160°; x = ?   d) a = 110°, b = 110°; x = ?
    e) a = 135°, b = 145°; x = ?   f) a = 125°, b = 155°; x = ?
    g) a = 150°, b = 150°; x = ?   h) a = 95°, b = 175°; x = ?
    i) a = 130°, b = 100°; x = ?   j) a = 160°, b = 140°; x = ?
    k) x = 110°, a = 120°; b = ?   l) x = 70°, a = 140°; b = ?
    Answers: a) x = 110°   b) x = 70°   c) x = 100°   d) x = 140°   e) x = 80°
             f) x = 80°   g) x = 60°   h) x = 90°   i) x = 130°   j) x = 60°
             k) b = 130°   l) b = 150°

M10. Standard figure, l ∥ m. Decide whether the two angles are equal or add up
     to 180°, then find x and both angles. (∠2, ∠3, ∠6, ∠7 are the acute
     angles.)
     a) ∠2 = (2x + 10)°, ∠5 = (3x + 20)°
     b) ∠1 = (3x + 5)°, ∠5 = (4x − 30)°
     c) ∠3 = (x + 30)°, ∠6 = 2x°
     d) ∠4 = 5x°, ∠6 = (x + 30)°
     e) ∠2 = (4x − 12)°, ∠7 = (3x + 8)°
     f) ∠1 = (6x − 2)°, ∠8 = (4x + 30)°
     g) ∠3 = (2x + 4)°, ∠5 = (5x + 1)°
     h) ∠6 = (x + 18)°, ∠8 = (3x − 2)°
     i) ∠2 = (7x − 30)°, ∠6 = (4x + 21)°
     j) ∠1 = (x + 70)°, ∠7 = (2x − 10)°
     k) ∠4 = (3x + 45)°, ∠5 = (5x + 5)°
     l) ∠3 = x°, ∠8 = (x + 60)°
     Answers:
       a) add to 180°: x = 30; ∠2 = 70°, ∠5 = 110°
       b) equal: x = 35; ∠1 = 110°, ∠5 = 110°
       c) equal: x = 30; ∠3 = 60°, ∠6 = 60°
       d) add to 180°: x = 25; ∠4 = 125°, ∠6 = 55°
       e) equal: x = 20; ∠2 = 68°, ∠7 = 68°
       f) equal: x = 16; ∠1 = 94°, ∠8 = 94°
       g) add to 180°: x = 25; ∠3 = 54°, ∠5 = 126°
       h) add to 180°: x = 41; ∠6 = 59°, ∠8 = 121°
       i) equal: x = 17; ∠2 = 89°, ∠6 = 89°
       j) add to 180°: x = 40; ∠1 = 110°, ∠7 = 70°
       k) equal: x = 20; ∠4 = 105°, ∠5 = 105°
       l) add to 180°: x = 60; ∠3 = 60°, ∠8 = 120°

M11. Angles on a straight line add up to 180°; angles round a point add up to
     360°. Find x (all angles in degrees).
     a) on a straight line: x, 2x, 3x
     b) on a straight line: x, x + 20, x + 40
     c) on a straight line: 2x, 3x, 90°
     d) on a straight line: 4x + 5, 2x − 5, 60°
     e) round a point: x, 2x, 3x, 4x
     f) round a point: 90°, x, 2x, 3x
     g) round a point: x, x, 120°, 100°
     h) round a point: 3x, 3x, 2x, 2x
     i) on a straight line: x, 5x, 3x
     j) round a point: x + 10, 2x + 20, 3x + 30, 60°
     k) on a straight line: 45°, x, x + 35
     l) round a point: 150°, 2x, x + 60
     Answers: a) x = 30   b) x = 40   c) x = 18   d) x = 20   e) x = 36
              f) x = 45   g) x = 70   h) x = 36   i) x = 20   j) x = 40
              k) x = 50   l) x = 50

M12. True or false? Correct the false ones.
     a) Vertically opposite angles are always equal.
     b) Corresponding angles are always equal.
     c) With parallel lines, alternate interior angles are equal.
     d) With parallel lines, co-interior angles are equal.
     e) A linear pair adds up to 180°.
     f) If co-interior angles add up to 180°, the lines are parallel.
     g) If ∠1 = ∠4 in the standard figure, then l ∥ m.
     h) A transversal of two parallel lines makes at most two angle sizes.
     i) If one angle is 90°, all eight angles are 90° (l ∥ m).
     j) Alternate exterior angles are equal when l ∥ m.
     k) In a zigzag between parallel lines, the angle at the bend is a + b.
     l) In an arrowhead between parallel lines, a + b + x = 180°.
     m) Angles round a point add up to 180°.
     n) If ∠2 = 70° and ∠6 = 70°, then l ∥ m.
     o) If ∠3 = 70° and ∠5 = 70°, then l ∥ m.
     p) Two lines perpendicular to the same line are parallel.
     Answers: a) true   b) false (only when the lines are parallel)   c) true
              d) false (they add to 180°)   e) true   f) true
              g) false (vertically opposite angles are always equal)   h) true
              i) true   j) true   k) true   l) false (360°)   m) false (360°)
              n) true (corresponding)   o) false (co-interior, sum 140°)
              p) true (corresponding 90°)

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: draw two parallel lines on lined paper and a transversal with a
  ruler; colour the four acute angles one colour and the four obtuse angles
  another; trace the F, Z and C shapes with a finger; always write the reason
  next to each angle; before solving an algebra question, say "equal" or "add to
  180" aloud. Harder: prove the converse facts, find angles in figures with
  three parallel lines or two transversals, prove that the angle sum of a
  quadrilateral is 360° using a diagonal, angle chasing in a parallelogram, and
  designing a zigzag or arrowhead puzzle with algebraic angles for a friend.


================================================================================
TOPIC 6.20 — TRIANGLES: ANGLE SUM, EXTERIOR ANGLE, CONGRUENCE CRITERIA (SSS,
             SAS, ASA, RHS)
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - ANGLE SUM: the three angles of every triangle add up to 180°. PROOF: draw
    the line through one vertex parallel to the opposite side; the two alternate
    angles and the angle at that vertex fill a straight line.
  - EXTERIOR ANGLE THEOREM: if one side is extended, the exterior angle equals
    the SUM of the two interior OPPOSITE angles. (It also makes 180° with the
    interior angle next to it.) The three exterior angles, one at each vertex,
    add up to 360°.
  - Special triangles: EQUILATERAL, three angles of 60°; ISOSCELES, the angles
    opposite the equal sides (base angles) are equal; RIGHT-ANGLED, the two
    acute angles add up to 90°. Use algebra when angles are given as
    expressions: add them, set equal to 180°, solve.
  - CONGRUENT triangles (≅) have exactly the same shape and size: all three
    sides and all three angles match. In ΔABC ≅ ΔPQR the ORDER of letters shows
    which parts correspond: A ↔ P, B ↔ Q, C ↔ R, so AB = PQ, BC = QR, CA = RP.
  - Four tests (criteria) PROVE two triangles congruent: SSS (three sides); SAS
    (two sides and the INCLUDED angle, the angle between them); ASA (two angles
    and the included side), or AAS (two angles and any matching side, since the
    third angle is then known as well); RHS (right angle, hypotenuse and one
    other side).
  - Two sets of data are NOT enough: AAA (three angles) gives the same shape but
    possibly a different size; SSA (two sides and a NON-included angle) can give
    two different triangles.
  - CPCTC: once triangles are proved congruent, their Corresponding Parts are
    equal (Corresponding Parts of Congruent Triangles are Congruent). This is
    how we prove that lengths or angles are equal.
  - A congruence proof: name the two triangles, list three pairs of equal parts
    with a REASON for each (given, common side, vertically opposite, ...), state
    the criterion, then use CPCTC. Write it in two columns (Statement | Reason)
    or in sentences.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. Prove that the angles of any triangle ABC add up to 180°.
           D ---------------- A ---------------- E
                        x   /   \   y
                           /     \
                          /       \
                         /         \
                      B ------------- C
    Answer: Through A draw DE parallel to BC. Then ∠DAB = ∠B and ∠EAC = ∠C
    (alternate angles), and ∠DAB + ∠BAC + ∠EAC = 180° (angles on the straight
    line DE). So ∠B + ∠A + ∠C = 180°.
    Teacher explains: The idea is to MOVE the angles B and C up to A, where they
    fit on a straight line, just as when we tore the corners off a paper
    triangle in Stage 5. Written as a proof:
           Statement                         Reason
           DE ∥ BC                           constructed through A
           x = ∠B                            alternate angles, AB transversal
           y = ∠C                            alternate angles, AC transversal
           x + ∠BAC + y = 180°               angles on a straight line DE
           ∠B + ∠BAC + ∠C = 180°             replace x and y
    Tearing and measuring only SHOW the fact for the triangles we tried; this
    proof works for EVERY triangle, because it uses only facts that are always
    true. Common mistake: using the fact we want to prove as a reason ("the
    angles add to 180° because it is a triangle"). Now you try: explain why a
    triangle cannot have two right angles. (they would already make 180°,
    leaving 0° for the third angle)

D2. In ΔABC, ∠A = 48° and ∠B = 67°. BC is extended to D. Find the exterior angle
    ∠ACD, and prove the exterior angle theorem.
                        A
                       / \
                      /   \
                     /     \
                    /       \
                 B ----------- C ------------ D
    Answer: ∠ACD = 48° + 67° = 115°.
    Teacher explains: Long way: ∠ACB = 180° − 48° − 67° = 65°, and
    ∠ACD = 180° − 65° = 115° (linear pair). Short way: ∠ACD = ∠A + ∠B = 115°.
    Why is the short way always right?
           ∠A + ∠B + ∠ACB = 180°      angle sum of ΔABC
           ∠ACD + ∠ACB = 180°         linear pair on line BD
           so ∠ACD = ∠A + ∠B          both equal 180° − ∠ACB
    Consequence: an exterior angle is always BIGGER than each of the two
    interior opposite angles. Common mistake: adding the interior angle NEXT to
    the exterior angle (∠ACB) instead of the two FAR ones. Now you try: interior
    opposite angles 35° and 90°. (exterior angle 125°)

D3. In ΔPQR, PQ = PR and ∠P = 40°. The side QR is extended beyond R to S. Find
    ∠Q, ∠PRQ and ∠PRS. Then: if an exterior angle at a base vertex of an
    isosceles triangle is 115°, find all three angles.
    Answer: ∠Q = ∠PRQ = 70°, ∠PRS = 110°. Second triangle: base angles 65°, apex
    50°.
    Teacher explains: PQ = PR, so the base angles opposite these sides are
    equal: ∠Q = ∠PRQ = (180° − 40°) ÷ 2 = 140° ÷ 2 = 70°.
    ∠PRS = 180° − 70° = 110° (linear pair). Check with the exterior angle
    theorem: ∠PRS = ∠P + ∠Q = 40° + 70° = 110°. Correct.
    For the second triangle: the interior base angle is 180° − 115° = 65°; the
    other base angle is also 65°; the apex is 180° − 2 × 65° = 50°. Check:
    65 + 65 + 50 = 180. Common mistake: taking the equal angles at the wrong
    vertices. The equal angles are OPPOSITE the equal sides; mark them on a
    sketch before calculating. Now you try: PQ = PR and ∠Q = 52°. Find ∠P and
    the exterior angle at R. (∠P = 76°, exterior 128°)

D4. The angles of a triangle are (x + 15)°, (2x − 10)° and (3x + 25)°. Find x
    and the angles. What type of triangle is it?
    Answer: x = 25; the angles are 40°, 40° and 100°. It is an obtuse-angled
    isosceles triangle.
    Teacher explains: The angle sum gives an equation:
           (x + 15) + (2x − 10) + (3x + 25) = 180
                                     6x + 30 = 180
                                          6x = 150
                                           x = 25
    Angles: 25 + 15 = 40, 2 × 25 − 10 = 40, 3 × 25 + 25 = 100. Check:
    40 + 40 + 100 = 180. Two angles are equal, so two sides are equal:
    isosceles. One angle is more than 90°: obtuse-angled. Common mistakes:
    forgetting to collect the numbers (15 − 10 + 25 = 30), and stopping at
    x = 25. Now you try: angles x°, (x + 20)° and (2x)°. (x = 40: 40°, 60°, 80°)

D5. (a) The angles of a triangle are in the ratio 2 : 3 : 4. Find them. (b) In a
    triangle the two interior opposite angles are (2x)° and (x + 30)°, and the
    exterior angle is (4x + 10)°. Find x and the three angles.
    Answer: (a) 40°, 60°, 80°. (b) x = 20; the interior opposite angles are 40°
    and 50°, the exterior angle is 90°.
    Teacher explains: (a) Ratio 2 : 3 : 4 means 2 + 3 + 4 = 9 equal parts make
    180°, so one part is 180° ÷ 9 = 20°. The angles are 2 × 20 = 40,
    3 × 20 = 60, 4 × 20 = 80. Check: 40 + 60 + 80 = 180.
    (b) Exterior angle = sum of the interior opposite angles:
           4x + 10 = 2x + (x + 30)
           4x + 10 = 3x + 30
                 x = 20
    So 2x = 40, x + 30 = 50 and 4x + 10 = 90 = 40 + 50. The third interior angle
    is 180° − 90° = 90°: the triangle is right-angled. Now you try: angles in
    the ratio 1 : 2 : 3. (30°, 60°, 90°)

D6. A triangle has angles 70°, 50° and 60°. Find the exterior angle at each
    vertex (one at each corner, all going the same way round). Prove that the
    three exterior angles of ANY triangle add up to 360°.
    Answer: 110°, 130° and 120°; 110 + 130 + 120 = 360.
    Teacher explains: Each exterior angle makes a straight line with its
    interior angle: 180 − 70 = 110, 180 − 50 = 130, 180 − 60 = 120. Total 360°.
           (180° − A) + (180° − B) + (180° − C)
           = 540° − (A + B + C)
           = 540° − 180°
           = 360°
    Picture it: walk round the triangle and turn at each corner by the exterior
    angle. After the three turns you face the way you started: one full turn,
    360°. The same is true for EVERY polygon (topic 6.22). Common mistake:
    taking BOTH exterior angles at a vertex; there are two at each corner (they
    are vertically opposite and equal), but we count only one. Now you try:
    exterior angles of an equilateral triangle. (each 120°; 3 × 120 = 360)

D7. Two set squares from the same box have the same shape and size. What does
    ΔABC ≅ ΔPQR mean? If AB = 5 cm, BC = 7 cm, ∠B = 70° and ∠C = 40°, which
    parts of ΔPQR can you find?
    Answer: The triangles are congruent: they fit exactly on top of each other
    with A on P, B on Q and C on R. PQ = 5 cm, QR = 7 cm, ∠Q = 70°, ∠R = 40° and
    ∠P = 70°.
    Teacher explains: "≅" means "is congruent to". The ORDER of the letters is
    the key: first letters match (A ↔ P), second letters match (B ↔ Q), third
    letters match (C ↔ R). So sides match by their letter pairs:
           ΔABC ≅ ΔPQR:   AB = PQ    BC = QR    CA = RP
                          ∠A = ∠P    ∠B = ∠Q    ∠C = ∠R
    Using this: PQ = AB = 5 cm, QR = BC = 7 cm, ∠Q = ∠B = 70°, ∠R = ∠C = 40°,
    and ∠P = ∠A = 180° − 70° − 40° = 70°. If instead ΔABC ≅ ΔQPR, then A ↔ Q and
    B ↔ P, so AB = QP (the same length) but BC = PR, a DIFFERENT side. Common
    mistake: matching letters by alphabetical position rather than by their
    place in the statement. Now you try: ΔXYZ ≅ ΔLMN. Which side equals YZ, and
    which angle equals ∠Z? (MN; ∠N)

D8. ABCD is a kite with AB = AD and CB = CD. Prove that ∠ABC = ∠ADC. Why are
    triangles used in bridges and roof frames?
                        A
                      /   \
                    /       \
                  B --------- D      (diagonal AC drawn from A
                    \       /        down to C)
                      \   /
                        C
    Answer: ΔABC ≅ ΔADC by SSS, so ∠ABC = ∠ADC (CPCTC).
    Teacher explains: Draw the diagonal AC. It splits the kite into triangles
    ABC and ADC.
           Statement               Reason
           AB = AD                 given
           CB = CD                 given
           AC = AC                 common side
           ΔABC ≅ ΔADC             SSS
           ∠ABC = ∠ADC             CPCTC
    Notice how the letters match: A ↔ A, B ↔ D, C ↔ C. SSS says: if three sides
    are fixed, the triangle is fixed; there is only ONE triangle with sides 3, 4
    and 5 cm, for example. That is why a triangle made of three rods is RIGID,
    while a square frame can be pushed into a rhombus. Engineers put triangles
    into bridges, electricity pylons and roof trusses to make them rigid. Now
    you try: in the kite, prove that AC bisects ∠BAD. (from the same congruence,
    ∠BAC = ∠DAC by CPCTC)

D9. Two straight rods AB and CD cross at O so that O is the midpoint of both
    (OA = OB and OC = OD). Prove that AC = BD.
                A               D
                  \           /
                    \       /
                       O
                    /       \
                  /           \
                C               B
    Answer: ΔAOC ≅ ΔBOD by SAS, so AC = BD (CPCTC).
    Teacher explains: We need two sides and the angle BETWEEN them in each
    triangle.
           Statement               Reason
           OA = OB                 given (O is the midpoint of AB)
           ∠AOC = ∠BOD             vertically opposite angles
           OC = OD                 given (O is the midpoint of CD)
           ΔAOC ≅ ΔBOD             SAS
           AC = BD                 CPCTC
    The angle ∠AOC is INCLUDED between the sides OA and OC; ∠BOD is included
    between OB and OD. That is exactly what SAS needs. Also, ∠OAC = ∠OBD by
    CPCTC; these are alternate angles, so AC ∥ BD as well. Common mistake: using
    an angle that is NOT between the two sides (that would be SSA, which is not
    a valid test). Now you try: in the same figure prove AD = CB. (ΔAOD ≅ ΔBOC
    by SAS)

D10. To find the width of a river, Mohan stands at A, exactly opposite a tree T
     on the far bank. He walks 20 m along the bank to a pole O and then another
     20 m to B. At B he turns at 90° away from the river and walks until the
     pole O and the tree T are in a straight line, at C. He finds BC = 35 m. How
     wide is the river?
                  T
                  |
            ======|===========================  far bank
                  |      river
            ------A------------O------------B---  near bank
                                             |
                                             C
     Answer: 35 m.
     Teacher explains: Compare ΔTAO and ΔCBO.
            Statement               Reason
            ∠TAO = ∠CBO = 90°       A is opposite T; Mohan turned at 90°
            AO = BO = 20 m          measured
            ∠AOT = ∠BOC             vertically opposite (T, O, C in a line)
            ΔTAO ≅ ΔCBO             ASA
            AT = BC = 35 m          CPCTC
     ASA: two angles and the side INCLUDED between them (AO lies between the
     angles at A and O). If the known side is not between the two angles, the
     test is AAS, which is also valid: two angles fix the third angle (180°
     minus the other two), so we can always turn AAS into ASA. The surveyor
     measured on dry land and never crossed the river! Now you try: which test
     proves ΔABC ≅ ΔPQR if ∠A = ∠P, ∠B = ∠Q and BC = QR? (AAS)

D11. In ΔABC, AB = AC, and AD is perpendicular to BC. Prove that D is the
     midpoint of BC and that ∠B = ∠C.
                         A
                        /|\
                       / | \
                      /  |  \
                     /   |   \
                  B ---- D ---- C
                         (AD ⊥ BC: right angles at D)
     Answer: ΔABD ≅ ΔACD by RHS, so BD = CD and ∠B = ∠C (CPCTC).
     Teacher explains: Both triangles have a right angle at D.
            Statement                  Reason
            ∠ADB = ∠ADC = 90°          given AD ⊥ BC
            AB = AC (hypotenuses)      given
            AD = AD                    common side
            ΔABD ≅ ΔACD                RHS
            BD = CD,  ∠B = ∠C          CPCTC
     So D is the midpoint of BC, and we have PROVED that the base angles of an
     isosceles triangle are equal. The HYPOTENUSE is the side opposite the right
     angle, the longest side. RHS works only for right-angled triangles. Real
     life: two equal ladders leaning against a vertical wall and reaching the
     same height must have their feet the same distance from the wall (RHS). Now
     you try: two right triangles have hypotenuse 13 cm and one side 5 cm.
     Congruent? (yes, RHS; the third sides are both 12 cm)

D12. Show with examples why AAA and SSA are NOT tests for congruence. (a) Two
     triangles both have angles 60°, 60°, 60°. (b) In ΔABC, AB = 8 cm, AC = 6 cm
     and ∠B = 40°.
                             A
                           /  \ \
                         /     \  \
                       /        \   \
                40°  B-----------C₁---C₂
                 AB = 8 cm, AC₁ = AC₂ = 6 cm (two positions of C)
     Answer: (a) An equilateral triangle of side 2 cm and one of side 5 cm have
     the same angles but different sizes: same shape, not congruent. (b) The arc
     of radius 6 cm from A cuts the line from B at TWO points, C₁ and C₂, so two
     different triangles fit the data.
     Teacher explains: (a) Angles fix only the SHAPE. Equal angles give SIMILAR
     triangles (topic 6.26), which may be enlargements of each other.
     (b) Construct it: draw BX with ∠B = 40°, mark A with AB = 8 cm, then draw
     an arc of radius 6 cm from A. The distance from A to the line BX is about
     5.1 cm, less than 6 cm, so the arc crosses the line twice. ΔABC₁ and ΔABC₂
     both have AB = 8, AC = 6 and ∠B = 40°, but BC₁ is short and BC₂ is long. So
     "Side-Side-Angle" does not fix the triangle.
     Why does RHS work although it is also two sides and a non-included angle?
     Because the given angle is 90°, opposite the LONGEST side: then the arc
     meets the line only once on each side, and both triangles would be mirror
     images and congruent. Summary: SSS, SAS, ASA, AAS, RHS work; AAA and SSA do
     not. Now you try: two triangles have angles 30°, 70°, 80° and one has a
     longest side 10 cm, the other 12 cm. Congruent? (no: AAA only; same shape,
     different sizes)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------

    f) AB = PQ, ∠A = ∠P, ∠B = ∠Q

M10. Lengths are in cm. Are the triangles congruent? If yes, give the test and
     write the congruence with the letters in the correct order.
     a) ΔABC: AB = 5, BC = 7, ∠B = 50°;  ΔPQR: PQ = 5, QR = 7, ∠Q = 50°
     b) ΔABC: AB = 5, BC = 7, ∠B = 50°;  ΔPQR: QR = 5, RP = 7, ∠R = 50°
     c) ΔABC: AB = 6, BC = 8, CA = 9;  ΔPQR: PQ = 9, QR = 8, RP = 6
     d) ΔABC: ∠A = 40°, ∠B = 60°, AB = 7;  ΔPQR: ∠P = 40°, ∠Q = 60°, PQ = 7
     e) ΔABC: ∠A = 40°, ∠B = 60°, BC = 7;  ΔPQR: ∠Q = 40°, ∠R = 60°, RP = 7
     f) ΔABC: ∠A = 40°, ∠B = 60°, BC = 7;  ΔPQR: ∠P = 40°, ∠Q = 60°, PR = 7
     g) ΔABC: AB = 8, CA = 6, ∠B = 40°;  ΔPQR: PQ = 8, RP = 6, ∠Q = 40°
     h) ΔABC: ∠A = 50°, ∠B = 60°, ∠C = 70°;  ΔPQR: ∠P = 50°, ∠Q = 60°, ∠R = 70°
     i) ΔABC: ∠B = 90°, CA = 13, AB = 5;  ΔPQR: ∠Q = 90°, RP = 13, PQ = 5
     j) ΔABC: ∠C = 90°, AB = 10, BC = 6;  ΔPQR: ∠P = 90°, QR = 10, PQ = 6
     k) ΔABC: AB = 4, BC = 5, CA = 6;  ΔPQR: PQ = 4, QR = 6, RP = 5
     l) ΔABC: AB = 5, ∠B = 70°, BC = 6;  ΔPQR: PQ = 6, ∠Q = 70°, QR = 5
     Answers:
       a) yes, SAS: ΔABC ≅ ΔPQR
       b) yes, SAS: ΔABC ≅ ΔQRP
       c) yes, SSS: ΔABC ≅ ΔPRQ
       d) yes, ASA: ΔABC ≅ ΔPQR
       e) yes, AAS: ΔABC ≅ ΔQRP
       f) no: the parts do not match (the 7 cm side is opposite 40° in ΔABC but
          opposite 60° in ΔPQR)
       g) cannot say (SSA is not a test)
       h) cannot say (AAA is not a test)
       i) yes, RHS: ΔABC ≅ ΔPQR
       j) yes, RHS: ΔABC ≅ ΔRQP
       k) yes, SSS: ΔABC ≅ ΔQPR
       l) yes, SAS: ΔABC ≅ ΔRQP

M11. Find the three angles of each triangle.
     a) angles in the ratio 1 : 2 : 3
     b) angles in the ratio 2 : 3 : 4
     c) angles in the ratio 1 : 1 : 2
     d) angles in the ratio 3 : 4 : 5

     f) angles in the ratio 5 : 6 : 7

     h) angles in the ratio 2 : 2 : 5

     l) angles x, 2x and x + 20
     Answers: a) 30°, 60°, 90°   b) 40°, 60°, 80°   c) 45°, 45°, 90°
              d) 45°, 60°, 75°   e) 15°, 60°, 105°   f) 50°, 60°, 70°
