              j) 15°, 75°, 90°   k) 120°, 30°, 30°   l) 40°, 80°, 60°

M12. True or false? Correct the false ones.
     a) The angles of every triangle add up to 180°.
     b) An exterior angle equals the sum of the two interior opposite angles.
     c) An exterior angle can be smaller than an interior opposite angle.
     d) The three exterior angles (one at each vertex) add up to 180°.
     e) A triangle can have two obtuse angles.
     f) Triangles with the same three angles are always congruent.
     g) Two sides and a non-included angle always prove congruence.
     h) RHS proves right-angled triangles congruent.
     i) ASA and AAS are both valid tests.
     j) If ΔABC ≅ ΔPQR, then BC = PR.
     k) If ΔABC ≅ ΔPQR, then ∠C = ∠R.
     l) The base angles of an isosceles triangle are equal.
     m) Each angle of an equilateral triangle is 60°.
     n) Any two triangles with sides 3 cm, 4 cm, 5 cm are congruent.
     o) For SAS the angle must be between the two sides.
     p) An exterior angle of a triangle can be 180°.
     Answers: a) true   b) true   c) false (it is always bigger)
              d) false (360°)   e) false   f) false (AAA: sizes may differ)
              g) false (SSA can give two triangles)   h) true   i) true
              j) false (BC = QR)   k) true   l) true   m) true   n) true (SSS)
              o) true   p) false (the interior angle would be 0°)

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: cut out paper triangles, tear the corners and fit them on a line;
  mark equal sides and angles with ticks and arcs before calculating; for
  congruence, colour the matching parts in the same colour and say the letters
  in order (A ↔ P, B ↔ Q, C ↔ R); build triangles from straws (three fixed
  lengths make only one triangle) and try to build two different triangles from
  SSA data. Harder: prove that the base angles of an isosceles triangle are
  equal using SAS on the angle bisector; prove that the diagonals of a
  parallelogram bisect each other; prove that a point on the perpendicular
  bisector of AB is equally far from A and B; find the angles in a star or a
  pattern of triangles; and explore SSA with GeoGebra by dragging the third
  vertex.


================================================================================
TOPIC 6.21 — PYTHAGORAS THEOREM (PROOFS AND APPLICATIONS) AND ITS CONVERSE
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - In a RIGHT-ANGLED triangle the longest side, opposite the right angle, is
    the HYPOTENUSE (call it c); the other two sides are the LEGS a and b.
    PYTHAGORAS' THEOREM: a² + b² = c². In words: the square on the hypotenuse
    equals the sum of the squares on the other two sides.
  - Finding the hypotenuse: c² = a² + b², so c = √(a² + b²). Finding a leg:
    a² = c² − b², so a = √(c² − b²). Square, add (or subtract), then take the
    square root. The answer may be a whole number (√25 = 5) or a surd such as
    √50 = 5√2 ≈ 7.07 (give 2 decimal places).
  - PYTHAGOREAN TRIPLES are whole numbers with a² + b² = c²: (3, 4, 5),
    (5, 12, 13), (8, 15, 17), (7, 24, 25), (20, 21, 29), (9, 40, 41), and every
    multiple such as (6, 8, 10). For whole numbers m > n, (m² − n², 2mn,
    m² + n²) is always a triple.
  - CONVERSE: if the sides of a triangle satisfy a² + b² = c² (c the longest
    side), then the angle opposite c is exactly 90°. If a² + b² > c² the
    triangle is acute-angled; if a² + b² < c² it is obtuse-angled.
  - PROOFS: (1) four copies of the triangle placed inside a square of side a + b
    leave a tilted square of area c², and comparing areas gives a² + b² = c².
    (2) The altitude to the hypotenuse makes two smaller triangles similar to
    the whole one.
  - APPLICATIONS: ladders against walls, diagonal of a rectangle d = √(l² + b²),
    TV screen sizes, distance between two grid points
    √((x₂ − x₁)² + (y₂ − y₁)²), height of an isosceles triangle, and the longest
    diagonal of a box √(l² + b² + h²).

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. Anu draws a right-angled triangle with legs 3 cm and 4 cm and measures the
    longest side: 5 cm. On each side she draws a square. What do the areas of
    the three squares show?
       side of triangle      3 cm     4 cm     5 cm (hypotenuse)
       area of its square    9 cm²    16 cm²   25 cm²
    Answer: The squares have areas 9 cm², 16 cm² and 25 cm², and 9 + 16 = 25.
    The two small squares together exactly equal the big square.
    Teacher explains: First name the sides. The side OPPOSITE the right angle is
    the HYPOTENUSE; it is always the longest side. The other two are the legs.
    Pythagoras' theorem says: in every right-angled triangle, a² + b² = c²,
    where c is the hypotenuse. Here 3² + 4² = 9 + 16 = 25 and 5² = 25. It is
    about AREAS of squares, not about the lengths themselves: 3 + 4 = 7 is not
    5.
    Common mistakes: calling any side c (c must be opposite the right angle),
    and using the rule for a triangle that has no right angle. Now you try:
    check the triangle with sides 6 cm, 8 cm and 10 cm. (36 + 64 = 100 = 10², so
    it works)

D2. A TV is sold as a "60-inch TV". Its screen is 48 inches wide and 36 inches
    high. What does "60-inch" measure?
    Answer: The DIAGONAL of the screen: √(48² + 36²) = √3600 = 60 inches.
    Teacher explains: The width, the height and the diagonal make a right-angled
    triangle with the diagonal as hypotenuse. Write it on the board:
       d² = 48² + 36²
          = 2304 + 1296
          = 3600
       d  = √3600 = 60
    So TV sizes measure corner to corner. The same method finds the diagonal of
    any rectangle: d = √(l² + b²). A shortcut: 48 = 12 × 4 and 36 = 12 × 3, so
    the triangle is the 3-4-5 triangle made 12 times bigger, and
    d = 12 × 5 = 60. Common mistake: adding 48 + 36 = 84. Now you try: a park is
    40 m by 30 m. How long is the diagonal path across it? (50 m)

D3. A ladder 10 m long leans against a wall. Its foot is 6 m from the wall. How
    high up the wall does it reach?
    Answer: 8 m. h² = 10² − 6² = 100 − 36 = 64, so h = √64 = 8 m.
    Teacher explains:
             |\
             | \
           h |  \  10 m (ladder = hypotenuse)
             |   \
             |_   \
             |_|___\
                6 m
    The ladder is opposite the right angle (between wall and ground), so it is
    the hypotenuse. We want a LEG, so we SUBTRACT: h² + 6² = 10²,
    h² = 100 − 36 = 64, h = 8 m. Check: 8² + 6² = 64 + 36 = 100 = 10².
    Common mistake: adding (10² + 6² = 136, h ≈ 11.66 m) — but a leg can never
    be longer than the hypotenuse, so 11.66 m is impossible. Always ask: "Is the
    side I want the longest side?" If yes, add; if no, subtract. Now you try: a
    13 m ladder with its foot 5 m from the wall. (12 m)

D4. The two legs of a right-angled triangle are 5 cm and 7 cm. Find the
    hypotenuse to 2 decimal places.
    Answer: √74 ≈ 8.60 cm.
    Teacher explains: c² = 5² + 7² = 25 + 49 = 74, so c = √74. 74 is not a
    perfect square, so the answer is a SURD. Estimate first: 8² = 64 and
    9² = 81, and 74 is between them, so c is between 8 and 9. A calculator gives
    8.6023..., so c ≈ 8.60 cm (2 decimal places). Check: 8.6 × 8.6 = 73.96, very
    close to 74.
    In exact form we leave the answer as √74 cm. Common mistakes: writing
    c = 74, or taking √25 + √49 = 12 (square roots do not split over +). Now you
    try: legs 4 cm and 6 cm. (√52 ≈ 7.21 cm)

D5. A right-angled triangle has hypotenuse 9 cm and one leg 4 cm. Find the other
    leg.
    Answer: √65 ≈ 8.06 cm.
    Teacher explains: b² = c² − a² = 81 − 16 = 65, so b = √65. Estimate: 8² = 64
    is just below 65, so b is a little more than 8. A calculator: 8.0622..., so
    b ≈ 8.06 cm. Check the answer makes sense: the leg 8.06 cm is shorter than
    the hypotenuse 9 cm, as it must be.
    Write the working in a column so the steps are clear: square both known
    sides, subtract (the bigger minus the smaller), then square root. Common
    mistake: subtracting the sides before squaring (9 − 4 = 5). Now you try:
    hypotenuse 7 cm, one leg 3 cm. (√40 ≈ 6.32 cm)

D6. A carpenter wants to check that the corner of a table is a right angle. He
    marks 30 cm along one edge, 40 cm along the other and measures the distance
    between the marks: 50 cm. Is the corner square?
    Answer: Yes. 30² + 40² = 900 + 1600 = 2500 = 50², so by the CONVERSE of
    Pythagoras' theorem the corner is exactly 90°.
    Teacher explains: The theorem says: right angle → a² + b² = c². The CONVERSE
    says the reverse: if a² + b² = c² (with c the longest side), then the angle
    opposite c is 90°. So we can TEST for a right angle using only a tape.
    Builders in India and ancient Egypt used a rope with 12 equal knots, pegged
    into a 3-4-5 triangle, to set out square corners.
    Method: put the sides in order, square the two shorter ones, add, and
    compare with the square of the longest. If the carpenter had measured 49 cm:
    49² = 2401, not 2500, so the corner would not be square. Now you try: is a
    triangle with sides 9 cm, 12 cm and 15 cm right-angled? (yes,
    81 + 144 = 225)

D7. Sides 6, 7, 9 and sides 7, 8, 11 are not right-angled triangles. Which one
    is acute-angled and which is obtuse-angled?
    Answer: 6, 7, 9 is acute (36 + 49 = 85 > 81). 7, 8, 11 is obtuse
    (49 + 64 = 113 < 121).
    Teacher explains: Compare a² + b² (the two shorter sides) with c² (the
    longest side):
       a² + b² = c²   right angle opposite c
       a² + b² > c²   c is "too short": the angle is less than 90°
       a² + b² < c²   c is "too long":  the angle is more than 90°
    Picture it: keep a and b fixed and open the angle between them. As the angle
    grows past 90°, the third side c gets longer. Since the angle opposite the
    longest side is the biggest angle, only that one needs testing. Common
    mistake: squaring the wrong side (always use the LONGEST side as c). Now you
    try: sides 8, 15, 16. (acute, 64 + 225 = 289 > 256)

D8. Explain the "rearrangement" proof of Pythagoras' theorem.
    Answer: Four copies of the right-angled triangle placed in the corners of a
    square of side a + b leave a tilted square of side c in the middle. Area:
    (a + b)² = 4 × (1/2 × a × b) + c², which simplifies to a² + b² = c².
    Teacher explains: Draw a big square with side a + b. In each corner put a
    copy of the triangle (legs a and b, hypotenuse c), turning each copy a
    quarter turn, so every side of the big square is split into a and b. The
    four hypotenuses form a tilted square in the middle: its sides are all c and
    its corners are 90° (the two acute angles of the triangle add to 90°, and
    the angles on a straight line make 180°).
       big square       = 4 triangles + middle square
       (a + b)²         = 4 × (1/2)ab + c²
       a² + 2ab + b²    = 2ab + c²
       a² + b²          = c²          (take 2ab from both sides)
    Check with a = 3, b = 4, c = 5: (3 + 4)² = 49 and 4 × 6 + 25 = 24 + 25 = 49.
    Make it from paper to see it. Now you try: check the area equation with
    a = 5, b = 12, c = 13. (17² = 289 = 4 × 30 + 169)

D9. In right-angled triangle ABC (right angle at C, sides a = 3, b = 4, c = 5),
    the altitude from C meets AB at D. Show how similar triangles prove the
    theorem, and find the two parts of AB.
    Answer: The parts are p = 1.8 and q = 3.2, and 3² = 5 × 1.8, 4² = 5 × 3.2;
    adding gives 3² + 4² = 5 × (1.8 + 3.2) = 5².
    Teacher explains: The altitude CD cuts the big triangle into two smaller
    right-angled triangles. Each shares one acute angle with the big triangle,
    so by AA all three triangles are SIMILAR (topic 6.26). Matching sides of the
    small triangle next to side a with the big triangle gives p/a = a/c, so
    a² = c × p. In the same way b² = c × q. Adding:
    a² + b² = c × (p + q) = c × c = c².
    With numbers: p = 9 ÷ 5 = 1.8 and q = 16 ÷ 5 = 3.2, and 1.8 + 3.2 = 5 = c.
    This proof is shorter but needs similarity; the rearrangement proof needs
    only areas. Now you try: for the 6-8-10 triangle find p and q.
    (p = 36 ÷ 10 = 3.6, q = 64 ÷ 10 = 6.4)

D10. How can we make as many Pythagorean triples as we like? Use the rule
     (m² − n², 2mn, m² + n²).
     Answer: Choose whole numbers m > n. For example m = 2, n = 1 gives
     (3, 4, 5); m = 3, n = 2 gives (5, 12, 13); m = 4, n = 1 gives (15, 8, 17).
     Teacher explains:
         m   n   m² − n²   2mn   m² + n²
         2   1      3        4       5
         3   1      8        6      10
         3   2      5       12      13
         4   1     15        8      17
         4   3      7       24      25
         5   2     21       20      29
     Why it works:
        (m² − n²)² + (2mn)² = m⁴ − 2m²n² + n⁴ + 4m²n²
                            = m⁴ + 2m²n² + n⁴ = (m² + n²)²
     Also every MULTIPLE of a triple is a triple:
     (3, 4, 5) × 2 = (6, 8, 10), × 3 = (9, 12, 15), because
     (ka)² + (kb)² = k²(a² + b²) = (kc)². Learn the first few by heart. Now you
     try: m = 5, n = 4. (9, 40, 41)

D11. On a grid, A is at (1, 2) and B is at (7, 10). How far apart are they in a
     straight line?
     Answer: 10 units. Across 7 − 1 = 6, up 10 − 2 = 8, and
     √(6² + 8²) = √100 = 10.
     Teacher explains: Draw the right-angled triangle whose hypotenuse is AB: go
     across from A and up to B. The horizontal leg is the difference of the
     x-coordinates, the vertical leg is the difference of the y-coordinates.
     Then AB² = 6² + 8² = 36 + 64 = 100, AB = 10. As a formula:
     AB = √((x₂ − x₁)² + (y₂ − y₁)²).
     With negatives take care: from (−2, 3) to (4, −5) the legs are 4 − (−2) = 6
     and −5 − 3 = −8; squaring removes the sign, and the distance is again 10.
     Common mistake: adding coordinates instead of subtracting them. Now you
     try: from (−2, 3) to (3, −9). (13 units)

D12. A box is 12 cm long, 4 cm wide and 3 cm high. What is the longest pencil
     that fits inside, from one bottom corner to the opposite top corner?
     Answer: 13 cm. Floor diagonal² = 12² + 4² = 160; space
     diagonal² = 160 + 3² = 169, and √169 = 13.
     Teacher explains: Use Pythagoras TWICE. Step 1: the floor diagonal:
     f² = 144 + 16 = 160 (no need to find f itself). Step 2: the floor diagonal,
     the vertical edge and the pencil make a right-angled triangle standing up
     inside the box: d² = f² + 3² = 160 + 9 = 169, d = 13 cm. Together:
     d = √(l² + b² + h²) = √(144 + 16 + 9) = 13.
     Common mistake: stopping at the floor diagonal (√160 ≈ 12.65 cm). Now you
     try: a box 2 m by 3 m by 6 m. (7 m)

M6. Is the triangle with these sides right-angled? Use the converse: add the
    squares of the two shorter sides and compare with the square of the longest.
    a) 3, 4, 5      b) 5, 12, 13    c) 6, 8, 11     d) 13, 5, 12
    e) 8, 15, 17    f) 7, 24, 26    g) 10, 24, 26   h) 9, 12, 16
    i) 25, 7, 24    j) 20, 21, 29   k) 2, 3, 4      l) 12, 16, 20
    m) 11, 60, 61   n) 15, 20, 24   o) 6, 6, 8      p) 40, 9, 41
    q) 16, 30, 34   r) 14, 48, 50   s) 1, 1, 2      t) 18, 80, 82
    u) 5, 5, 7      v) 33, 56, 65   w) 12, 35, 36   x) 28, 45, 53

             c) no (36 + 64 = 100 ≠ 121)   d) yes (25 + 144 = 169)

             g) yes (100 + 576 = 676)   h) no (81 + 144 = 225 ≠ 256)
             i) yes (49 + 576 = 625)   j) yes (400 + 441 = 841)

M7. Classify each triangle as acute-angled, right-angled or obtuse-angled by

M10. Find the distance between the two points. Give an exact answer; for a surd
     also give 2 decimal places.
     a) (1, 2), (4, 6)      b) (0, 0), (5, 12)     c) (2, 3), (10, 9)

M12. Find the height h of the triangle (the perpendicular from the top to the
     base). Isosceles: equal sides s and base b, so h² = s² − (b/2)².
     Equilateral with side a. Exact answer; surds also to 2 decimal places.
     a) s = 5, b = 6          b) s = 13, b = 10
     c) s = 10, b = 12        d) s = 17, b = 16
     e) s = 25, b = 14        f) s = 25, b = 48
     g) s = 41, b = 18        h) s = 5, b = 8
     i) s = 6, b = 4          j) s = 10, b = 10
     k) equilateral, a = 2    l) equilateral, a = 4
     m) equilateral, a = 6    n) equilateral, a = 10
     o) equilateral, a = 1    p) equilateral, a = 12
     Answers: a) 4   b) 12   c) 8   d) 15   e) 24   f) 7   g) 40   h) 3
              i) √32 = 4√2 ≈ 5.66   j) √75 = 5√3 ≈ 8.66   k) √3 ≈ 1.73
              l) √12 = 2√3 ≈ 3.46   m) √27 = 3√3 ≈ 5.20   n) √75 = 5√3 ≈ 8.66
              o) √3/2 ≈ 0.87   p) √108 = 6√3 ≈ 10.39

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: draw the triangle every time, mark the right angle and ring the
  hypotenuse before writing anything; say "longest side: add the squares;
  shorter side: subtract"; keep a table of squares 1² to 30² in the notebook;
  start with the 3-4-5 and 5-12-13 families and cut out squares of centimetre
  paper to check 9 + 16 = 25. Harder: prove the converse (build a right-angled
  triangle with legs a and b; its hypotenuse is c, so the two triangles are
  congruent by SSS); show that (m² − n², 2mn, m² + n²) always works by
  expanding; find all triples with hypotenuse 25 ((7, 24, 25) and (15, 20, 25));
  find the diagonal of a cube of side s (s√3); investigate Garfield's trapezium
  proof.


================================================================================
TOPIC 6.22 — QUADRILATERALS AND POLYGONS: PROPERTIES, INTERIOR/EXTERIOR ANGLE
             SUMS
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - PARALLELOGRAM: both pairs of opposite sides parallel and equal; opposite
    angles equal; neighbouring angles add up to 180°; the diagonals BISECT each
    other (cut each other in half).
  - RECTANGLE = parallelogram with four right angles; its diagonals are also
    EQUAL. RHOMBUS = parallelogram with four equal sides; its diagonals are
    PERPENDICULAR and bisect the corner angles. SQUARE = both a rectangle and a
    rhombus, so it has every one of these properties.
  - TRAPEZIUM: exactly one pair of parallel sides; the two angles along each
    slanting side add up to 180°. In an ISOSCELES trapezium the slanting sides
    are equal, the base angles are equal and the diagonals are equal. KITE: two
    pairs of equal NEIGHBOURING sides; one pair of opposite angles equal;
    diagonals perpendicular, and one of them is a line of symmetry.
  - The INTERIOR angles of a polygon with n sides add up to (n − 2) × 180°,
    because diagonals from one corner cut it into n − 2 triangles: triangle
    180°, quadrilateral 360°, pentagon 540°, hexagon 720°, octagon 1080°,
    decagon 1440°, 20-gon 3240°.
  - The EXTERIOR angles (one at each corner, made by extending a side) of ANY
    convex polygon add up to 360° — one full turn. At each corner
    interior + exterior = 180°.
  - REGULAR polygon (all sides and all angles equal): each exterior angle is
    360° ÷ n; each interior angle is 180° − exterior, which equals
    (n − 2) × 180° ÷ n. Backwards: n = 360° ÷ exterior angle; if this is not a
    whole number, no such regular polygon exists.
  - Missing angles: add the known angles and subtract from the angle sum; with
    algebra, write "sum of the expressions = angle sum" and solve for x.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. In parallelogram ABCD, AB = 9 cm, BC = 5 cm and ∠A = 65°. The diagonals meet
    at O and AO = 4 cm. Find the other sides, the other angles, the perimeter
    and the length of diagonal AC.
    Answer: CD = 9 cm, DA = 5 cm; ∠B = 115°, ∠C = 65°, ∠D = 115°;
    perimeter = 28 cm; AC = 8 cm.
    Teacher explains:
            D +-----------------+ C
             /  .           .  /
            /      .  O  .    /     AB ∥ DC, AD ∥ BC
           /    .       .    /
        A +-----------------+ B
    Opposite sides of a parallelogram are equal: CD = AB = 9 cm and
    DA = BC = 5 cm, so the perimeter is 2 × (9 + 5) = 28 cm. AD ∥ BC, so ∠A and
    ∠B are co-interior angles (topic 6.19) and add up to 180°:
    ∠B = 180° − 65° = 115°. Opposite angles are equal: ∠C = 65°, ∠D = 115°.
    Check: 65 + 115 + 65 + 115 = 360.
    The diagonals BISECT each other, so O is the midpoint of AC:
    AC = 2 × 4 = 8 cm. Common mistake: thinking the diagonals of every
    parallelogram are equal — that is only true for rectangles. Now you try:
    ∠A = 108°. (∠B = 72°, ∠C = 108°, ∠D = 72°)

D2. Rectangle ABCD is 8 cm long and 6 cm wide, and its diagonals AC and BD meet
    at O. How long is each diagonal, and what kind of triangle is AOB? How does
    a carpenter use this to check that a frame is truly rectangular?
    Answer: Each diagonal is 10 cm (√(64 + 36) = 10). OA = OB = 5 cm, so
    triangle AOB is isosceles. A carpenter measures both diagonals: in a
    rectangle they must be equal.
    Teacher explains: A rectangle is a parallelogram, so its diagonals bisect
    each other. Its diagonals are also EQUAL: triangles ABC and BAD are
    congruent by SAS (AB common, BC = AD, both right angles), so AC = BD. By
    Pythagoras AC² = 8² + 6² = 64 + 36 = 100, AC = 10 cm, and
    OA = OB = OC = OD = 5 cm.
    A frame that has been pushed out of shape is a parallelogram: its sides are
    still equal in pairs, but one diagonal becomes longer than the other. So
    "equal diagonals" is the test for a right-angled frame. Now you try: a
    rectangle 12 cm by 5 cm. Find a diagonal and OA. (13 cm, 6.5 cm)

D3. The diagonals of a rhombus are 12 cm and 16 cm. Find the length of a side,
    the perimeter and the area.
    Answer: Side 10 cm, perimeter 40 cm, area 96 cm².
    Teacher explains:
                  .
                / | \
              /   8   \       diagonals cross at 90°
            /  6  |  6  \     halves: 6 cm and 8 cm
            \     |     /
              \   8   /
                \ | /
                  .
    The diagonals of a rhombus bisect each other AT RIGHT ANGLES. So they cut
    the rhombus into 4 congruent right-angled triangles with legs 12 ÷ 2 = 6 cm
    and 16 ÷ 2 = 8 cm. Each side is a hypotenuse: √(6² + 8²) = √100 = 10 cm, and
    the perimeter is 4 × 10 = 40 cm.
    Area: the four triangles each have area 1/2 × 6 × 8 = 24 cm², total
    4 × 24 = 96 cm². Shortcut: area = 1/2 × d₁ × d₂ = 1/2 × 12 × 16 = 96. This
    works for every quadrilateral with perpendicular diagonals (rhombus, square,
    kite). Now you try: diagonals 10 cm and 24 cm. (side 13 cm, area 120 cm²)

D4. Riya says, "A square is a rhombus, but a rhombus is not always a square." Is
    she right? Explain with the family tree of quadrilaterals.
    Answer: Yes. A square has four equal sides, so it is a rhombus. A rhombus
    need not have right angles (for example angles 60°, 120°, 60°, 120°), so it
    need not be a square.
    Teacher explains:
                       QUADRILATERAL
                    /        |        \
            TRAPEZIUM  PARALLELOGRAM   KITE
                         /       \     /
                   RECTANGLE    RHOMBUS
                         \       /
                          SQUARE
    Read the tree downwards: each shape is a SPECIAL CASE of every shape above
    it joined by lines, and it has all their properties. A square is a rectangle
    (four right angles), a rhombus (four equal sides), a parallelogram and even
    a kite. Reading upwards needs care: a parallelogram is not always a
    rectangle.
    To decide "Is every A a B?", check whether the DEFINITION of B is true for
    every A. Common mistake: thinking a square is "not a rectangle" because it
    looks different. Now you try: true or false — every rectangle is a
    parallelogram. (true)

D5. Kite ABCD has AB = AD and CB = CD, with ∠A = 120° and ∠C = 50°. Find ∠B and
    ∠D. Trapezium PQRS has PQ ∥ SR, ∠P = 70° and ∠Q = 55°. Find ∠S and ∠R.
    Answer: ∠B = ∠D = 95°. ∠S = 110° and ∠R = 125°.
    Teacher explains: KITE: the diagonal AC is a line of symmetry, so the angles
    B and D on either side of it are equal. The angle sum is 360°:
    ∠B + ∠D = 360° − (120° + 50°) = 190°, and each is 190° ÷ 2 = 95°. Note: ∠A
    and ∠C (between the unequal pairs of sides) are NOT equal.
    TRAPEZIUM: PS and QR cross the parallel sides, so the two angles on each
    slanting side are co-interior and add up to 180°: ∠S = 180° − 70° = 110° and
    ∠R = 180° − 55° = 125°. Check: 70 + 55 + 125 + 110 = 360. Common mistake:
    pairing the angles along a PARALLEL side (P and Q) instead of along a
    slanting side. Now you try: an isosceles trapezium has one base angle 64°.
    Find the other three. (64°, 116°, 116°)

D6. Why do the interior angles of a polygon with n sides add up to
    (n − 2) × 180°? Find the angle sum of a 15-sided polygon.
    Answer: From one corner, draw all the diagonals: they make n − 2 triangles,
    each with angle sum 180°. For n = 15: (15 − 2) × 180° = 2340°.
    Teacher explains:
       sides n     3     4     5     6     7      8      9     10
       triangles   1     2     3     4     5      6      7      8
       angle sum  180   360   540   720   900   1080   1260   1440
    From corner A you cannot draw a diagonal to A itself or to its two
    neighbours, so there are n − 3 diagonals, cutting the polygon into n − 2
    triangles. The angles of these triangles fit together exactly into the
    angles of the polygon. So the sum is (n − 2) × 180°.
    A second way: join the centre to all n corners. That gives n triangles,
    n × 180°, but the angles round the centre (360°) are not polygon angles:
    n × 180° − 360° = (n − 2) × 180°. For n = 15:
    15 × 180 − 360 = 2700 − 360 = 2340. Each extra side adds 180°. Now you try:
    a 20-sided polygon. (3240°)

D7. Find each interior angle and each exterior angle of a regular 12-sided
    polygon (a dodecagon).
    Answer: Interior 150°, exterior 30°.
    Teacher explains: Method 1: angle sum (12 − 2) × 180° = 1800°; the 12 angles
    are equal, so each is 1800° ÷ 12 = 150°. Exterior = 180° − 150° = 30°.
    Method 2 (faster): the exterior angles add up to 360°, so each is
    360° ÷ 12 = 30°, and the interior angle is 180° − 30° = 150°. For large n
    this is much easier.
       n           3     4     5     6     8     9    10    12
       exterior   120    90    72    60    45    40    36    30
       interior    60    90   108   120   135   140   144   150
    Common mistake: dividing 360° by n and calling it the INTERIOR angle. Now
    you try: a regular 20-sided polygon. (interior 162°, exterior 18°)

D8. Meena walks once round a park shaped like a pentagon. At the five corners
    she turns left through 70°, 60°, 80°, 75° and 75°. What is her total turn,
    and why?
    Answer: 70° + 60° + 80° + 75° + 75° = 360°: one full turn. She ends facing
    the way she started.
    Teacher explains: The angle she TURNS at each corner is the EXTERIOR angle.
    After walking all the way round she faces her starting direction again, so
    the turns together make exactly one complete turn, 360°. This is true for
    every convex polygon, whatever its number of sides.
    Proof with algebra: at each corner interior + exterior = 180°, so all n
    corners give n × 180°. The interior angles take (n − 2) × 180° of this,
    leaving n × 180° − (n − 2) × 180° = 2 × 180° = 360° for the exterior angles.
    Now you try: five exterior angles of a hexagon are 50°, 60°, 70°, 40° and
    80°. Find the sixth. (60°)

D9. A regular polygon has each exterior angle 24°. How many sides has it? Is
    there a regular polygon with each interior angle 130°?
    Answer: 360° ÷ 24° = 15 sides. No: the exterior angle would be 50°, and
    360 ÷ 50 = 7.2 is not a whole number.
    Teacher explains: Work backwards through the exterior angle, because the
    exterior angles of a regular polygon are n equal parts of 360°:
    n = 360° ÷ exterior. From an interior angle, first find the exterior:
    180° − interior. For example interior 156° gives exterior 24°, so n = 15
    again.
    For interior 130°: exterior 180° − 130° = 50°, and 360 ÷ 50 = 7.2. A polygon
    cannot have 7.2 sides, so no regular polygon has angles of 130°. The
    exterior angle must be a FACTOR of 360. Now you try: each interior angle is
    165°. How many sides? (24)

D10. The angles of a hexagon are x, x + 10°, x + 20°, x + 30°, x + 40° and
     x + 50°. Find x and the angles.
     Answer: x = 95°; the angles are 95°, 105°, 115°, 125°, 135°, 145°.
     Teacher explains: Angle sum of a hexagon: (6 − 2) × 180° = 720°. Add the
     expressions: six x's and 10 + 20 + 30 + 40 + 50 = 150. So
        6x + 150 = 720
              6x = 570     (subtract 150 from both sides)
               x = 95      (divide both sides by 6)
     Check: 95 + 105 + 115 + 125 + 135 + 145 = 720. Always check that every
     angle is sensible (for a convex polygon each is less than 180°). Common
     mistake: using 360° for every polygon. Now you try: a pentagon has angles
     2x, 3x, 3x, 4x and 3x. (x = 36°; angles 72°, 108°, 108°, 144°, 108°)

D11. Floor tiles in many Indian homes are squares or hexagons. Which regular
     polygons can cover a floor with no gaps (tessellate) on their own? Can
     regular octagons do it?
     Answer: Only equilateral triangles (6 × 60° = 360°), squares
     (4 × 90° = 360°) and regular hexagons (3 × 120° = 360°). Octagons alone
     cannot (360 ÷ 135 is not whole), but two octagons and a square fit:
     135° + 135° + 90° = 360°.
     Teacher explains: Where tiles meet at a point, their angles must add up to
     exactly 360°. So the interior angle must divide 360 exactly. Pentagon:
     108°, and 3 × 108 = 324 leaves a gap, 4 × 108 = 432 overlaps. Any regular
     polygon with more than 6 sides has an interior angle between 120° and 180°,
     so 2 tiles are too few and 3 are too many.
     Mixing shapes gives more patterns: octagon-octagon-square is common on
     bathroom floors. Now you try: can two regular 12-gons and one equilateral
     triangle meet at a point? (yes, 150 + 150 + 60 = 360)

D12. In a parallelogram two opposite angles are (3x + 10)° and (5x − 30)°. Find
     x and all four angles.
     Answer: x = 20; the angles are 70°, 110°, 70°, 110°.
     Teacher explains: Choose the right property FIRST. Opposite angles of a
     parallelogram are equal, so
        3x + 10 = 5x − 30
             40 = 2x          (add 30, subtract 3x on both sides)
              x = 20
     Then 3 × 20 + 10 = 70 and 5 × 20 − 30 = 70 (equal, as they must be).
     Neighbouring angles add to 180°, so the other two angles are
     180° − 70° = 110° each. If the two angles were NEIGHBOURS instead, the
     equation would be (sum) = 180. Now you try: two neighbouring angles are
     (2x + 15)° and (3x − 5)°. (x = 34; 83° and 97°)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. Pa = parallelogram, Re = rectangle, Rh = rhombus, Sq = square,
    Tr = trapezium (exactly one pair of parallel sides), Ki = kite (two pairs of
    equal neighbouring sides, not a rhombus). Which of the six ALWAYS have the
    property?
    a) both pairs of opposite sides parallel
    b) exactly one pair of parallel sides
    c) both pairs of opposite sides equal
    d) all four sides equal
    e) two pairs of equal neighbouring sides
    f) all four angles 90°
    g) both pairs of opposite angles equal
    h) exactly one pair of opposite angles equal
    i) every two neighbouring angles add to 180°
    j) diagonals bisect each other
    k) diagonals equal in length
    l) diagonals perpendicular
    m) each diagonal bisects the two corner angles it joins
    n) a diagonal is a line of symmetry
    o) at least one line of symmetry
    p) at least two lines of symmetry
    q) four lines of symmetry
    r) rotational symmetry (order 2 or more)
    s) area = 1/2 × d₁ × d₂ (d₁, d₂ the diagonals)
    t) area = base × perpendicular height
    u) angle sum 360°
    v) one diagonal cuts the other in half at 90°
    Answers: a) Pa, Re, Rh, Sq   b) Tr   c) Pa, Re, Rh, Sq   d) Rh, Sq
             e) Rh, Sq, Ki   f) Re, Sq   g) Pa, Re, Rh, Sq   h) Ki
             i) Pa, Re, Rh, Sq   j) Pa, Re, Rh, Sq   k) Re, Sq   l) Rh, Sq, Ki
             m) Rh, Sq   n) Rh, Sq, Ki   o) Re, Rh, Sq, Ki   p) Re, Rh, Sq
             q) Sq   r) Pa, Re, Rh, Sq   s) Rh, Sq, Ki   t) Pa, Re, Rh, Sq
             u) Pa, Re, Rh, Sq, Tr, Ki   v) Rh, Sq, Ki

M2. Name the most special quadrilateral that is ALWAYS described.
    a) parallelogram with one right angle
    b) rhombus with one right angle
    c) rectangle with two neighbouring sides equal
    d) parallelogram with two neighbouring sides equal
    e) parallelogram with perpendicular diagonals
    f) parallelogram with equal diagonals
    g) quadrilateral whose diagonals bisect each other
    h) diagonals bisect each other and are equal
    i) diagonals bisect each other at right angles
    j) diagonals equal and bisect each other at right angles
    k) rhombus with equal diagonals
    l) rectangle with perpendicular diagonals
    m) exactly one pair of parallel sides
    n) trapezium whose slanting sides are equal (not parallel)
    o) two pairs of equal neighbouring sides, not all four equal
    p) four equal sides and four equal angles
    q) four equal angles
    r) four equal sides
    Answers: a) rectangle   b) square   c) square   d) rhombus   e) rhombus
             f) rectangle   g) parallelogram   h) rectangle   i) rhombus
             j) square   k) square   l) square   m) trapezium
             n) isosceles trapezium   o) kite   p) square   q) rectangle
             r) rhombus

M3. True or false?
    a) Every square is a rectangle.
    b) Every rectangle is a square.
    c) Every square is a rhombus.
    d) Every rhombus is a parallelogram.
    e) Every parallelogram is a rhombus.
    f) Every rectangle is a parallelogram.
    g) A trapezium can have two right angles.
    h) The diagonals of every parallelogram are equal.
    i) The diagonals of a rhombus are perpendicular.
    j) The diagonals of a rectangle are perpendicular.
    k) A kite has two pairs of equal opposite sides.
    l) A square has 4 lines of symmetry.
    m) A parallelogram can have exactly three right angles.
    n) A quadrilateral can have angles 100°, 100°, 100°, 60°.
    o) A quadrilateral can have four obtuse angles.
    p) The diagonals of an isosceles trapezium are equal.
    Answers: a) true   b) false   c) true   d) true   e) false   f) true
             g) true   h) false   i) true   j) false   k) false   l) true
             m) false   n) true   o) false   p) true

M4. Find the angles asked for.
    a) parallelogram ABCD, ∠A = 72°: ∠B, ∠C, ∠D
    b) parallelogram ABCD, ∠A = 125°: ∠B, ∠C, ∠D
    c) parallelogram ABCD, ∠A = 90°: ∠B, ∠C, ∠D
    d) parallelogram ABCD, ∠A = 38°: ∠B, ∠C, ∠D
    e) kite ABCD (∠B = ∠D), ∠A = 100°, ∠C = 60°: ∠B
    f) kite ABCD (∠B = ∠D), ∠A = 140°, ∠C = 70°: ∠B
    g) kite ABCD (∠B = ∠D), ∠A = 90°, ∠C = 64°: ∠B
    h) isosceles trapezium, one base angle 70°: other three
    i) isosceles trapezium, one base angle 115°: other three
    j) isosceles trapezium, one base angle 52°: other three
    k) trapezium ABCD, AB ∥ DC, ∠A = 80°, ∠B = 65°: ∠D, ∠C
    l) trapezium ABCD, AB ∥ DC, ∠A = 110°, ∠B = 95°: ∠D, ∠C
    m) rhombus ABCD, ∠A = 50°: ∠BAC and ∠ABD
    n) rhombus ABCD, ∠A = 124°: ∠BAC and ∠ABD
    o) rectangle ABCD, diagonals meet at O, ∠AOB = 110°: ∠OAB
    p) rectangle ABCD, diagonals meet at O, ∠AOB = 64°: ∠OAB
    Answers: a) 108°, 72°, 108°   b) 55°, 125°, 55°   c) 90°, 90°, 90°
             d) 142°, 38°, 142°   e) 100°   f) 75°   g) 103°
             h) 70°, 110°, 110°   i) 115°, 65°, 65°   j) 52°, 128°, 128°
             k) 100°, 115°   l) 70°, 85°   m) 25°, 65°   n) 62°, 28°   o) 35°
             p) 58°

M5. Find the sum of the interior angles, (n − 2) × 180°, of a polygon with n
    sides.
    a) n = 3    b) n = 4    c) n = 5    d) n = 6    e) n = 7    f) n = 8
    g) n = 9    h) n = 10   i) n = 11   j) n = 12   k) n = 13   l) n = 14
    m) n = 15   n) n = 16   o) n = 17   p) n = 18   q) n = 19   r) n = 20
    Answers: a) 180°   b) 360°   c) 540°   d) 720°   e) 900°   f) 1080°
             g) 1260°   h) 1440°   i) 1620°   j) 1800°   k) 1980°   l) 2160°
             m) 2340°   n) 2520°   o) 2700°   p) 2880°   q) 3060°   r) 3240°

M6. For the REGULAR polygon with n sides find each exterior angle (360° ÷ n) and
    each interior angle (180° − exterior).
    a) n = 3     b) n = 4     c) n = 5     d) n = 6     e) n = 7     f) n = 8
    g) n = 9     h) n = 10    i) n = 12    j) n = 15    k) n = 18    l) n = 20
    m) n = 24    n) n = 30    o) n = 36    p) n = 40    q) n = 45    r) n = 60
    s) n = 72    t) n = 90    u) n = 180   v) n = 360
    Answers: a) ext 120°, int 60°   b) ext 90°, int 90°   c) ext 72°, int 108°
             d) ext 60°, int 120°   e) ext 51 3/7°, int 128 4/7°
             f) ext 45°, int 135°   g) ext 40°, int 140°   h) ext 36°, int 144°
             i) ext 30°, int 150°   j) ext 24°, int 156°   k) ext 20°, int 160°
             l) ext 18°, int 162°   m) ext 15°, int 165°   n) ext 12°, int 168°
             o) ext 10°, int 170°   p) ext 9°, int 171°   q) ext 8°, int 172°
             r) ext 6°, int 174°   s) ext 5°, int 175°   t) ext 4°, int 176°
             u) ext 2°, int 178°   v) ext 1°, int 179°

M7. Each exterior angle of a regular polygon is given. How many sides has it?
    Write "none" if no regular polygon has this angle.
    a) 120°   b) 90°    c) 72°    d) 60°    e) 45°    f) 40°    g) 36°
    h) 30°    i) 24°    j) 20°    k) 18°    l) 15°    m) 12°    n) 10°
    o) 9°     p) 8°     q) 6°     r) 5°     s) 4°     t) 3°     u) 2°
    v) 1°     w) 25°    x) 7°
    Answers: a) 3   b) 4   c) 5   d) 6   e) 8   f) 9   g) 10   h) 12   i) 15
             j) 18   k) 20   l) 24   m) 30   n) 36   o) 40   p) 45   q) 60
             r) 72   s) 90   t) 120   u) 180   v) 360   w) none   x) none

M8. Each interior angle of a regular polygon is given. How many sides has it?
    Write "none" if no regular polygon has this angle.
    a) 60°    b) 90°    c) 108°   d) 120°   e) 135°   f) 140°   g) 144°
    h) 150°   i) 156°   j) 160°   k) 162°   l) 165°   m) 168°   n) 170°
    o) 171°   p) 172°   q) 174°   r) 175°   s) 176°   t) 178°   u) 179°
    v) 100°   w) 130°   x) 145°
    Answers: a) 3   b) 4   c) 5   d) 6   e) 8   f) 9   g) 10   h) 12   i) 15
             j) 18   k) 20   l) 24   m) 30   n) 36   o) 40   p) 45   q) 60
             r) 72   s) 90   t) 180   u) 360   v) none   w) none   x) none

M9. The interior angles of a polygon add up to the given total. How many sides
    has it? Write "none" if it is impossible.
    a) 180°    b) 360°    c) 540°    d) 720°    e) 900°    f) 1080°   g) 1260°
    h) 1440°   i) 1620°   j) 1800°   k) 1980°   l) 2160°   m) 2340°   n) 2520°
    o) 2700°   p) 2880°   q) 3240°   r) 3600°   s) 1000°   t) 450°    u) 1700°
    v) 5400°
    Answers: a) 3   b) 4   c) 5   d) 6   e) 7   f) 8   g) 9   h) 10   i) 11
             j) 12   k) 13   l) 14   m) 15   n) 16   o) 17   p) 18   q) 20
             r) 22   s) none   t) none   u) none   v) 32

M10. All the interior angles but one are given. Find the missing angle.
     a) pentagon: 145°, 115°, 120°, 100°
     b) hexagon: 95°, 170°, 120°, 120°, 100°
     c) heptagon: 125°, 135°, 110°, 135°, 150°, 140°
     d) octagon: 140°, 140°, 110°, 105°, 170°, 165°, 145°
     e) pentagon: 95°, 110°, 105°, 150°
     f) hexagon: 105°, 140°, 105°, 120°, 125°
     g) heptagon: 165°, 130°, 120°, 130°, 120°, 135°
     h) octagon: 175°, 150°, 120°, 115°, 120°, 150°, 165°
     i) pentagon: 120°, 105°, 100°, 140°
     j) hexagon: 105°, 125°, 170°, 115°, 115°
     k) octagon: 110°, 150°, 175°, 120°, 125°, 170°, 130°
     l) heptagon: 145°, 155°, 170°, 100°, 125°, 95°
     Answers: a) 60°   b) 115°   c) 105°   d) 105°   e) 80°   f) 125°   g) 100°
              h) 85°   i) 75°   j) 90°   k) 100°   l) 110°

M11. In a) to j) all the exterior angles but one are given (one at each corner):
     find the missing one. In k) to r) each interior angle of a regular polygon
     is k times each exterior angle: find the exterior angle and the number of
     sides.
     a) 120°, 120°
     b) 65°, 120°, 65°
     c) 30°, 65°, 55°, 120°
     d) 60°, 115°, 70°, 45°, 50°
     e) 50°, 75°, 85°, 45°, 35°, 45°
     f) 105°, 85°, 100°
     g) 105°, 55°, 110°, 20°
     h) 50°, 115°, 35°, 35°, 35°
     i) 20°, 65°, 45°, 25°, 60°, 105°, 20°
     j) 45°, 90°, 55°, 85°
     k) k = 1
     l) k = 2
     m) k = 3
     n) k = 4
     o) k = 5
     p) k = 7
     q) k = 1.5
     r) k = 11
     Answers: a) 120°   b) 110°   c) 90°   d) 20°   e) 25°   f) 70°   g) 70°
              h) 90°   i) 20°   j) 85°   k) ext 90°, n = 4   l) ext 60°, n = 6
              m) ext 45°, n = 8   n) ext 36°, n = 10   o) ext 30°, n = 12
              p) ext 22 1/2°, n = 16   q) ext 72°, n = 5   r) ext 15°, n = 24

M12. The interior angles (in degrees) are given in terms of x. Find x.
     a) quadrilateral: x, 2x, 3x, 4x
     b) quadrilateral: x, x, 2x, 2x
     c) quadrilateral: x, x + 20, x + 40, x + 60
     d) quadrilateral: 2x + 10, 3x, x + 50, 2x + 20
     e) pentagon: x, 2x, 2x, 2x, 2x
     f) pentagon: x, x + 10, x + 20, x + 30, x + 40
     g) pentagon: 3x, 3x, 2x, 2x, 2x
     h) pentagon: 2x, 2x, 2x, 3x − 30, 3x − 30
     i) hexagon: x, x, x, 2x, 2x, 2x
     j) hexagon: x, x + 20, x + 40, x + 60, x + 80, x + 100
     k) hexagon: 4x, 4x, 5x, 5x, 6x, 6x
     l) hexagon: 2x + 30, 2x + 30, 2x + 30, 3x, 3x, 3x
     m) octagon: x, x, x, x, 2x − 45, 2x − 45, 2x − 45, 2x − 45
     n) triangle: x, 2x, 3x
     o) triangle: 2x + 10, 3x + 20, 5x − 30
     p) heptagon: x, x, x, x, x, x, x − 10
     Answers: a) x = 36   b) x = 60   c) x = 60   d) x = 35   e) x = 60
              f) x = 88   g) x = 45   h) x = 50   i) x = 80   j) x = 70
              k) x = 24   l) x = 42   m) x = 105   n) x = 30   o) x = 18
              p) x = 130

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: build the shapes from geostrips or straws and measure sides,
  angles and diagonals; fill in a big property table (rows: properties; columns:
  the six shapes) with ticks; learn the family tree; for angle sums always draw
  the triangles from one corner and write "(n − 2) × 180°" before substituting;
  for regular polygons start from the exterior angle 360° ÷ n. Harder: prove
  that the diagonals of a parallelogram bisect each other using congruent
  triangles (ASA); show the number of diagonals of an n-gon is n(n − 3)/2 (a
  hexagon has 9); find which pairs of regular polygons can tile together with a
  triangle; show that no polygon can have more than three acute interior angles;
  work out the interior angle sum of a star-shaped (concave) polygon.


================================================================================
TOPIC 6.23 — CIRCLES: CIRCUMFERENCE, AREA, π
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - For EVERY circle, circumference ÷ diameter is the same number, called π
    ("pi"). π = 3.14159... never ends and never repeats (it is irrational).
    Useful values: π ≈ 3.14 and π ≈ 22/7 (use 22/7 when the radius is a multiple
    of 7). Always say which value you used.
  - CIRCUMFERENCE (perimeter of a circle): C = πd = 2πr, because the diameter d
    is twice the radius r.
  - AREA: A = πr² (square the radius, NOT the diameter). Cutting a circle into
    thin sectors and rearranging them gives a near-rectangle πr long and r high.
  - EXACT answers are left "in terms of π": r = 5 gives C = 10π and A = 25π.
    Backwards: r = C ÷ 2π; r² = A ÷ π, then take the square root.
  - SEMICIRCLE: curved part πr (half of 2πr), perimeter πr + 2r (do not forget
    the diameter), area πr²/2. QUADRANT (quarter circle): arc πr/2, perimeter
    πr/2 + 2r, area πr²/4.
  - RING (annulus) between radii R and r: area = πR² − πr² = π(R² − r²). SECTOR
    with angle θ: arc length = (θ/360) × 2πr, area = (θ/360) × πr².
  - WHEELS: in one revolution a wheel rolls one circumference. Distance = number
    of revolutions × circumference. Change km and m to cm (or the other way)
    before dividing.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. Class 7 wraps a string round some round objects and measures the string (the
    circumference C) and the width across the middle (the diameter d). What do
    they notice?
       object     d (cm)    C (cm)    C ÷ d
       bangle       7        22       3.14...
       plate       25        78.5     3.14
       coin         2.5       7.9     3.16
    Answer: C ÷ d is always a little more than 3, about 3.14, whatever the size
    of the circle. This number is π.
    Teacher explains: Work out each ratio: 22 ÷ 7 ≈ 3.14, 78.5 ÷ 25 = 3.14,
    7.9 ÷ 2.5 = 3.16. Small differences come from measuring. The TRUE value,
    found by mathematicians, is π = 3.14159265..., a decimal that never ends and
    never repeats. Aryabhata (499 CE) gave 3.1416.
    So C = π × d. Since d = 2r, also C = 2πr. For quick work we use π ≈ 3.14 or
    π ≈ 22/7 (22 ÷ 7 = 3.142857...). Neither is exact. Common mistake: thinking
    π = 22/7 exactly. Now you try: a bicycle wheel has d = 60 cm and C = 188 cm.
    Find C ÷ d to 2 decimal places. (3.13)

D2. A circular park has radius 35 m. How long is the fence round it? Fencing
    costs ₹15 per metre. What is the cost? (Use π = 22/7.)
    Answer: 220 m; ₹3300.
    Teacher explains:
       C = 2πr
         = 2 × 22/7 × 35     (35 ÷ 7 = 5 first)
         = 2 × 22 × 5
         = 220 m
    Cancelling the 7 first keeps the numbers small: 35 ÷ 7 = 5. Cost:
    220 × 15 = ₹3300. Choose 22/7 when the radius or diameter is a multiple of
    7. Common mistakes: using πr (half the circumference), or πr² (that is area,
    in m²). Now you try: radius 21 m. (132 m)

D3. A pizza has diameter 30 cm. Find its circumference exactly (in terms of π)
    and using π = 3.14.
    Answer: C = 30π cm ≈ 94.2 cm.
    Teacher explains: With the diameter use C = πd directly: C = π × 30 = 30π
    cm. This is the EXACT answer. For a number: 30 × 3.14 = 94.2 cm. With the
    radius we would write C = 2 × π × 15 = 30π — the same.
    In exact form the number goes in front: write 30π, not π30. Leaving π in the
    answer avoids rounding errors, so we use it in later working and only turn
    it into a decimal at the end. Now you try: diameter 12 cm. (12π ≈ 37.68 cm)

D4. Why is the area of a circle πr²? Use the picture: the circle is cut into 16
    thin sectors, and they are arranged top-to-tail.
         /\/\/\/\/\/\/\/\
        /\/\/\/\/\/\/\/\/     height r
        <----- half of C = πr ----->
    Answer: The sectors make an almost-rectangle of length πr (half the
    circumference) and height r, so the area is πr × r = πr².
    Teacher explains: Half the sectors point up and half point down, so each
    long side uses half the curved edge: half of 2πr is πr. The height is the
    radius. The more sectors we cut, the straighter the edges and the closer the
    shape is to a rectangle, so A = πr × r = πr².
    Example: r = 7 cm, π = 22/7: A = 22/7 × 7 × 7 = 22 × 7 = 154 cm². Area is in
    SQUARE units. Common mistake: squaring the diameter, or computing 2πr (the
    circumference). Now you try: r = 14 cm. (616 cm²)

D5. A circular rangoli has radius 1.5 m. What area does it cover? (Use
    π = 3.14.)
    Answer: 7.065 m² (exactly 2.25π m²).
    Teacher explains:
       A = πr²
         = 3.14 × 1.5 × 1.5
         = 3.14 × 2.25
         = 7.065 m²
    Square the radius FIRST: 1.5 × 1.5 = 2.25 (not 3). Then multiply by 3.14:
    3.14 × 2 = 6.28, 3.14 × 0.25 = 0.785, total 7.065. Count decimal places to
    check: 2 + 2 = 4 places in 7.0650. Estimate: 3 × 2 = 6, so about 7 is
    reasonable. Now you try: radius 2.5 m. (19.625 m²)

D6. A wire 88 cm long is bent into a circle. Find the radius and the area
    enclosed. (π = 22/7)
    Answer: r = 14 cm, area = 616 cm².
    Teacher explains: Work backwards from C = 2πr:
       2 × 22/7 × r = 88
            44/7 × r = 88
                  r = 88 × 7 ÷ 44 = 14 cm
    Then A = 22/7 × 14 × 14 = 616 cm². If the same wire is bent into a square,
    each side is 88 ÷ 4 = 22 cm and the area is 22 × 22 = 484 cm² — less. For a
    given perimeter, the circle encloses the most area. Common mistake: dividing
    88 by 22/7 and forgetting the 2. Now you try: a 44 cm wire. (r = 7 cm, area
    154 cm²)

D7. A circular flower bed has area 154 m². Find its radius. (π = 22/7) Also: a
    circle has area 36π cm². What is its radius?
    Answer: r = 7 m; r = 6 cm.
    Teacher explains: A = πr², so r² = A ÷ π. First:
    r² = 154 ÷ (22/7) = 154 × 7 ÷ 22 = 49, and r = √49 = 7 m. Second: 36π = πr²,
    so r² = 36 and r = 6 cm (π cancels — another reason exact answers are nice).
    With 3.14: area 314 m² gives r² = 314 ÷ 3.14 = 100, r = 10 m. Common
    mistake: stopping at r² (writing r = 49). Always finish with the square root
    and check: 22/7 × 7 × 7 = 154. Now you try: area 616 cm², π = 22/7.
    (r = 14 cm)

D8. A protractor is a semicircle of diameter 14 cm. Find its perimeter and its
    area. (π = 22/7)
    Answer: Perimeter 36 cm; area 77 cm².
    Teacher explains:
              . - ~ - .
           .´           `.     curved edge = half of πd
          +-------+-------+
                14 cm
    Curved edge: half of the circumference, 1/2 × 22/7 × 14 = 22 cm. The
    perimeter goes all the way round, so ADD the straight edge (the diameter):
    22 + 14 = 36 cm. Area: half of πr² with r = 7: 1/2 × 22/7 × 7 × 7 = 77 cm².
    Common mistakes: forgetting the diameter in the perimeter, and using the
    diameter 14 in πr². Now you try: a semicircle of diameter 28 cm. (perimeter
    44 + 28 = 72 cm, area 308 cm²)

D9. A flower bed in the corner of a lawn is a quadrant (quarter circle) of
    radius 14 m. Find the length of its edging (all round) and its area.
    (π = 22/7)
    Answer: Perimeter 50 m; area 154 m².
    Teacher explains: A quadrant has ONE curved edge and TWO straight edges (two
    radii). Arc = 1/4 × 2 × 22/7 × 14 = 1/4 × 88 = 22 m.
    Perimeter = 22 + 14 + 14 = 50 m.
    Area = 1/4 × 22/7 × 14 × 14 = 1/4 × 616 = 154 m².
       shape        curved part     perimeter        area
       circle       2πr             2πr              πr²
       semicircle   πr              πr + 2r          πr²/2
       quadrant     πr/2            πr/2 + 2r        πr²/4
    Now you try: a quadrant of radius 7 cm. (perimeter 25 cm, area 38.5 cm²)

D10. A circular pond of radius 14 m has a path 7 m wide all round it. Find the
     area of the path. (π = 22/7)
     Answer: 770 m².
     Teacher explains: The path is a RING (annulus): the big circle minus the
     small circle. Outer radius R = 14 + 7 = 21 m, inner radius r = 14 m.
        area = πR² − πr² = π(R² − r²)
             = 22/7 × (441 − 196)
             = 22/7 × 245
             = 770 m²
     Common mistakes: using the width 7 as a radius (π × 7² is a small circle,
     not the ring), and subtracting the radii before squaring (21 − 14 = 7). Now
     you try: radii 5 cm and 3 cm; give the area in terms of π. (16π cm²)

D11. The minute hand of a clock tower is 21 cm long. How far does its tip travel
     in 15 minutes, and what area does the hand sweep? (π = 22/7)
     Answer: Arc 33 cm; area 346.5 cm².
     Teacher explains: In 60 minutes the hand turns 360°, so in 15 minutes it
     turns 90°, a QUARTER of a turn. The tip moves along a quarter of the
     circle: arc = 90/360 × 2 × 22/7 × 21 = 1/4 × 132 = 33 cm. The swept region
     is a SECTOR: area = 90/360 × 22/7 × 21 × 21 = 1/4 × 1386 = 346.5 cm².
     General rule for angle θ: take the fraction θ/360 of the whole
     circumference (arc) or of the whole area (sector). Now you try: 20 minutes
     (120°). (arc 44 cm, area 462 cm²)

D12. A bicycle wheel has diameter 70 cm. How many complete turns does it make in
     travelling 1.1 km? (π = 22/7)
     Answer: 500 revolutions.
     Teacher explains: In one revolution the wheel rolls forward exactly one
     circumference: C = 22/7 × 70 = 220 cm. Put the distance in the SAME unit:
     1.1 km = 1100 m = 110,000 cm. Number of revolutions = 110,000 ÷ 220 = 500.
     The other direction: distance = revolutions × circumference, e.g. 250 turns
     cover 250 × 220 = 55,000 cm = 550 m. Common mistake: dividing km by cm
     without converting. Now you try: a wheel of radius 42 cm makes 1000
     revolutions. How far does it go? (264,000 cm = 2.64 km)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------

M3. Find the circumference exactly, in terms of π (lengths in cm).
    a) r = 1     b) r = 2     c) r = 3     d) r = 4     e) r = 5     f) r = 6

M4. Find the area A using π = 22/7 (lengths in cm, area in cm²).

M6. Find the area exactly, in terms of π.

     Answers: a) 16π   b) 64π   c) 25π   d) 24π   e) 15π   f) 32π   g) 17π
              h) 144π   i) 300π   j) 3π   k) 462   l) 770   m) 1386   n) 1078
              o) 308   p) 115.5

M11. A sector has radius r and angle θ. Find the arc length and the sector area
     in terms of π.
     a) r = 6, θ = 60°     b) r = 12, θ = 90°    c) r = 10, θ = 36°
     d) r = 9, θ = 120°    e) r = 4, θ = 45°     f) r = 18, θ = 30°
     g) r = 5, θ = 72°     h) r = 8, θ = 270°    i) r = 3, θ = 180°
     j) r = 20, θ = 18°    k) r = 15, θ = 240°   l) r = 12, θ = 150°
     m) r = 6, θ = 300°    n) r = 2, θ = 90°     o) r = 24, θ = 15°
     p) r = 10, θ = 270°
     Answers: a) arc 2π, area 6π   b) arc 6π, area 36π   c) arc 2π, area 10π
              d) arc 6π, area 27π   e) arc π, area 2π   f) arc 3π, area 27π
              g) arc 2π, area 5π   h) arc 12π, area 48π   i) arc 3π, area 4.5π
              j) arc 2π, area 20π   k) arc 20π, area 150π   l) arc 10π, area 60π
              m) arc 10π, area 30π   n) arc π, area π   o) arc 2π, area 24π
              p) arc 15π, area 75π

M12. Wheels (π = 22/7). In a) to h) find the number of revolutions for the
     distance; in i) to p) find the distance in metres for the number of
     revolutions.
     a) d = 70 cm, 2.2 km             b) d = 56 cm, 176 m
     c) r = 35 cm, 1.1 km             d) r = 21 cm, 264 m
     e) d = 1.4 m, 4.4 km             f) r = 0.35 m, 11 km
     g) d = 42 cm, 66 m               h) d = 84 cm, 26.4 m
     i) r = 14 cm, 500 revolutions    j) d = 70 cm, 1000 revolutions
     k) r = 42 cm, 250 revolutions    l) d = 28 cm, 100 revolutions
     m) r = 0.7 m, 50 revolutions     n) d = 7 cm, 30 revolutions
     o) r = 3.5 cm, 400 revolutions   p) d = 63 cm, 20 revolutions
     Answers: a) 1000 revolutions   b) 100 revolutions   c) 500 revolutions
              d) 200 revolutions   e) 1000 revolutions   f) 5000 revolutions
              g) 50 revolutions   h) 10 revolutions   i) 440 m   j) 2200 m
              k) 660 m   l) 88 m   m) 220 m   n) 6.6 m   o) 88 m   p) 39.6 m

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: measure real circles (bangles, plates, bottle tops) with string
  and find C ÷ d many times; write "r or d?" next to every question and halve
  the diameter before using πr²; keep the formula card C = 2πr, A = πr² on the
  desk; with π = 22/7 always cancel the 7 first; draw semicircles and quadrants
  and trace the perimeter with a finger to see the straight edges. Harder: show
  that doubling the radius doubles the circumference but multiplies the area by
  4; find the area of the shaded region between a square of side 14 cm and its
  inscribed circle (196 − 154 = 42 cm²); find how far the tip of a 7 cm hour
  hand travels in a day (88 cm); research Archimedes' method of trapping π
  between polygons of 96 sides; estimate π by dropping grains of rice on a
  square with a quadrant drawn in it.


================================================================================
TOPIC 6.24 — SURFACE AREA AND VOLUME: PRISMS, CYLINDERS, CONES, SPHERES,
             PYRAMIDS
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - VOLUME = space inside (cm³, m³); SURFACE AREA = total area of all the faces
    or curved surfaces (cm², m²). CURVED (or lateral) surface area leaves out
    the ends; TOTAL surface area includes them.
  - PRISM (same cross-section all along): V = area of cross-section × length.
    Cuboid: V = lbh, TSA = 2(lb + bh + lh), walls only = 2(l + b) × h. Cube:
    V = s³, TSA = 6s². Triangular prism: V = 1/2 × base × height × length.
  - CYLINDER (a circular prism): V = πr²h; curved surface CSA = 2πrh (the label
    unrolls to a rectangle 2πr by h); TSA = 2πrh + 2πr².
  - CONE: V = 1/3 × πr²h (one third of the cylinder with the same base and
    height); slant height l = √(r² + h²); CSA = πrl; TSA = πrl + πr².
  - SPHERE: V = 4/3 × πr³, SA = 4πr². HEMISPHERE: V = 2/3 × πr³, curved surface
    2πr², total (with the flat circle) 3πr².
  - PYRAMID: V = 1/3 × base area × height. For a square pyramid the slant height
    of a face comes from Pythagoras, and TSA = base + 4 triangles.
  - UNITS: 1 cm³ = 1 mL; 1000 cm³ = 1 L; 1 m³ = 1000 L = 1,000,000 cm³;
    1 m² = 10,000 cm². Composite solids: add (or subtract) volumes; for surface
    area count only the surfaces on the outside.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. A classroom is 5 m long, 4 m wide and 3 m high. Find the volume of air in it
    and the area of the four walls. Painting the walls and the ceiling costs ₹25
    per m². What is the cost?
    Answer: Volume 60 m³; walls 54 m²; walls + ceiling 74 m², cost ₹1850.
    Teacher explains: Volume: V = l × b × h = 5 × 4 × 3 = 60 m³.
    Walls: two walls are 5 × 3 and two are 4 × 3. Quicker: walk round the room —
    the perimeter 2 × (5 + 4) = 18 m — and multiply by the height:
    18 × 3 = 54 m². This is the LATERAL surface area 2(l + b) × h. Ceiling:
    5 × 4 = 20 m². Total to paint: 54 + 20 = 74 m², cost 74 × 25 = ₹1850 (the
    floor is not painted).
    Common mistake: using the full TSA 2(lb + bh + lh) = 94 m², which includes
    the floor. Read what is to be covered. Now you try: a room 6 m × 4 m × 3 m.
    Area of the four walls? (60 m²)

D2. A tent is a triangular prism. Its end is a triangle with base 3 m and height
    2 m, and the tent is 5 m long. Find its volume and its total surface area.
              /\
             /  \ 2.5 m       end: base 3 m, height 2 m
            / 2 m\            length 5 m
           /__|___\
             3 m
    Answer: V = 15 m³; TSA = 46 m².
    Teacher explains: Volume of any prism = cross-section area × length.
    Cross-section: 1/2 × 3 × 2 = 3 m². V = 3 × 5 = 15 m³.
    Surface: 2 triangles (2 × 3 = 6 m²) and 3 rectangles. The sloping sides need
    Pythagoras: half the base is 1.5 m, so the slope is
    √(1.5² + 2²) = √6.25 = 2.5 m. Rectangles: (2.5 + 2.5 + 3) × 5 = 40 m².
    TSA = 6 + 40 = 46 m². (Canvas without the floor: 46 − 15 = 31 m².) Tip: the
    rectangles together are perimeter of the triangle × length. Now you try: a
    prism whose end is a right-angled triangle with sides 6, 8, 10 cm, length
    12 cm. (V = 288 cm³, TSA = 336 cm²)

D3. A cylindrical water tank has radius 0.7 m and height 2 m. How many litres
    does it hold? (π = 22/7)
    Answer: 3.08 m³ = 3080 L.
    Teacher explains:
       V = πr²h
         = 22/7 × 0.7 × 0.7 × 2
         = 22 × 0.1 × 0.7 × 2     (0.7 ÷ 7 = 0.1)
         = 3.08 m³
    A cylinder is a prism with a circular cross-section, so its volume is the
    area of the circle times the height: πr² × h. Then 1 m³ = 1000 L, so
    3.08 m³ = 3080 L.
    Common mistakes: using the diameter as r, and forgetting to square the
    radius. Now you try: radius 1.4 m, height 1 m. (6.16 m³ = 6160 L)

D4. A tin can has radius 7 cm and height 10 cm. Find the area of the label round
    its side and the total area of metal. (π = 22/7)
    Answer: Label (curved surface) 440 cm²; total surface 748 cm².
    Teacher explains:
         ( top )
       +----------------------+
       |      label           | h = 10
       +----------------------+
            length 2πr = 44
         (bottom)
    Peel off the label: it is a RECTANGLE. Its length is the distance round the
    can, 2πr = 2 × 22/7 × 7 = 44 cm, and its width is the height 10 cm.
    CSA = 44 × 10 = 440 cm² (= 2πrh). The two ends are circles of area
    22/7 × 7 × 7 = 154 cm² each. TSA = 440 + 2 × 154 = 748 cm².
    An open tank or a pipe uses fewer ends: open at top, CSA + 1 circle; a pipe,
    CSA only. Now you try: radius 3.5 cm, height 10 cm. (CSA 220 cm², TSA
    297 cm²)

D5. A birthday cap is a cone with radius 6 cm and height 8 cm. Find its slant
    height, the paper needed (curved surface) and the volume inside. Give exact
    answers and then use π = 3.14.
    Answer: l = 10 cm; CSA = 60π ≈ 188.4 cm²; V = 96π ≈ 301.44 cm³.
    Teacher explains: The height, the radius and the slant height make a
    right-angled triangle inside the cone, with l as hypotenuse:
    l = √(6² + 8²) = √100 = 10 cm.
    Curved surface: CSA = πrl = π × 6 × 10 = 60π ≈ 60 × 3.14 = 188.4 cm².
    Volume:
    V = 1/3 × π × 6² × 8 = 1/3 × π × 36 × 8 = 96π ≈ 96 × 3.14 = 301.44 cm³. With
    a base the TSA would be 60π + 36π = 96π cm².
    Common mistake: using the height h in πrl instead of the slant height l (and
    l in the volume instead of h). Now you try: r = 5 cm, h = 12 cm. (l = 13 cm,
    CSA 65π cm², V 100π cm³)

D6. Why does a cone have 1/3 in its volume formula? A cone of ice cream has
    radius 7 cm and height 12 cm. Find its volume. (π = 22/7)
    Answer: A cone holds exactly one third of the cylinder with the same base
    and height. V = 1/3 × 22/7 × 7 × 7 × 12 = 616 cm³.
    Teacher explains: Experiment: fill a paper cone with rice and pour it into a
    cylinder with the same radius and height. It takes exactly THREE cones to
    fill the cylinder. So V(cone) = 1/3 × πr²h. The same is true for a pyramid
    and the prism on the same base.
       cylinder: 22/7 × 7 × 7 × 12 = 1848 cm³
       cone:     1848 ÷ 3            = 616 cm³
    Now you try: a cone with radius 7 cm and height 9 cm. (462 cm³)

D7. A large decorative globe is a sphere of radius 21 cm. Find its surface area
    and volume. (π = 22/7)
    Answer: SA = 5544 cm²; V = 38,808 cm³.
    Teacher explains:
    SA = 4πr² = 4 × 22/7 × 21 × 21 = 4 × 22 × 3 × 21 = 5544 cm².
    V = 4/3 × πr³ = 4/3 × 22/7 × 21 × 21 × 21 = 38,808 cm³ (cancel: 21 ÷ 7 = 3
    and 21 ÷ 3 = 7, then 4 × 22 × 3 × 7 × 21 = 38,808).
    Archimedes showed the surface of a sphere is exactly 4 times the area of its
    great circle (πr²). In terms of π, a ball of radius 3 has
    SA = 4 × π × 9 = 36π and V = 4/3 × π × 27 = 36π — the same number, but
    different units (cm² and cm³). Common mistake: 4/3 × πr² (mixing the
    formulas). Now you try: radius 6 cm, in terms of π. (SA 144π cm², V 288π
    cm³)

D8. A steel bowl is a hemisphere of radius 7 cm. How much does it hold? What is
    the area of its curved surface? What is the total surface area of a SOLID
    hemisphere of the same radius? (π = 22/7)
    Answer: V = 718 2/3 cm³ (about 0.72 L); curved surface 308 cm²; solid
    hemisphere TSA 462 cm².
    Teacher explains: A hemisphere is half a sphere, so
    V = 2/3 × πr³ = 2/3 × 22/7 × 343 = 2156/3 = 718 2/3 cm³. Curved
    surface = half of 4πr² = 2πr² = 2 × 22/7 × 49 = 308 cm².
    A solid hemisphere also has a flat circular face πr² = 154 cm², so its
    TSA = 2πr² + πr² = 3πr² = 462 cm². A bowl has no lid, so only the curved
    part counts. Now you try: a hemisphere of radius 21 cm. (curved surface
    2772 cm², V 19,404 cm³)

D9. A square pyramid has base side 10 m and vertical height 12 m. Find its
    volume, the slant height of each triangular face, and its total surface
    area.
    Answer: V = 400 m³; slant height 13 m; TSA = 360 m².
    Teacher explains: The base area is 10 × 10 = 100 m², so
    V = 1/3 × 100 × 12 = 400 m³ (one third of base area × height).
    Slant height: from the top, drop straight down to the centre of the base,
    then go to the middle of one edge (half of 10 m is 5 m). The slant height is
    the hypotenuse: √(12² + 5²) = √169 = 13 m.
    TSA = base + 4 triangles = 100 + 4 × 1/2 × 10 × 13 = 100 + 260 = 360 m².
    Common mistake: using the vertical height 12 as the height of the triangular
    faces. Now you try: base side 6 cm, height 4 cm. (V = 48 cm³, slant height
    5 cm, TSA = 96 cm²)

D10. A cylindrical jug has radius 7 cm and height 20 cm. How many litres does it
     hold, and how many 200 mL glasses can be filled from it? (π = 22/7)
     Answer: 3080 cm³ = 3.08 L; 15 full glasses (3080 ÷ 200 = 15.4).
     Teacher explains: V = 22/7 × 7 × 7 × 20 = 154 × 20 = 3080 cm³. Now convert:
        1 cm³ = 1 mL          1000 cm³ = 1 L
        1 m³ = 1000 L          1 m³ = 1,000,000 cm³
        1 m² = 10,000 cm²      1 cm² = 100 mm²
     So 3080 cm³ = 3080 mL = 3.08 L. Glasses: 3080 ÷ 200 = 15.4, so 15 glasses
     can be FILLED (the 16th is less than half full). Why is
     1 m³ = 1,000,000 cm³ and not 100 cm³? A cube of edge 1 m = 100 cm has
     volume 100 × 100 × 100 cm³. Now you try: how many 250 mL glasses from the
     same jug? (12, since 3080 ÷ 250 = 12.32)

D11. A toy is a cone of height 4 cm standing on a hemisphere of radius 3 cm
     (same radius). Find its volume and its surface area in terms of π.
     Answer: V = 30π cm³; SA = 33π cm².
     Teacher explains: Volume: add the parts. Hemisphere 2/3 × π × 27 = 18π;
     cone 1/3 × π × 9 × 4 = 12π. Total 18π + 12π = 30π cm³.
     Surface: only what you can touch from outside — the curved part of the
     hemisphere 2π × 9 = 18π and the curved part of the cone. Slant height
     l = √(3² + 4²) = 5, CSA = π × 3 × 5 = 15π. Total 18π + 15π = 33π cm². The
     circle where they join is INSIDE, so it is not counted. Now you try: a
     capsule is a cylinder of radius 3 cm and length 10 cm with a hemisphere on
     each end. (V = 90π + 36π = 126π cm³, SA = 60π + 36π = 96π cm²)

D12. A cone, a sphere and a cylinder all have radius 3 cm, and the cone and
     cylinder have height 6 cm (the same as the sphere's diameter). Compare
     their volumes.
     Answer: Cone 18π, sphere 36π, cylinder 54π cm³: in the ratio 1 : 2 : 3.
     Teacher explains: Cone: 1/3 × π × 9 × 6 = 18π. Sphere: 4/3 × π × 27 = 36π.
     Cylinder: π × 9 × 6 = 54π. Divide by 18π: 1 : 2 : 3.
     Archimedes was so proud of finding that the sphere is 2/3 of the cylinder
     around it that he asked for a sphere in a cylinder to be carved on his
     tomb. It is true for every radius: with height 2r, cone 2/3 × πr³, sphere
     4/3 × πr³, cylinder 2πr³. Now you try: radius 6 cm, height 12 cm. (144π,
     288π, 432π cm³)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. Find the volume V and the total surface area TSA of the cuboid l × b × h or
    the cube of edge s (lengths in cm).
    a) 2 × 3 × 4       b) 5 × 4 × 3       c) 10 × 6 × 4
    d) 8 × 5 × 3       e) 12 × 10 × 5     f) 7 × 7 × 2

    p) cube, s = 5     q) cube, s = 8     r) cube, s = 10
    s) cube, s = 12    t) cube, s = 1.5

             d) V = 120, TSA = 158   e) V = 600, TSA = 460

             l) V = 12, TSA = 58   m) V = 1, TSA = 6   n) V = 8, TSA = 24
             o) V = 27, TSA = 54   p) V = 125, TSA = 150   q) V = 512, TSA = 384
             r) V = 1000, TSA = 600   s) V = 1728, TSA = 864

M2. In a) to j) the prism's cross-section is a right-angled triangle with legs a
    and b; the prism has length L. Find V and TSA (find the hypotenuse first).
    In k) to n) find V of the prism from its cross-section area A and length L.
    a) a = 3, b = 4, L = 10     b) a = 6, b = 8, L = 5
    c) a = 5, b = 12, L = 10    d) a = 8, b = 15, L = 4

    m) A = 6.5, L = 4           n) A = 40, L = 2.5
    Answers: a) V = 60, TSA = 132   b) V = 120, TSA = 168
             c) V = 300, TSA = 360   d) V = 240, TSA = 280   e) V = 6, TSA = 24

             h) V = 288, TSA = 336   i) V = 288, TSA = 336
             j) V = 2100, TSA = 1120   k) V = 120   l) V = 240   m) V = 26
             n) V = 100

M3. Find the volume of the cylinder, V = πr²h, with π = 22/7 (lengths in cm).
    a) r = 7, h = 10     b) r = 14, h = 5     c) r = 3.5, h = 4
    d) r = 7, h = 2      e) r = 21, h = 10    f) r = 0.7, h = 10

M8. Spheres. In a) to l) find SA = 4πr² and V = 4/3 × πr³ in terms of π. In m)
    to q) use π = 22/7.
    a) r = 1     b) r = 2     c) r = 3     d) r = 4
    e) r = 5     f) r = 6     g) r = 9     h) r = 10
