             g) SA = 324π, V = 972π   h) SA = 400π, V = 4000π/3
             i) SA = 576π, V = 2304π   j) SA = π, V = π/6   k) SA = 9π, V = 4.5π
             l) SA = 3600π, V = 36,000π   m) SA = 616, V = 1437 1/3
             n) SA = 2464, V = 11,498 2/3   o) SA = 5544, V = 38,808
             p) SA = 154, V = 179 2/3   q) SA = 22,176, V = 310,464

M9. Hemispheres. Find the curved surface area 2πr², the total surface area 3πr²
    (solid) and V = 2/3 × πr³, in terms of π.
    a) r = 1     b) r = 2     c) r = 3     d) r = 4
    e) r = 5     f) r = 6     g) r = 7     h) r = 9
    i) r = 10    j) r = 12    k) r = 15    l) r = 0.5
    Answers: a) CSA = 2π, TSA = 3π, V = 2π/3   b) CSA = 8π, TSA = 12π, V = 16π/3
             c) CSA = 18π, TSA = 27π, V = 18π
             d) CSA = 32π, TSA = 48π, V = 128π/3
             e) CSA = 50π, TSA = 75π, V = 250π/3
             f) CSA = 72π, TSA = 108π, V = 144π
             g) CSA = 98π, TSA = 147π, V = 686π/3
             h) CSA = 162π, TSA = 243π, V = 486π
             i) CSA = 200π, TSA = 300π, V = 2000π/3
             j) CSA = 288π, TSA = 432π, V = 1152π
             k) CSA = 450π, TSA = 675π, V = 2250π
             l) CSA = 0.5π, TSA = 0.75π, V = π/12

M10. Pyramids. In a) to k) the base is a square of side s and the height is h:
     find V, the slant height l of a face and TSA. In l) to n) find
     V = 1/3 × base area × h.
     a) s = 6, h = 4             b) s = 10, h = 12
     c) s = 16, h = 6            d) s = 8, h = 3
     e) s = 24, h = 5            f) s = 14, h = 24
     g) s = 30, h = 8            h) s = 12, h = 8
     i) s = 18, h = 12           j) s = 40, h = 15
     k) s = 42, h = 20           l) base area 36, h = 10
     m) base area 12, h = 5      n) rectangle 8 × 6, h = 9
     Answers: a) V = 48, l = 5, TSA = 96   b) V = 400, l = 13, TSA = 360
              c) V = 512, l = 10, TSA = 576   d) V = 64, l = 5, TSA = 144
              e) V = 960, l = 13, TSA = 1200   f) V = 1568, l = 25, TSA = 896
              g) V = 2400, l = 17, TSA = 1920   h) V = 384, l = 10, TSA = 384
              i) V = 1296, l = 15, TSA = 864   j) V = 8000, l = 25, TSA = 3600
              k) V = 11,760, l = 29, TSA = 4200   l) V = 120   m) V = 20
              n) V = 144

M11. Convert.
     a) 3 L = __ cm³          b) 2.5 L = __ cm³        c) 750 cm³ = __ L
     d) 4500 mL = __ L        e) 1 m³ = __ L           f) 2.4 m³ = __ L
     g) 500 L = __ m³         h) 1 m³ = __ cm³         i) 0.3 m³ = __ cm³
     j) 250,000 cm³ = __ m³   k) 1 cm³ = __ mL         l) 8.5 L = __ mL
     m) 1 m² = __ cm²         n) 3.5 m² = __ cm²       o) 25,000 cm² = __ m²
     p) 1 cm² = __ mm²        q) 1 cm³ = __ mm³        r) 65 L = __ cm³
     s) 0.08 m³ = __ L        t) 12,000 L = __ m³      u) 1.2 L = __ mL
     v) 90 mL = __ L          w) 3080 cm³ = __ L       x) 6160 L = __ m³
     Answers: a) 3000   b) 2500   c) 0.75   d) 4.5   e) 1000   f) 2400   g) 0.5
              h) 1,000,000   i) 300,000   j) 0.25   k) 1   l) 8500   m) 10,000
              n) 35,000   o) 2.5   p) 100   q) 1000   r) 65,000   s) 80   t) 12
              u) 1200   v) 0.09   w) 3.08   x) 6.16

M12. Work backwards: find the unknown length.
     a) cylinder, V = 1540, r = 7 (π = 22/7): h
     b) cone, V = 12π, r = 3: h
     c) sphere, V = 36π: r
     d) sphere, SA = 100π: r
     e) cube, V = 343: s
     f) cuboid, V = 120, l = 5, b = 4: h
     g) cylinder, CSA = 440, r = 7 (π = 22/7): h
     h) cone, CSA = 15π, r = 3: l
     i) hemisphere, V = 18π: r
     j) cube, TSA = 150: s
     k) sphere, V = 288π: r
     l) cylinder, V = 90π, h = 10: r
     m) square pyramid, V = 400, s = 10: h
     n) cone, V = 616, r = 7 (π = 22/7): h
     o) sphere, SA = 616 (π = 22/7): r
     p) cylinder, V = 3.08 L, r = 7 cm (π = 22/7): h in cm
     Answers: a) h = 10   b) h = 4   c) r = 3   d) r = 5   e) s = 7   f) h = 6
              g) h = 10   h) l = 5   i) r = 3   j) s = 5   k) r = 6   l) r = 3
              m) h = 12   n) h = 12   o) r = 7   p) h = 20

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: handle real objects (a tin, a funnel, a ball, a box) and unroll or
  unfold them to see the net; list the parts of the surface before calculating;
  keep one formula card for each solid; always write the unit (cm² for area, cm³
  for volume); with π = 22/7 cancel the 7s first; check the answer with a rough
  estimate using π ≈ 3. Harder: find how the volume changes when the radius of a
  cylinder is doubled (× 4) and when its height is doubled (× 2); find the
  volume of metal in a pipe of outer radius 5 cm, inner radius 4 cm and length
  70 cm (π(25 − 16) × 70 = 630π cm³); a sphere of radius 6 cm is melted and
  recast into a cylinder of radius 4 cm — find its height (288π ÷ 16π = 18 cm);
  find the volume of a frustum (a cone with its top cut off).


================================================================================
TOPIC 6.25 — TRANSFORMATIONS: TRANSLATION, ROTATION, REFLECTION, DILATION
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - A TRANSFORMATION moves every point of a shape (the OBJECT) to a new position
    (the IMAGE). Image points are named with a dash: A → A'.
  - TRANSLATION (slide) by a vector: the column vector with top a and bottom b
    (written [a, b] in these notes) means a right (left if negative) and b up
    (down if negative): (x, y) → (x + a, y + b).
  - REFLECTION in a mirror line: each image point is the same distance behind
    the mirror, on the perpendicular. Rules: x-axis (x, y) → (x, −y); y-axis
    (x, y) → (−x, y); line y = x (x, y) → (y, x); line y = −x (x, y) → (−y, −x);
    line x = k (x, y) → (2k − x, y); line y = k (x, y) → (x, 2k − y). Points on
    the mirror do not move.
  - ROTATION about a centre through an angle (anticlockwise unless we say
    clockwise). About the origin O: 90° anticlockwise (x, y) → (−y, x); 180°
    (x, y) → (−x, −y); 90° clockwise = 270° anticlockwise (x, y) → (y, −x). For
    another centre, move the centre to O, rotate, and move back.
  - DILATION (enlargement) with centre O and scale factor k: (x, y) → (kx, ky).
    k > 1 enlarges, 0 < k < 1 reduces; every length is multiplied by k, every
    area by k², and angles do not change.
  - To DESCRIBE a single transformation fully: translation — the vector;
    reflection — the mirror line; rotation — centre, angle and direction;
    dilation — centre and scale factor.
  - Translations, reflections and rotations keep size and shape (the image is
    CONGRUENT); a reflection reverses the order of the labels (clockwise becomes
    anticlockwise). A dilation keeps shape but not size (the image is SIMILAR).

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. Triangle A(1, 2), B(4, 2), C(1, 5) is translated by the vector [5, −3] (5
    right, 3 down). Find the image.
    Answer: A'(6, −1), B'(9, −1), C'(6, 2).
    Teacher explains: A translation is a slide: every point moves the SAME way
    by the SAME amount. In a textbook the vector is written as a column:
          (  5 )     top: x-step (+ right, − left)
          ( −3 )     bottom: y-step (+ up, − down)
    Add the top number to every x and the bottom number to every y:
    A(1, 2) → (1 + 5, 2 − 3) = (6, −1); B(4, 2) → (9, −1); C(1, 5) → (6, 2). The
    image has the same size, shape and direction, so a translation is described
    completely by its vector.
    Common mistake: adding the vector to x only, or mixing up the order (top is
    ALWAYS the x-step). Now you try: P(−2, 4) translated by [3, −6]. ((1, −2))

D2. The point P(−2, 3) is moved by a translation to P'(4, −1). What is the
    vector? Which vector moves P' back to P?
    Answer: [6, −4]; back: [−6, 4].
    Teacher explains: Vector = image − object, done separately for x and y:
    x-step 4 − (−2) = 6, y-step −1 − 3 = −4. So the vector is [6, −4]: 6 right,
    4 down. Check: (−2 + 6, 3 − 4) = (4, −1).
    To undo a translation, go the opposite way: change both signs, [−6, 4].
    Common mistake: object − image, which gives the reverse vector. Now you try:
    (5, 1) is translated to (2, 7). ([−3, 6])

D3. Reflect triangle A(2, 1), B(5, 1), C(5, 3) in the x-axis, and then
    (separately) in the y-axis.
    Answer: x-axis: (2, −1), (5, −1), (5, −3). y-axis: (−2, 1), (−5, 1),
    (−5, 3).
    Teacher explains:
              y
              |        + C(5, 3)
              |   A +--+ B
         -----+---------------- x     mirror: the x-axis
              |   A'+--+ B'
              |        + C'(5, −3)
    In the x-axis the mirror is horizontal: x stays the same and y changes sign,
    (x, y) → (x, −y). A point 3 above the axis goes to 3 below it. In the y-axis
    the mirror is vertical: (x, y) → (−x, y). A point ON the mirror, such as
    (4, 0) in the x-axis, does not move.
    The image is congruent but "flipped": going A → B → C was anticlockwise, and
    A' → B' → C' is clockwise. Common mistake: changing the wrong coordinate —
    remember "x-axis: change y". Now you try: reflect (−3, −7) in the y-axis.
    ((3, −7))

D4. Reflect the point (3, 1) in the line y = x and in the line y = −x.
    Answer: In y = x: (1, 3). In y = −x: (−1, −3).
    Teacher explains: The line y = x goes through (0, 0), (1, 1), (2, 2)... at
    45°. Reflecting in it SWAPS the coordinates: (x, y) → (y, x). Fold squared
    paper along y = x and the point (3, 1) lands exactly on (1, 3).
    The line y = −x goes through (1, −1), (2, −2)... Reflecting in it swaps AND
    changes both signs: (x, y) → (−y, −x). So (3, 1) → (−1, −3). Check: the
    midpoint of (3, 1) and (−1, −3) is (1, −1), which is on y = −x, as it must
    be.
    Common mistake: only swapping for y = −x. Now you try: reflect (−2, 5) in
    y = −x. ((−5, 2))

D5. Reflect the point (5, 1) in the line x = 2, and the point (3, 4) in the line
    y = −1.
    Answer: (−1, 1) and (3, −6).
    Teacher explains: x = 2 is a VERTICAL line through (2, 0). So y stays the
    same. The point is 5 − 2 = 3 units to the right of the mirror, so the image
    is 3 units to the left: x = 2 − 3 = −1. The image is (−1, 1). Formula: new
    x = 2k − x = 4 − 5 = −1.
    y = −1 is a HORIZONTAL line. Now x stays the same: the point is 4 − (−1) = 5
    units above the mirror, so the image is 5 below it: y = −1 − 5 = −6.
    Formula: new y = 2k − y = −2 − 4 = −6.
    Common mistake: x = 2 "looks like" it is about x, so pupils change y. Draw
    the line first! Now you try: reflect (−3, 4) in y = 1. ((−3, −2))

D6. Rotate the point (3, 1) through 90° anticlockwise about the origin. Then
    rotate triangle (1, 1), (4, 1), (4, 3) in the same way.
    Answer: (3, 1) → (−1, 3). Triangle: (−1, 1), (−1, 4), (−3, 4).
    Teacher explains: Use tracing paper: trace the axes and the point, put a
    pencil point on O, turn the paper a quarter turn anticlockwise (the traced
    x-axis now lies on the y-axis) and read where the point has gone: (−1, 3).
    The rule: (x, y) → (−y, x). Swap the coordinates and change the sign of the
    NEW first one. For the triangle: (1, 1) → (−1, 1), (4, 1) → (−1, 4),
    (4, 3) → (−3, 4). The distance from O stays the same, and the line from O to
    the point turns through 90°.
    Common mistake: using (y, −x), which is the CLOCKWISE rule. Now you try:
    rotate (2, 5) through 90° anticlockwise about O. ((−5, 2))

D7. Rotate (3, 1) about the origin through 180°, and through 90° clockwise. What
    is a rotation of 270° anticlockwise?
    Answer: 180°: (−3, −1). 90° clockwise: (1, −3). 270° anticlockwise is the
    same as 90° clockwise.
    Teacher explains:
       rotation about O               rule
       90° anticlockwise              (x, y) → (−y, x)
       180° (either direction)        (x, y) → (−x, −y)
       90° clockwise = 270° anti.     (x, y) → (y, −x)
    A half turn changes both signs; the image is directly opposite the point
    through O. Three quarter turns anticlockwise end in the same place as one
    quarter turn clockwise, because 270° + 90° = 360°.
    Check a rule with an easy point: (1, 0) rotated 90° clockwise must go to
    (0, −1); the rule (y, −x) gives (0, −1). Now you try: rotate (−4, 2) through
    90° clockwise about O. ((2, 4))

D8. Rotate P(5, 2) through 90° anticlockwise about the centre C(2, 1).
    Answer: P' = (1, 4).
    Teacher explains: The rules work only about the origin, so MOVE the centre
    to the origin first:
       step 1  P relative to C:  (5 − 2, 2 − 1) = (3, 1)
       step 2  rotate 90° anti:  (3, 1) → (−1, 3)
       step 3  move back:        (−1 + 2, 3 + 1) = (1, 4)
    Check: CP and CP' have the same length (both √10), and the step from C to P,
    (3, 1), has turned into (−1, 3), a right-angle turn. Tracing paper with the
    pencil on C gives the same answer. Now you try: rotate (4, 3) through 180°
    about (1, 1). ((−2, −1))

D9. Dilate triangle A(2, 1), B(4, 1), C(2, 4) with centre O and scale factor 3.
    Compare lengths, angles and area. What happens with scale factor 1/2 to the
    point (8, 6)?
    Answer: A'(6, 3), B'(12, 3), C'(6, 12). Lengths × 3, angles the same,
    area × 9. (8, 6) → (4, 3).
    Teacher explains: Multiply both coordinates by k: (x, y) → (3x, 3y). Each
    image point is on the line from O through the object point, 3 times as far
    from O.
    AB = 4 − 2 = 2 and A'B' = 12 − 6 = 6 (× 3); AC = 3 and A'C' = 9 (× 3). The
    angle at A is still 90°. Area: 1/2 × 2 × 3 = 3 becomes 1/2 × 6 × 9 = 27,
    which is 9 = 3² times as big. With k = 1/2 the image is SMALLER:
    (8, 6) → (4, 3). A scale factor between 0 and 1 still counts as a dilation
    ("enlargement").
    Now you try: dilate (6, −4) with centre O and scale factor 1/2. ((3, −2))

D10. A(2, 3) → A'(6, 9) and B(4, 1) → B'(12, 3) under a dilation. Find the scale
     factor and the centre.
     Answer: Scale factor 3, centre O(0, 0).
     Teacher explains: Every coordinate has been multiplied by 3: 2 × 3 = 6,
     3 × 3 = 9, 4 × 3 = 12, 1 × 3 = 3. That is the rule (x, y) → (3x, 3y), a
     dilation with centre O and k = 3.
     Check with lengths: AB² = 2² + 2² = 8 and A'B'² = 6² + 6² = 72 = 9 × 8, so
     A'B' = 3 × AB. To find a centre in general, join A to A' and B to B' and
     extend the lines: they cross at the centre. Here both lines pass through O.
     Now you try: (4, 8) → (1, 2) and (6, −2) → (1.5, −0.5). Find k. (k = 1/4)

D11. Triangle P(1, 2), Q(3, 2), R(3, 5) is mapped to P'(−2, 1), Q'(−2, 3),
     R'(−5, 3). Describe the single transformation fully.
     Answer: A rotation of 90° anticlockwise about the origin.
     Teacher explains: Step 1: same size? PQ = 2 and P'Q' = 2, QR = 3 and
     Q'R' = 3, so it is not a dilation. Step 2: test the rules on P(1, 2):
     (x, −y) gives (1, −2), (y, x) gives (2, 1), (−y, x) gives (−2, 1). The
     rotation rule fits. Step 3: test it on Q and R too: (3, 2) → (−2, 3) and
     (3, 5) → (−5, 3). All fit.
     A full description needs the centre (the origin), the angle (90°) and the
     direction (anticlockwise). "It turned" is not enough. Now you try: (1, 2),
     (4, 2), (4, 6) → (2, 1), (2, 4), (6, 4). (reflection in the line y = x)

D12. Which transformations keep the size of a shape? Which keep its shape? Which
     reverse the order of the labels?
     Answer: Translations, reflections and rotations keep size and shape
     (congruent images). Dilations keep shape only (similar images). Only
     reflections reverse the order of the labels.
     Teacher explains:
                       lengths   angles   area    label order
        translation     same      same     same    same
        reflection      same      same     same    reversed
        rotation        same      same     same    same
        dilation (k)    × k       same     × k²    same
     Translations, reflections and rotations are called ISOMETRIES ("same
     measure"). A shape and its image under an isometry are congruent. Under a
     dilation the image is similar (topic 6.26). A reflection turns a left hand
     into a right hand — you cannot slide or turn a left glove to make a right
     glove. Now you try: a triangle of area 5 cm² is dilated with k = 2. What is
     the new area? (20 cm²)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. Translate the point by the vector [a, b] (a = x-step, b = y-step).
    a) (3, 4) by [2, 5]      b) (1, 1) by [−1, 3]     c) (0, 0) by [4, −2]
    d) (−3, 2) by [5, 0]     e) (6, −1) by [−6, 1]    f) (−4, −5) by [3, 7]
    g) (2, 7) by [0, −9]     h) (−1, 0) by [−4, −4]   i) (5, 5) by [−5, −5]
    j) (8, −3) by [−2, 6]    k) (−6, 4) by [10, −1]   l) (0, −7) by [−3, 7]
    m) (3, −3) by [3, −3]    n) (−2, −8) by [−5, 2]   o) (7, 0) by [0, 7]
    p) (1, −6) by [−8, 4]    q) (−9, 3) by [4, −10]   r) (4, 4) by [−1, −9]
    s) (−5, −1) by [5, 1]    t) (2, 3) by [−2, −3]
    Answers: a) (5, 9)   b) (0, 4)   c) (4, −2)   d) (2, 2)   e) (0, 0)
             f) (−1, 2)   g) (2, −2)   h) (−5, −4)   i) (0, 0)   j) (6, 3)
             k) (4, 3)   l) (−3, 0)   m) (6, −6)   n) (−7, −6)   o) (7, 7)
             p) (−7, −2)   q) (−5, −7)   r) (3, −5)   s) (0, 0)   t) (0, 0)

M2. Find the vector of the translation that maps the first point onto the
    second, and the vector that maps it back.
    a) (1, 2) → (4, 6)       b) (0, 0) → (−3, 5)      c) (3, −1) → (3, 4)
    d) (−2, −2) → (5, −7)    e) (6, 3) → (1, 3)       f) (−4, 5) → (0, 0)
    g) (2, 8) → (−6, 1)      h) (−1, −3) → (−7, −3)   i) (5, 0) → (0, 5)
    j) (−3, 4) → (4, −3)     k) (7, 7) → (7, 7)       l) (0, −5) → (−9, 2)
    m) (10, −2) → (1, 6)     n) (−8, 1) → (−2, −4)    o) (4, −6) → (−4, 6)
    p) (1, 1) → (−10, 11)
    Answers: a) [3, 4], back [−3, −4]   b) [−3, 5], back [3, −5]
             c) [0, 5], back [0, −5]   d) [7, −5], back [−7, 5]
             e) [−5, 0], back [5, 0]   f) [4, −5], back [−4, 5]
             g) [−8, −7], back [8, 7]   h) [−6, 0], back [6, 0]
             i) [−5, 5], back [5, −5]   j) [7, −7], back [−7, 7]
             k) [0, 0], back [0, 0]   l) [−9, 7], back [9, −7]
             m) [−9, 8], back [9, −8]   n) [6, −5], back [−6, 5]
             o) [−8, 12], back [8, −12]   p) [−11, 10], back [11, −10]

M3. Reflect the point in the given axis.
    a) (3, 5) in x-axis     b) (3, 5) in y-axis     c) (−2, 4) in x-axis
    d) (−2, 4) in y-axis    e) (6, −1) in x-axis    f) (6, −1) in y-axis
    g) (−7, −3) in x-axis   h) (−7, −3) in y-axis   i) (4, 0) in x-axis
    j) (4, 0) in y-axis     k) (0, −6) in x-axis    l) (0, −6) in y-axis
    m) (1, 1) in x-axis     n) (−5, 2) in y-axis    o) (8, −8) in x-axis
    p) (−9, −1) in y-axis   q) (0, 0) in x-axis     r) (2, −10) in y-axis
    s) (−3, 7) in x-axis    t) (11, 4) in y-axis
    Answers: a) (3, −5)   b) (−3, 5)   c) (−2, −4)   d) (2, 4)   e) (6, 1)
             f) (−6, −1)   g) (−7, 3)   h) (7, −3)   i) (4, 0)   j) (−4, 0)
             k) (0, 6)   l) (0, −6)   m) (1, −1)   n) (5, 2)   o) (8, 8)
             p) (9, −1)   q) (0, 0)   r) (−2, −10)   s) (−3, −7)   t) (−11, 4)

M4. Reflect the point in the line y = x or y = −x.
    a) (3, 1) in y = x      b) (3, 1) in y = −x     c) (−2, 5) in y = x
    d) (−2, 5) in y = −x    e) (4, −6) in y = x     f) (4, −6) in y = −x
    g) (−1, −7) in y = x    h) (−1, −7) in y = −x   i) (5, 5) in y = x
    j) (5, 5) in y = −x     k) (2, −2) in y = x     l) (2, −2) in y = −x
    m) (0, 3) in y = x      n) (0, 3) in y = −x     o) (−8, 0) in y = x
    p) (−8, 0) in y = −x    q) (6, 9) in y = x      r) (−4, −4) in y = −x
    s) (7, −3) in y = −x    t) (−9, 2) in y = x
    Answers: a) (1, 3)   b) (−1, −3)   c) (5, −2)   d) (−5, 2)   e) (−6, 4)
             f) (6, −4)   g) (−7, −1)   h) (7, 1)   i) (5, 5)   j) (−5, −5)
             k) (−2, 2)   l) (2, −2)   m) (3, 0)   n) (−3, 0)   o) (0, −8)
             p) (0, 8)   q) (9, 6)   r) (4, 4)   s) (3, −7)   t) (2, −9)

M5. Reflect the point in the line x = k (vertical) or y = k (horizontal).
    a) (5, 1) in x = 2     b) (3, 4) in y = −1    c) (0, 0) in x = 3
    d) (0, 0) in y = 3     e) (−2, 5) in x = 1    f) (6, −3) in y = 2
    g) (4, 7) in x = 4     h) (1, 1) in y = 1     i) (−3, 2) in x = −1
    j) (5, −6) in y = −2   k) (7, 3) in x = −2    l) (−4, −4) in y = 0
    m) (2, 9) in x = 5     n) (8, 1) in y = 4     o) (−6, 0) in x = −3
    p) (3, −5) in y = −5   q) (1, 2) in x = 0     r) (0, 6) in y = −3
    s) (10, 10) in x = 6   t) (−7, 2) in y = 5
    Answers: a) (−1, 1)   b) (3, −6)   c) (6, 0)   d) (0, 6)   e) (4, 5)
             f) (6, 7)   g) (4, 7)   h) (1, 1)   i) (1, 2)   j) (5, 2)
             k) (−11, 3)   l) (−4, 4)   m) (8, 9)   n) (8, 7)   o) (0, 0)
             p) (3, −5)   q) (−1, 2)   r) (0, −12)   s) (2, 10)   t) (−7, 8)

M6. Rotate the point through 90° anticlockwise about the origin,
    (x, y) → (−y, x).
    a) (3, 1)     b) (2, 5)     c) (4, 0)     d) (0, 3)
    e) (−2, 3)    f) (−5, −1)   g) (6, −2)    h) (−3, −4)
    i) (1, 1)     j) (−1, 1)    k) (7, 2)     l) (0, −6)
    m) (−8, 0)    n) (2, −9)    o) (−4, 5)    p) (5, 5)
    Answers: a) (−1, 3)   b) (−5, 2)   c) (0, 4)   d) (−3, 0)   e) (−3, −2)
             f) (1, −5)   g) (2, 6)   h) (4, −3)   i) (−1, 1)   j) (−1, −1)
             k) (−2, 7)   l) (6, 0)   m) (0, −8)   n) (9, 2)   o) (−5, −4)
             p) (−5, 5)

M7. Rotate the point about the origin as stated.
    a) (3, 1), 180°                  b) (3, 1), 90° clockwise
    c) (−4, 2), 90° clockwise        d) (−4, 2), 180°
    e) (5, −2), 180°                 f) (5, −2), 90° clockwise
    g) (0, 4), 180°                  h) (0, 4), 90° clockwise
    i) (−6, −3), 180°                j) (−6, −3), 90° clockwise
    k) (2, 2), 90° clockwise         l) (1, −7), 180°
    m) (8, 0), 90° clockwise         n) (−5, 5), 180°
    o) (3, 1), 270° anticlockwise    p) (−2, 6), 270° anticlockwise
    q) (4, −3), 90° clockwise        r) (−1, −8), 90° clockwise
    s) (9, 4), 180°                  t) (0, −2), 270° anticlockwise
    Answers: a) (−3, −1)   b) (1, −3)   c) (2, 4)   d) (4, −2)   e) (−5, 2)
             f) (−2, −5)   g) (0, −4)   h) (4, 0)   i) (6, 3)   j) (−3, 6)
             k) (2, −2)   l) (−1, 7)   m) (0, −8)   n) (5, −5)   o) (1, −3)
             p) (6, 2)   q) (−3, −4)   r) (−8, 1)   s) (−9, −4)   t) (−2, 0)

M8. Rotate the point about the given centre.
    a) (5, 2), 90° anticlockwise about (2, 1)
    b) (4, 3), 180° about (1, 1)
    c) (3, 0), 90° clockwise about (1, 1)
    d) (0, 0), 90° anticlockwise about (2, 2)
    e) (6, 4), 180° about (3, 2)
    f) (2, 5), 90° clockwise about (0, 3)
    g) (−1, 2), 90° anticlockwise about (1, −1)
    h) (7, −2), 180° about (4, 0)
    i) (3, 3), 90° clockwise about (5, 1)
    j) (−2, −3), 90° anticlockwise about (−1, −1)
    k) (1, 6), 180° about (−2, 3)
    l) (4, 1), 90° clockwise about (4, −2)
    Answers: a) (1, 4)   b) (−2, −1)   c) (0, −1)   d) (4, 0)   e) (0, 0)
             f) (2, 1)   g) (−2, −3)   h) (1, 2)   i) (7, 3)   j) (1, −2)
             k) (−5, 0)   l) (7, −2)

M9. Dilate the point with centre O and scale factor k: (x, y) → (kx, ky).
    a) (3, 4), k = 2        b) (−2, 5), k = 3       c) (6, −8), k = 1/2
    d) (9, 3), k = 1/3      e) (4, −2), k = 1.5     f) (1, 1), k = 4
    g) (−8, 12), k = 1/4    h) (0, 5), k = 5        i) (−3, −6), k = 2/3
    j) (10, 0), k = 0.1     k) (2, −7), k = 2       l) (−4, −4), k = 1/2
    m) (6, 9), k = 1/3      n) (5, −3), k = 3       o) (−10, 20), k = 1/5
    p) (7, 2), k = 1        q) (2, 3), k = 2.5      r) (−6, 4), k = 1.5
    s) (12, −8), k = 3/4    t) (0, 0), k = 7
    Answers: a) (6, 8)   b) (−6, 15)   c) (3, −4)   d) (3, 1)   e) (6, −3)
             f) (4, 4)   g) (−2, 3)   h) (0, 25)   i) (−2, −4)   j) (1, 0)
             k) (4, −14)   l) (−2, −2)   m) (2, 3)   n) (15, −9)   o) (−2, 4)
             p) (7, 2)   q) (5, 7.5)   r) (−9, 6)   s) (9, −6)   t) (0, 0)

M10. Triangle A(1, 1), B(4, 1), C(1, 3). Write the coordinates of A', B', C'
     under each transformation.
     a) translation by [2, 3]
     b) translation by [−5, −1]
     c) reflection in the x-axis
     d) reflection in the y-axis
     e) reflection in y = x
     f) reflection in y = −x
     g) reflection in x = 5
     h) reflection in y = −1
     i) rotation 90° anticlockwise about O
     j) rotation 180° about O
     k) rotation 90° clockwise about O
     l) dilation, centre O, k = 2
     Answers: a) (3, 4), (6, 4), (3, 6)   b) (−4, 0), (−1, 0), (−4, 2)
              c) (1, −1), (4, −1), (1, −3)   d) (−1, 1), (−4, 1), (−1, 3)
              e) (1, 1), (1, 4), (3, 1)   f) (−1, −1), (−1, −4), (−3, −1)
              g) (9, 1), (6, 1), (9, 3)   h) (1, −3), (4, −3), (1, −5)
              i) (−1, 1), (−1, 4), (−3, 1)   j) (−1, −1), (−4, −1), (−1, −3)
              k) (1, −1), (1, −4), (3, −1)   l) (2, 2), (8, 2), (2, 6)

M11. Describe fully the single transformation that maps the first triangle onto
     the second.
     a) (1, 2), (3, 2), (3, 5) → (−2, 1), (−2, 3), (−5, 3)
     b) (1, 2), (4, 2), (4, 6) → (2, 1), (2, 4), (6, 4)
     c) (2, 1), (5, 1), (5, 3) → (2, −1), (5, −1), (5, −3)
     d) (2, 1), (5, 1), (5, 3) → (−2, 3), (1, 3), (1, 5)
     e) (1, 1), (3, 1), (1, 4) → (3, 3), (9, 3), (3, 12)
     f) (2, 3), (4, 3), (4, 6) → (−2, −3), (−4, −3), (−4, −6)
     g) (−3, 1), (−1, 1), (−1, 4) → (3, 1), (1, 1), (1, 4)
     h) (1, 2), (2, 5), (4, 1) → (2, −1), (5, −2), (1, −4)
     i) (1, 1), (2, 3), (4, 2) → (9, 1), (8, 3), (6, 2)
     j) (4, 8), (8, 4), (8, 8) → (1, 2), (2, 1), (2, 2)
     k) (2, 1), (3, 4), (5, 2) → (−1, −2), (−4, −3), (−2, −5)
     l) (0, 1), (2, 2), (1, 4) → (0, −5), (2, −6), (1, −8)
     Answers: a) rotation 90° anticlockwise about O   b) reflection in y = x
              c) reflection in the x-axis   d) translation by [−4, 2]
              e) dilation, centre O, k = 3   f) rotation 180° about O
              g) reflection in the y-axis   h) rotation 90° clockwise about O
              i) reflection in x = 5   j) dilation, centre O, k = 1/4
              k) reflection in y = −x   l) reflection in y = −2

M12. In a) to l) apply the first transformation, then the second, and give the
     final point. In m) to r) name the single transformation equal to "first,
     then second".
     a) (3, 1): reflection in the x-axis, then reflection in the y-axis
     b) (3, 1): reflection in the y-axis, then reflection in the x-axis
     c) (2, 5): rotation 90° anticlockwise about O, then rotation 90°
        anticlockwise about O
     d) (4, −1): reflection in y = x, then reflection in the x-axis
     e) (−2, 3): translation by [2, 3], then translation by [−5, 1]
     f) (1, 4): rotation 90° anticlockwise about O, then reflection in the
        x-axis
     g) (5, 2): reflection in x = 1, then reflection in x = 4
     h) (−3, −2): dilation, centre O, k = 2, then translation by [1, 1]
     i) (6, 0): rotation 90° clockwise about O, then reflection in y = x
     j) (2, −4): dilation, centre O, k = 3, then dilation, centre O, k = 1/2
     k) (0, 7): reflection in y = −x, then reflection in y = x
     l) (−1, 6): reflection in y = 2, then reflection in y = −1
     m) reflection in the x-axis, then reflection in the y-axis
     n) rotation 90° anticlockwise about O, then rotation 90° anticlockwise
        about O
     o) reflection in y = x, then reflection in the x-axis
     p) translation by [2, 3], then translation by [−5, 1]
     q) reflection in x = 1, then reflection in x = 4
     r) dilation, centre O, k = 3, then dilation, centre O, k = 1/2
     Answers: a) (−3, −1)   b) (−3, −1)   c) (−2, −5)   d) (−1, −4)   e) (−5, 7)
              f) (−4, −1)   g) (11, 2)   h) (−5, −3)   i) (−6, 0)   j) (3, −6)
              k) (0, −7)   l) (−1, 0)   m) rotation 180° about O
              n) rotation 180° about O   o) rotation 90° clockwise about O
              p) translation by [−3, 4]   q) translation by [6, 0]
              r) dilation, centre O, k = 1.5

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: use squared paper and tracing paper for every question; say the
  rule aloud before using it ("x-axis: change y"); draw the mirror line in
  colour before reflecting; for rotations turn the book itself; check every
  answer by plotting the object and the image; begin with points in the first
  quadrant, then use negatives. Harder: find the single transformation equal to
  two reflections in parallel lines (a translation by twice the gap) and in
  perpendicular lines (a half turn about the crossing point); find the centre of
  a rotation from a shape and its image using perpendicular bisectors; dilate
  with a negative scale factor (k = −2 sends (1, 3) to (−2, −6)); design a
  wallpaper pattern using translations and reflections.


================================================================================
TOPIC 6.26 — SIMILARITY AND SCALE DRAWINGS
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - Two figures are SIMILAR (symbol ~) if one is an exact enlargement or
    reduction of the other: all corresponding ANGLES are equal AND all
    corresponding SIDES are in the same ratio. Congruent figures are similar
    with ratio 1.
  - SCALE FACTOR k = image length ÷ object length (the same for every pair of
    corresponding sides). k > 1 enlarges, k < 1 reduces. Always match sides by
    their angles, not by their position on the page.
  - For TRIANGLES one condition is enough. AA: two pairs of equal angles (the
    third pair is then equal too). SSS: all three pairs of sides in the same
    ratio. SAS: two pairs of sides in the same ratio and the angles between them
    equal. For other polygons both angles and sides must be checked.
  - Missing sides: find k from a known pair, then multiply (or divide) the other
    side by k; or write a proportion x/6 = 10/4 and cross-multiply. A line
    parallel to one side of a triangle cuts off a small triangle similar to the
    whole one.
  - Shadows at the same time of day: sun rays are parallel, so objects and their
    shadows make similar right-angled triangles: height ÷ shadow is the same for
    all.
  - SCALE DRAWINGS and MAPS: a scale 1 : n means 1 cm on the drawing represents
    n cm in real life. Real length = drawing × n; drawing = real ÷ n; convert
    units last (100 cm = 1 m, 100,000 cm = 1 km, so 1 : 100,000 — one lakh —
    means 1 cm represents 1 km).
  - If lengths are multiplied by k, AREAS are multiplied by k² and VOLUMES by
    k³.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. A photo 4 cm by 6 cm is enlarged to 10 cm by 15 cm. Is the enlargement
    similar to the original? What about a print 8 cm by 10 cm?
    Answer: 10 × 15: yes, k = 2.5 for both sides. 8 × 10: no — 8 ÷ 4 = 2 but
    10 ÷ 6 ≈ 1.67, so faces would look stretched.
    Teacher explains: Divide corresponding sides, short with short and long with
    long: 10 ÷ 4 = 2.5 and 15 ÷ 6 = 2.5. The same ratio, so every length is
    multiplied by the same scale factor k = 2.5, and all the angles are still
    90°. The rectangles are similar.
    For 8 × 10: 8 ÷ 4 = 2 but 10 ÷ 6 = 1 2/3. Different ratios, so the shape has
    changed: NOT similar. A quick check: the long side ÷ the short side must be
    the same: 6 ÷ 4 = 1.5 = 15 ÷ 10, but 10 ÷ 8 = 1.25. Now you try: is a 6 × 9
    rectangle similar to a 4 × 6 rectangle? (yes, k = 1.5)

D2. A square of side 3 cm and a rhombus of side 5 cm with angles 60° and 120°
    have their sides in the same ratio. Are they similar? A 2 × 6 rectangle and
    a 4 × 4 square have equal angles. Are they similar?
    Answer: Neither pair is similar. The first pair has proportional sides but
    different angles; the second has equal angles but sides not in proportion.
    Teacher explains: For polygons with 4 or more sides, BOTH conditions are
    needed: equal angles AND sides in the same ratio. The square and the rhombus
    fail on angles (90° against 60° and 120°): the rhombus is a "pushed over"
    square. The rectangle and the square fail on sides: 4 ÷ 2 = 2 but
    4 ÷ 6 = 2/3.
    Triangles are special: if the angles are equal, the sides are automatically
    in proportion, and the other way round. That is why AA or SSS alone is
    enough for triangles. Now you try: are all squares similar? Are all
    rectangles similar? (all squares yes; rectangles no)

D3. One triangle has angles 50° and 60°. Another has angles 60° and 70°. Are
    they similar?
    Answer: Yes. The third angles are 70° and 50°, so both triangles have angles
    50°, 60°, 70° (AA).
    Teacher explains: Find the third angle of each: 180° − (50° + 60°) = 70° and
    180° − (60° + 70°) = 50°. Both triangles have exactly the angles 50°, 60°
    and 70°, so they are similar. Two pairs of equal angles are always enough
    (AA), because the third angles must then be equal too.
    When you name them, keep the order of equal angles: if ∠A = ∠P, ∠B = ∠Q,
    ∠C = ∠R, write ΔABC ~ ΔPQR. Then AB matches PQ, BC matches QR and CA matches
    RP. Now you try: angles 40° and 85° in one triangle, 55° and 85° in another.
    (similar: both are 40°, 55°, 85°)

D4. ΔABC ~ ΔPQR with AB = 6 cm, BC = 8 cm, AC = 9 cm and PQ = 9 cm. Find QR and
    PR.
    Answer: k = 1.5; QR = 12 cm, PR = 13.5 cm.
    Teacher explains:
       k = PQ ÷ AB = 9 ÷ 6 = 1.5
       QR = BC × k = 8 × 1.5 = 12 cm
       PR = AC × k = 9 × 1.5 = 13.5 cm
    Step 1: find a pair of corresponding sides where BOTH lengths are known (AB
    and PQ). Step 2: scale factor = new ÷ old. Step 3: multiply every other side
    by k. Check the ratios: 12 ÷ 8 = 1.5 and 13.5 ÷ 9 = 1.5.
    The same with a proportion: QR/8 = 9/6, so QR = 8 × 9 ÷ 6 = 12. Common
    mistake: adding (9 − 6 = 3, so QR = 8 + 3 = 11). Similar figures MULTIPLY,
    they do not add. Now you try: PQ = 12 cm. (QR = 16 cm, PR = 18 cm)

D5. In ΔXYZ, XY = 15 cm, YZ = 12 cm and XZ = 9 cm. ΔLMN is similar to it with
    ∠L = ∠X, ∠M = ∠Y, ∠N = ∠Z, and LM = 10 cm. Find MN and LN.
    Answer: k = 2/3; MN = 8 cm, LN = 6 cm.
    Teacher explains: The equal angles tell us the matching: L ↔ X, M ↔ Y,
    N ↔ Z, so LM matches XY, MN matches YZ and LN matches XZ. Scale factor
    k = 10/15 = 2/3 (a reduction, because LMN is smaller).
    MN = 12 × 2/3 = 8 cm and LN = 9 × 2/3 = 6 cm. Check: 8 ÷ 12 = 2/3 and
    6 ÷ 9 = 2/3.
    Common mistake: matching sides by where they are drawn. Figures may be
    turned or flipped; always match through the angles. Now you try: LM = 5 cm.
    (MN = 4 cm, LN = 3 cm)

D6. In ΔABC, D is on AB and E is on AC with DE ∥ BC. AD = 4 cm, DB = 6 cm and
    DE = 5 cm. Find BC.
                  A
                 / \
              4 /   \
               D-----E      DE = 5
            6 /       \
             B---------C    BC = ?
    Answer: BC = 12.5 cm.
    Teacher explains: DE ∥ BC, so ∠ADE = ∠ABC (corresponding angles) and ∠A is
    shared. By AA, ΔADE ~ ΔABC. Match the WHOLE side AB with AD:
    AB = 4 + 6 = 10 cm, so k = 10 ÷ 4 = 2.5 and BC = 5 × 2.5 = 12.5 cm.
    Common mistake: using DB instead of AB (6 ÷ 4 = 1.5 gives 7.5 cm, wrong).
    The small triangle is ADE and the big one is ABC — their sides are AD and
    AB, not AD and DB. Now you try: AD = 3 cm, DB = 9 cm, DE = 4 cm.
    (BC = 16 cm)

D7. At 4 p.m. a 2 m pole casts a shadow 3 m long. At the same moment a tree
    casts a shadow 18 m long. How tall is the tree?
    Answer: 12 m.
    Teacher explains: The sun's rays are parallel, so the rays make the same
    angle with the ground everywhere. Pole + shadow and tree + shadow form two
    right-angled triangles with equal angles: similar (AA).
       height ÷ shadow is the same:
       h ÷ 18 = 2 ÷ 3
            h = 18 × 2 ÷ 3 = 12 m
    Or: the tree's shadow is 18 ÷ 3 = 6 times as long, so the tree is 6 times as
    tall: 2 × 6 = 12 m. This is how Thales measured the height of a pyramid. Now
    you try: a girl 1.5 m tall has a shadow 2 m long; a building's shadow is
    40 m. How tall is the building? (30 m)

D8. To find the width AB of a pond, Arjun puts pegs so that AE and BD are
    straight lines crossing at C, with DE ∥ AB. He measures AC = 12 m, CE = 4 m
    and DE = 3 m. How wide is the pond?
         A ~~~~~~~ B      pond
           \       /
             \   /
               C
             /   \
           /       \
         D --------- E
    Answer: AB = 9 m.
    Teacher explains: The two triangles ABC and EDC make a "bow tie". The angles
    at C are vertically opposite, hence equal, and ∠BAC = ∠DEC (alternate
    angles, AB ∥ DE). By AA, ΔABC ~ ΔEDC, with A ↔ E, B ↔ D.
    So AB/ED = AC/EC: AB = 3 × 12 ÷ 4 = 9 m. The big triangle is 3 times the
    small one (12 ÷ 4 = 3), so AB = 3 × 3 = 9 m. Surveyors use such tricks to
    measure distances they cannot walk across. Now you try: AC = 15 m, CE = 5 m,
    DE = 2.4 m. (7.2 m)

D9. The plan of a classroom is drawn to the scale 1 : 50. On the plan the room
    is 16 cm by 12 cm. What are the real measurements? A cupboard is 4.5 m long.
    How long is it on the plan?
    Answer: The room is 8 m by 6 m. The cupboard is 9 cm long on the plan.
    Teacher explains: 1 : 50 means 1 cm on the plan represents 50 cm in the
    room. Real = plan × 50: 16 × 50 = 800 cm = 8 m and 12 × 50 = 600 cm = 6 m.
    Backwards, plan = real ÷ 50, but change to cm FIRST: 4.5 m = 450 cm, and
    450 ÷ 50 = 9 cm.
    Common mistake: dividing 4.5 by 50 and getting 0.09 cm — the units were
    mixed. Always write the unit at every step. A scale has no units: 1 : 50 is
    the same in cm, m or inches. Now you try: a plan at 1 : 100 shows a wall
    7.5 cm long. How long is the real wall? (7.5 m)

D10. A trekking map has scale 1 : 50,000. Two villages are 7.4 cm apart on the
     map. How far apart are they really? A road is 12 km long; how long is it on
     the map?
     Answer: 3.7 km; 24 cm.
     Teacher explains: 1 cm on the map represents 50,000 cm = 500 m = 0.5 km. So
     7.4 cm × 50,000 = 370,000 cm = 3.7 km. Or: 7.4 × 0.5 km = 3.7 km.
     Backwards: 12 km = 1,200,000 cm, and 1,200,000 ÷ 50,000 = 24 cm.
        1 m  = 100 cm
        1 km = 1000 m = 100,000 cm   (one lakh centimetres)
        so 1 : 100,000 means 1 cm represents 1 km
     Common mistake: forgetting that 1 km is 100,000 cm, not 1000 cm. Now you
     try: on a 1 : 200,000 map two towns are 5.5 cm apart. (11 km)

D11. A photo 4 cm by 6 cm is enlarged with scale factor 3. How do the perimeter
     and the area change? A floor plan has scale 1 : 100; a room measures 5 cm
     by 4 cm on it. What is the real floor area?
     Answer: Perimeter × 3 (20 cm → 60 cm); area × 9 (24 cm² → 216 cm²). The
     room is 20 m².
     Teacher explains: The new photo is 12 cm by 18 cm. Perimeter: 20 cm becomes
     60 cm, × 3 like every length. Area: 4 × 6 = 24 cm² becomes
     12 × 18 = 216 cm² = 9 × 24. Area is length × length, so it is multiplied by
     k × k = k² = 9. Draw it: the big photo holds 9 copies of the small one (3
     rows of 3).
     On the plan, 1 cm represents 100 cm = 1 m, so 1 cm² represents
     1 m × 1 m = 1 m². The room is 5 m × 4 m = 20 m² (plan area
     20 cm² × 100² = 200,000 cm² = 20 m²). Now you try: on a 1 : 200 plan a room
     is 3 cm by 2 cm. Real floor area? (24 m²)

D12. Two similar jars have heights 10 cm and 15 cm. The small one holds 400 mL.
     How much does the large one hold?
     Answer: 1350 mL. k = 1.5, volume factor 1.5³ = 3.375, and
     400 × 3.375 = 1350.
     Teacher explains: Volume is length × length × length, so if every length is
     multiplied by k, the volume is multiplied by k³. Here k = 15 ÷ 10 = 1.5 and
     k³ = 1.5 × 1.5 × 1.5 = 3.375. The large jar holds 400 × 3.375 = 1350 mL.
     Its label (surface area) is k² = 2.25 times as big.
        lengths × k      areas × k²      volumes × k³
        k = 2            × 4             × 8
        k = 3            × 9             × 27
        k = 1/2          × 1/4           × 1/8
     Common mistake: multiplying the volume by k only (600 mL). Now you try:
     cubes of edge 2 cm and 6 cm. How many times the volume is the big one? (27
     times)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. Is the second rectangle similar to the first? If yes, give the scale factor
    k from the first to the second.
    a) 2 × 3 and 4 × 6      b) 2 × 3 and 6 × 8      c) 5 × 10 and 2 × 4
    d) 4 × 6 and 10 × 15    e) 3 × 5 and 9 × 15     f) 6 × 8 and 9 × 12
    g) 4 × 7 and 8 × 13     h) 12 × 16 and 3 × 4    i) 1 × 2 and 5 × 10
    j) 2 × 5 and 5 × 8      k) 10 × 25 and 4 × 10   l) 3 × 4 and 4 × 5
    m) 6 × 9 and 4 × 6      n) 7 × 7 and 3 × 3      o) 8 × 12 and 6 × 9
    p) 5 × 8 and 15 × 20
    Answers: a) yes, k = 2   b) no   c) yes, k = 2/5   d) yes, k = 2.5
             e) yes, k = 3   f) yes, k = 1.5   g) no   h) yes, k = 1/4
             i) yes, k = 5   j) no   k) yes, k = 2/5   l) no   m) yes, k = 2/3
             n) yes, k = 3/7   o) yes, k = 3/4   p) no

M2. The sides of two triangles are given, in matching order. Are the triangles
    similar (SSS)? If yes, give k from the first to the second.
    a) 3, 4, 5 and 6, 8, 10        b) 3, 4, 5 and 9, 12, 16
    c) 2, 3, 4 and 4, 6, 8         d) 5, 5, 8 and 10, 10, 16
    e) 6, 8, 9 and 9, 12, 13.5     f) 4, 6, 7 and 6, 9, 10
    g) 5, 12, 13 and 2.5, 6, 6.5   h) 10, 15, 20 and 4, 6, 8
    i) 7, 8, 9 and 14, 16, 17      j) 3, 3, 3 and 7, 7, 7
    k) 8, 10, 12 and 12, 15, 18    l) 6, 7, 8 and 12, 14, 15
    m) 9, 12, 15 and 3, 4, 5       n) 2, 4, 5 and 5, 10, 12
    o) 12, 18, 24 and 10, 15, 20   p) 1, 2, 2 and 4, 8, 8
    Answers: a) yes, k = 2   b) no   c) yes, k = 2   d) yes, k = 2
             e) yes, k = 1.5   f) no   g) yes, k = 1/2   h) yes, k = 2/5   i) no
             j) yes, k = 7/3   k) yes, k = 1.5   l) no   m) yes, k = 1/3   n) no
             o) yes, k = 5/6   p) yes, k = 4

M3. Two angles of each of two triangles are given. Find the third angles and
    decide whether the triangles are similar (AA).
    a) 50°, 60° and 60°, 70°    b) 40°, 80° and 80°, 60°
    c) 90°, 30° and 60°, 90°    d) 45°, 45° and 90°, 45°
    e) 35°, 75° and 70°, 75°    f) 100°, 40° and 40°, 50°
    g) 20°, 30° and 130°, 30°   h) 65°, 65° and 50°, 65°
    i) 55°, 25° and 100°, 25°   j) 72°, 36° and 72°, 72°
    k) 110°, 35° and 35°, 45°   l) 25°, 90° and 65°, 90°
    m) 60°, 60° and 60°, 70°    n) 15°, 120° and 45°, 15°
    o) 80°, 50° and 50°, 60°    p) 33°, 57° and 90°, 55°
    Answers: a) yes (50°, 60°, 70°)   b) yes (40°, 60°, 80°)
             c) yes (30°, 60°, 90°)   d) yes (45°, 45°, 90°)
             e) yes (35°, 70°, 75°)   f) no (40°, 40°, 100° and 40°, 50°, 90°)
             g) yes (20°, 30°, 130°)   h) yes (50°, 65°, 65°)
             i) yes (25°, 55°, 100°)   j) yes (36°, 72°, 72°)
             k) no (35°, 35°, 110° and 35°, 45°, 100°)   l) yes (25°, 65°, 90°)
             m) no (60°, 60°, 60° and 50°, 60°, 70°)   n) yes (15°, 45°, 120°)
             o) no (50°, 50°, 80° and 50°, 60°, 70°)
             p) no (33°, 57°, 90° and 35°, 55°, 90°)

M4. A length on the object and the matching length on the image are given. Find
    the scale factor k (as a fraction for reductions). Say whether it is an
    enlargement or a reduction.
    a) 4 → 12      b) 5 → 20      c) 6 → 9       d) 10 → 4
    e) 8 → 2       f) 3 → 7.5     g) 12 → 18     h) 15 → 5
    i) 9 → 6       j) 2 → 11      k) 20 → 5      l) 7 → 21
    m) 6 → 1.5     n) 2.5 → 10    o) 16 → 12     p) 1.2 → 3.6
    Answers: a) k = 3, enlargement   b) k = 4, enlargement
             c) k = 1.5, enlargement   d) k = 2/5, reduction
             e) k = 1/4, reduction   f) k = 2.5, enlargement
             g) k = 1.5, enlargement   h) k = 1/3, reduction
             i) k = 2/3, reduction   j) k = 5.5, enlargement
             k) k = 1/4, reduction   l) k = 3, enlargement
             m) k = 1/4, reduction   n) k = 4, enlargement
             o) k = 3/4, reduction   p) k = 3, enlargement

M5. ΔABC ~ ΔPQR. The sides AB, BC, AC and the side PQ are given. Find QR and PR.
    a) AB = 4, BC = 6, AC = 5, PQ = 10
    b) AB = 6, BC = 8, AC = 10, PQ = 9
    c) AB = 3, BC = 5, AC = 7, PQ = 12
    d) AB = 9, BC = 12, AC = 15, PQ = 6
    e) AB = 10, BC = 14, AC = 18, PQ = 5
    f) AB = 8, BC = 6, AC = 7, PQ = 12
    g) AB = 5, BC = 5, AC = 6, PQ = 15
    h) AB = 12, BC = 16, AC = 20, PQ = 3
    i) AB = 2, BC = 3, AC = 4, PQ = 5
    j) AB = 15, BC = 9, AC = 12, PQ = 10
    k) AB = 7, BC = 8, AC = 10, PQ = 21
    l) AB = 20, BC = 12, AC = 16, PQ = 25
    m) AB = 6, BC = 4, AC = 9, PQ = 4
    n) AB = 1.5, BC = 2, AC = 2.5, PQ = 6
    o) AB = 14, BC = 21, AC = 28, PQ = 4
    p) AB = 18, BC = 24, AC = 12, PQ = 3
    Answers: a) QR = 15, PR = 12.5   b) QR = 12, PR = 15   c) QR = 20, PR = 28
             d) QR = 8, PR = 10   e) QR = 7, PR = 9   f) QR = 9, PR = 10.5
             g) QR = 15, PR = 18   h) QR = 4, PR = 5   i) QR = 7.5, PR = 10
             j) QR = 6, PR = 8   k) QR = 24, PR = 30   l) QR = 15, PR = 20
             m) QR = 2 2/3, PR = 6   n) QR = 8, PR = 10   o) QR = 6, PR = 8
             p) QR = 4, PR = 2

M6. Solve the proportion x/a = b/c for x (so x = a × b ÷ c).
    a) x/6 = 10/4    b) x/4 = 9/6     c) x/5 = 12/10   d) x/3 = 7/21
    e) x/8 = 15/12   f) x/10 = 3/5    g) x/12 = 5/8    h) x/2 = 9/6
    i) x/7 = 4/14    j) x/9 = 10/6    k) x/15 = 8/20   l) x/6 = 7/3
    m) x/16 = 3/12   n) x/4 = 15/10   o) x/20 = 6/8    p) x/2.5 = 4/5
    q) x/1.5 = 6/9   r) x/11 = 6/3    s) x/9 = 20/12   t) x/18 = 5/15
    Answers: a) x = 15   b) x = 6   c) x = 6   d) x = 1   e) x = 10   f) x = 6
             g) x = 7.5   h) x = 3   i) x = 2   j) x = 15   k) x = 6   l) x = 14
             m) x = 4   n) x = 6   o) x = 15   p) x = 2   q) x = 1   r) x = 22
             s) x = 15   t) x = 6

M7. In ΔABC, D is on AB and E is on AC with DE ∥ BC, so ΔADE ~ ΔABC and
    AD/DB = AE/EC. Find the length asked for.
    a) AD = 4, DB = 6, DE = 5: BC       b) AD = 3, DB = 9, DE = 4: BC
    c) AD = 2, DB = 3, DE = 6: BC       d) AD = 5, DB = 5, DE = 7: BC
    e) AD = 6, DB = 2, DE = 9: BC       f) AD = 1.5, DB = 4.5, DE = 2: BC
    g) AD = 4, AB = 10, BC = 15: DE     h) AD = 3, AB = 12, BC = 8: DE
    i) AD = 5, AB = 20, BC = 12: DE     j) AD = 4, DE = 6, BC = 18: AB
    k) AD = 2, DE = 5, BC = 15: AB      l) AD = 3, AE = 4, EC = 6: DB
    m) AD = 5, AE = 2, EC = 10: DB      n) AD = 8, AE = 6, EC = 12: DB
    Answers: a) BC = 12.5   b) BC = 16   c) BC = 15   d) BC = 14   e) BC = 12
             f) BC = 8   g) DE = 6   h) DE = 2   i) DE = 3   j) AB = 12
             k) AB = 6   l) DB = 4.5   m) DB = 25   n) DB = 16

M8. Shadows at the same time of day. In a) to h) a pole of height h₁ has shadow
    s₁; find the height of an object with shadow s₂. In i) to n) find the shadow
    of an object of height h₂ (all in m).
    a) h₁ = 2, s₁ = 3, s₂ = 18       b) h₁ = 1.5, s₁ = 2, s₂ = 40
    c) h₁ = 1, s₁ = 0.5, s₂ = 12     d) h₁ = 3, s₁ = 4, s₂ = 20

    m) h₁ = 6, s₁ = 9, h₂ = 14       n) h₁ = 2, s₁ = 5, h₂ = 3
    Answers: a) h₂ = 12   b) h₂ = 30   c) h₂ = 24   d) h₂ = 15   e) h₂ = 12
             f) h₂ = 22.5   g) h₂ = 15   h) h₂ = 45   i) s₂ = 15   j) s₂ = 60
             k) s₂ = 15   l) s₂ = 10.5   m) s₂ = 21   n) s₂ = 7.5

M9. Scale 1 : n and a length on the drawing or map are given. Find the real
    length in the unit shown.

M11. Write the scale in the form 1 : n.

M12. In a) to h) lengths are multiplied by k: by what are areas and volumes
     multiplied? In i) to r) a figure is enlarged by k: find the new area (cm²)
     or volume (cm³) from the old one.
     a) k = 2                 b) k = 3
     c) k = 4                 d) k = 5
     e) k = 10                f) k = 1/2
     g) k = 1/3               h) k = 1.5
     i) area 20, k = 3        j) area 15, k = 2
     k) area 4, k = 5         l) area 36, k = 1/2
     m) area 2.5, k = 4       n) volume 50, k = 2
     o) volume 10, k = 3      p) volume 2, k = 10
     q) volume 400, k = 1/2   r) volume 400, k = 1.5
     Answers: a) areas × 4, volumes × 8   b) areas × 9, volumes × 27
              c) areas × 16, volumes × 64   d) areas × 25, volumes × 125
              e) areas × 100, volumes × 1000   f) areas × 1/4, volumes × 1/8
              g) areas × 1/9, volumes × 1/27   h) areas × 2.25, volumes × 3.375
              i) 180   j) 60   k) 100   l) 9   m) 40   n) 400   o) 270   p) 2000
              q) 50   r) 1350

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: use photo enlargements and squared paper to draw similar shapes;
  always mark equal angles with the same colour and write the matching letters
  under each other (A B C over P Q R); find k before anything else and ask
  "bigger or smaller?"; for scales, write 1 cm represents ... on the page and
  convert units only at the end. Harder: prove that the altitude to the
  hypotenuse makes two triangles similar to the whole (and deduce Pythagoras'
  theorem, topic 6.21); find the height of a flagpole with a mirror on the
  ground (angle of incidence = angle of reflection); make a 1 : 100 plan of your
  home; explain why an ant scaled up 100 times could not stand
  (weight × 1,000,000, leg area × 10,000); find the map area of a 6 km² lake on
  a 1 : 50,000 map (24 cm²).


================================================================================
TOPIC 6.27 — VISUALISING SOLID SHAPES; EULER'S FORMULA V − E + F = 2
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - A POLYHEDRON is a solid whose surface is made only of flat POLYGON faces,
    meeting in straight EDGES at corners called VERTICES (one vertex, many
    vertices). Cylinders, cones, spheres and hemispheres have curved surfaces,
    so they are NOT polyhedra.
  - EULER'S FORMULA: for every polyhedron without a hole, V − E + F = 2
    (vertices − edges + faces). Use it to check a count or to find a missing
    number: V = 2 + E − F, E = V + F − 2, F = 2 + E − V. A solid with a hole
    through it (like a picture frame) gives V − E + F = 0 instead.
  - A PRISM on an n-sided base has V = 2n, E = 3n, F = n + 2. A PYRAMID on an
    n-sided base has V = n + 1, E = 2n, F = n + 1. So the edges of a prism are
    always a multiple of 3 and the edges of a pyramid are always even.
  - Counting edges from faces: add up the sides of all the faces and divide by
    2, because every edge is shared by exactly 2 faces. If every vertex has k
    faces meeting at it, V = (total corners of all faces) ÷ k.
  - There are exactly FIVE PLATONIC (regular) solids, with identical regular
    faces and the same number meeting at every vertex: tetrahedron (V 4, E 6, F
    4), cube (8, 12, 6), octahedron (6, 12, 8), dodecahedron (20, 30, 12) and
    icosahedron (12, 30, 20). There are only five because the face angles at a
    vertex must add up to less than 360°.
  - A solid can be shown by its PLAN (the view from directly above) and its
    ELEVATIONS (the views from the front and from the side). Each view shows the
    outline only, not the depth, so we usually need all three to know the solid.
  - Counting cubes in a stack: count layer by layer (or column by column) and
    remember the hidden cubes that hold up the ones you can see. A large cube
    made of n × n × n small cubes has n³ small cubes.
  - A CROSS-SECTION is the flat shape you see when a solid is cut by a plane. A
    prism cut parallel to its ends always shows its end shape. A cube can be cut
    to show a square, a rectangle, a triangle, a trapezium, a pentagon or even a
    regular hexagon. A sphere is always cut in a circle.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. An unsharpened pencil is a hexagonal prism. Count its vertices, edges and
    faces, and check that V − E + F = 2.
    Answer: V = 12, E = 18, F = 8, and 12 − 18 + 8 = 2.
    Teacher explains: Count in groups, never one by one. FACES: 2 hexagonal
    ends + 6 rectangles round the side = 8. VERTICES: 6 on each hexagon,
    6 × 2 = 12. EDGES: 6 round each hexagon (12) + 6 long edges joining the two
    hexagons = 18. Euler: V − E + F = 12 − 18 + 8 = 2. The formula is a check:
    if your count gives 1 or 3, you have missed or double-counted something.
    Common mistake: counting the long edges only once for the whole pencil and
    forgetting the edges round the ends. Now you try: a pentagonal prism. (V 10,
    E 15, F 7; 10 − 15 + 7 = 2)

D2. Explain why a prism whose end is an n-sided polygon has 2n vertices, 3n
    edges and n + 2 faces. Use this to find V, E and F for a decagonal
    (10-sided) prism.
    Answer: V = 20, E = 30, F = 12 for the decagonal prism; the general reason
    is below.
    Teacher explains: Picture the prism as two copies of the n-gon (top and
    bottom) joined by upright edges. VERTICES: each n-gon has n corners, so 2n
    in all. EDGES: n round the top + n round the bottom + n upright edges = 3n.
    FACES: the 2 ends + one rectangle for each side of the n-gon = n + 2. Check
    with Euler for any n: V − E + F = 2n − 3n + (n + 2) = 2. For n = 10:
    V = 2 × 10 = 20, E = 3 × 10 = 30, F = 10 + 2 = 12, and 20 − 30 + 12 = 2.
    This is a short PROOF: the algebra shows Euler works for every prism at
    once, not just the ones we counted. Now you try: an octagonal prism. (V 16,
    E 24, F 10)

D3. The Great Pyramid of Giza is a square pyramid. Find its V, E and F. Then
    find the rule for a pyramid on an n-sided base and use it for an octagonal
    pyramid.
    Answer: Square pyramid: V = 5, E = 8, F = 5. Rule: V = n + 1, E = 2n, F =
    n + 1. Octagonal pyramid: V = 9, E = 16, F = 9.
    Teacher explains: A pyramid is a base polygon joined to one top point, the
    APEX. VERTICES: n corners of the base + 1 apex = n + 1. EDGES: n round the
    base + n slanting edges up to the apex = 2n. FACES: 1 base + n triangles =
    n + 1. Square pyramid (n = 4): V = 5, E = 8, F = 5. Octagonal (n = 8):
    V = 9, E = 16, F = 9. Euler for every pyramid: (n + 1) − 2n + (n + 1) = 2.
    Notice that a pyramid always has V = F. Common mistake: forgetting that the
    base is a face too. Now you try: a hexagonal pyramid. (V 7, E 12, F 7)

D4. A polyhedron has 12 faces and 30 edges. How many vertices does it have?
    Answer: 20 vertices.
    Teacher explains: Write Euler's formula and put in what you know:
    V − E + F = 2 gives V − 30 + 12 = 2, so V − 18 = 2 and V = 20. Rearranged
    once for all: V = 2 + E − F = 2 + 30 − 12 = 20. This solid is the regular
    dodecahedron. Common mistake: using V + E + F = 2 or mixing up which letter
    is subtracted. A good memory aid: the EDGES are the biggest number, and they
    are the ones taken away. Now you try: a polyhedron has 6 vertices and 8
    faces. How many edges? (E = V + F − 2 = 6 + 8 − 2 = 12)

D5. Arjun says he has made a polyhedron (without a hole) with 10 vertices, 15
    edges and 8 faces. Can he be right?
    Answer: No. 10 − 15 + 8 = 3, not 2, so these numbers are impossible for a
    polyhedron without a hole.
    Teacher explains: Every polyhedron without a hole must give V − E + F = 2.
    Here 10 − 15 + 8 = 3, so Arjun has miscounted. With 10 vertices and 15 edges
    he would need F = 2 + 15 − 10 = 7 faces (a pentagonal prism has exactly
    these numbers). Euler's formula can prove that a solid is IMPOSSIBLE; but
    numbers that do satisfy it are not automatically possible (for example
    V = 3, E = 3, F = 2 passes the formula, yet no polyhedron has only 2 faces).
    Now you try: can a polyhedron have V = 8, E = 12, F = 6? (yes:
    8 − 12 + 6 = 2, the cube)

D6. The five Platonic solids have identical regular faces with the same number
    of faces at each vertex. Explain why there cannot be a sixth.
    Answer: At each vertex at least 3 faces must meet, and their angles must add
    up to LESS than 360°. That leaves only 5 possibilities: 3, 4 or 5
    equilateral triangles, 3 squares, or 3 regular pentagons.
    Teacher explains: If the angles at a vertex added up to 360°, the faces
    would lie flat; more than 360° cannot fold round a point. Check each regular
    polygon:
    faces       angle   3 faces   4 faces   5 faces   6 faces
    triangle     60°     180°      240°      300°      360° flat
    square       90°     270°      360° flat
    pentagon    108°     324°      432° too big
    hexagon     120°     360° flat
    So we get: 3 triangles (tetrahedron), 4 triangles (octahedron), 5 triangles
    (icosahedron), 3 squares (cube) and 3 pentagons (dodecahedron). Polygons
    with 6 or more sides have angles of 120° or more, and 3 × 120° = 360°
    already lies flat. That is a complete proof that there are exactly five. Now
    you try: how many faces meet at each vertex of an octahedron? (4)

D7. A football is stitched from 12 pentagons and 20 hexagons, with 3 patches
    meeting at every corner. How many edges (seams) and vertices does it have?
    Check with Euler.
    Answer: E = 90, V = 60, F = 32, and 60 − 90 + 32 = 2.
    Teacher explains: Count the SIDES of all the patches:
    12 × 5 + 20 × 6 = 60 + 120 = 180. Every seam joins exactly two patches, so
    it has been counted twice: E = 180 ÷ 2 = 90. Every corner is shared by 3
    patches, so V = 180 ÷ 3 = 60. Faces: 12 + 20 = 32. Euler: 60 − 90 + 32 = 2.
    This "count the sides and divide" trick works for any polyhedron: for a
    cube, 6 × 4 = 24 sides, E = 24 ÷ 2 = 12, V = 24 ÷ 3 = 8. Common mistake:
    forgetting to divide by 2 for the edges. Now you try: 8 triangles with 4 at
    each vertex (octahedron). (E = 24 ÷ 2 = 12, V = 24 ÷ 4 = 6)

D8. Six cubes are stacked as shown by this PLAN (top view). The numbers tell how
    many cubes are in each stack. The bottom row of the plan is at the FRONT.
    Draw the plan, the front elevation and the side elevation seen from the
    right.
    back    3 1
    front   1 1
    Answer: Plan: a 2 × 2 square. Front elevation: columns of heights 3 and 1.
    Right side elevation: columns of heights 1 (front row) and 3 (back row).
    Teacher explains: The PLAN shows only which squares are covered: a 2 × 2
    square. The FRONT ELEVATION: look from the front; in each column you see the
    tallest stack in that column: left column max(3, 1) = 3, right column max(1,
    1) = 1. The RIGHT SIDE ELEVATION: stand on the right and look left; the
    front row is on your left and the back row on your right; the tallest stack
    in the front row is 1 and in the back row it is 3.
       plan            front          right side
      [][]             []                     []
      [][]             []                     []
                       [][]                 [][]
    Total cubes: 3 + 1 + 1 + 1 = 6. Common mistake: drawing heights you cannot
    see (hidden stacks behind taller ones do not show). Now you try: plan back
    row 2 2, front row 1 3. Front elevation? (heights 2 and 3)

D9. A step pyramid is built from cubes: a 4 × 4 layer at the bottom, then 3 × 3,
    then 2 × 2, then one cube on top. How many cubes are there? How many would a
    pyramid with a 6 × 6 bottom layer need?
    Answer: 30 cubes; the 6-layer pyramid needs 91.
    Teacher explains: Count layer by layer, including the cubes you cannot see
    inside: 16 + 9 + 4 + 1 = 30. The layers are the square numbers, so the total
    is a sum of squares. With a 6 × 6 bottom: 36 + 25 + 16 + 9 + 4 + 1 = 91.
    (There is a formula: n(n + 1)(2n + 1) ÷ 6; for n = 6: 6 × 7 × 13 ÷ 6 = 91.)
    Common mistake: counting only the cubes that show on the outside. From the
    front you see 4 + 3 + 2 + 1 = 10 faces, but most of the 30 cubes are hidden.
    Now you try: a 5 × 5 bottom layer. (55)

D10. A cube of cheese is cut straight through with one slice of a knife. Which
     shapes can the cut face be? Can it be a triangle? A regular hexagon? A
     circle?
     Answer: A square, a rectangle, a triangle, a trapezium, a pentagon or a
     hexagon (even a regular hexagon); never a circle and never a polygon with
     more than 6 sides.
     Teacher explains: Picture the knife as a flat plane. Cut parallel to a
     face: a SQUARE the same size as the face. Tilt the knife but keep it
     parallel to one edge: a RECTANGLE (longer than the square). Slice off a
     corner: a TRIANGLE (cutting three faces near one vertex). Cut through the
     midpoints of six edges, square to a long diagonal of the cube: a REGULAR
     HEXAGON. Each side of the cross-section lies on one face of the cube, and a
     cube has 6 faces, so at most 6 sides; a flat cut of a flat-faced solid
     never gives a curve. Common mistake: thinking the cut must be parallel to a
     face. Try it with a potato or clay cube. Now you try: what cross-section do
     you get when a triangular prism is cut parallel to its ends? (a triangle
     the same as the ends)

D11. Which of these are polyhedra: cube, cylinder, cone, sphere, tetrahedron,
     hemisphere, triangular prism? For those that are not, why does Euler's
     formula not apply?
     Answer: Polyhedra: cube, tetrahedron, triangular prism. Not polyhedra:
     cylinder, cone, sphere, hemisphere (they have curved surfaces).
     Teacher explains: A polyhedron must have ONLY flat polygon faces with
     straight edges. A cylinder has 2 flat circles and a curved surface; its
     "edges" are circles, not straight lines, and it has no vertices, so V, E
     and F are not defined in Euler's sense. The same goes for the cone (1 flat
     face, 1 curved surface, 1 apex), the sphere (1 curved surface) and the
     hemisphere (1 flat face and 1 curved surface). Cross-sections of these
     curved solids can be curved: a cylinder cut parallel to its ends gives a
     circle, cut slantwise an ellipse (oval), and cut down through its axis a
     rectangle; a cone cut through the apex and the centre of the base gives a
     triangle. Now you try: is a cuboid a polyhedron? (yes: 6 flat rectangular
     faces)

D12. A wooden picture frame is a square ring. Its top and bottom are each split
     into 4 trapezia, and it has 4 outer and 4 inner rectangular sides. Count V,
     E, F. Does Euler's formula hold?
     Answer: V = 16, E = 32, F = 16, so V − E + F = 0, not 2. Euler's formula
     V − E + F = 2 is only for solids without a hole.
     Teacher explains: FACES: 4 trapezia on top + 4 underneath + 4 outer
     sides + 4 inner sides = 16. VERTICES: 4 outer corners and 4 inner corners
     on top, and the same underneath: 8 + 8 = 16. EDGES: on top, 4 outer + 4
     inner + 4 short slanting edges joining them = 12; underneath another 12;
     plus 4 outer and 4 inner upright edges = 8. Total 12 + 12 + 8 = 32.
     V − E + F = 16 − 32 + 16 = 0. The hole changes the answer: every solid with
     one hole gives 0, with two holes −2, and so on. So V − E + F tells
     mathematicians how many holes a surface has, one of the first ideas of the
     branch called TOPOLOGY. Now you try: what is V − E + F for a cube?
     (8 − 12 + 6 = 2)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. Prisms. Find the number of vertices V, edges E and faces F. (Use V = 2n,
    E = 3n, F = n + 2.)
    a) triangular prism (n = 3)      b) square prism (n = 4)
    c) pentagonal prism (n = 5)      d) hexagonal prism (n = 6)
    e) heptagonal prism (n = 7)      f) octagonal prism (n = 8)
    g) nonagonal prism (n = 9)       h) decagonal prism (n = 10)
    i) hendecagonal prism (n = 11)   j) dodecagonal prism (n = 12)
    k) prism on a 15-gon             l) prism on a 20-gon
    m) prism on a 50-gon             n) prism on a 100-gon
    Answers: a) V 6, E 9, F 5   b) V 8, E 12, F 6   c) V 10, E 15, F 7
             d) V 12, E 18, F 8   e) V 14, E 21, F 9   f) V 16, E 24, F 10
             g) V 18, E 27, F 11   h) V 20, E 30, F 12   i) V 22, E 33, F 13
             j) V 24, E 36, F 14   k) V 30, E 45, F 17   l) V 40, E 60, F 22
             m) V 100, E 150, F 52   n) V 200, E 300, F 102

M2. Pyramids. Find V, E and F. (Use V = n + 1, E = 2n, F = n + 1.)
    a) triangular pyramid (n = 3)      b) square pyramid (n = 4)
    c) pentagonal pyramid (n = 5)      d) hexagonal pyramid (n = 6)
    e) heptagonal pyramid (n = 7)      f) octagonal pyramid (n = 8)
    g) nonagonal pyramid (n = 9)       h) decagonal pyramid (n = 10)
    i) hendecagonal pyramid (n = 11)   j) dodecagonal pyramid (n = 12)
    k) pyramid on a 15-gon             l) pyramid on a 20-gon
    m) pyramid on a 50-gon             n) pyramid on a 100-gon

             d) V 7, E 12, F 7   e) V 8, E 14, F 8   f) V 9, E 16, F 9
             g) V 10, E 18, F 10   h) V 11, E 20, F 11   i) V 12, E 22, F 12
             j) V 13, E 24, F 13   k) V 16, E 30, F 16   l) V 21, E 40, F 21
             m) V 51, E 100, F 51   n) V 101, E 200, F 101

M3. Use V − E + F = 2 to find the missing number.
    a) V = 8, E = __, F = 6     b) V = 6, E = __, F = 5
    c) V = __, E = 9, F = 5     d) V = __, E = 12, F = 8
    e) V = 12, E = 30, F = __   f) V = 20, E = 30, F = __
    g) V = __, E = 30, F = 12   h) V = 4, E = __, F = 4
    i) V = 10, E = 15, F = __   j) V = __, E = 18, F = 8
    k) V = 7, E = __, F = 7     l) V = __, E = 16, F = 9
    m) V = 16, E = 24, F = __   n) V = __, E = 90, F = 32
    o) V = 24, E = __, F = 14   p) V = 12, E = __, F = 8
    q) V = __, E = 36, F = 14   r) V = 9, E = 16, F = __
    s) V = __, E = 60, F = 32   t) V = 14, E = __, F = 9
    u) V = __, E = 20, F = 10   v) V = 30, E = 60, F = __
    Answers: a) E = 12   b) E = 9   c) V = 6   d) V = 6   e) F = 20   f) F = 12
             g) V = 20   h) E = 6   i) F = 7   j) V = 12   k) E = 12   l) V = 9
             m) F = 10   n) V = 60   o) E = 36   p) E = 18   q) V = 24
             r) F = 9   s) V = 30   t) E = 21   u) V = 12   v) F = 32

M4. Could these be the numbers for a polyhedron without a hole? Work out
    V − E + F; write yes if it is 2 and no if it is not.
    a) V 8, E 12, F 6     b) V 6, E 9, F 5      c) V 5, E 8, F 5
    d) V 10, E 15, F 8    e) V 12, E 18, F 8    f) V 6, E 10, F 5
    g) V 4, E 6, F 4      h) V 20, E 30, F 12   i) V 12, E 30, F 20
    j) V 7, E 12, F 6     k) V 9, E 16, F 9     l) V 16, E 24, F 9
    m) V 6, E 12, F 8     n) V 60, E 90, F 32   o) V 14, E 21, F 9
    p) V 11, E 20, F 11   q) V 24, E 36, F 14   r) V 8, E 14, F 7
    Answers: a) 8 − 12 + 6 = 2, yes   b) 6 − 9 + 5 = 2, yes
             c) 5 − 8 + 5 = 2, yes   d) 10 − 15 + 8 = 3, no
             e) 12 − 18 + 8 = 2, yes   f) 6 − 10 + 5 = 1, no
             g) 4 − 6 + 4 = 2, yes   h) 20 − 30 + 12 = 2, yes
             i) 12 − 30 + 20 = 2, yes   j) 7 − 12 + 6 = 1, no
             k) 9 − 16 + 9 = 2, yes   l) 16 − 24 + 9 = 1, no
             m) 6 − 12 + 8 = 2, yes   n) 60 − 90 + 32 = 2, yes
             o) 14 − 21 + 9 = 2, yes   p) 11 − 20 + 11 = 2, yes
             q) 24 − 36 + 14 = 2, yes   r) 8 − 14 + 7 = 1, no

M5. Platonic solids. In a) to e) find E and V from the faces (each edge is
    shared by 2 faces), then check V − E + F = 2. In f) to o) find the angle sum
    at a vertex (triangle 60°, square 90°, pentagon 108°, hexagon 120°, octagon
    135°) and say whether a regular solid is possible, the faces lie flat, or
    the sum is too big.
    a) tetrahedron: 4 triangles, 3 at each vertex
    b) cube: 6 squares, 3 at each vertex
    c) octahedron: 8 triangles, 4 at each vertex
    d) dodecahedron: 12 pentagons, 3 at each vertex
    e) icosahedron: 20 triangles, 5 at each vertex
    f) angle sum at a vertex of 3 triangles
    g) angle sum at a vertex of 4 triangles
    h) angle sum at a vertex of 5 triangles
    i) angle sum at a vertex of 6 triangles
    j) angle sum at a vertex of 3 squares
    k) angle sum at a vertex of 4 squares
    l) angle sum at a vertex of 3 pentagons
    m) angle sum at a vertex of 4 pentagons
    n) angle sum at a vertex of 3 hexagons
    o) angle sum at a vertex of 3 octagons
    Answers: a) E = 4 × 3 ÷ 2 = 6, V = 4 × 3 ÷ 3 = 4; 4 − 6 + 4 = 2
             b) E = 6 × 4 ÷ 2 = 12, V = 6 × 4 ÷ 3 = 8; 8 − 12 + 6 = 2
             c) E = 8 × 3 ÷ 2 = 12, V = 8 × 3 ÷ 4 = 6; 6 − 12 + 8 = 2
             d) E = 12 × 5 ÷ 2 = 30, V = 12 × 5 ÷ 3 = 20; 20 − 30 + 12 = 2
             e) E = 20 × 3 ÷ 2 = 30, V = 20 × 3 ÷ 5 = 12; 12 − 30 + 20 = 2
             f) 180°, solid possible   g) 240°, solid possible
             h) 300°, solid possible   i) 360°, flat   j) 270°, solid possible
             k) 360°, flat   l) 324°, solid possible   m) 432°, too big
             n) 360°, flat   o) 405°, too big

M6. Each list gives all the faces of a polyhedron without a hole. Find the total
    number of sides, then E (total ÷ 2), then V from V = 2 + E − F.
    a) 2 triangles, 3 rectangles
    b) 6 squares
    c) 4 triangles
    d) 1 square, 4 triangles
    e) 2 hexagons, 6 rectangles
    f) 1 hexagon, 6 triangles
    g) 8 triangles
    h) 12 pentagons
    i) 20 triangles

    k) 6 triangles (two tetrahedra glued)
    l) 10 triangles (two pentagonal pyramids glued)
    m) 2 squares, 8 triangles
    n) 2 pentagons, 10 triangles
    o) 6 squares, 8 triangles
    p) 4 triangles, 4 hexagons
    q) 8 triangles, 6 octagons
    r) 12 pentagons, 20 hexagons
    s) 2 octagons, 8 rectangles
    t) 1 decagon, 10 triangles
    Answers: a) E = 18 ÷ 2 = 9, V = 6   b) E = 24 ÷ 2 = 12, V = 8
             c) E = 12 ÷ 2 = 6, V = 4   d) E = 16 ÷ 2 = 8, V = 5
             e) E = 36 ÷ 2 = 18, V = 12   f) E = 24 ÷ 2 = 12, V = 7
             g) E = 24 ÷ 2 = 12, V = 6   h) E = 60 ÷ 2 = 30, V = 20
             i) E = 60 ÷ 2 = 30, V = 12   j) E = 24 ÷ 2 = 12, V = 8
             k) E = 18 ÷ 2 = 9, V = 5   l) E = 30 ÷ 2 = 15, V = 7
             m) E = 32 ÷ 2 = 16, V = 8   n) E = 40 ÷ 2 = 20, V = 10
             o) E = 48 ÷ 2 = 24, V = 12   p) E = 36 ÷ 2 = 18, V = 12
             q) E = 72 ÷ 2 = 36, V = 24   r) E = 180 ÷ 2 = 90, V = 60
             s) E = 48 ÷ 2 = 24, V = 16   t) E = 40 ÷ 2 = 20, V = 11

M7. Find the number of sides n of the base, then V, E and F. Write "impossible"
    if no such prism or pyramid exists.
    a) a prism with E = 24     b) a prism with F = 9

             n) n = 20; V 40, E 60, F 22   o) n = 3; V 4, E 6, F 4
             p) n = 3; V 6, E 9, F 5

M8. Plans with heights. Each number is the height of a stack of cubes; "/"
    starts the next row and the FIRST row is at the BACK. Find the number of
    cubes, the front elevation (tallest in each column, left to right) and the
    right side elevation (tallest in each row, FRONT row first, as seen from the
    right).
    a) 3 1 / 1 1               b) 2 2 / 1 3

    i) 0 3 0 / 1 2 1           j) 2 3 1 / 3 0 2 / 1 1 4

    Answers: a) 6 cubes; front 3 1; right 1 3   b) 8 cubes; front 2 3; right 3 2

M9. Count the small cubes. (In the painted cubes the outside of the big cube is

M10. Name the shape of the cross-section.
     a) cube, cut parallel to a face
     b) cube, cut off one corner
     c) cube, cut through two opposite edges (diagonally)
     d) cube, cut through the midpoints of six edges
     e) cuboid, cut parallel to an end
     f) triangular prism, cut parallel to the ends
     g) triangular prism, cut down along its length, parallel to a rectangular
        face

     n) cone, cut at a slant, not touching the base

     r) tetrahedron, cut parallel to a face

              s) circle   t) semicircle

M11. In a) to p) write yes or no: is it a polyhedron? For each yes, give the
     number of faces. In q) to v) two solids are glued together along matching
     faces (no two faces end up flat with each other). Find V, E and F of the
     new solid: the glued face vanishes from both, and its corners and edges are

              s) V 7, E 12, F 7   t) V 11, E 20, F 11   u) V 5, E 9, F 6
              v) V 13, E 24, F 13

M12. Only the front elevation (heights of the columns, left to right) and the
     side elevation (heights of the rows, front to back) of a stack of cubes are
     given; squares of the plan may be empty. Find the LEAST and the GREATEST
     number of cubes that fit both views.
     a) front 2 1; side 2 1       b) front 1 2; side 2 2
     c) front 3 1 2; side 3 2     d) front 2 2 2; side 2 2
     e) front 1 1 1; side 1 1 1   f) front 3 2 1; side 1 2 3
     g) front 2 3; side 3 1 2     h) front 1 2 3; side 3 3 3
     i) front 2 1 2; side 1 2     j) front 3 3 1; side 2 3 1
     k) front 1 3; side 3 1       l) front 2 2; side 2 2 2

              c) least 6, greatest 11   d) least 6, greatest 12
              e) least 3, greatest 9   f) least 6, greatest 14
